bacteriocins (Ruhe et al., 2013). CDI was first observed in E. coli isolate EC93, which deployed a two-partner (type V) secretion system to inhibit other strains of E. coli (Aoki et al., 2005, 2011). Subsequently, a type VI secretion system was also found to mediate interbacterial competition in a contact-dependent manner (Basler and Mekalanos, 2012). These CDI systems enable bacteria to game each other in a deadly match of life, resulting in death involving the deployment of a potent weapon. Both systems confer a substantial competitive growth advantage, suggesting that contact-dependent inhibition plays an important role in shaping bacterial communities. The evidence establishes a paradigm for interbacterial competition based on defensive counterattack with temporal and special precision. Moreover, CDI could facilitate kin discrimination by preventing related but non-isogenic strains from participating in group behaviors. CDI differs from antibiotics-mediated inhibition, since the latter does not require cell–cell contact (Benz and Meinhart, 2014). CDI may have a similar function in host invasion and colonization by some pathogens. Recent studies show that CDI also plays roles in cooperative behaviors such as biofilm formation. The mechanism underlying biofilm phenotype is unclear, but evidence indicates that expression of CDI often mediates cell–cell adhesion, which can promote biofilm formation (Aoki et al., 2011). The coincident expression of CDI with bacterial group behavior suggests that such growth inhibition may be used to enforce cooperation and “punish” cheaters in the populations. In Gram-positive bacteria, type II toxin–antitoxin (TA) systems that are antibacterial effector–immunity modules mediating toxin-inducible death of cells without immunity may be also involved in contact-dependent growth inhibition, playing a role in ecology of microbial biofilms (Benz and Meinhart, 2014; see Section 6 and Chapter 2.7). 

## 21.1.3.5 Escape from competition

Microbial biofilms are social consortia of cells that undergo developmental programs (Webb et al., 2003). It is well known that the final stage of the biofilm life cycle often includes active detachment or dispersal of bacterial cells from biofilms (McDougald et al., 2012). Some bacteria actively detach from a biofilm in response to nutrient depletion, which allows the bacteria to escape from nutrient competition or to move toward a location with better nutrients. Other bacteria disperse from biofilms in response to intra- or extracellular signal molecules, such as cyclic di-GMP (guanosine monophosphate) or quorumsensing signaling molecules (Solano et al., 2014; Wood, 2014; see Section 22). Recent studies show that many bacteria use quorum sensing not only to switch to a biofilm lifestyle but also to regulate biofilm disassembly in a coordinated manner (Solano et al., 2014). In particular, highly motile bacteria that can rapidly disperse into the surroundings can be considered as a strategy for the bacteria to escape from competition in biofilms. As a result, highly motile organisms may have better options to escape from local competition. Some studies also show that active dispersal from biofilms may be typically preceded by localized death or lysis of bacterial cells in the center of mature biofilms (Webb et al., 2003). The dispersal cells escape by coordinated evacuation from breakup points, leading to the characteristic hollowing of biofilm microcolonies that are observed during the dispersal stage of biofilms (Rice and Bayles, 2008). In the dispersal cells, genes that regulate the biofilm phenotypes are often downregulated, whereas genes that encode factors important for a motile lifestyle, including flagella and proteins involved in chemotaxis, are upregulated (Rollet et al., 2009; see Section 22). The evolutionary aspects of biofilm dispersal are now explored through the integration of microbiology and eukaryotic ecology theories, which provide a broad conceptual framework for diversity of specific mechanisms underlying biofilm dispersal (McDougald et al., 2012). 

## 21.1.4 Altruistic cell suicide (programmed cell death) in biofilms

Programmed cell death (PCD) refers to any form of cell death mediated by a genetically regulated program (Rice and Bayles, 2008). Traditionally, PCD is associated with eukaryotic multicellular organisms, also called apoptosis, playing critical roles in the regulation of cell development and homeostasis of eukaryotic organisms by eliminating damaged, excess, or defective cells (Lewis, 2000). PCD is also identified in various bacterial species and is largely involved in regulation of altruistic cell suicide in the developmental cycles of bacteria, such as sporulation in Bacillus subtilis (Lopez et al., 2009), fruiting-body formation in Myxococcus xanthus (Velicer et al., 2000), genetic competence in Streptococcus pneumoniae (Claverys et al., 2006), and biofilm development in Pseudomonas aeruginosa and many other bacteria (Juhas et al., 2005). For example, the life cycle of the Gram-positive bacterium B. subtilis may switch to form endospores when it encounters starvation or an extreme stress (Lopez et al., 2009). During sporulation, a subpopulation of the cells undergoes altruistic cell suicide, called cannibalism (siblings), which may provide last-resort nutrients for the dormant cells (the endospores) that undergo a genetically determined program of sporulation and survive nutritional stress. Interestingly, the subpopulation of cells that differentiate into cannibals is the same subpopulation that produces an extracellular matrix that allows B. subtilis cells to hold together in biofilms (Lopez et al., 2009). It has been found that the developmental pathways of sporulation, cannibalism, and biofilm matrix production are strongly interconnected and regulated by the same master regulator, Spo0A. Interestingly, entry into sporulation in B. subtilis is finely regulated by quorum-sensing-mediated cooperation. 

Competence-induced fratricide in S. pneumoniae is another good example of altruistic cell suicide in bacteria (Claverys and Havarstein, 2007). It is well known that S. pneumoniae develops competence for genetic transformation, which is controlled by a quorum-sensing system (Claverys and Havarstein, 2007). During competence development, a subpopulation of cells become competent and produce two dedicated immunity proteins that can protect themselves from the allolysis factors that trigger fratricide of noncompetent cells. The noncompetence cells commit suicide, leading to the release of cytoplasmic contents, including DNA, into the local environment. This extracellular DNA (eDNA) can contribute to gene transfer, antibiotic resistance, nutrient supply, and biofilm formation (Claverys and Havarstein, 2007; Gilmore and Hass, 2005). 

Induction of both sporulation in B. subtilis and genetic competence in S. pneumoniae involves cell–cell communication mediated by quorum-sensing mechanisms (Claverys and Havarstein, 2007). This suggests that population-wide cooperation is necessary for regulation of sporulation and competence along with altruistic cell suicide of subpopulations. It is an apparently paradoxical behavior, as it does not benefit the cells undergoing suicide but can altruistically benefit survivors through generation of public goods (Tanouchi et al., 2013). As a result, the survivors can pass their genes to the next generation under an extreme condition. This property provides a potential explanation on how PCD can evolve as an extreme form of bacterial cooperation (Engelberg-Kulka et al., 2006). In addition to altruistic cell suicide observed during sporulation or competence, PCD is widely considered as a common mechanism via toxin– antitoxin (TA) systems in many bacteria (Tanouchi et al., 2013; see Section 6 and Chapter 2.7). PCD is also reported to affect biofilm formation and architecture (Sadykov and Bayles, 2012; Webb et al., 2003). 

## 21.1.5 Conflict in bacterial cooperation

It is widely accepted that intraspecies cooperation is a common phenomenon in bacteria. One of the best examples is that Mycococcus xanthus uses quorum-sensing-mediated social cooperation to form fruiting bodies (Velicer et al., 2000). Through cooperation, a subpopulation survives to starvation by forming the fruiting bodies, but most cells in the population that provide cooperation are sacrificed (West et al., 2006). Bacterial cooperative activities controlled by quorum sensing have been described in many other bacteria. From an evolutionary point of view, however, bacterial cooperation for social activities may create a conflict of interest and even potential risk to the population, because individual cells that cooperate can be exploited by selfish individuals or “cheaters” that do not cooperate but reap the benefits from cooperators (Czaran and Hoekstra, 2009; Diggle et al., 2007; Velicer et al., 2000). Specifically, natural selection favors the traits that increase personal reproduction. Any behavior that decreases reproduction but increases that of another organism requires additional explanation. The advantage of cooperation is easy to understand if a population is monoclonal or the fitness cost to individual cells is outweighed by the benefit to the population. However, in many realistic situations, microbial populations are not monoclonal but rather heterogeneous, where cooperators and noncooperators interact. Cooperation provides fitness benefits or public goods to the population, but the population benefits often come at a cost to individuals. Therefore, noncooperators that reap the benefits from cooperators could invade and take over the population, resulting in the tragedy of the commons (Czaran and Hoekstra, 2009). For example, Diggle et al. (2007) examined the social evolution of quorum-sensing populations of Pseudomonas aeruginosa. They found that quorum sensing provided a benefit at the group level, but exploitative cells could reap the benefit and avoid the costs of producing QS signals or performing the cooperative behavior coordinated by QS. They showed that these noncooperators could spread in the populations. This is a conflict between the fitness of individual cells and the fitness of the group. A question then arises as to why individual cells produce costly public goods at their own cost if this increases the fitness of other cells. Cheaters are found to arise in many microbial systems and constitute a major problem for explanation of cooperation. This seems to go completely against Darwin’s idea of natural selection, “survival of the fittest.” Evolutionary biologists suggest that the appearance of group cooperative traits should be viewed with caution, as natural selection almost invariably favors selfishness or cheaters. However, there are at least five mechanisms in which such a conflict can be resolved and evolve in nature (Nowark, 2006). Among these mechanisms, Hamilton’s kin selection theory, which states that cooperation evolves between genetically related individuals or relatives, has been widely used to explain the conflict of cooperation (West et al., 2006). 

Recently, Morris et al. (2012) proposed a new hypothesis, the Black Queen Hypothesis, which provides a perspective explanation on the ecology and evolution of bacterial cooperation. This hypothesis suggests that cooperation among species could be automatic and based upon purely selfish traits. This hypothesis stands in contrast to the Red Queen Hypothesis, which states that species are in constant evolutionary conflict. The Black Queen Hypothesis makes two basic assumptions for bacterial cooperation and community evolution. The first assumption is that some bacterial functions are often leaky, so that these bacteria unavoidably produce publicly usable resources or by-products that benefit others or enhance the fitness of others that can use the products. Examples of such leaky systems include catalase–peroxidase activity (enzymes that break down toxic hydrogen peroxide), production of iron chelators (which make iron soluble for metabolism), and the reduction of sulfur (which is often growth limiting). Such leakiness might be unavoidable because of the nature of diffusion gradients and the need for bacteria to maintain membrane permeability. The second assumption is that an organism that uses another’s byproducts will benefit from deleting its own costly pathways for the products. When genes of vital functions are lost, this builds dependency of the organism into the interaction (Morris et al., 2012). Both assumptions can be applied to cooperation within or among bacterial species. The Black Queen Hypothesis appears to be supported by some empirical studies indicating that many bacteria produce metabolic by-products that are also useful to other species (Freilich et al., 2011; Sachs and Hollowell, 2012). Although not explicitly required in their model, an emergent prediction is that initiation of such dependency can favor the spread of more obligate coevolved partnerships. This new paradigm suggests that bacteria might often form interdependent cooperative interactions in communities. These interactions play important roles in maintaining the diversity and homeostasis in a biofilm community and represent unique opportunities for testing ecological and evolutionary theories. 

## 21.1.6 Coevolution of bacterial cooperation and competition

The impact of local competition on the evolution of microbial cooperation has received growing attention in recent years from both theoretical and experimental studies. For cooperation to be maintained and to evolve within a population, costs due to competition have to be outweighed by the benefits gained through cooperation. Otherwise, defector cells that do not cooperate but reap the benefits from cooperation can thrive and eventually drive the cooperation phenotypes to extinction (Celiker and Gore, 2013; West et al., 2006). How cooperation can evolve within microbial populations remains a major debatable topic. It is generally accepted that bacterial cells of the same species or genotype often cooperate to increase their inclusive fitness (Griffin et al., 2004). However, cooperation between different species is evolutionally harder to explain, especially when nutrient competition is strong (Foster and Bell, 2012). Nevertheless, multispecies biofilms represent a platform where cooperation and competition among species or even strains may potentially coevolve. This is indicated by many examples reporting overall biomass production, synergistic metabolic interactions, and enhanced stress resistance of a microbial community, from which all present species may benefit under certain conditions, depending on a selective pressure (Boyle et al., 2013). For example, coevolution of two bacterial species has been directly demonstrated for a commensal interaction, in which Pseudomonas putida depends on the partner organism, Accinetobacter sp. C6, in order to grow on benzyl alcohol as a sole carbon source. Such an interaction has led to a greater overall growth yield in the co-culture biofilm, despite having a detrimental effect on the growth of Acinetobacter sp. strain C6 (Hansen et al., 2007). Indirect evidence for coevolution of a competitive interaction has also been demonstrated among oral bacteria. Streptococcus mutans, a leading cariogenic pathogen that causes tooth decay, is known to inhibit the growth of many oral species by producing lactic acid from fermentable carbohydrates (Figure 21.1.1). Interestingly, Streptococcus oligofermentans, another resident member in the same community, has developed the counteroffensive strategy of using this lactic acid to generate hydrogen peroxide that is inhibitory to S. mutans (Tong et al., 2007). Importantly, such interbacterial interactions in a community affect its overall function, antimicrobial resistance, virulence, and pathogenic potential, which are all important to human health and diseases. In addition, bacterial species in complex communities have coevolved for thousands of years, which may lead to interspecies dependency, as described by the Black Queen Hypothesis (Morris et al., 2012). Due to the development of such dependencies, it is possible that the interacting organisms in a community may function better together than individually alone, particularly under environmental stresses. We have learned from recent studies that bacterial competition, by driving diversification, can create bacterial groups that are both more productive and more resistant to defection (Hibbing et al., 2010; Xavier, 2011). Thus, intermicrobial interactions can be considered as major driving forces for coevolution of diverse species in natural biofilms. 

## 21.1.7 Concluding remarks

Bacteria in nature live predominantly in biofilms, which allows the resident members to establish complex social interactions and networks in the biofilm community. Bacterial interactions can occur among or within species and can be either competitive or cooperative (see Section 22). It is now well recognized that cooperation within the same species against competitors is a widespread phenomenon and can occur more frequently in nature than was previously thought. Indeed, cooperation is needed for evolution to construct new levels of organization. However, natural selection implies that competition opposes cooperation unless a specific mechanism is at work. In recent years, the mechanisms behind this conflict have been elucidated by evolutionary theories and experiments. Great progress has been made in our understanding of bacterial cooperation, competition, and effects on microbial ecology and evolution. In particular, the discovery of widespread quorum-sensing mechanisms in bacteria is crucial for guiding researchers to study bacterial multicellular behaviors, rather than the previous emphasis on individual cell biology. Quorumsensing-mediated activities are emerging as an integral component of bacterial social cooperation, profoundly affecting bacterial physiology, ecology, and evolution. Analyses of bacterial interactions have shown that bacteria often cooperate to resist competition of other species. It is therefore reasonable to speculate that similar cooperative traits may be required for a given pathogen to initiate an infectious disease. However, research on how bacterial quorum sensing works mechanistically in biofilms and the extent to which bacterial cooperation can be maintained in nature remains to be studied. Answering these questions will undoubtedly provide new insights into bacterial cooperative behaviors, ecology, and roles in infectious diseases.

## Kok-Gan Chan, Wai-Fong Yin, and Kar-Wai Hong

Division of Genetics and Molecular Biology, Institute of Biological Sciences, Faculty of Science, University of Malaya, Kuala Lumpur, Malaysia 

## 21.2.1 Introduction

Natural environments harbor a dazzlingly complicated microbiome in which bacteria live in communities with fascinating complexity, forming complicated networks of antagonistic and facilitative interactions (Becker et al., 2012; Cordero et al., 2012). Individual bacterial cells communicate via quorum sensing (QS), cooperate to obtain benefits from collective behaviors, compete for resources via all kinds of strategies, and even kill one another. Nature is lavish with examples of complex communication, cooperation, interference of communication, and even cheating among organisms (Hibbing et al., 2010; Strassmann and Queller, 2011). QS is widespread throughout the Eubacteria domain, enabling bacterial populations to coordinately regulate gene expression in a population-dependent manner in response to the diffusible chemical signals, which are commonly known as autoinducers, produced and released into the surrounding environment by themselves or other bacteria of either the same or different species. As the bacterial population thrives, the concentration of the autoinducer increases and reaches the threshold concentration at which the autoinducer binds to and activates the targeted cognate autoinducer receptor protein, provoking a series of gene expression reprogramming and thus triggering physiological responses in all members of the population. Consequently, this allows the bacterial population to alter their growth dynamics and act as a community (Atkinson and Williams, 2009; Fuqua et al., 2001; Williams et al., 2007; see also Chapter 2.18). 

## 21.2.2 Cell-to-cell communication in the microbial world

The field of cell-to-cell signaling and coordinated microbial group behavior was initiated half a century ago (Tomasz, 1965). Throughout this half-century, several QS signaling molecules have been identified. Gram-negative bacteria, mainly from the phylum Proteobacteria, employ the low-molecular-weight N-acylhomoserine lactone (AHL) to communicate. The AHL molecules diffuse across the membranes and bind to the LuxR proteins in the cytoplasm, hence acting as transcriptional regulators of an array of genes (Withers et al., 2001; see Chapter 21.3). One of the members of Proteobacteria, Pseudomonas aeruginosa, appears to be very “multilingual” as more than one type of signaling molecule is employed. P. aeruginosa has a complex yet hierarchical QS network that integrates the AHLdependent QS system with another QS system, which uses 2- heptyl 3-hydroxy-4-quinolone (the Pseudomonas quinolone signal) (PQS) and 4-hydroxy-2-heptyl-quinolone (HHQ) as signaling molecules (Diggle et al., 2003; Pesci et al., 1999). In 1999, Holden and colleagues discovered the third signaling molecule of P. aeruginosa − diketopiperazines (DKP) (Holden et al., 1999). Some Gram-negative bacteria respond to lipid-based signaling molecules such as 3-hydroxypalmitic acid methyl ester (3OH-PAME) and cis-11-methyl-2-dodecenoic acid (diffusible signal factor (DSF), which are involved in regulating the virulence of Ralstonia solanacearum and Xanthomonas campestris pv. campestris, respectively (Flavier et al., 1997; Wang et al., 2004a,b). P. aeruginosa also produces cis-2- decenoic acid, which is a member of the DSF signal family, in order to regulate biofilm dispersion and development (Davies and Marques, 2009; see Section 22). A furanosyl borate diester (autoinducer-2) (AI2)-dependent QS system has been reported in both Gram-positive and Gram-negative bacteria. The precursor of AI2 − 4,5-dihydroxy-2,3-pentanedione (DPD) – exists in equilibrium with several interconvertible isoforms in solution, all of which are referred to as the family of AI2 signaling molecules (Schauder et al., 2001; Xavier and Bassler, 2003). Another group of signaling molecules from the γ-butyrolactones family, 2-isocapryloyl-3R-hydroxymethyl-γ-butyrolactone (Afactor), has been reported to regulate antibiotic production and morphological differentiation in Streptomyces species (Ohnishi et al., 1999). Besides these lower molecular weight signaling molecules, high-molecular-weight posttranslationally modified peptides are engaged by Gram-positive bacteria as signaling molecules. These signaling peptides, known as autoinducing peptides (AIPs), range from 5 to 34 amino acids in length and are in either linear or cyclical conformations, forming three different families of AIPs (i.e., oligopeptide lantibiotics, thiolactone peptides, and isoprenylated tryptophan peptides) (Thoendel and Horswill, 2010; Williams et al., 2007) and regulating a wide spectrum of cellular activities, such as competence development (Tortosa and Dubnau, 1999) and production of virulence factors (Chan et al., 2004; Jimenez and Federle, 2014; Lyon et al., 2002). 

The QS mechanism, mediated by AHLs, is a well-understood and frequently investigated bacterial communication mechanism. AHLs are essentially composed of homoserine lactone rings that are connected via an amide bond to an acyl chain with variable lengths and different degrees of saturation of the acyl side chain. The third carbon position may carry different functional groups – such as a carbonyl group, a hydroxyl group, and fully reduced forms (Bandara et al., 2012; Shaw et al., 1997). AHL-based QS comprises three main parts: (1) biosynthesis of AHL by LuxI (AHL-synthase protein); (2) transportation of AHL molecules, notably long-chain AHLs; and (3) binding of AHL molecules to the cognate AHL receptor protein (LuxR), followed by activation or deactivation of QSregulated genes (Fuqua and Greenberg, 2002). The biosynthesis of AHL by the LuxI protein proceeds via a sequential ordered reaction mechanism, utilizing S-adenosylmethionine (SAM) as the amino donor in the formation of the homoserine lactone ring moiety and an acylated acyl carrier protein (Acyl-ACP) as the acyl side chain donor. The amide bond formation between the amino nitrogen on the methionine moiety of SAM and the first carbon of the acyl chain conjugates these precursors, impelling the release of apo-ACP. AHL is released upon the lactonization of the homoserine ring along with the 5′ - methylthioadenosine, which derived from SAM (Figure 21.2.1a) (Fuqua and Parsek 2002; Parsek et al., 1999). Non-fatty acyl groups can also be incorporated into the homoserine lactone– based signaling molecules, such as p-coumaryl-homoserine lactone, which is produced from p-coumaric acid rather than fatty acid (Schaefer et al., 2008). 

Over 100 species of Gram-negative Proteobacteria are known to produce AHLs, regulating a broad spectrum of bacterial phenotypes, including biofilm formation, root nodulation, sporulation, plasmid conjugation, as well as biosynthesis of secondary metabolites, including virulence factors (Miller and Bassler, 2001; Williams et al., 2007). Interestingly, AHL production has also been observed in extremophiles such as the haloalkaliphilic archaeon Natronococcus occultus, which inhabits an alkaline biotope (pH 10) (Paggi et al., 2003), as well as the acidophilic γ-Proteobacterium Acidithiobacillus ferroxidans, which resides in an acidic environment (pH 1.5) (Farah et al., 2005; Rivas et al., 2007). Another non-Proteobacteria that also produces AHLs or AHL-like molecules as signaling molecules is the epilithic cyanobacterium Gloeothece sp. 

strain PCC6909 (Sharif et al., 2008). In 2010, Tenacibaculum maritimum, a filamentous, biofilm-forming member of the Cytophaga–Flavobacterium–Bacteroides group, was also found to produce AHLs (Romero et al., 2010). Furthermore, a member from the methanogenic archaeon − Methanosaeta harundinacea 6Ac – has been reported to synthesize a group of carboxylated AHL with 10 to 14 carbon atoms in the acyl chain (Zhang et al., 2012). 

## 21.2.3 Jamming the communication

Microbes regulate a wide spectrum of competitive determinants via QS in order to triumph in belligerent environments. One possible strategy by which the microbes could prevent succumbing to the competition would be to interfere with the signaling system of other microbial species (Hibbing et al., 2010). Interference with a bacterial communication system, commonly known as quorum quenching (QQ), can be achieved by targeting the AHL biosynthesis mechanism, the AHL molecules, as well as the AHL receptor proteins (Hong et al., 2012). The AHL biosynthesis mechanism can be interrupted by inhibiting the enzymes involved, such as the enzymes involved in the biosynthesis of acyl carrier protein (ACP), SAM synthase, and LuxI proteins (Hong et al., 2012; Parveen and Cornell, 2011). Besides, inhibition of the AHL efflux protein has been suggested as one of the mechanisms that interferes with QS, impeding the accumulation of AHLs in the environment (Dong et al., 2007). The inactivation of AHL molecules causes the molecules to lose their function as signaling molecules. Several groups of AHL-inactivating enzymes have been reported (i.e., AHL-lactonase, AHL-acylase, as well as AHL-oxidoreductase) (Hong et al., 2012). Preventing the AHL molecules from binding to the LuxR protein will definitely halt the communication and thus prevent the expression of QS-regulated genes (Chen et al., 2011; Koch et al., 2005). 

## 21.2.4 Quorum-quenching enzymes

Based on the chemical structure of the AHL molecules, Dong and Zhang postulated four possible ways that AHL degradation may occur: lactonase and decarboxylase, which hydrolyze the lactone ring, as well as acylase and deaminase, which separate the acyl side chain from the homoserine lactone moiety (Dong and Zhang, 2005). An AHL-lactonase hydrolyzes the ester bond of the lactone ring, forming acyl homoserine, causing the failure of docking to the LuxR proteins, and thus attenuating the QS mechanism (Figure 21.2.1) (Dong et al., 2000). In comparison, AHL-acylase, which is also known as AHLamidohydrolase, cleaves the amide bond of the lactone ring, releasing the homoserine lactone moiety and the fatty acid, and causing significant loss of function of the signaling molecules (Figure 21.2.1, parts 3a and 3b) (Lin et al., 2003). 

In 2005, another group of AHL-inactivating enzymes was discovered. These are AHL-oxidoreductases, which do not involve destruction of AHL but retain the function of AHL as signaling molecules; the modified AHL may fail to bind to its cognate LuxR protein, hence causing disruption of QS-regulated gene expression (Figure 21.2.1, parts 1 and 2) (Hong et al., 2012).

Figure 21.2.1 (a) The biosynthesis of the signaling molecule N-acyl homoserine lactone (AHL) involves a sequential ordered reaction mechanism, in which the S-adenosylmethionine (SAM) and acylated acyl carrier protein (acyl-ACP) donate the homoserine lactone ring and acyl side chain, respectively, forming the AHL. (b) The low-molecular-weight AHLs will diffuse across the membrane bilayer of bacteria. (c) When the AHLs in the external environment are high, and therefore the AHLs molecules diffuse back into the cell and thus bind to the LuxR receptor protein. (d) This complex then undergoes a signaling cascade, activating a series of QS-regulated genes. (e) When the AHLs are in the external environment, they are exposed to a series of QQ enzymes. (1) CYP102A1 of Bacillus megaterium reduces the acyl chain of AHLs at the ω-1, ω-2, and ω-3 positions. (2) Oxidoreductases from Rhodococcus erythropolis W2 and Burkholderia sp. GG4 substitute the oxo group at the C3 position with the hydroxy group, causing signaling confusion. (3) Acylases cleave the amide bond of AHLs by releasing fatty acids and homoserine lactones. (4) AHL-lactonases hydrolyze the ester bond of lactone rings, forming acyl homoserine.


## 21.2.5 AHL-lactonases

AHL-lactonases have been discovered in diverse organisms, spanning across different phyla and even kingdoms, from Proteobacteria to Actinobacteria, and from Archaea to Fungi. In general, AHL-lactonases can be categorized into several protein superfamilies: metallo-β-lactamase (MBL), phosphotriesteraselike lactonase (PLL), the α/β hydrolase fold, paraoxonase, the glycosyl hydrolase family, the dienelactone hydrolase family, and others (LaSarre and Federle, 2013). 

One of the first described and best characterized AHLlactonases is AiiA (autoinducer inactivation protein), which was purified from Bacillus sp. strain 240B1 and various species of the Bacillus genus (Dong et al., 2000, 2002). It is a 250-amino-acid AHL-lactonase that belongs to the MBL superfamily with a characteristic conserved zinc-binding motif of HXHXDH∼H. The crystal structure of AiiA from the B. thuringiensis strain shows the presence of two Zn2+ atoms at the active site of the enzyme. The presence of the HXHXDH∼H zinc-binding motif serves as the canonical signature motif for the enzyme to be classified as AHL-lactonase (Kim et al., 2005). According to Wang and colleagues, the hydrolysis of the ester bond of AHL is metal independent (2004a,b); however, subsequent work by three different groups, using different isoforms of AiiA from different strains of B. thuringiensis (i.e., B. thuringiensis subsp kurstaki and 

B. thuringiensis strain 4A3), has shown that both isoforms of AiiA lactonases are stereoselective dinuclear metalloproteins with two $Z \mathrm { n } ^ { 2 + }$ ions bound in close proximity to each other at the active site (Kim et al., 2005; Liu et al., 2005; Thomas et al., 2005). This led to the proposal of a nucleophilic attack by a single oxygen of the bridging carboxylate and a water–hydroxide bridging the two zinc ions at the dinuclear site on the carbonyl carbon of the AHL molecules (Liao et al., 2009; Liu et al., 2005; Momb et al., 2008). Furthermore, Momb and colleagues have shown that the zinc ions play a crucial role in maintaining proper folding of the protein (Momb et al., 2008). 

In the past few years, besides members of the genus Bacillus, several AHL-lactonase-producing bacteria along with their lactonase that belong to the MBL superfamily have been identified, such as AttM and AiiB of Agrobacterium tumefaciens (Carlier et al., 2003; Khan and Farrand, 2009; Liu et al., 2007; Zhang et al., 2002), AhlD of Arthrobacter sp. IBN110 (Park et al., 2003), AidC of Chryseobacterium StRB126 (Rashid et al., 2011; Wang et al., 2012), AhlK of Klebsiella pneumoniae (Park et al., 2003), QsdR1 of Rhizobium sp. NGR234 (Krysciak et al., 2011), and AhlS of Solibacillus silvestris (Morohoshi et al., 2009, 2012). Additionally, QlcA, which is an AHL-lactonase from the MBL superfamily, has been identified from a soil metagenomic study (Riaz et al., 2007). Phylogenetic studies of QlcA reveal that the closest relative is found in the Bradyrhizobium spp., members of the α-Proteobacteria (Riaz et al., 2007). In general, AHL-lactonases from the MBL superfamily exhibit a broad spectrum of AHL specificity, especially medium- to long-chain AHLs, regardless of the substitution on carbon 3 (Table 21.2.1). 

In 2010, Chow and colleagues discovered a thermostable AHL-lactonase, GKL, purified from Geobacillus kaustophilus HTA426 (Chow et al., 2010). AHL-lactonase GKL is a member of the phosphotriesterase (PTE)-like lactonase (PLL) group of enzymes within the amidohydrolase superfamily, which bear a signature dinuclear metal center within a $( \beta / \alpha ) _ { 8 } .$ -barrel structural scaffold, commonly known as the triosephosphate isomerase (TIM)-barrel (Chow et al., 2010). This dinuclear metal center engages in the activation of the water molecules in order to initiate a nucleophilic attack on an activated scissile bond of the AHLs. Resolution of the reaction intermediate results in subsequent hydrolysis, yielding the hydrolyzed acylhomoserine (Chow et al., 2010). GKL displays a low meta-dependency on N-acylhomoserine lactonase activity, and $Z \mathrm { n } ^ { 2 + }$ -reconstituted GKL displayed a substrate preference for medium- to long-chain AHLs. Furthermore, it shows a high affinity and high efficiency in the degradation of γ-nonalactone and δ-nonalactone. However, it has a relatively lower paraoxonase activity, suggesting the native substrate profile of GKL does not involve phosphate esters (Afriat et al., 2006; Chow et al., 2010). Similarly, G. stearothermophilus has been reported to produce a highly thermostable PTE-like lactonase (i.e., lactonase GsP). GsP exhibits relatively low AHL lactonase activity toward N-hexanoyl-homoserine lactone (C6-HSL) but a relatively high catalytic efficiency toward methyl paraoxon and δ-undecanoic lactone, suggesting that GsP can barely be regarded as an AHL-lactonase (Hawwa et al., 2009). 

Besides members from the Eubacteria domain, several members from the Archaea domain have been reported to produce PLL group AHL-lactonases. Sulfolobus solfataricus strain MT4 has been reported to produce an AHL-lactonase – SsoPox, which is an aryldialkylphosphatase (EC 3.1.8.1), a member of the PLL group of the amidohydrolase superfamily metalloenzymes (Afriat et al., 2006; Merone et al., 2005; Ng et al., 2011). SsoPox carries a heterobinuclear center, located at the C-terminus of the β-barrel, constituted by an iron and a cobalt cation at the α and β sites, respectively (Elias et al., 2008). The heterobinuclear metal center consists of $\mathrm { C o } ^ { 2 + }$ and $\mathrm { F e } ^ { 3 + }$ , assisting substrate binding together with an activated water molecule in order to hydrolyze the AHLs (Elias et al., 2008). A similar AHL-lactonase has been discovered in S. islandicus, which produces a PLL group AHL-lactonase, SisLac, which is able to hydrolyze various AHLs as well as γ- and δ-lactones. Both enzymes (i.e., SsoPox and SisLac) have a preference for long aliphilic chain lactones, preferably the N-octanoyl-homoserine lactone (C8-HSL) or longer (Hiblot et al., 2012; Ng et al., 2011). 

Other than GKL of G. kaustophilus HTA426, SsoPox of S. solfataricus, and SisLac of S. islandicus, several AHLlactonases from the PLL group have been discovered, such as QsdA (or AhlA) in Rhodococcus erythropolis W2 (Afriat et al., 2006; Uroz et al., 2008), MCP in Mycobacterium avium subsp. paratuberculosis K-10 (Chow et al., 2009), and PPH in Mycobacterium tuberculosis (Table 21.2.1) (Afriat et al., 2006). 

Besides MBL and PLL types of AHL-lactonase, AHLlactonases from other protein superfamilies have been discovered and characterized. For example, AidH of Ochrobactrum sp. T63 (Mei et al., 2010) and AiiM of Microbacterium testaceum StLB037 (Wang et al., 2010) both are from the α/β hydrolase fold family. The crystal structure of AidH suggests that the catalytic mechanism of AidH is metal independent, and its catalytic mechanism is proposed to be a typical acid–base catalysis and covalent catalysis (Gao et al., 2012). QsdH of Pseudoalteromonas byunsanensis encompasses two catalytic domains $( i . e .$ , a N-terminal GDSL hydrolase fold family protein and a C-terminal RND-type multidrug efflux transporter domain) (Huang et al., 2012). AHL-lactonase DlhR of Rhizobium sp. NGR234 as well as BpiB07, identified from the soil metagenome, are the members of the dienelactone hydrolase family (Krysciak et al., 2011; Schipper et al., 2009). From the metagenomic sample, several other unique AHL-lactonases have been discovered, such as BpiB04, which belongs to the glycosyl hydrolase family, as well as BpiB01 and BpiB05, which reveal no conserved domains (Bijtenhoorn et al., 2011a,b; Schipper et al., 2009). 

In 2004, Chun and colleagues discovered a significant AHL inactivation activity in human epithelial cells (Chun et al., 2004). Subsequent investigations have identified this AHLinactivating enzyme as paraoxonase, which hydrolyzes the AHL molecules via lactonase-like activity (Yang et al., 2005). PONs,

including PON1, PON2, and PON3, are human enzymes that hydrolyze organophosphates, esters, and lactones. The genes that encode these enzymes are located adjacent to each other on the long arm of chromosome 7q21.3-22.1 (Draganov et al., 2005; Primo-Parmo et al., 1996). PON1 is a six-bladed β- propeller with a unique active-site lid and two $\mathrm { C a } ^ { 2 + }$ ions in the central tunnel (Harel et al., 2007). PONs play an important role in lipid metabolism (Harel et al., 2007), organophosphate detoxification, and even defense against bacterial infections (Draganov et al., 2005; Harel et al., 2007). PONs are commonly viewed as the exclusive eukaryotic AHL-inactivating enzymes. In 2013, Bar-Rogovsky and colleagues identified a family of bacterial PONs, namely PONX OCCAL, from the α-Proteobacteria Oceanicaulis alexandrii HTCC2633 (Bar-Rogovsky et al., 2013). PONX OCCAL degrades N-3-oxododecanoyl-homoserine lactone (3-oxo-C12-HSL) effectively, and, like the mammalian PON2, it lacks the lactonase activity against the lipophilic $\gamma -$ and δ-lactones (Bar-Rogovsky et al., 2013). 

Lactonases comparatively have a very broad AHL substrate specificity (Table 21.2.1). It is likely due to the facts that the targeted homoserine lactone ring is universally conserved among all AHL species and the variable acyl chain interacts only nonspecifically with the active-site cavity of the lactonase. 

## 21.2.6 AHL-acylases

AHL-acylases hydrolyze the acyl–amide bond between the acyl tail and lactone ring of AHLs in an irreversible manner, resulting in the release of a fatty acid chain and a homoserine lactone moiety (Figure 21.2.1, parts 3a and 3b) (Hong et al., 2012). The first reported AHL-acylase originates from Variovorax paradoxus VAI-C, which is able to utilize AHL molecules as the source of energy and nitrogen (Leadbetter and Greenberg, 2000). 

Shortly after the discovery of an AHL-acylase, another AHLacylase, PvdQ (PA2385), from P. aeruginosa was identified (Huang et al., 2003). This enzyme belongs to the N-terminal nucleophile aminohydrolase (Ntn hydrolase) superfamily, based on its signature heterodimeric organization (Bokhove et al., 2010; Oinonen and Rouvinen, 2000). PvdQ is expressed as a proenzyme that is autoproteolytically activated posttranslationally, resulting in the excision of a 23-residue prosegment, followed by the formation of a 60 kDa β-chain and an 18 kDa α-chain, forming a highly conserved four-layered catalytically active αββα-core structure (Bokhove et al., 2010; Oinonen and Rouvinen, 2000). PvdQ degrades medium- to long-chain AHLs regardless of the substitution group at the C3 position. Detailed descriptions of the PvdQ protein structure and the catalytic mechanism of AHL hydrolysis of PvdQ are discussed by Bokhove and colleagues (Bokhove et al., 2010). Numerous reports have shown that P. aeruginosa produces 3-oxo-C12- HSL’s (Jimenez et al., 2012). Therefore, the concurrent presence of both QS and QQ systems within the same microorganism suggests the possible roles of PvdQ acylase in regulating the level of AHLs being produced and thus manipulating the phenotype of the bacteria. Huang and colleagues also showed that pvdQ knockout mutants are capable of metabolizing 3- oxo-C12-HSL, indicating the possible presence of another AHLdegrading enzyme (Huang et al., 2003). Soon after, Huang and colleagues discovered another AHL-acylase, QuiP (PA1032), residing in the genome of P. aeruginosa (Huang et al., 2006). This second AHL-acylase shares 21% amino acid identities with PvdQ and belongs to the Ntn hydrolase superfamily. It is predicted to be secreted into the periplasm and predicted to be cleaved into four peptides: a signal sequence, two peptides corresponding to the α and β subunits, and a spacer peptide. Similar to PvdQ acylase, QuiP has the preference to degrade mediumto long-chain AHLs (Huang et al., 2003). 

Another two AHL-acylases, HacA (Psyr 1971) and HacB (Psyr 4858), were discovered in the genome of Pseudomonas syringae B728a. Both belong to the Ntn hydrolase superfamily, in which the former is a secreted acylase that degrades only long-chain-AHLs, ranging from C8-HSL to C12-HSL, while the latter is a nonsecreted acylase that possesses a wide AHL substrate specificity, that is, from N-butanoyl-homoserine lactone (C4-HSL) to N-dodecanoyl-homoserine lactone (C12-HSL) (Shepherd and Lindow, 2009). 

Other than the described AHL-acylases produced by P. aeruginosa, several other AHL-acylases have been discovered, such as AiiD of Ralstonia sp. XJ12B (Lin et al., 2003), AaC of R. solanacearum strain GM1000 (Chen et al., 2009), AhlM of Streptomyces sp. strain M664 (Park et al., 2005), AaC of Shewanella sp. strain M1B015 (Morohoshi et al., 2008), AiiO of Ochrobactrum sp. strain A44 (Czajkowski et al., 2011), AiiC of Anabaena sp. PCC7120 (Romero et al., 2008), and AibP of Brucella melitensis strain 16M (ATCC 23456) (Terwagne et al., 2013). There are several microorganisms that have been reported to produce AHL-acylase, but the AHLacylase-encoding genes have not been found, for example R. erythropolis strain W2 (Uroz et al., 2005), Comamonas sp. D1 (Uroz et al., 2007), and Tenacibaculum maritimum NCIMB2154 (Table 21.2.1) (Romero et al., 2010). 

## 21.2.7 AHL-oxidoreductases

An alternative strategy for the interference of AHL-dependent communication is via enzymatic modification of the signaling molecules, which occurs commonly at the N-acyl side chain, such that the signaling molecules are not destroyed but modified. The signaling molecules are no longer able to bind specifically to cognate LuxR receptor proteins, thereby causing disturbance of the activation of the AHL-mediated genes. This phenomenon is known as signaling confusion (Hong et al., 2012). 

The first AHL-oxidoreductase was reported by Uroz and colleagues, in which the R. erythropolis W2 reduces the 3-oxosubstituted AHLs to their corresponding 3-hydroxy derivatives (Figure 21.2.1, part 2) (Uroz et al., 2005). This oxidoreductase activity is not stereospecific as it reduces both - and - isomers of 3-oxo-C12-HSL. Additionally, this enzyme reduces diverse substrates ranging from 3-oxo-AHLs to N-(3-oxo-6- phenylhexanoyl) homoserine lactone, which possess an aromatic acyl chain substituent, and 3-oxo-dodecanamide, which lacks the homoserine lactone ring (Uroz et al., 2005). As R. erythropolis W2 possesses a QsdA AHL-lactonase, an AHLacylase, and an AHL-oxidoreductase, it will first modify the AHL molecules via AHL-oxidoreductase followed by the degradation of modified AHLs through an amidolytic activity (Uroz et al., 2005). 

Burkholderia sp. strain GG4, isolated from a ginger rhizosphere, is also capable of reducing 3-oxo-AHLs to the corresponding hydroxyl-AHL (Chan et al., 2011). This will impede the bacteria that rely on 3-oxo-AHLs as signaling molecules. At the same time, this will trigger a premature expression of QS-regulated genes among the bacteria that rely on hydroxyl-AHLs as signaling molecules, due to the binding of nonnative hydroxyl-AHL to its LuxR receptor. Therefore, signaling confusion will have an impact on the QS system in microbial microhabitats. 

The cytochrome P450 monooxygenase CYP102A1 from B. megaterium CYP102A1, which is also known as P450BM-3, catalyzes the hydroxylation of the AHLs as well as the lactonaseand acylase-degraded products of AHLs, namely acyl homoserine and fatty acids, at the ω-1, ω-2, and ω-3 carbon of the acyl chain, independent of the presence or absence of the 3-oxo group at the C3 position (Figure 21.2.1, part 1). The oxidized AHL products still retain the function as signaling molecules but are significantly less active than the native unmodified AHLs (Chowdhary et al., 2007). 

A metagenome-derived AHL-oxidoreductase (i.e., BpiB09) catalyzes the reduction of 3-oxo-C12-HSL to 3-hydroxy-C12- HSL. It is a member of the short-chain dehydrogenase–reductase (SDR) superfamily that requires NADPH as a cofactor, and it has a typical Rossman fold with a central β-sheet flanked by three helices at both sides (Bijtenhoorn et al., 2011a,b). The active substrate binding site is situated at the variable C-terminal region with the presence of a catalytic tetrad. Expression of bpiB09 heterologously in P. aeruginosa PA01 impedes the transcription of QS-regulated genes; reduces pyocyanin production, motility, and biofilm formation; and attenuates virulence in Caenorhabditis elegans. BpiB09 also interferes with the biosynthesis of 3-oxo-C12-HSL by reducing free 3-oxo-acyl-ACP in the cell (Bijtenhoorn et al., 2011a,b). 

## 21.2.8 Quorum-quenching fungi

AHL-degrading activities have been reported among the fungal species, such as Meliniomyces variabilis JH23 and Trichosporon loubieri strain WW1C, as well as strains belonging to the Ascomycota and Basidiomycota lineages (Uroz and Heinonsalo, 2008; Wong et al., 2013). Cheng and colleagues have shown that T. loubieri strain WW1C possesses a heatsensitive lactonase that has a substrate specificity toward shortchain AHLs and metabolizes AHL molecules as the source of energy (Wong et al., 2013). Similarly, Meliniomyces variabilis JH23 and mycorrhizal basiodiomycote isolate JH7 have been shown to produce AHL-lactonase, but the ascomycete isolate JH8 showed a non-lactonase AHL degradation activity, suggesting the occurrence of another enzymatic activity in this fungus (Table 21.2.1) (Uroz and Heinonsalo, 2008). 

## 21.2.9 The biological role of quorum quenching

The wide spread of QQ activities across multiple phyla within the domain of Eubacteria and even across domains (Archaea and Eukaryota) is striking. This has raised questions regarding the biological role of QQ (Table 21.2.1). 

A possible explanation regarding the occurrence of AHLdegrading enzymes in the kingdom of fungi could be for the purpose of metabolism and survival. The fungus T. loubieri strain WW1C has been reported to possess the capability to metabolize AHLs as a source of energy and nitrogen (Wong et al., 2013). 

According to Chan and colleagues, the fungal AHL-lactonases could play an important role in winning the harsh competition of the rhizosphere (Chan et al., 2013). Pseudomonas has been proposed as a biocontrol agent for phytopathogens due to its production of potent antifungal agents, such as phenazine, which is regulated by AHL (Chin-A-Woeng et al., 2000; Walsh et al., 2001; Wood et al., 1997). Hence, it is conceivable that the fungi have evolutionarily generated an antidote to prevent the production of antifungal phenazine, which is destructive to their fitness (Chan et al., 2013). 

All three mammalian PONs – PON1, PON2, and PON3 – are able to hydrolyze 3-oxo-C12-HSL, the signaling molecule of P. aeruginosa, which plays a crucial role in regulating the expression of virulence factors, reducing biofilm formation, and downregulating other virulence factor production (Draganov et al., 2005; Ozer et al., 2005; Teiber et al., 2008). 

In microorganisms, one of the possible roles of AHL degradation could be related to competition. According to Czajkowski and Jafra, the production of AHL-inactivating enzymes increases the fitness of the QQ enzyme-producers, and they thus outcompete the AHL-producing species in a natural environment where the resources are scarce (Czajkowski and Jafra, 2009). This postulation was supported by an experiment where an aiiA-defective B. thuringiensis mutant exhibited a relatively lower survival rate as well as reduced rhizosphere competence and adaptability in comparison with the wild type on the plant root system (Park et al., 2008). 

Kaufmann and colleagues have shown that the signaling molecule 3-oxo-C12-HSL can spontaneously reorganize to form a tetramic acid degradation product, 3-(1-hydroxydecylidene)- 5-(2-hydroxyethyl)pyrrolidine-2,4-dione, non-enzymatically. Both 3-oxo-C12-HSL and the tetramic acids are potent antibacterial agents for diverse Gram-positive bacteria (Kaufmann et al., 2005). Moreover, these tetramic acids are effective metal-chelating agents, targeting a wide spectrum of metal cations including iron, acting as a primordial siderophore. Some tetramic acid–metal complexes display elevated toxicity, thus suggesting that the 3-oxo-C12-HSL, tetramic acids, as well as the tetramic acid–metal complexes can serve as antibacterial agents and give the AHL-producer (i.e., P. aeruginosa) an increased fitness. Therefore, by degrading the deleterious 3-oxo-AHLs, the toxicity of the AHLs is mitigated and the formation of the tetramic acids and tetramic acid complexes is obviated, thus interfering with the signaling network of the competitors, allaying the competition for iron in the natural environment, impairing the fitness of the AHL-producers, and finally enhancing the survival of the AHL-inactivating enzyme producers (Kaufmann et al., 2005). 

AHL-oxidoreductase of B. megaterium CYP102A1 decreases AHL-regulated activities but does not cause a loss-of-function of the signaling molecules. It is hypothesized that this mechanism renders the reduced N-acyl homoserine as well as the modified AHLs more membrane permeable and more water soluble, hence preventing the accumulation of AHLs within the cell by enhancing their diffusion out of the cell (Chowdhary et al., 2007). 

A. tumefaciens expresses two AHL-lactonases, AttM and AiiB, in order to modulate the QS-regulated conjugation and transfer of tumor-including (Ti) plasmid, by regulating the level of 3-oxo-C8-HSL (Haudecoeur et al., 2009; Hwang et al., 1994). AttM appears to lower the Ti plasmid conjugation frequencies at early stages of tumor development. It also reduces the aggressiveness of the bacteria on plants. Haudecoeur and colleagues reported that the aiiB mutant was more virulent than the corresponding wild type, suggesting that AiiB plays an important role in regulating the virulence of the bacteria (Haudecoeur et al., 2009). These prevent the bacteria from killing the plant; therefore, the bacteria can continue to survive and propagate on the plant by imbibing vital resources from the plant (Chan et al., 2013). 

P. aeruginosa harbors two AHL-acylases, PvdQ and QuiP. Several theories regarding the biological roles of these AHLacylases have been suggested. Huang and colleagues suggested that QuiP might play a role in AHL utilization, maturation of pyoverdine siderophore, as well as modulation of the level of 3-oxo-C12-HSL, which in turn regulates the 3-oxo-C12-HSLregulated physiological functions (Huang et al., 2003; Lehoux et al., 2000). Huang and colleagues also suggested that the QuiP could be playing an important role in distinguishing the subpopulation spatially, particularly in the biofilm state (see Section 22) (Huang et al., 2006). Furthermore, according to Jimener, PvdQ also participates in iron homeostasis (see Section 19) by playing a role in pyoverdine synthesis (Jimenez et al., 2010). Sio and colleagues suggested that the PvdQ modulates QS-regulated virulence phenotypes, including the swarming motility, biofilm formation, as well as production of virulence factors such as elastase and pyocyanin (Sio et al., 2006). Two independent reports have shown that lasI and pvdQ mutants display reduced swarming motility, suggesting that a specific concentration of 3-oxo-C12- HSL is important for the swarming motility (Wang et al., 2011; Yeung et al., 2011). According to Wang and colleagues, PvdQ could be involved in antibiotic resistance by altering the membrane permeability of the outer membrane via upregulation of the lipopolysaccharide-related operon (see Section 18) (Wang et al., 2011). PvdQ is believed to be involved in modulating the expression of the outer membrane TonB-dependent receptors, OprD and OprF, in which OprD facilitates the transport of basic amino acids and the imipenem antibiotic, while OprF participates in the binding of gamma interferon (IFNγ) (a cytokine that is critical for both innate and adaptive immunity against infections) (Bodilis and Barray, 2006; Fito-Boncompte et al., 2011; Ochs et al., 1999; Schoenborn and Wilson, 2007). It also modulates cell adhesion and activation of the QS pathway (Wang et al., 2011). Wang and colleagues also suggested that PvdQ could be playing a role in regulating the flagellum-dependent motions (see Chapter 22.1) that in turn affect biofilm formation as well as swarming motility (Wang et al., 2011). According to Hannauer, both AHL-acylases of P. aeruginosa might play a crucial role in preventing premature production of virulence factors, which could trigger the host immune response (Hannauer et al., 2012). 

Similar to P. aeruginosa and A. tumefaciens, R. solanacearum possesses both QS and QQ systems. Chen and colleagues suggested that the bacteria metabolize the AHL degradation product (i.e., fatty acid) via β-oxidation during cultivation. Besides, the AHL-acylase might modulate the level of AHL and thus regulate the expression of QS-regulated genes (Chen et al., 2009; Lin et al., 2003).

The endophytic M. testaceum StLB037 produces AiiMlactonase, which is believed to be an effective biocontrol agent in which the AHL-lactonase disrupts the QS pathways of plant pathogens, thereby providing protection to the host against pathogens invasion (Wang et al., 2010). Similarly, Chryseobacterium utilizes the degraded products of AHL for energy and growth. In return, these plant-associated bacteria provide protection to the plant from pathogens; these plants and bacteria live in symbiosis (Rashid et al., 2011). R. erythropolis possesses three different types of AHL-inactivating enzymes. However, the biological functions of these QQ enzymes remain unknown. Uroz and colleagues have proposed that the QsdA (or AhlA) lactonase might be involved in fatty acid metabolism as several neighboring sequences of the qsdA are involved in the metabolism of fatty acids (Uroz et al., 2008). AiiC acylase of Anabaena sp. PCC7120 is believed to play an important role in disrupting the communication system within complex microbial communities, as well as to modulate the level of AHLs (Romero et al., 2008). Romero and colleagues have showed that AHLs are able to inhibit the nitrogen fixation pathway of Anabaena, exhibiting a deleterious cytotoxic effect on Anabaena (Romero et al., 2011). Therefore, the presence of QQ enzyme could have an important role in defense and detoxification mechanisms.

Several other possible biological functions of AHLinactivating enzymes have been proposed, such as competition, source of energy, defense and detoxification, regulation of physiological functions, as well as symbiotic interactions with the host. There is no conceivable evidence able to prove the conclusion, like between QQ and the ability to gain competitive advantage (Hibbing et al., 2010). The function of QQ enzymes in the QQ bacteria and the role of QQ enzymes in the complex microbial communities remain bewildering. Hence, further in-depth investigations are needed in order to comprehend its function and role.
