# LuxR-type quorum-sensing regulators that are antagonized by cognate pheromones

Stephen C. Winans1, Ching-Sung Tsai1, Gina T. Ryan1, Ana Lidia Flores-Mireles1, Esther Costa1, Kevin Y. Shih1, Thomas C. Winans1, Youngchang Kim2, Robert Jedrzejczak2, and Gekleng Chhor2 

1Department of Microbiology, Cornell University, Ithaca, New York, USA 

2Bioscience Division/Structural Biology Center, Argonne National Laboratory, Argonne, Illinois, USA 

## 21.3.1 Introduction

The past 15 years have seen a revolution in our understanding of how bacteria use pheromones to communicate and coordinate their physiologies. Bacteria synthesize and detect a rich variety of diffusible chemical signals that impact diverse behaviours, including bioluminescence, horizontal DNA transfer, biofilm formation, pathogenesis, and the production of antimicrobials and other secondary metabolites (Ng and Bassler, 2009; Pappas et al., 2004; Waters and Bassler, 2005; Winans, 2011; see also Chapter 2.18). In fact, entirely new classes of pheromones have been described in the past few years (discussed further in this chapter), and more will likely be described in the near future. It has become perhaps too widely accepted that pheromones enable bacteria to estimate their population density, a process sometimes referred to as quorum sensing. Importantly, these signals allow large numbers of bacteria to act in synchrony, which perhaps blurs the distinction between single cells and multicellular organisms. 

Several classes of diffusible signals, or pheromones, have been described in bacteria. Communication among the members of the Firmicutes often requires oligopeptides that are synthesized on ribosomes and processed by proteolysis during export (Cook and Federle, 2014; Monnet et al., 2014; Singh and Ray, 2014). Some of these peptides are cyclized or otherwise covalently modified. Some are detected by membranespanning two-component kinases, while others are imported into the cytoplasm and detected by cytoplasmic receptors. An entirely different class of signaling molecules has evolved in the Actinobacteria, including Streptomyces spp. and their allies. These molecules are collectively referred to as γ-butyrolactones and include the A-factor of S. coelicolor. These signals play important roles in secondary metabolism and cell differentiation (Liu et al., 2013). 

Signaling among Proteobacteria appears to have evolved several times independently, and a variety of signals have been described. One type of signal used by a variety of Proteobacteria are long-chain fatty acids that are generally monounsaturated, branched, or both (Deng et al., 2011). The founding member of this family of signals is DSF (diffusible signaling factor, or cis-11- methyl-2-dodecenoic acid), and it was found in Xanthomonas campestris. The genes required for synthesis and detection of similar signals are widely distributed throughout the Proteobacteria. Another family of signals found in diverse Proteobacteria are composed α-hydroxyketones (AHK), exemplified by Cholera autoinducer-1 (CAI-1, or 3-hydroxytridecan-4-one) and Legionella autoinducer-1 (LAI-1, or 3-hydroxypentadecan-4-one) (Pereira et al., 2013; Tiaden and Hilbi, 2012; Tiaden et al., 2010). Another signal, denoted autoinducer-2 (AI2), is synthesized by many species of bacteria, and might therefore serve as an intergeneric signal (Pereira et al., 2013), though it remains possible that some of the bacteria that make AI2 may simply excrete it as a waste product rather than use it as a signal. 

Yet another family of signals that are distributed throughout the Proteobacteria are denoted N-acylhomoserine lactones (AHLs), and they have identical homoserine lactone head groups and a variety of hydrophobic acyl groups that differ in length, oxidation, and desaturation (Ng and Bassler, 2009; Waters and Bassler, 2005). Though most AHLs have straight chain acyl groups, a few have branched chains or aromatic groups (Ahlgren et al., 2011; Lindemann et al., 2011; Schaefer et al., 2008). The first described member of this family is OHHL (N-3-oxohexanoyl-l-homoserine lactone) of the marine bioluminescent bacterium Allivibrio fischeri. OHHL is synthesized by LuxI and detected by the OHHL-dependent transcription factor LuxR (Eberhard et al., 1981; Engebrecht et al., 1983). LuxR has two domains, an N-terminal domain (NTD) that binds pheromone, and a C-terminal domain (CTD) that binds DNA (Choi and Greenberg, 1991; Hanzelka and Greenberg, 1995). As the population density of V. fischeri cells increases, the concentration of external OHHL increases. When the concentration of this signal reaches the nanomolar range, its passive efflux from the cells becomes balanced by an influx, so that it can interact with LuxR. LuxR–OHHL complexes bind the promoter of the luxICDABEG operon and activate its transcription; the fact that the first gene in this operon is luxI creates a positive feedback loop. Proteins homologous to LuxI and LuxR are widely distributed among the Proteobacteria. The best studied examples are the TraI–TraR system of Agrobacterium tumefaciens, which uses N-3-oxooctanoyl-l-homoserine lactone (OOHL) as a signal and regulates the conjugation and replication genes of the Ti plasmid (White and Winans, 2007), and the LasI–LasR system of Pseudomonas aeruginosa, which uses the signal N-3- oxododecyl-l-homoserine lactone (ODdHL) to regulate a large number of genes, including several that encode pathogenesisassociated proteins (Schuster et al., 2013). 

Most LuxR-type proteins cannot function in the absence of AHLs. In at least some cases, including LuxR, TraR, LasR, and CepR, AHLs are required for protein folding and protease resistance (Pappas et al., 2004). However, a smaller number of LuxRtype receptors are atypical in that they function preferentially in the absence of their cognate pheromone and are inhibited by these cognate signals (Tsai and Winans, 2010). We refer to LuxRtype proteins having this property as quorum hindered (Figure 21.3.1, bottom two panels). Some LuxR-type proteins of this type form a well-defined monophyletic clade within the LuxR family, including the founding member, EsaR of Pantoea stewartii; ExpR of Pectobacterium carotorovum; YenR of Yersinia enterocolitica, studied in the author’s laboratory; and others. At least two quorum-hindered LuxR-type proteins lie well outside of the EsaR clade. These include the CepR2 protein of Burkholderia cenocepacia (also studied in the author’s laboratory) and VjbR of Brucella spp. (discussed further in this chapter). This review focuses primarily on quorum-hindered members of the LuxR family. 

## 21.3.2 EsaR of Pantoea stewartii is inhibited by its cognate AHL

As described in Section 21.3.1, a small number of LuxR homologs appear to function only as apo-proteins, in that they are fully active in the absence of any AHL, and their activity is blocked by cognate AHLs. Among the best characterized of these systems is the EsaR–EsaI system of Pantoea stewartii (formerly Erwinia stewartii), a pathogen of maize. In 1995, Beck von Bodman and Farrand reported that P. stewartii produces OHHL, the same AHL as made by V. fischeri, and isolated the esaI and esaR genes from a cosmid library (Beck von Bodman and Farrand, 1995). The two genes are transcribed convergently, and their reading frames overlap by 21 nucleotides at their 3′ ends (Figure 21.3.2). EsaR represses transcription of its own gene but does not directly affect expression of esaI. Disruption of esaI caused a sharp decrease in exopolysaccharide accumulation, and production was restored by adding OHHL (Beck von Bodman and Farrand, 1995). In a subsequent study, it was reported that EsaR mutants overproduce the same exopolysaccharide (von Bodman et al., 1998), indicating that null mutations in esaR and esaI have opposite phenotypes. Apparently, whatever EsaR does to regulate EPS was antagonized by OHHL. An esaR–esaI double mutant had the same phenotype as an esaR mutant, indicating that (as expected if EsaR is absent) the presence or absence of OHHL is of no consequence. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/d76ebbf80a851939c68627f8e9b5f322b8c574e8a7b718723e2aa54c7c73897f.jpg)



Figure 21.3.1 Four ways that an AHL-type pheromone could impact gene expression. The first of these, where the pheromone stimulates an activator, is the most common, and it is exemplified by LuxR, TraR, LasR, CepR, and many others. The second, in which a pheromone stimulates a repressor, may not yet have been found in nature but has been constructed in the laboratory. The third and fourth, in which a pheromone antagonizes an activator or a repressor, respectively, is exemplified by members of the EsaR clade, which can repress some promoters and activate others, but which are always antagonized by their cognate AHLs.


It was later discovered that apo-EsaR directly represses the promoter of rcsA, a gene required for exopolysaccharide synthesis (Minogue et al., 2005). The fact that EsaR repressed rcsA and esaR was unusual among LuxR proteins, which generally activate transcription. However, many transcription factors can activate some promoters while repressing others, often depending on whether they bind upstream of the target promoter, or within or downstream of the promoter. Both LuxR and TraR have been converted to repressors simply by moving their binding sites (Egland and Greenberg, 2000; Luo and Farrand, 1999), even though they are not known to repress any natural promoters. The fact that EsaR was a repressor could therefore be explained easily. In contrast, the fact that OHHL antagonized 


Figure 21.3.2 All members of the EsaR clade are encoded by genes that overlap at their 3′ ends with their cognate AHL synthase genes. Although the translational overlap is short, the transcription overlap could be far longer, and expression of any one of these genes could antagonize expression of a convergent one, either via head-on collisions of RNA polymerases or by heteroduplexes between the two mRNAs.


![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/b220d21cd421353a2b44ba0ec0212acf3e17c55dbae36b48643c69786e2ea491.jpg)


EsaR function was completely unprecedented, and in stark contrast to the properties of LuxR, LasR, TraR, and most related proteins. 

EsaR negative autoregulation was reconstituted in E. coli, where OHHL again blocked repression (Minogue et al., 2002), providing more evidence for a direct interaction between EsaR and the esaR promoter. Purified EsaR bound to the esaR and rcsA promoter fragments in gel shift assays (Minogue et al., 2002, 2005). At the esaR promoter, EsaR bound to a DNA sequence that contained an imperfect rotational symmetry. EsaR bound the esaR promoter as a dimer and without cooperativity (Minogue et al., 2002). Surprisingly, OHHL did not have any effect on binding affinity in gel shift assays, though it did inhibit EsaR binding to its binding site in assays using surface plasmon resonance (Minogue et al., 2002). OHHL also altered intrinsic fluorescence of EsaR tryptophan residues and increased EsaR thermostability (Minogue et al., 2002). 

As described here, EsaR was first characterized as a repressor. However, data published in 2003 showed that EsaR can also serve as a transcriptional activator. EsaR was produced in an E. coli strain that also expressed the luxICDABEG operon of V. fischeri. EsaR activated transcription of this operon, but only in the absence of OHHL (von Bodman et al., 2003), and did so more efficiently when the LuxR-binding site was replaced with an EsaR-binding site. A native gene, esaS, of P. stewartii also is activated by EsaR (Schu et al., 2009). This gene expresses a small, noncoding RNA that is divergent from esaR. As expected, OHHL antagonized EsaR in the activation of esaS. A single EsaR-binding site located between esaR and esaS is required for repression of esaR and for activation of esaS. This binding site is centered 60 nucleotides upstream of a possible esaS transcription start site. In a recent study comparing the transcriptome of an EsaIR mutant with that of an EsaI mutant, 260 genes were differentially regulated at least twofold. Of these, 184 genes were repressed by apo-EsaR, while 76 genes were activated. The role of OHHL was not tested but presumably would have antagonized EsaR at each of these promoters. 

## 21.3.3 The exoenzyme regulators ExpR of Pectobacterium carotovorum and Erwinia chrysanthemi are inhibited by OHHL and OOHL

P. carotovorum (formerly Erwinia carotovora) and E. chrysanthemi are plant pathogens that macerate plant tissues by releasing enzymes capable of degrading pectate, cellulose, and protein. Expression of these enzymes is controlled by two transcription factors, ExpR and ExpR2 (Burr et al., 2006; Cui et al., 2005; Sjoblom ¨ et al., 2006), and by the AHL synthase ExpI, whose gene overlaps that of ExpR (Figure 21.3.2). Mutations in expI (which directs the synthesis of OHHL) abolished expression of all such genes (Pirhonen et al., 1993), while mutations in expR caused a modest increase in plant tissue maceration and pectate lyase activity, and overproduction of ExpR caused a decrease (Andersson et al., 2000). Mutants lacking ExpR1 and ExpR2 produced these exoenzymes constitutively (Sjoblom ¨ et al., 2006). ExpR1 and ExpR2 therefore have overlapping functions. However, in a different strain of P. carotovorum, this second receptor (referred to as VirR) was reported to be solely responsible for exoenzyme production (Burr et al., 2006). 

E. chrysanthemi also expresses pectate lyase in a quorumdependent fashion and has orthologous expI and expR genes (Nasser et al., 1998). Mutation of expI abolished production of two AHLs, but did not affect the production of a third one, suggesting the existence of at least one more AHL synthase gene. Mutations of expI and expR had little effect on pectate lyase synthesis, which remained quorum regulated. This provided additional evidence for a second quorum-sensing system. Despite this genetic evidence that ExpR does not (at least solely) regulate pectate lyase genes, the protein was tested for binding to five different pel gene promoters. Apo-ExpR caused a gel shift at all five promoters. For the one promoter tested, OHHL blocked DNA binding in gel shift assays. DNase I protection assays were performed using three of these promoters. In the absence of OHHL, footprints were detected at two promoters. The gel shift experiments make a strong prediction that OHHL would weaken or abolish binding in the footprint assay. Inexplicably, the opposite was reported, as OHHL caused longer and more distinct footprints at these promoters. Strangely enough, no effort was made to reconcile these apparently contradictory results. Apo-ExpR also autorepressed its synthesis, while OHHL almost fully blocked autorepression (Castang et al., 2006), providing further evidence that OHHL antagonizes ExpR. Purified apo-ExpR bound the expR promoter without cooperativity, forming two high-affinity complexes (containing one or two ExpR dimers, respectively). OHHL largely disrupted these complexes. ExpR protected a region extending from −40 to +6 of the expR promoter from DNase I digestion. This 46-nucleotide region is sufficiently long to account for the two complexes observed in gel shift assays. Similar to the pectate lyase promoters described here, protection was more pronounced in the presence of OHHL than in its absence. No efforts were made to reconcile these extremely puzzling data. RNA polymerase bound an overlapping but longer region, probably displacing ExpR, although the authors concluded that both proteins bound simultaneously. Apo-ExpR blocked open complex formation at the expR promoter, while OHHL restored open complex formation. Similar results were obtained using runoff transcription assays (Castang et al., 2006). 

## 21.3.4 SmaR and SmaI act antagonistically to regulate antibiotic biosynthesis

The smaR and smaI genes of Serratia sp. 39006 are also members of the esaR–esaI clade and are also expressed convergently. SmaI synthesizes predominantly butanoyl-HSL (BHL) and smaller amounts of hexanoyl-HSL (HHL). A smaI mutation abolishes the synthesis of the antibiotic carbapenem, the pigment prodigiosin, and several hydrolytic enzymes (Thomson et al., 2000), while a smaR–smaI double mutant restores their production (Slater et al., 2003). Apo-SmaR is thought to bind to the promoters of the carR gene and the car (carbapenem) biosynthetic operon and repress their expression, while binding is blocked by BHL or HHL (Fineran et al., 2005; Slater et al., 2003). CarR is another member of the LuxR family and also directly activates the car operon. CarR is rather closely related to members of the EsaR family (Figure 21.3.3), which might suggest that its activity could be blocked by cognate AHLs. CarR has been referred to as AHL independent, as it was able to activate the car operon of P. carotovorum in a strain lacking AHLs (Barnard and Salmond, 2007). It seems highly plausible that CarR could be antagonized by AHLs, especially given the lack of counterevidence and its sequence similarity to members of the EsaR clade. 

Serratia marcescens strain SS-1 is similar to Serratia sp. 39006 in that its SpnI–SpnR system controls production of prodigiosin, endonuclease, and a surfactant that affects motility (this strain does not synthesize carbapenem) (Horng et al., 2002). SpnR is thought to directly repress target promoters, while the AHL synthesized by SpnI OHHL) antagonizes SpnR. SpnR activates the spnR promoter, providing positive autoregulation. Surprisingly, it was reported that SpnR does so more effectively in the presence of OHHL than in its absence (Horng et al., 2002). It is not surprising that SpnR could activate some promoters and repress others, as several EsaR-type proteins can do both (as discussed here). However, the finding that OHHL would block SpnR function at one promoter and enhance it at another is unprecedented and counterintuitive. No explanation was offered. The spnR/I genes are located on a mobile genetic element, and SpnR represses transcription of the Tn3-type transposase of this element (Wei et al., 2006). 

## 21.3.5 Three pathogenic species of Yersinia encode EsaR-type systems

Yersinia pestis, Yersinia pseudotuberculosis, and Yersinia enterocolitica encode additional members of the EsaR clade. Y. pestis and Y. pseudotuberculosis (which have virtually identical chromosomes and differ largely in their plasmid content) each encode two complete LuxR–LuxI systems. All four pairs of genes are transcribed convergently and overlap at their 3′ ends. The published literature on these genes is in places difficult to interpret, as the two systems may have redundant or overlapping functions such that the phenotypes of single mutants may be masked. Also, in some studies, wild-type bacteria were compared to R/I double mutants. Given that I proteins may antagonize R proteins, a double mutant might resemble wild-type bacteria. It might therefore be more informative to compare an I mutant to an R/I double mutant. Best of all would be to delete all four genes, individually express the two transcription factors individually, and compare the transcriptomes of these strains in the presence or absence of pheromone. 


Figure 21.3.3 A representative phylogeny of LuxR-type proteins. Members that are known to be quorum-hindered are labelled with an asterisk. Most members are monophyletic (the EsaR clade), while two quorum-hindered proteins lie well outside this clade. Nearest neighbour relationships were analysed using Clustal-W (Thompson et al., 1994).


![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/a3006b9c129a0b993a12dd068b353f81fa177d8b146b3c6b8402f164fbbb003d.jpg)


Y. enterocolitica encodes one complete system, YenIR (Throup et al., 1995), and an orphan AHL receptor protein, YetR (Thomson et al., 2006). The published literature so far has focused entirely on YenIR. In an effort to identify YenRregulated genes, chromatin immunoprecipitation experiments were carried out and yielded a DNA fragment that lies directly upstream of the yenR gene (Tsai and Winans, 2011). However, YenR does not activate the yenR gene. Rather, it activates a gene, designated yenS, which lies adjacent to and divergent from yenR and is reminiscent of esaS of Pantoea stewartii (as described in this chapter). YenR activates yenS preferentially in the absence of the cognate pheromone 3-oxohexanoylhomoserine lactone (OHHL). Purified apo-YenR bound noncooperatively to two 20- nucleotide sites that lie upstream of yenS. Binding occurred in the absence of OHHL, and purified YenR was largely released from the DNA by this pheromone (Tsai and Winans, 2011). 

Importantly, this indicates that fully folded apo-YenR can bind pheromone, which is not the case for TraR or LasR (Schuster et al., 2004; Zhu and Winans, 2001). To date, yenS is the only gene known to be directly regulated by YenR. A small RNA (sRNA) corresponding to YenS was also identified in Y. pseudotuberculosis (Schiano et al., 2014). 

The yenS gene encodes two nontranslated RNAs 169 and 105 nucleotides long that share the same $5 ^ { \prime }$ end but have different $3 ^ { \prime }$ ends. TargetRNA (Tjaden, 2008) was used to search the Y. enterocolitica genome for possible messenger RNAs (mRNAs) that could be regulated by YenS. This program predicted that the YenS sRNA could bind near the ribosomebinding site and translation start site of the YenI mRNA, and might thereby inhibit YenI stability or translation (Waters and Storz, 2009). We confirmed this prediction by showing that one or both YenS RNAs inhibited the translation and accumulation of the yenI mRNA. Inhibition was detected only in constructs that contained this predicted binding site (Tsai and Winans, 2011). A mutation in yenI strongly stimulated swarming motility on the surface of semisolid agar, while exogenous OHHL completely suppressed this phenotype. Hypermotility in yenI mutants was also suppressed by mutations in yenR or yenS, suggesting that YenS plays a direct, stimulatory role in swarming motility. A second sRNA, yenT, is encoded directly downstream of yenS and appears to antagonize yenS, as a yenT mutation enhances swarming. YenT and YenS are complementary over 13 nucleotides, suggesting that they could form destabilizing heteroduplexes. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/d03277123701dc85e3c4fccf6361f2bd44940964d9d51294052c93983e27e8fa.jpg)


![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/98937a25135e845fd63f5aa1cf1c37c967d8542724d8f56b8f2a31181094aad6.jpg)



Figure 21.3.4 Regulatory circuitry of (a) YenR and (b) CepR and CepR2. (a) At low cell density, pheromone does not accumulate, so YenR is in the active form and expresses YenS. YenS inhibits expression of YenI, decreasing pheromone production, which tends to lock the system into the low-density state. At high density, pheromone somehow does accumulate and blocks YenR function, so YenS is not made, and expression of the pheromone is elevated, which tends to lock the system into the high-density state. Hysteresis could be further reinforced by antagonism between YenR mRNA and YenI mRNA. (b) Octanoyl-homoserine lactone (OHL) is synthesized by CepI and is detected by CepR and CepR2. CepR is an OHL-dependent activator, while CepR2 is an OHL-antagonized repressor. In both cases, target genes are induced at high cell density, but by opposite mechanisms.


Many LuxR–LuxI systems are thought to exhibit hysteresis, meaning that their responses to quorum-sensing stimuli are conditioned by their recent history (von Bodman et al., 2008). Simply put, when in the inactive state these systems tend to stay inactive despite environmental perturbations, and vice versa. For typical LuxR–LuxI systems, hysteresis is thought to result from positive regulation of the AHL synthase gene by the AHL receptor protein. The YenR–YenI system could also exhibit hysteresis, but by a completely different mechanism. At low cell densities, AHLs accumulates very poorly, so apo-YenR accumulates and activates the yenS promoter (Figure 21.3.4a). YenS inhibits production of AHLs, and this inhibition ensures continued accumulation of active apo-YenR, which makes more YenS, which then inhibits YenI more strongly. At high cell densities, accumulated AHLs inactivate YenR, so the production of YenS falls. This stimulates AHL production, which ensures that YenR remains inactive. Furthermore, the convergent and overlapping YenR and YenI mRNAs may hybridize, which could destabilize either or both. If heteroduplexes are degraded, then the more abundant mRNA would “win.” At low cell densities, apo-YenR would, through YenS, decrease levels of YenI mRNA. This potential excess of YenR mRNA could stoichiometrically degrade all yenI mRNA, until only YenR mRNA remained. The lack of YenI mRNA would ensure continued accumulation of apo-YenR. At high cell densities, YenR–AHL complexes would fail to activate the yenS promoter, causing an increase in YenI mRNA. The potential excess of YenI mRNA could cause stoichiometric degradation of YenR mRNA, further decreasing the production of YenR. Furthermore, RNA polymerases transcribing the two genes could collide, possibly causing one to be dislodged and the other to continue. Similarly, elongating RNA polymerases might dislodge convergently oriented RNA polymerases bound at the opposite promoter, so-called “sitting duck” inhibition (Callen et al., 2004). In all of these scenarios, the hysteresis would seem so complete as to make it almost impossible for the system to ever flip between the two states. Moreover, this model could well apply to all members of the EsaR clade, as all the genes in this clade are convergent and overlapping. 

## 21.3.6 EsaR-type regulators form a monophyletic clade

A phylogenetic dendrogram of LuxR homologs shows that all the quorum-hindered proteins discussed so far form a single clade (Figure 21.3.3). Members of the EsaR clade have several similarities in addition to their being antagonized by cognate AHLs. (i) All confirmed members are found only within the Enterobacterialaes; (ii) all but one of these proteins preferentially bind OHHL (the exception is SmaR); (iii) while most LuxRtype proteins activate expression of their cognate AHL synthase genes, members of the EsaR subfamily generally neither activate nor repress these genes, at least not directly; (iv) EsaR-type proteins can function as repressors or activators, while most LuxRtype proteins are thought to act solely as activators; and (v) all EsaR-type proteins are encoded by genes that overlap their cognate AHL synthase genes (Figure 21.3.2). Although the translation overlap is short, the transcription overlap would certainly be longer, possibly encompassing the entire length of the two genes. 

## 21.3.7 At least two quorum-hindered LuxR-type proteins lie outside of the EsaR clade

B. cenocepacia is a member of the Betaproteobacteria and is an opportunistic human pathogen that is especially problematic in victims of cystic fibrosis. This organism encodes two LuxItype acylhomoserine lactone (AHL) synthases and three LuxRtype AHL receptors. Of these, cepI and cepR form a cognate synthase–receptor pair, as do cciI and cciR, while cepR2 lacks a genetically linked AHL synthase gene. CepI and CepR are highly conserved in virtually all species of Burkholderia, while the other three proteins are found only in a subset of B. cenocepacia strains and must have invaded the genome by horizontal gene transfer. 

In one study, a cepR2 mutation caused overexpression of a cluster of adjacent genes that appear to direct the production of an uncharacterized secondary metabolite (Malott et al., 2009). In a separate study, these same genes were upregulated by octanoylhomoserine lactone (OHL), which is synthesized by CepI (Ryan et al., 2013). Taken together, these data suggest that several cepR2-linked promoters are repressed by CepR2 and that CepR2 is antagonized by OHL. Fusions of two divergent promoters to lacZ were constructed to test and confirm these hypotheses (Ryan et al., 2013). Promoter resections and DNase I footprinting assays revealed a single CepR2 binding site between two divergent promoters. This binding site lies well upstream of both promoters, suggesting an unusual mode of repression. Adjacent to the cepR2 gene is a gene designated cepS, which encodes an AraC-type transcription factor. CepS is essential for expression of both promoters, regardless of the CepR2 status or OHL concentration. CepS therefore acts downstream of CepR2, and CepR2 may function solely as a CepS anti-activator. Summarizing, CepR and CepR2 both detect OHL, but respond in opposite ways, in that OHL converts the CepR activator into a functional form, while OHL converts the CepR2 repressor to a nonfunctional form (Figure 21.3.4b). Thus, CepR-regulated genes and CepR2-regulated genes are stimulated at high cell densities, but by completely opposite mechanisms. 

CepR2 was tested for inhibition by eight different AHLs (Ryan et al., 2013). Of these, OHL was the most effective antagonist, and the only other pheromone that showed significant activity was OOHL. Decanoyl-HSL (DHL) showed a trace of activity when provided at high concentrations, while five other AHL pheromones (hexanoyl-HSL, 3-oxo-hexanoyl-HSL, 3-oxodecanoyl, dodecanoyl-HSL, and 3-oxo-dodecanoyl-HSL) were inactive. CepR2 was able to sequester OHL and several similar AHLs when overexpressed in E. coli, indicating a direct interaction between the protein and the pheromone. CepR2 was able to fold into a soluble, protease-resistant form in the presence or absence of AHLs, which is similar to YenR and several EsaR-type regulators, but completely unlike several AHL-stimulated LuxR proteins, including LuxR, TraR, LasR, and CepR (Ryan et al., 2013). Solubility was greatly enhanced when GroESL was overexpressed. 

Another quorum-hindered LuxR-type transcription factor that lies outside of the EsaR clade is found in Brucella melitensis and its relatives, which are Betaproteobacterial pathogens of domesticated animals, and occasionally of humans. Brucella spp. survive within host cells by replicating within vacuoles of phagocytic and nonphagocytic cells, and by controlling the host’s intracellular trafficking to avoid lysosome-mediated degradation (Celli, 2015). Its ability to evade innate immunity requires a Type IV secretion system (T4SS), which injects particular effector proteins from the vacuole into the host cytoplasm. The expression of the T4SS is under control of a quorum-sensing system (Arocena et al., 2010). 

B. melitensis encodes two LuxR-type proteins, VjbR and BlxR. This organism synthesizes and secretes 3-oxododecanoylhomserine lactone (OdoDHL), although, surprisingly, its genome does not encode any proteins that resemble AHL synthases. In transcriptome profiling experiments, expression of several dozen genes was affected by a VjbR mutation when compared to a wild-type strain (Weeks et al., 2010). In a parallel experiment, changes in the transcriptome were monitored when OdoDHL was added to a wild-type strain. Many genes were regulated by VjbR and by OdoDHL in opposite ways. Some genes were regulated by OdoDHL even in a mutant lacking VjbR, perhaps due to the second receptor, BlxR (whose expression incidentally was increased by OdoDHL). In another study, BlxR was found to regulate the T4SS, indicating some level of functional redundancy (Rambow-Larsen et al., 2008). 

## 21.3.8 X-ray crystallography of YenR and CepR2

To date, the structures of five LuxR-type proteins have been determined. The first, full-length TraR of Agrobacterium tumefaciens was crystallized with the pheromone OOHL and DNA (Vannini et al., 2002; Zhang et al., 2002). CviR of Chromobacter violaceum was crystallized as a full-length dimer complexed with the antagonist chlorolactone (4-(chlorophenoxy)- butanoyl-HSL), as a full-length monomer with the ligand hexanoyl-homoserine lactone, and as dimers of the N-terminal domain complexes with a variety of ligands (Chen et al., 2011). The N-terminal domain of LasR of P. aeruginosa was crystallized with 3-oxo-dodecanoyl-HSL (Bottomley et al., 2007; Zou and Nair, 2009), while full-length QscR of the same organism was crystallized with the same ligand (Lintz et al., 2011). Fulllength SdiA was crystallized with two OOHLs, OHHL, and an unexpected compound octanoyl-rac-glycerol of unknown origin (Nguyen et al., 2015). In a separate study, full-length SdiA was crystallized without ligand, though the binding pocket contained two molecules of tetraethylene glycol, a component of the crystallization buffer (Kim et al., 2014). 

We have undertaken a structural analysis of two of the quorum-hindered proteins described in this chapter, YenR and CepR2. To date, we have high-resolution structures of the N-terminal domains of each protein, which contains their pheromone binding sites (unpublished data). In the case of YenR, we were able to introduce the cognate pheromone into the crystals, allowing a comparison of apo–YenR and YenR–OHHL complexes. 

In all of these proteins, the pheromone is completely or significantly buried within the protein, with little if any exposure to solvent. In all cases, the binding pockets are composed largely of hydrophobic or aromatic residues. The residues that bind the homoserine ring are strongly conserved. Hydrogen bonds contribute to pheromone binding, including one between Trp57 (TraR numbering) and the ring keto group, another between Asp70 and the imino group, and another between Tyr53 and the 1-oxo group. All of these contacts are completely conserved. In contrast, interactions between these proteins and the acyl groups of their pheromones are less conserved. For example, many of these pheromones include a 3-oxo group on the fatty acid, and the interactions between these groups and their receptors is highly diverse. TraR residue Thr129 makes a water-mediated hydrogen bond with the 3-oxo group of OOHL. SdiA residue Ser134 makes a water-mediated bond to the 3-oxo group of OOHL. LasR residues Tyr56 and Tyr64 make a watermediated hydrogen bond to OdoDHL. QscR residues Tyr58 and Tyr66 make a water-mediated hydrogen bond to OdoDHL. YenR residues Ser33 and Asn126 make direct hydrogen bonds to the 3-oxo group of OHHL. 

The dimerization interfaces of these proteins are also quite diverse. The structures of full-length TraR, CviR, SdiA, and QscR dimers are available. For TraR, SdiA, and QscR, the NTDs contribute most of the dimerization interface, while the CTDs make a smaller but significant contribution to dimerization. In contrast, the two CTDs of CviR make no contact with each other. 

The NTDs of each dimer make highly diverse contacts with the opposing subunit. The NTDs of the TraR dimer contact one another almost exclusively via two long parallel helices (one per subunit, residues 145–160) (Figure 21.3.5a). The corresponding helices of CviA are also approximately parallel and make similar contacts. For all other structures, the two corresponding helices lie at 90◦ to one another and make only limited contacts (Figure 21.3.5b). 

The interdomain linkers are also quite diverse. The TraR linkers are 12 residues in length, and in the crystal structure only one of the two is sufficiently structured to be resolvable. Similarly with CviR complexed with the chlorolactone inhibitor, the linkers are unresolved. In contrast, the linkers of SdiA are approximately the same length but are well resolved, and the linkers of QscR contain only a few residues and are also well resolved. 

YenR is so far the only member of the EsaR clade to be crystallized and one of the few LuxR-type proteins to be resolved in the presence and absence of its ligand. The overall structure of the YenR NTD dimer was similar in the presence and absence of OHHL. However, several displacements were readily discernible (Figure 21.3.5c). Residues 93–95 were disordered in the absence of OOHL, while in its presence, these residues became fully ordered. This suggested that YenR may have some of the character of a so-called molten globule in the absence of ligand, and that it gains structure when ligand is bound. 

Of the four oxygen atoms and one nitrogen of OHHL, all except the ring oxygen formed direct (as opposed to watermediated) hydrogen bonds with nearby amino acid residues. In most cases, hydrogen bonding required extensive movement of the side chains, and in some cases, the backbones were also displaced, possibly leading to more global conformation changes.

Figure 21.3.5 Diverse interactions between NTDs. (a) The NTDs of TraR dimerize primarily via two parallel helices (red). (b) The NTDs of YenR dimerize primarily via turns between beta-strands. The C-terminal helices (red) lie at a 90◦ angle and make little contact. (c) Superposition of YenR NTDs with and without OHHL. Although the overall structure is conserved, several perturbations are readily detectable. Bound AHLs are depicted as space-filling spheres.


Trp55 moved out of the binding site by OHHL so as to avoid a steric clash with the acyl group. In its new position, the ring nitrogen of Trp55 hydrogen bonds with the ring oxo group of OHHL. Asp68 moved slightly to hydrogen bond with the imino group of OHHL. Tyr51 moved closer to OHHL in order to hydrogen bond with the 1-oxo group. Ser33 swiveled almost 180◦ in order to make a hydrogen bond with the 3-oxo group of OHHL. This movement also disrupted a hydrogen bond that had existed in the apo-protein between Tyr51 and Asn126. 

The aromatic group of Phe99 made the largest movement, swinging toward OHHL to make contacts with the ring carbons. Interestingly, this movement also created a water-mediated hydrogen bond between two backbone oxo groups, those of Phe99 and Ile107. This hydrogen bond could block a possible channel between OHHL and bulk solvent and could help to trap the pheromone within the protein.

## 21.3.9 Conclusions and perspectives



Callen BP, Shearwin KE, Egan JB. 2004. Transcriptional interference between convergent promoters caused by elongation over the promoter. Molecular Cell 14(5): 647–656. 





Ryan GT, Wei Y, Winans SC. 2013. A LuxR-type repressor of Burkholderia cenocepacia inhibits transcription via antiactivation and is inactivated by its cognate acylhomoserine lactone. Molecular Microbiology 87(1): 94–111. 



When the LuxR–LuxI regulators were first discovered, it may have been assumed that they were unique in nature. This was disproven when the second and third members of this family were discovered in the early 1990s (Fuqua and Winans, 1994; Gambello and Iglewski, 1991). However, it was then assumed that the properties of all members of the family would be similar. No one predicted that some members used pheromones having branched-chain or aromatic side groups. It appears that some members may detect plant-released compounds that do not resemble AHLs (Patel et al., 2013). It also was never predicted that some LuxR proteins were inhibited by their cognate ligands. Who therefore can predict the future surprises that lie just over the horizon? 



Castang S, Reverchon S, Gouet P, Nasser W. 2006. Direct evidence for the modulation of the activity of the Erwinia chrysanthemi quorumsensing regulator ExpR by acylhomoserine lactone pheromone. Journal of Biological Chemistry 281(40): 29972–29987. 





Schaefer AL, Greenberg EP, Oliver CM, Oda Y, Huang JJ, Bittan-Banin G, et al. 2008. A new class of homoserine lactone quorum-sensing signals. Nature 454(7204): 595–599. 



In future studies, we will seek to understand exactly how pheromones convert their receptors from an active state to an inactive one. Several AHL-dependent receptors require their pheromones for protein folding into soluble and proteaseresistant forms (Schuster et al., 2004; Urbanowski et al., 2004; Weingart et al., 2005; Zhu and Winans, 2001). This cannot be true of quorum-hindered regulators, as they are fully functional as apo-proteins. Experiments with purified YenR suggest that OHHL decreases affinity for DNA-binding sites, but we don’t know how this occurs. Given the long length and probable flexibility of the interdomain linkers, it seems unlikely that conformational changes in the NTD could be propagated along the linker to the CTD. OHHL could plausibly cause YenR dimers to dissociate into monomers, which would have low affinity for YenR-binding sites. However, the crystal structure of the YenR NTD did not show obvious changes in the dimer interface when OHHL was introduced. Alternatively, OHHL could cause the two CTDs of a YenR dimer to bind to their NTDs in a way that prevents stable association with DNA.

In the Yersinia system, the orphan receptor (YetR) remains completely uncharacterized. Its sequence suggests that it may bind the same pheromone as YenR, but could bind to different DNA sequences. We have no idea whether YetR is AHL dependent or AHL hindered. We would also like to identify the regulons that YenR and YetR control, especially given that these proteins have close homologs in one of humankind’s most dangerous pathogens, Yersinia pestis.

We believe that the complementarity between YenI mRNA and YenR mRNA may lead to destabilizing heteroduplexes, as would complementarity between YenI and YenS and between YenS and YenT. It will be interesting to alter the complementarity and then make compensatory alterations in its partner, to confirm that these molecules do indeed form heteroduplexes. If so, then perturbing the abundance of any one of these RNAs could alter the abundance of all three others in predictable ways.

LuxR–LuxI family regulators are often assumed to exhibit hysteresis (von Bodman et al., 2008), but this is almost never studied directly. It would be interesting to document hysteresis in the YenR–YenI system and to dissect the mechanisms involved. It would be especially interesting to learn about the consequences of the overlap between the AHL synthase genes and the AHL receptor genes. It seems plausible that the antagonism between receptor and pheromone could be mirrored by an antagonism between the two genes. If so, this could play a role in hysteresis. As is always the case in science, each new observation leads to a plethora of new questions.

# Adaptation to environmental stresses in Streptococcus mutans through the production of its quorum-sensing peptide pheromone

Delphine Dufour, Vincent Leung, and Celine M. L ´ evesque ´ 

Dental Research Institute, Department of Oral Microbiology, Faculty of Dentistry, University of Toronto, Toronto, Ontario, Canada 

## 21.4.1 Introduction

Single-celled organisms such as bacteria are believed to be the oldest form of life. Bacteria play important roles in the global ecosystem. They are widespread in occurrence, being found in almost all kinds of habitats ranging from thermal water and frozen glaciers to the bottom of the deepest oceans. Bacteria are thus confronted with stressful and changing environments. Because bacteria encounter a myriad of stresses, they have evolved and perfected a plethora of regulators to integrate their cellular response to environmental perturbations. Most bacteria in nature do not exist independently but persist in coordinated, spatially organized, and metabolically integrated microbial communities, called biofilms (Costerton et al., 1978; Donlan, 2002; Lopez ´ et al., 2010; see Section 22). Biofilms are diverse microbial communities attached to distinct inert or living surfaces surrounded by an extracellular matrix of polymers of microbial origin, including polysaccharides, proteins, glycoproteins, glycolipids, and extracellular DNA (eDNA). This physical barrier is believed to provide protection against environmental stresses like heat shock (see Section 13) and desiccation (see Section 12), as well as against the host immune system. There are several similarities between bacteria growing in a biofilm and multicellular organisms. Indeed, there is a phenotypic heterogeneity within the biofilm that can be interpreted as a specialization or division of labor similar to the cellular differentiation seen in multicellular organisms (Dufour et al., 2012; see Chapter 21.1). Bacterial cells in biofilms interact with each other using small diffusible signal molecules. In a process called quorum sensing, bacteria exhibit and regulate cooperative behavioral patterns providing significant benefits to the bacteria during host colonization, defense against competitors, and adaptation to stressful and changing environments (Ng and Bassler, 2009; see also Chapter 2.18). In streptococci, the quorum-sensing diffusible signal molecules are small peptide pheromones called CSPs (competence-stimulating peptides). In the biofilm organism Streptococcus mutans, it has been demonstrated that its CSP pheromone is a stress-inducible alarmone capable of conveying sophisticated messages in the bacterial population (Dufour and Levesque, 2013a). The focus of this chapter is ´ primarily on the quorum-sensing CSP pheromone or alarmone and its role in stress survival of streptococci. 

## 21.4.2 Streptococcus mutans, an opportunistic pathogen adapted to its environment

Oral microbial biofilms are some of the most complex human microbiota (Aas et al., 2005). The community of microorganisms that forms on the tooth surface is called the dental plaque biofilm (Kolenbrander et al., 2006). Over 700 different bacterial species have been found associated with the dental plaque biofilm (Keijser et al., 2008; Zaura et al., 2009). The most prevalent oral infections are dental caries (tooth decay or cavities) and periodontal diseases affecting the bone and supportive tissues, and they are initiated by the dental plaque biofilm (Kuramitsu et al., 2007; see Section 22). S. mutans is a well-characterized resident of the dental plaque biofilm. This bacterium infects more than half of the world’s human population. S. mutans is widely recognized as a key etiological agent of dental caries, one of the most prevalent chronic infectious diseases worldwide (Loesche, 1986; Takahashi and Nyvad, 2008). S. mutans is also among the oral microorganisms that can cause infective endocarditis, a cardiovascular disease, when entering the bloodstream following trauma, such as root canal treatment and tooth extraction (Nakano and Ooshima, 2009). Its main cariogenic virulence factors are its ability to promote adhesion and accumulation on teeth, and its ability to produce (acidogenicity) and survive (aciduricity) an acidic environment due to the fermentation of dietary carbohydrates (Mitchell, 2003). 

As a resident of the oral cavity, S. mutans must strive to optimize its adaptation to the environmental constraints of the mouth. Indeed, the natural habitat of the dental plaque biofilm is constantly under fluctuating and adverse environmental conditions due to constant changes in available nutrients, metal ions, pH, oxygen levels, temperature, osmolarity, and host oxidative immune response. S. mutans has evolved a network of regulators to integrate its cellular response to environmental perturbations. Regulatory systems such as two-component systems (TCSs; see Section 3), carbon catabolite repression, bacteriocin production, and acid tolerance response (see Section 16) are efficient tools used by S. mutans to adapt itself to a very peculiar ecological niche. For more complete coverage of the metabolic pathways and TCSs involved in stress tolerance of S. mutans, please refer to some recommended reviews on the topic (Lemos and Burne, 2008; Merritt and Qi, 2012; Singh et al., 2014; Smith and Spatafora, 2012). 

Recently, Cornejo and colleagues (2013) analyzed the genomes of 57 S. mutans isolates to identify the potential adaptive features of the dispensable and core components of the S. mutans genome. Interestingly, they found that 73 genes were present in every one of the 57 human strains and absent in closely related species (Streptococcus ratti, Streptococcus criceti, and Streptococcus macaccae) residing in the oral cavities of various animals like rats, hamsters, and monkeys. Within this set of S. mutans unique core genes, 36 genes encode hypothetical proteins. The other genes encode transcriptional regulators and proteins involved in carbohydrate metabolism, acid resistance, oxidative stress, metal and peptide translocation, and adhesion to host tissue. Altogether, these genes are mainly associated with metabolic processes contributing to the successful adaptation of S. mutans to the human oral cavity. 

## 21.4.3 S. mutans uses its quorum-sensing CSP pheromone as a stress-inducible alarmone

Bacteria use quorum-sensing to communicate between cells using small signal molecules produced by individuals (Rutherford and Bassler, 2012; Waters and Bassler, 2005; see also Chapters 21.1 and 21.3). These signal molecules increase in concentration as a function of bacterial cell density. Once a threshold concentration (quorum) is reached, the signal molecule initiates a signaling cascade culminating in a population-wide differential regulation of target genes to derive a competitive advantage. Quorum-sensing controls a variety of cellular processes, including biofilm development, bioluminescence, sporulation, motility, conjugation, genetic competence, bacteriocin production, and pathogenesis. Thus, quorum-sensing allows bacteria to display a unified response that benefits the bacteria during adaptation to environment, host colonization, and defense against competitors. There is now strong evidence that quorum-sensing constitutes a global regulatory system in many bacterial species (Platt and Fuqua, 2010). Low-GC-content Gram-positive bacteria utilize small peptides as signal molecules. Some of these small peptides undergo significant modifications to become a soluble extracellular signal (Horinouchi et al., 2010). 

Among streptococci, the best studied quorum-sensing system is the CSP–ComDE system. This system was first discovered and characterized in Streptococcus pneumoniae, a resident of the human upper respiratory tract. The CSP–ComDE quorumsensing system is composed of the secreted, linear signal peptide pheromone (CSP, or competence-stimulating peptide) encoded by the comC gene, and the ComDE TCS composed of the ComD membrane-bound histidine kinase sensor and its cognate cytoplasmic ComE response regulator (Havarstein˚ et al., 1995; Pestova et al., 1996). Although the comC gene is found in the genome of several streptococcal strains, the CSP pheromone induces a powerful strain-specific activity (Tian et al., 2009). Early work in S. pneumoniae showed that genetic competence, the ability of cells to take up and integrate eDNA, was initiated by the CSP pheromone as cell density increases. In S. mutans, the CSP–ComDE quorum-sensing system is also essential for genetic competence and functions optimally in biofilms (Li et al., 2001b, 2002). 

In S. mutans UA159, the genome sequence reference strain, the CSP pheromone is the product of a propeptide of 46 amino acid residues containing a leader sequence typical of the nonlantibiotic bacteriocins with a characteristic double glycine (GG) motif (Li et al., 2001b). The propeptide is processed concomitantly with export by a specific adenosine triphosphate (ATP)- binding cassette (ABC) transporter, the ComAB transport system, to generate the CSP-21 peptide (Figure 21.4.1) (Petersen and Scheie, 2000). A final posttranslational modification of the CSP-21 peptide is mediated extracellularly by the SepM endoprotease belonging to the Lon-like protease family to generate the mature CSP-18 peptide (denoted as the CSP pheromone hereafter) (Hossain and Biswas, 2012; Petersen et al., 2006). 

When the CSP pheromones accumulate above a threshold level required for detection, the extracellular CSP pheromones interact with the ComD sensor, resulting in its autophosphorylation. This event initiates a phosphorelay cascade that results in the activation of the ComE response regulator. Activated ComE then directly binds at direct repeat sequences upstream of promoters of CSP-responsive genes, where it activates transcription. Microarray-based expression profiling performed with stimulation by exogenous synthetic CSP showed that the CSP regulon is composed of 277 genes (Perry et al., 2009b). Among these CSP-responsive genes, bacteriocin-related genes were found to be directly regulated by ComE regulator (Table 21.4.1) (Kreth et al., 2006; Perry et al., 2009b; van der Ploeg, 2005). 

Among the ComE-regulated genes is cipB encoding a peptide regulator (CipBIn) for the transcriptional control of the CSP-dependent phenotypes via the alternative sigma factor SigX (also known as ComX) of the σ-70 family (Dufour et al., 2011). CipB can also function as an antimicrobial peptide (bacteriocin)

Recently, it has been demonstrated that the ComR–ComS system is the proximal regulator of SigX in S. mutans (Mashburn-Warren et al., 2010). SigX assembles with core polymerase to recognize a set of noncanonical promoter sites possessing a 9 bp CIN-box sequence. SigX factor was initially identified as a competence-specific transcription modulator required for the expression of late competence genes involved in DNA processing, uptake, and recombination (Aspiras et al., 2004; Lee and Morrison, 1999). However, DNA microarray analysis of CSP-induced genes has revealed that a large number of genes unrelated to competence are also regulated by SigX (Perry et al., 2009b). Our hypothesis is that CipBIn, concomitantly with the ComR transcriptional regulator and XIP peptide encoded by the comS gene (Mashburn-Warren et al., 2010), connects the CSP–ComDE system to the master regulator SigX (Figure 21.4.2). Once activated, SigX directly or indirectly regulates the expression of genes controlling cooperative behavioral patterns providing significant benefits to S. mutans. 

In many bacteria, sigma factors are key regulators of general stress responses (Fux et al., 2005; see Chapters 2.4 and 2.15; Section 4). 

While studying the S. mutans competence pathway, the authors’ group made an important discovery. The S. mutans CSP pheromone was not acting as a classical quorum-sensing signal by accumulating passively in proportion to cell density. In fact, they demonstrated that particular environmental stresses (e.g., antibiotic, nutrient starvation, heat (see Section 13), oxidative stress (see Section 10), and acid shock; see Section 16) induced the expression of the comC gene encoding for the CSP signal molecule (Perry et al., 2009b). Their results suggested that S. mutans employs its CSP pheromone as a secreted stressinducible alarmone to give an early warning signal of hostile environmental conditions, and mount a specific response to adapt to and survive environmental assaults. The results from their studies and those of several other groups strongly suggested that the S. mutans CSP–ComDE quorum-sensing system was most likely part of a larger stress response regulon enabling

CSP-induced cells to cope with adverse environmental conditions. In the following sections of this chapter, we will discuss how S. mutans uses its CSP pheromone to synchronize the gene expression of a specific group of cells to coordinate important biological activities. 

## 21.4.4 Regulation of the competence state for the acquisition of genetic information

While S. mutans reproduces asexually by cell division, its genetic repertoire can gain some plasticity through natural genetic transformation, a mechanism of horizontal gene transfer (HGT). The physiological state that allows cells to take up, assimilate, and integrate DNA is called competence. Naturally competent bacteria possess genes encoding proteins required for the binding of eDNA, its uptake, and its genomic integration (Claverys et al., 2009; Johnsborg et al., 2007). DNA transformation in S. mutans was demonstrated by Perry and Kuramitsu (1981). They succeeded at integrating in the genome of three S. mutans isolates a spectinomycin antibiotic resistance gene that was acquired from spectinomycin-resistant clones of S. mutans and Streptococcus sanguinis demonstrating successful transformation in S. mutans by homologous and heterologous DNA, respectively. Subsequently, development of genetic competence was the first mechanism identified as a CSPregulated phenotype in S. mutans, concomitantly with the discovery of its CSP–ComDE quorum-sensing system (Li et al., 2001b). The ComDE TCS detects extracellular CSP pheromone and initiates a signal leading to transcription of the competence regulon. In addition, the gene encoding the RNA polymerase alternative sigma factor SigX is transcribed as part of the competence regulon. SigX regulates the transcription of genes encoding proteins involved in DNA processing, uptake, and recombination. Any disruption of comC, comD, comE, or sigX genes dramatically affects the transformation efficiency of S. mutans. Although a basal level of competence exists in ΔcomD- and ΔcomE-deficient mutants related to the existence of a ComDEindependent competence pathway (Ahn et al., 2006; Perry et al., 2008, 2009b), without a functional comC gene and sigX gene, no competence develops in S. mutans. Since the loss of ComR regulator abolishes sigX expression, competence development is also inhibited in a ΔcomR mutant of S. mutans (Mashburn-Warren et al., 2010). Despite the fact that natural competence in S. mutans is a transient regulated process affecting only a small fraction of the population (∼0.001%), addition of synthetic CSP pheromone to S. mutans cultures raises transformation efficiencies by at least 100-fold. 

Although no in vivo studies have demonstrated the induction of S. mutans competence state under environmental stresses, the fact that the genome of several S. mutans isolates contains multiple genes involved in stress resistance (e.g., acidic pH (see Section 16), oxidative stress (see Section 10), and metal translocation; see Section 20) acquired by HGT from other oral bacteria suggests a regulatory link between competence induction and acquisition of fitness-enhancing genes. S. mutans is a normal inhabitant of the dental plaque biofilm. This ecological niche provides the proper setting for DNA exchange as the cells are in close proximity. In fact, competence development in S. mutans is more efficient in biofilms than in free-floating planktonic cells. Hence, transformation efficiencies during S. mutans biofilm growth can approach levels 10- to 600- fold higher than their planktonic counterparts (Li et al., 2001b). Moreover, the large amount of eDNA found in the biofilm matrix (Flemming et al., 2007) could provide a source of genetic material for natural transformation. This concept thus has dramatic implications when considering the potential for the transfer of antibiotic resistance genes to pathogens that may transiently reside in the human mouth and upper respiratory tract. 

## 21.4.5 Biofilm formation for survival and persistence of S. mutans in its natural ecosystem

When bacteria are presented with a surface and adequate growth conditions, they assemble and grow within biofilm communities. Biofilms constitute a unique mode of growth that allows bacteria to survive a wide range of hostile environments, such as UV exposure, dehydration, salinity, and several disinfectants and antimicrobial agents (see Section 22). S. mutans has evolved a biofilm lifestyle for survival and persistence in the dental plaque biofilm. It uses its CSP–ComDE quorum-sensing system to form biofilms on dental surfaces. It was shown that mutations in comC, comD, or comE genes resulted in strains that produced aberrant biofilms (Cvitkovitch et al., 2003). Particularly, the ΔcomC mutant formed a biofilm that lacked the wild-type architecture, whereas the ΔcomD and ΔcomE mutants formed biofilms with a reduced biomass (Li et al., 2002). It was suggested that the architectural change in the ΔcomC mutant biofilms could be associated with a defect in cell separation since longer chains were observed for the ΔcomC mutant (Li et al., 2002). Interestingly, a ΔsigX mutant displayed a phenotype similar to that of the ΔcomD and ΔcomE mutants. These results signified the importance of an intact CSP–ComDE quorum-sensing system initiating from CSP to the eventual activation of the sigX gene, and suggest that biofilm-associated genes are regulated by this alternative sigma factor. Using a transcriptional fusion of the sigX promoter with a green fluorescent protein reporter, Aspiras and colleagues (Aspiras et al., 2004) showed active sigX promoter activity in high-cell-density areas within the biofilm. These results suggested for the first time a positive correlation between SigX activity and competence development in S. mutans biofilms. 

## 21.4.6 Acid adaptation to survive killing pH

The pH of most oral surfaces is regulated by saliva, keeping the acidity of the mouth within a pH value of 6.2 to 7.4. Following consumption of acidic foods and drinks, the pH in dental plaque can fall rapidly below 5.0 and the acid can dissolve the hydroxyapatite, the main component of tooth enamel. S. mutans has the ability to metabolize a wide variety of sugars that result in the production of acid (mainly lactic acid), causing erosion of tooth enamel leading to dental caries (see Section 16). Consequently, the ability of S. mutans to tolerate the acid conditions encountered in the oral cavity is central to its survival in the dental plaque biofilm. To survive pH variations, S. mutans has developed both constitutive (proton-translocating $\mathrm { F } _ { 1 } \mathrm { F } _ { 0 }$ ATPase) and inducible (acid tolerance response, or ATR) mechanisms (Quivey et al., 2001; Lemos et al., 2005; Lemos and Burne, 2008). Early after its discovery, the CSP–ComDE quorum-sensing system has been linked to the induction of the ATR in S. mutans. In their study, Li et al. (2001a) first observed better cell survival when high cell population density of S. mutans was challenged with acid pH compared with lower cell density. When they inactivated the comC, comD, or comE genes, they found that the quorum-sensing deficient mutants had diminished logphase ATR. Addition of synthetic CSP to the ΔcomC mutant cultures almost completely restored the log-phase ATR, demonstrating that the CSP pheromone was contributing to the induction of the ATR. 

## 21.4.7 Regulation of bacteriocin (mutacin) production for interspecies competition

Bacteriocins are small ribosomally synthesized peptides or proteins produced by bacteria to kill or inhibit the growth of other bacterial species (Nes et al., 2007). Bacteriocin production is highly advantageous in biofilms where closely related organisms are in direct competition for nutrients. Different classes of bacteriocins (lantibiotics and nonlantibiotics) have been defined according to their size, heat sensitivity, chemical structure, and posttranslational modification. The lantibiotics contain the amino acids lanthionine or ß-methyllanthionine, while nonlantibiotic bacteriocins do not contain lanthionine modifications. The majority of bacteriocins have a net positive charge and contain sequences of hydrophobic and/or amphiphilic nature, allowing them to insert into the negatively charged cytoplasmic membrane of bacteria, creating pores in target cells (Hechard and Sahl, 2002). The result is a disruption of the proton motive force, ATP depletion, and leakage of nutrients and metabolites, leading to the eventual death of the target cells. Bacteriocinproducing cells are naturally protected from their own bacteriocins through the concomitant production of dedicated immunity proteins. 

S. mutans is known to produce a vast repertoire of bacteriocins (also called mutacins in S. mutans), which are secreted into the environment and are active against closely related species (Hale et al., 2005; Merritt and Qi, 2012). The lantibiotics produced by S. mutans tend to have a wide spectrum of activity against Gram-positive bacteria, whereas the nonlantibiotics are primarily active against closely related species (Merritt and Qi, 2012). Interestingly, the vast majority of bacteriocins and bacteriocin-like peptides identified in S. mutans UA159, the genome sequence reference strain (Ajdic et al., 2002), are located in a 13.5 kb bacteriocin-related genomic island that harbors the genes encoding the CSP–ComDE quorum-sensing system (comC, comD, and comE) and the ComAB transporter (comA and comB) for the export of the CSP signal molecule. Several studies have shown that the expression of multiple bacteriocin genes is directly regulated via the CSP–ComDE quorum-sensing system (Kreth et al., 2005, 2006; Perry et al., 2009b; van der Ploeg, 2005). This includes mutacin IV, mutacin V, mutacin VI, and the putative bacteriocins encoded by the genes SMU.1905 and SMU.1906 (Table 21.4.1). 

In vitro experiments performed by Kreth et al. (2006) showed that the most potent inducer of bacteriocin transcription in S. mutans was related to cell density. Indeed, the authors showed a strong induction of mutacins IV, V, and VI using in vitro conditions mimicking the high cell density of the dental biofilm. These results suggest that the regulation of bacteriocin production through the CSP–ComDE quorum-sensing system confers a considerable ecological advantage to S. mutans in its natural ecosystem. Interesting work performed by Kreth’s group also showed that S. mutans has evolved a unique mechanism for coordinating the control of bacteriocin production and competence development using its CSP–ComDE quorum-sensing system (Kreth et al., 2005). The ecological advantage for a combined control of bacteriocin production and genetic competence is an increased competitiveness of S. mutans in a multispecies biofilm where there is a large pool of eDNA released from competitors killed by the bacteriocins produced by S. mutans (Merritt and Qi, 2012). 

## 21.4.8 Programmed cell death or bacterial “suicide”

Programmed cell death (PCD) refers to any form of cell death genetically regulated by an intracellular death program (Ameisen, 1996; Jacobson et al., 1997). It is classically known as apoptosis in multicellular organisms. It has been demonstrated that PCD also occurs in unicellular organisms. PCD that appears undesirable to a single-celled organism may, however, be advantageous to a bacterial population at the multicellular level such as a biofilm. Several biological roles have been proposed for bacterial PCD (Hu et al., 2010; Tanouchi et al., 2013). For example, a bacterial cell may commit “suicide” before damage (e.g., DNA damage) becomes incompatible with vitality. Bacterial PCD may also be activated by stress conditions. For instance, it may be beneficial for a fraction of a bacterial population to undergo suicide under nutrient starvation, providing nutrients for the remaining surviving siblings. It was also proposed that bacterial PCD may have evolved as a defense mechanism to inhibit the proliferation of bacteriophages in bacterial populations (Engelberg-Kulka et al., 2004). Biofilm cells are enclosed in structures that resemble “mushrooms” or simple “towers” (see Section 22). These structures possess elaborate levels of organization, including open channels that facilitate transport of water and nutrients (Donlan, 2002; Lopez ´ et al., 2010). It has been hypothesized that altruistic death occurred inside microcolonies in biofilms, and formation of open channels was the result from the region of cell killing and lysis (Mai-Prochnow et al., 2004). 

Work performed by some members of our group showed that particular environmental stresses activate the expression of the S. mutans comC gene encoding the CSP propeptide, and S. mutans cells responded to high levels of CSP pheromone by inducing death in a fraction of the population (Perry et al., 2009b; Qi et al., 2005). Using fluorescent staining for viability to quantify the extent of cell death, it was found that <10% of the S. mutans population stains with propidium iodide because of cell death (Perry et al., 2009b). The CipB bacteriocin directly regulated by the ComE regulator of the CSP–ComDE quorum-sensing system was identified as a major effector in the CSP-induced PCD process or bacterial suicide. Importantly, we demonstrated that S. mutans suicide was due to intracellular accumulation of the unprocessed form of CipB bacteriocin $( \mathrm { C i p B } ^ { I n } )$ in the producing cells (Figure 21.4.2). CSPinduced suicide can be prevented by the action of the immunity protein CipI (Perry et al., 2009b). Interestingly, CipBIn participates in S. mutans suicide at the transcriptional level by regulating SigX-dependent genes (Dufour et al., 2011). The authors’ most recent data showed that the conserved streptococcal LytF murein hydrolase is SigX dependent and is the major player during CSP-induced PCD causing autolysis (Dufour and Levesque, ´ 2013b). The fact that LytF enzyme and its functional homolog CbpD are encoded within the chromosome of nearly all streptococcal species (Berg et al., 2012) suggests that CSP-induced PCD might constitute a common mechanism in streptococci. 

Although the mechanism of action of CipI immunity protein is currently unknown, we can hypothesize that CipI sequesters CipBIn when found at low density (uninduced CSP cells or unstressed conditions) and prevents its regulatory function. In contrast, when CipBIn is found at high cell density (CSP-induced cells or stress conditions), unsequestered CipBIn can act as a peptide regulator to activate lytF gene expression leading to cell lysis of a fraction of the S. mutans population. We have presented evidence that CSP-induced PCD is involved in the release of eDNA in the biofilm matrix, which contributes to its architecture and may provide a mechanism for the dissemination of genes under stress (Perry et al., 2009a). Recently, CSP-induced PCD has been associated with long-term survival by enabling a fraction of the S. mutans population to survive by feeding on the nutrients released from their dead siblings (Dufour and Levesque, ´ 2013b). 

## 21.4.9 Formation of dormant persister cells

Persisters are a subpopulation of nongrowing bacterial cells that are tolerant to all antibiotics currently in use, a phenomenon known as multidrug tolerance (Lewis, 2010). In contrast to the typical antibiotic resistance mechanisms in which inherited resistance can result from mutations in existing genes or the acquisition of external antibiotic resistance genes, multidrug tolerance is non-inherited and purely phenotypic (Wiuff et al., 2005). Persisters are dormant cells that neither grow nor die in the presence of high doses of microbicidal antibiotics. They arise primarily in biofilms and stationary-phase cultures. Persisters are found with a frequency of 0.0001 to 0.001% in the planktonic cultures and as high as 1% in biofilms. Formation of persisters is the main factor responsible for the tolerance of pathogens to antibiotics. In fact, persisters are the key to biofilm resilience because survival of just a few persisters allows the biofilm to repopulate (Lewis, 2007). Persisters can be formed through random intrinsic fluctuations of protein levels, such as the well-studied stable toxins expressed by chromosomal toxin– antitoxin modules (Gerdes and Maisonneuve, 2012; Schuster and Bertram, 2013). Spontaneous gene expression and noise during transcription and translation, amplified by feedback regulatory processes, also give rise to fluctuating protein levels of persister genes that result in a dormant multidrug-tolerant state. The redundancy in the formation of persisters hampers efforts to prevent tolerance toward antimicrobial drugs through the targeting of a single mechanism (Balaban, 2011; Jayaraman, 2008; Veening et al., 2008). 

While most molecular studies on persister formation are primarily done in Gram-negative bacteria, especially Escherichia coli, studies have been performed in S. mutans by some members of the authors’ group. They first demonstrated that the CSP–ComDE quorum-sensing system was used by S. mutans as a deterministic mechanism for persister formation (Leung and Levesque, 2012). Specifically, the CSP pheromone´ functions as a stress-inducible alarmone in response to various adverse environmental stressors. This inducible persister phenotype was abolished when any quorum-sensing knockout mutants (ΔcomC, ΔcomD, ΔcomE) were unable to respond toward CSP, signifying the importance of the CSP–ComDE quorum-sensing system. Interestingly, their results also show that S. mutans persisters accumulate as the biofilm matures, probably due to altered microenvironments within the biofilm (Leung and Levesque, 2012; see Section 22). Since biofilm´ infections show a surprising ability to resist killing by antibiotics without having any obvious drug resistance mechanisms, these results reinforce the hypothesis that persisters play a significant role in biofilm drug tolerance (Dufour et al., 2012). More importantly, persisters become a reservoir from which recurrence of infection will develop and are mainly responsible for chronic infections. 

The authors’ group had also recently shown that disrupting the signal relay from ComDE TCS to the ComRS system, and ultimately the SigX regulator itself through gene knockouts, prevents the inducible persistence (Leung et al., 2015). In the same study, it was discovered that the ortholog of the LexA transcriptional regulator encoded in the genome of S. mutans plays a significant regulatory role in the formation of these persister cells induced by the CSP pheromone. Additionally, transcriptome analyses identified key genes contributing to these induced persisters regulated by LexA, specifically those involved in sugar and amino acid metabolism, the CRISPR (clustered regularly interspaced short palindromic repeat) system (see Chapter 2.11), and an autolysin. Altogether, these results show the importance of cell-to-cell communication via quorumsensing signaling for initiating an adaptive stress response within a subpopulation of S. mutans to form multidrug-tolerant persisters for survival. 

## 21.4.10 Concluding remarks



Hechard Y, Sahl HG. 2002. Mode of action of modified and unmodified bacteriocins from Gram-positive bacteria. Biochimie 84: 545– 557. 





Qi F, Kreth J, Levesque CM, Kay O, Mair RW, ´ et al. 2005. Peptide pheromone induced cell death of Streptococcus mutans. FEMS Microbiology Letters 251: 321–326. 



Bacteria possess the amazing ability of occupying almost all possible ecological niches. In order to successfully colonize a particular site, bacteria have to adapt and adjust to the prevailing conditions of the specific habitat. They have evolved several mechanisms for sensing and responding to environmental conditions, including the interactions among themselves and other cells. It appears that the ability to form biofilms is one of the most remarkable characteristics of bacteria. When living in a biofilm, bacteria are capable of cell-to-cell communication in the form of diffusible extracellular signal molecules to coordinate gene expression in accordance with population density, a phenomenon known as quorum-sensing. Importantly, recent work in S. mutans and S. pneumoniae, two important human pathogens, demonstrated a link between the quorum-sensing CSP pheromone and the coordination of the general stress response (Dufour and Levesque, 2013a; Prudhomme ´ et al., 2006). In this chapter, we discussed the various phenotypes controlled by the CSP pheromone, a stress-inducible alarmone, in the biofilm organism S. mutans. The many striking observations reported to date in the literature should encourage researchers to develop quorum-sensing inhibitors that could interfere with the quorum-sensing-dependent phenotypes. The administration of such quorum-sensing inhibitors combined with antimicrobial drugs could represent an attractive strategy to combat recurrent biofilm infections of medical importance.
