# Quorum sensing in Bacillus cereus in relation to cysteine metabolism and the oxidative stress response

Eugenie Huillet and Michel Gohar´ 

INRA, AgroParisTech, Gen´ etique microbienne et Environnement UMR1319 MICALIS, Jouy-en-Josas, France ´ 

## 21.5.1 Introduction

Quorum sensing (QS) allows bacteria to share information about cell density to adjust gene expression accordingly. This cell–cell communication relies on secreted signal molecules, called autoinducers (AI) or pheromones. This system enables bacteria to undergo energetically expensive processes as a collective only when the impact of these processes on their environment is more likely to be efficient (Dunny and Leonard, 1997; see Chapters 21.1, 21.3, 21.4 and Chapter 2.18). 

Intercellular communication processes are crucial in coordinating growth, adaptation, pathogenicity, biofilm formation, and sporulation in the Bacillus cereus group (Slamti et al., 2014). The B. cereus group includes well-known spore-forming pathogens of mammals (Bacillus anthracis and B. cereus) and insects (Bacillus thuringiensis). B. cereus is frequently associated with foodborne infections causing gastroenteritis (Stenfors Arnesen et al., 2008). B. cereus has also been described as the cause of nosocomial infections in immunocompromised patients and is involved in endocarditis, endophthalmitis, and meningitis that have proved fatal (Stenfors Arnesen et al., 2008). The capacity of B. cereus to sporulate allows this bacterium to resist the usual cleaning procedures used in the food industry, resulting in the presence of B. cereus in many raw and processed foods, such as rice, spices, milk, vegetables, meats, and various desserts (Abee et al., 2011; see Chapter 22.7). 

The PlcR–PapR QS system controls the expression of genes encoding exported virulence factors, including degradative enzymes, enterotoxins, and hemolysins in B. cereus and B. thuringiensis species (Agaisse et al., 1999). The transcription of these virulence genes in bacteria growing in a rich medium is activated at the onset of the stationary phase or transition phase by the active quorum sensor PlcR. PlcR is activated by binding to PapR, a signalling peptide produced as a propeptide under the control of PlcR (Figure 21.5.1). PapR undergoes extracellular processing, to generate an active heptapeptide (Bouillaut et al., 2008), which is then re-imported into the bacterial cell via the oligopeptide permease system, OppABCDF. Within the cell, PapR interacts with PlcR and the resulting complex binds PlcR target sites on DNA (Slamti and Lereclus, 2002), resulting in the activation of the PlcR regulon, which contains 45 genes (Gohar et al., 2008). For a more detailed overview of the PlcR–PapRa system, we recommend a recent review by Slamti (Slamti et al., 2014) 

Determination of the structure of PlcR resulted in the identification of a new family of central regulatory quorum sensors (the RNPP family) found exclusively in Gram-positive bacteria with a low G+C content (Declerck et al., 2007). These quorum sensors include PlcRa and NprR from B. cereus (Huillet et al., 2012; Perchat et al., 2011), PrgX from Enteroccocus faecalis (Shi et al., 2005), and RAP phosphatases from Bacillus subtilis (Parashar et al., 2011; Solomon et al., 1996). All these RNPP regulators are activated through a secreted signalling peptide that interacts with the TPR activation domain. 

We recently characterized PlcRa, which activates the oxidative stress response and cysteine metabolism in transition state cells (Huillet et al., 2012). PlcRa displays about 29% sequence identity and about 50% similarity to PlcR. We found that the PlcRa protein is a 3D structural homolog of PlcR, suggesting that the TPR peptide-binding domain may be arranged similarly to form a pocket that is responsible for peptide binding. PlcRa is activated by the product of the papRa gene, a secreted signalling peptide (Figure 21.5.1) (Huillet et al., 2012). Biochemical and genetic analyses demonstrated the role of the heptapeptide PapRa in PlcRa activity. Purified PlcRa specifically binds to a PlcRa-controlled promoter in the presence of PapRa heptapeptide (abrB2 gene) (Huillet et al., 2012). The production, the maturation of PapRa, and the binding of PapRa with the TPR activation domain remain to be established (Figure 21.5.1). 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/d267264921905486e8d89df6ae83c29f2121b5c599f9697df46a94857b8dcab1.jpg)



Figure 21.5.1 Schematic representation of the PlcR–PapR and PlcRa–PapRa QS systems in B. cereus: production of AIP (PapR and PapRa), activation of cognate quorum sensor (PlcR and PlcRa), and presentation of major gene targets. The product of the papR gene is a 48 amino acid peptide. PapR is secreted, probably via the Sec machinery, and processed by the NprB protease and presumably other peptidases. PapR is found in the extracellular compartment and in the bacterial cytoplasm, mainly as a seven amino acid peptide. It is imported in the cell via the oligopeptide permease Opp. Once inside the cell, PapR binds to PlcR, allowing it to activate the expression of 45 genes composing the PlcR regulon, including plcR and papR genes. PlcRa activates the expression of 18 genes involved in cysteine metabolism, and it activates the expression of five genes involved in oxidative stress response. The product of the papRa gene is a 93 amino acid peptide. As described for PapR, a typical Gram-positive N-terminal signal peptide has been identified for PapRa, suggesting that PapRa is also probably secreted via the Sec machinery. ‘?’ Indicates that maturation and import of PapRa have not been reported yet. Black arrows represent a positive effect on gene transcription. Green lines indicate the circuit (production, export, processing, import, and activation) followed by PapR and PapRa peptides.


In Gram-negative bacteria, QS systems use mainly acylated homoserine lactones as signal molecules, while linear or cyclic peptides fulfil this function in Gram-positive bacteria. However, a conserved quorum-sensing system is found in both Gram-positive and Gram-negative bacteria (Pereira et al., 2013). The signal molecule of this system is AI2 (autoinducer-2), a detoxification product of the activated methyl cycle or Sadenosylmethionine (SAM) recycling linked to cysteine biosynthesis from methionine (Figure 21.5.2). AI2 is a derivative of (S) 4,5-dihydro-2,3-pentanedione (DPD). DPD itself is a byproduct of the activated methyl cycle (AMC), which generates the methyl donor, SAM, required for a number of metabolic reactions including methylations and polyamine biosynthesis. Release of methyl from SAM results in the production of Sadenosyl homocysteine (SAH), a toxic compound whose conversion to DPD and homocysteine is catalysed by the PfS and LuxS enzymes. DPD spontaneously converts to AI2 while homocysteine is recycled to SAM by the MetE, MetH, and MetK enzymes. Therefore, AI2 is both a product of the central metabolism and a signal molecule. 

AI2 is exported by still-unknown mechanisms. When its threshold concentration in the extracellular milieu is reached, the signal molecule is re-imported in the cell by a specific channel – the LsrACD transporter in Bacillacae (Figure 21.5.3). Once in the cell, AI2 is phosphorylated by the LrsK kinase, enabling its binding to, and the inactivation of, the LrsR transcriptional repressor. Genes regulated by LrsB can therefore be transcribed (Figure21.5.3 and 21.5.4). 

Despite the presence of PfS and LuxS in most bacteria, not all of these bacteria use AI2 as a signal molecule for quorumsensing purposes, since some of them lack the AI2 receptor (Rezzonico and Duffy, 2008). However, pathogens of the B. cereus group (B. anthracis, B. cereus, and B. thuringiensis) possess the genetic equipment for AI2 secretion, importation, and processing. In addition, the LuxS-dependent production of AI2 and its effect on growth have been demonstrated in B. anthracis (Jones and Blaser, 2003). 

In this review, we provide an overview of PlcRa and Lsrlike QS systems in B. cereus with the AI2 connection between the two systems (Figure 21.5.4) and discuss in the concluding remarks (Section 21.5.4) the physiological effects of this QS interplay during bacterial growth. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/734d1690d4c2200e02c5b18316bb557dd7394bc5f63599e4b423c722e064747a.jpg)



Figure 21.5.2 Biosynthesis of DPD. The transfer of a methyl moiety from SAM (S-adenosyl methionine) to a substrate (R1) generates SAH (S-adenosyl homocysteine), which is converted to SRH (S-ribosyl homocysteine) by PfS. LuxS catalyses the production of DPD (S-4,5-dihydroxy-2,3-pentanedione) and homocysteine. SAM is then regenerated by MetE, MetH, and MetK. R2 stands for THF (Nmethyltetrahydrofolate) or THPG (5-methyltetrahydropteroyl glutamate).


## 21.5.2 AIP-mediated signalling in B. cereus: PlcRa system

In this section, we first provide a brief overview of cysteine metabolism in Bacillus and present the major regulatory system that governs it (the cysteine metabolism repressor (CymR) regulon) in the Gram-positive model bacterium, B. subtilis. Then, we present transcriptomic changes in relation to the CymR regulon induced by deletion of the plcRa gene in B. cereus. Finally, we provide a brief overview of the oxidative stress defence mechanisms including CymR control in B. subtilis, and then present phenotypic changes in the plcRa mutant strain in the presence of hydrogen peroxide and diamide. 

## 21.5.2.1 CymR: master regulator of cysteine metabolism in B. subtilis

The sulphur-containing amino acid, cysteine, plays a major role in cellular physiology, and its synthesis is tightly controlled due to its reactivity. Cysteine is a biosynthetic precursor of methionine, thiamine, biotin, lipoic acid, coenzyme A, and coenzyme M. Cysteine is also required for the biogenesis of [Fe–S] clusters, it is found in the catalytic site of several enzymes, and it helps protein folding and assembly by forming disulphide bonds. 

Cysteine metabolism is well characterized in B. subtilis (Burguiere et al., 2004; Even et al., 2006; Hullo et al., 2007). In this bacterium, cysteine is synthesized either from homocysteine via the reverse trans-sulphuration pathway (Hullo et al., 2007) or from sulphide or thiosulphate via the thiolation pathway that directly incorporates these compounds into O-acetyl-L-serine (OAS) (Figure 21.5.5). Sulphide is obtained from the transport and reduction of inorganic sulphate or from organic sulphonates via their oxidation. Finally, methionine degradation is a biosynthesis pathway of cysteine formation, and it proceeds via the formation of homocysteine using the Sadenosyl-methionine (SAM) recycling pathway followed by reverse trans-sulphuration, involving the MccA cystathionine β- synthase, and the MccB cystathionine γ-lyase (Figure 21.5.5). 

In B. subtilis (Even et al., 2006; Tanous et al., 2008), CymR is the master regulator of cysteine metabolism. CymR represses the transcription of a large set of genes involved in cystine uptake and cysteine biosynthesis. The CymR repressor belongs to the widespread and poorly characterized Rrf2 family of transcription factors (Shepard et al., 2011; Tanous et al., 2008). CymR displays a particular mechanism of control of its activity, with the formation of a regulatory complex with CysK, an OAS-thiollyase. In this complex, CymR is the DNA-binding protein, and CysK increases the stability of the interaction between CymR and DNA (Tanous et al., 2008). 

In the signal transduction pathway controlling cysteine metabolism, CysK, via its substrate OAS, is the sensor of the cysteine pool in the cell for the regulatory complex, and transmits this information to the CymR repressor (Hullo et al., 2007). OAS is produced from acetyl-CoA and serine by CysE, the serine acetyltransferase while the OAS-thiol-lyase, CysK, further condenses sulphide and OAS to form cysteine. Cysteine limits the OAS pool via its negative control of CysE synthesis and activity. In the presence of cysteine, the intracellular concentration of OAS is low, the CymR–CysK complex is formed, and transcriptional repression of the CymR regulon occurs (Figure 21.5.6a). Under conditions of cysteine limitation, the pool of OAS increases until it reaches a concentration preventing the formation of the CymR–CysK complex or dissociating the complex in vitro (Figure 21.5.6b). Thus, no repression of transcription of the CymR regulon occurs when cysteine is limiting (Even et al., 2006; Hullo et al., 2007; Tanous et al., 2008).

Figure 21.5.3 Organization of the lsr locus in B. cereus. The lsr locus includes genes encoding an import channel (in green), the AI2 receptor (in pink), the transcriptional repressor LsrR (in orange), the AI2 kinase (in blue), and an isomerase involved in the degradation of AI2 (in yellow). Putative promoters are figured by dark arrows.

Black dashed arrow indicates a role of the cysteine metabolism in oxidant protection. Black arrows represent a positive effect on gene transcription, and the cross ends of the arrows represent a negative effect on gene transcription. Orange lines indicate the circuit followed by AI2, and blue lines represent the circuit followed by AIP.


## 21.5.2.2 PlcRa controls the expression of the CymR regulon

We characterized the regulatory role of PlcRa, by comparing the transcriptomes of the ΔplcRa and wild-type B. cereus ATCC 14579 strains during the early stationary phase in a complex rich medium, lysogeny broth (LB). Assessments were carried out 1 h after the onset of the stationary phase $( t _ { 1 } )$ , when plcRa expression increases, and 2 h after the onset of the stationary phase $( t _ { 2 } ) _ { : }$ , when plcRa expression has reached a plateau (Huillet et al., 2012). 

Twenty of the 49 genes upregulated by PlcRa encode proteins involved in sulphur metabolism (Table 21.5.1). These genes were found to be differentially expressed 2 h after the onset of the stationary phase (Huillet et al., 2012). The sulphur metabolism genes of B. cereus remain poorly annotated, despite the availability of several B. cereus genomes. We therefore reconstructed the sulphur metabolism pathway, by searching for orthologues of B. subtilis genes in the B. cereus ATCC 14579 genome (Figure 21.5.5) (Huillet et al., 2012). The transport of sulphur sources (Burguiere et al., 2004) and the two major cysteine biosynthetic pathways in B. subtilis – the thiolation pathway, which requires sulphide, and the reverse trans-sulphuration pathway, which converts homocysteine to cysteine, with cystathionine formed as an intermediate (Even et al., 2006; Hullo et al., 2007) – are conserved in B. cereus ATCC 14579. We were able to identify all the enzymes and transporters required for these pathways other than those for the reduction of sulphite to sulphide (Table 21.5.1 and Figure 21.5.5) (Huillet et al., 2012). 

A total of 18 PlcRa-controlled genes are involved in cysteine metabolism: transporters (tcyP) and biosynthesis of cysteine from sulphate (cysH operon and cysK gene) or methionine (yrrT–mtnN–mccAB operon and luxS gene) (Table 21.5.1 and Figure 21.5.5). 

BC4393, which shares 76% identity with CymR from B. subtilis, is probably the global regulator of cysteine metabolism in B. cereus. The CymR DNA-binding motif (WTAW2N4ATWNRWNANAN2GGAATT) is present in the promoter region of seven PlcRa-controlled genes or operons involved in cysteine metabolism (Figure 21.5.7) (Huillet et al., 2012). A total of 14 PlcRa-controlled genes belong to the CymR regulon (Table 21.5.1).

Figure 21.5.5 Reconstruction of the sulphur metabolism pathway in B. cereus: transport and biosynthesis of sulphur-containing amino acids. The putative proteins involved in the uptake and assimilation of inorganic (sulphate) and organic sulphur sources (sulphonates, cystine, and methionine) are indicated by the corresponding genes. Cysteine is typically present in its oxidized form (cystine) in the extracellular environment. The BC numbers (ATCC14579 strain) for B. cereus genes are shown, with gene names according to the orthologues in B. subtilis. “?” Indicates genes probably involved in the pathway, or a step for which a gene is lacking or remains to be identified. All the PlcRa-regulated genes involved in sulphur metabolism are indicated by a downward black arrow, and the putative functions of all the corresponding proteins are presented in Table 21.5.1 Presumed direct targets of CymR are indicated in bold typeface. OAS, O-acetyl-serine; SAM, S-adenosyl-methionine.


No repression of transcription of the CymR regulon occurs when cysteine is limiting in B. subtilis. We hypothesized that PlcRa activates CymR-controlled genes when cysteine is limiting during the transition phase in B. cereus. Indeed, we found that the expression of the PyrrT’–lacZ fusion was induced at the onset of the stationary phase in the wild-type strain, suggesting cysteine depletion in the growth medium leading to the induction of the methionine-to-cysteine conversion pathway (Huillet et al., 2012). No such induction was observed in the plcRa mutant strain (Huillet et al., 2012). Figure 21.5.6a,b shows a proposed model for the global regulation of gene expression in response to cysteine availability in B. cereus. During the vegetative growth, in the presence of the preferred sulphur sources, sulphate or cysteine, CymR is active and represses the expression of CymR-controlled genes. Upon sulphate or cysteine limitation, as presumably observed at the onset of the stationary phase, the CymR repressor is inactive, and repression is thus relieved (see the legend in Figure 21.5.6a,b for details). In this growth condition, PlcRa activator, active during this transition phase, activates CymR-controlled genes. 

## 21.5.2.3 Role of the cysteine metabolism in oxidant protection in Bacillus

Many agents that restrict bacterial growth and developmental behaviour induce the production and/or accumulation of reactive substances, such as reactive oxygen species (ROS) (see Section 10), reactive nitrogen species (RNS) (see Section 17), and toxic electrophiles. Increased levels of reactive species accompany stress in many of its manifestations and lead to macromolecular damage, an increased rate of mutagenesis, and cell death (Zuber, 2009). Bacillus respond to 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/7e82a88fb5459ed9333b0e5eff74c6ec411e27f93804424fda5df6eb9a128f5e.jpg)



Figure 21.5.6 (a,b) Schematic representation of the interplay between PlcRa and CymR regulators in B. cereus. Cysteine is typically present in its oxidized form – cystine – in the extracellular environment. (a) In the presence of cystine, as indicated by $^ { \ast } + \ast ,$ the intracellular concentration of O-acetyl-serine (OAS) is low, the CymR–CysK complex is formed, and transcriptional repression of the CymR regulon occurs. (b) Under conditions of cystine limitation, as indicated by “−”, the pool of OAS increases until it reaches a concentration that prevents the formation of the CymR–CysK complex. Thus, no repression of transcription of the CymR regulon occurs when cysteine is limiting. In this growth condition, PlcRa activates CymR-controlled genes. “?” Indicates that the exact mechanism of PlcRa positive control remains to be defined. Black arrows represent a positive effect on gene transcription, and the cross ends of the arrows represent a negative effect on gene transcription. Green arrows indicate cysteine biosynthesis pathways. Plain arrow, high uptake and biosynthesis; dashed arrow, low uptake and biosynthesis; Int, intracellular; Ext, extracellular.


![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/ea59e237226ebe6f952aa26fd59a6ba757b0f6cb92b046a3d383a8d75c96619f.jpg)


oxidative stress by the activation of different cellular defence mechanisms (Zuber, 2009). These are composed of scavenging enzymes, as well as protection and repair systems presumably organized in highly sophisticated networks (Mols and Abee, 2011; Zuber, 2009). Cysteine alone, cysteine molecules, and cysteine proteins are major actors in oxidant protection. In particular, cysteine is the direct precursor of the low-molecularmass antioxidant molecule, Bacillithiol, a key actor in the maintenance of the cytosolic redox balance and in the adaptation to reactive oxygen species in Bacilli (Helmann, 2011; Newton et al., 2009). In addition, S-cysteinylation is a general mechanism for thiol protection of B. subtilis proteins after oxidative stress (Hochgrafe et al., 2007). Consequently, the CymR regulon involved in cysteine biosynthesis is involved in oxidant protection. It has been shown that a cymR mutant is highly sensitive to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ , disulphide, paraquat, copper, and tellurite-induced stresses in B. subtilis (Soutourina et al., 2009). The CymR regulon is induced during disulphide or superoxide stresses in B. subtilis (Mostertz et al., 2004).

## 21.5.2.4 PlcRa: role in the oxidative stress response

PlcRa is mainly involved in the activation of genes that function in oxidant protection through control of the expression of the CymR regulon (Huillet et al., 2012). PlcRa also activates expression of the ahpCF operon encoding the alkyl hydroperoxyreductase (AhpR), a detoxification system composed of two enzymes that catalyses the breakdown of molecular oxygen to hydrogen peroxide $\begin{array} { r l } { ( \mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } ) , } \end{array}$ , which is reduced by the second enzyme to water. PlcRa activates the expression of the dps2 gene encoding a Dps-like mini-ferritin protein (BC5044), which is homologous to the Dps2 protein of B. anthracis that has recently been shown to play a major role in oxidative stress resistance (Huillet et al., 2012). These genes are induced during $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ stress in B. cereus (Ceragioli et al., 2010). 

We tested the effect of plcRa inactivation on the susceptibility of B. cereus to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ and diamide stresses. Diamide causes thiol oxidation and disulphide stress. The viability of the ΔplcRa mutant and the parental strain was tested after the addition of $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ (1 mM). A sevenfold reduction in survival was observed for the ΔplcRa mutant compared to the wild-type strain. The complementation of the ΔplcRa mutant restored a wild-type phenotype. Then, the viability of the ΔplcRa mutant and the parental strain was tested after the addition of diamide (10 mM). A fourfold reduction in survival was observed for the plcRa mutant compared to the wild-type strain, while the introduction of plcRa in trans restored a wild-type phenotype (Huillet et al., 2012). These results showed that plcRa inactivation led to an increased sensitivity to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ and disulphide stresses. We also investigated the effects of cystine addition during peroxide stress (Huillet et al., 2012). Cystine (1 mM) was added to the culture in the mid-exponential growth phase (Figure 21.5.8). Two hours later, at the end of the exponential phase, $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } ( 0 . 4 $ mM) was added to the medium. Both strains presented a growth arrest 1 h after the $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ addition, which was characterized by an $\mathrm { O D } _ { 6 0 0 }$ measurements drop. In addition, in the presence of $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } , \mathrm { O D } _ { 6 0 0 }$ measures of the ΔplcRa mutant strain were lower than those of the wild-type strain. When cystine was added, the growth arrest for both strains was abolished. Thus, cystine significantly reduced the sensitivity of these cells to $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ stress in our growth conditions. Moreover, these results strongly suggest that cystine transport is efficient in the ΔplcRa mutant, as in the wild type, and it might be due at least partly to the TcyABC system (BC0872–BC0873–BC0874) that is not controlled by PlcRa (Figure 21.5.5 and 21.5.6).

An alignment of the promoter regions of the ssuB, yrrT, tcyP, luxS, cysK, cysH, and BC1090 genes is presented. The consensus sequence for the CymR-binding site was determined with the WebLogo tool (http://weblogo.berkeley.edu). 


There is accumulating evidence suggesting that metabolic imbalances also induce bacterial processes that are normally associated with the oxidative stress response and/or with the general stress response. Altered transcriptional activities in the plcRa mutant (117 genes differentially expressed with a more than threefold difference) probably resulted in metabolic changes that seem to have caused general and oxidative stresses (Huillet et al., 2012). Indeed, in the plcRa mutant, in LB medium without treatment, 10 genes that belong to the $\sigma ^ { \mathrm { B } }$ regulon are strongly expressed (Huillet et al., 2012). This includes $\bar { \sigma } ^ { \mathrm { B } }$ and its associated regulatory proteins RbsV, RbsW, and RbsP. In B. subtilis, elevated activity of $\mathsf { \Pi } _ { \mathtt { O } } \mathsf { B }$ factor and induction of the $\sigma ^ { \mathrm { B } }$ general stress regulon confer increased oxidant resistance (Zuber, 2009). Upon exposure of B. cereus to mild and lethal $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ concentrations, the expression of numerous genes was affected, including genes involved in the $\sigma ^ { \mathrm { B } }$ common response to general stresses (Ceragioli et al., 2010). Oxidative and disulphide stresses regulation of prophage genes has been reported in Lactobacillus plantarum, Lactobacillus reuteri, and B. subtilis (Wilson et al., 2012). Notably, in a plcRa mutant, the expression of 31 prophage genes is higher (Huillet et al., 2012). 

## 21.5.3 AI2-mediated signalling in B. cereus: a Lsr-like system

The production of AI2 has been shown in B. anthracis to be LuxS dependent, and this production is maximal at the end of the exponential phase of growth and decreased in the stationary phase (Jones and Blaser, 2003). In B. cereus too, production of AI2 increased in the exponential phase and decreased in the stationary phase (Figure 21.5.8), suggesting that this quorumsensing system is active during the transition between the exponential and the stationary phases of growth. In the same species, the effect of exogenously added AI2 in a microtiter plate biofilm assay was assayed. AI2 inhibited in a dose-dependent way the production of biofilm in these conditions. It also induced a decrease in biofilm biomass when this compound was added to mature biofilms (Figure 21.5.9) (Auger et al., 2006). 

In Gram-positive as well as in Gram-negative bacteria, the AI2 quorum-sensing system controls a number of phenotypes, including chemotaxis or toxin production in pathogens. However, the phenotype most frequently targeted by this quorum-sensing system is biofilm formation (Pereira et al., 2013; see Section 22). Interestingly, while AI2 promotes biofilm formation in most species, the inverse effect is observed in B. cereus. This effect might be of importance in the formation of multispecies biofilm, where AI2, working as an interspecies communication molecule, could modulate the balance of biofilm species.

While the lsr system appears to be fully functional in pathogenic bacteria of the B. cereus group, this is not a general rule in all bacteria of the Firmicutes phylum. In Listeria monocytogenes, Clostridium difficile, or L. reuteri, for instance, the effect of luxS deletion is not a consequence of AI2 absence, but is due to a perturbation of the bacterium metabolism (Carter et al., 2005; Challan Belval et al., 2006; Garmyn et al., 2009).

## 21.5.4 Concluding remarks: interplay between PlcRa and Lsr-like QS during the transition between the vegetative and sporulation phases



Ceragioli M, Mols M, Moezelaar R, Ghelardi E, Senesi S, Abee T. 2010. Comparative transcriptomic and phenotypic analysis of the responses of Bacillus cereus to various disinfectant treatments. Applied and Environmental Microbiology 76: 3352–3360. 



At the end of the vegetative growth phase, bacterial cells face a number of challenges, including a decrease in the nutrient content of their environment and accumulation of oxidative products. Under these conditions, spore-forming bacteria may initiate sporulation, producing spores that can survive in unfavourable environmental conditions (Piggot and Hilbert, 2004). Bacteria make use of various strategies to cope with environmental changes during the transition between the vegetative and sporulation phases (Phillips and Strauch, 2002). 



Challan Belval S, Gal L, Margiewes S, Garmyn D, Piveteau P, et al. 2006. Assessment of the roles of LuxS, S-ribosyl homocysteine, and autoinducer 2 in cell attachment during biofilm formation by Listeria monocytogenes EGD-e. Applied and Environmental Microbiology 72: 2644–2650. 



Alternatively to sporulation, bacteria can enter another developmental state, the biofilm (see Section 22). The choice between sporulation and biofilm is likely to be a consequence of the speed at which Spo0A, the sporulation master regulator, is activated by kinases. Low Spo0A∼P concentrations, although unable to initiate the sporulation cascade of events, can promote biofilm formation by repressing or activating the transcription of other regulator genes (namely, abrB and sinI) (Fujita and Losick, 2005). 



Declerck N, Bouillaut L, Chaix D, Rugani N, Slamti L, et al. 2007. Structure of PlcR: Insights into virulence regulation and evolution of quorum sensing in Gram-positive bacteria. Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the USA 104: 18490–18495. 





Dunny GM, Leonard BA. 1997. Cell-cell communication in grampositive bacteria. Annual Review of Microbiology 51: 527–564. 



In this review, we presented a new peptide-mediated QS system, PlcRa–PapRa, involved in AI2 production and in the control of cysteine biosynthesis during the transition between the vegetative and sporulation phases. The two QS systems, PlcRa and Lsr-like, are interconnected through AI2 production. In this review, we highlight that cysteine metabolism is an essential component of B. cereus physiology at the entry of the stationary phase for three major reasons: (i) adaptation to sulphur-poor conditions, (ii) oxidative stress response, and (iii) biofilm control. AI2 is found in many bacterial species and thus proposed to enable interspecies communication. We identified a unique transcriptional control of AI2 production via CymR and PlcRa regulators. This control is specific to B. cereus and B. thuringiensis species. 



Even S, Burguiere P, Auger S, Soutourina O, Danchin A, et al. 2006. Global control of cysteine metabolism by CymR in Bacillus subtilis. Journal of Bacteriology 188: 2184–2197. 



However, many specific and global questions surround Lsrlike and PlcRa–PapRa systems: PapRa signal production, AI2 signal transduction, Lsr receptor characterization, full phenotypic characterization of a AI2–Lsr-like system, role of PlcRa– papRa in virulence, and what is the benefit of AI2 tight transcriptional control for biofilm control? What is the benefit of QS-mediated control of cysteine metabolism during the infectious process of B. cereus? 



Fujita M, Losick R. 2005. Evidence that entry into sporulation in Bacillus subtilis is governed by a gradual increase in the level and activity of the master regulator Spo0A. Genes & Development 19: 2236– 2244.

## Chemotaxis and biofilm formation

## Rasika M. Harshey

Department of Molecular Biosciences and Institute of Cellular and Molecular Biology, University of Texas at Austin, Austin, Texas, USA 

## 22.1.1 Introduction

Biological systems function by a supply-and-demand economy, where negative and positive feedback loops operate to optimize output. In the well-studied motile bacterium Escherichia coli, the flagellar rotary motor is at the output end of a sensory response mediated by a chemoreceptor-linked kinase, which transmits chemical information about the environment to the motor via a response regulator, modulating the clockwise (CW)– counterclockwise (CCW) bias of the motor, and enabling the bacterium to seek optimal habitats (Berg, 2003). A machinery that regulates rotational bias might be expected to be perturbed if rotation stops; however, jamming or slowing the motors has no feedback effect on the kinase (Shimizu et al., 2006). Surprisingly, jamming the motor directs the bacteria to turn on programs that enable surface colonization either by promoting motility on an agar surface (swarming) or by promoting adhesion to surfaces (biofilm formation). These observations reveal that the flagellar motor is itself a sensory device. How such a device functions is not yet understood. This review will begin with a description of the flagellum and its assembly, then examine the evidence implicating a sensory function to the motor in a variety of bacteria, and finally suggest a common sensing mechanism. 

## 22.1.2 The flagellum

The flagellum and its assembly are best characterized in Salmonella enterica (Chevance and Hughes, 2008; Macnab, 2003), where the flagellum is composed of three main continuous structural elements – the basal body (BB), the hook (H), and the filament (Figure 22.1.1a). The first two structures are generally refered to as HBB. This basic structural arrangement is common to all the bacteria discussed in this article, whether Grampositive or Gram-negative, and whether positioned at polar or lateral locations. The BB is constituted by the C ring, MS ring, rod, L ring, P ring, and stators. The stators conduct protons that power rotation, and the L/P rings serve as bushings for the rod; 

both of these are stationary. The entire HBB complex, along with the attached filament, rotates. 

Flagellum assembly is thought to begin with polymerization of the MS ring (FliF protein) in the inner membrane. This ring serves as a platform for self-assembly of a cytoplasmic C ring, made of three proteins FliG, FliM, and FliN (FliY in Bacillus subtilis). The Type III secretion apparatus (T3S) docks in the membrane patch enclosed by the MS ring and begins secreting proximal, then distal, flagellar components (the periplasmic rod and the external hook, respectively). A secreted molecular ruler, FliK, monitors completion of the HBB. The C ring makes critical contacts with the T3S apparatus and is essential for HBB assembly. Upon completion of the HBB, conformational changes in a subset of the T3S proteins switch the secretion pattern to allow export of late substrates. The first of these substrates is an anti-$\sigma ^ { 2 8 }$ factor, FlgM. Secretion of FlgM relieves the repression of σ28, allowing the synthesis, secretion, and assembly of hook–filament junction proteins (HAPs), followed by assembly of the long filament (Figure 22.1.1b). The filament is generally built from a single flagellin subunit type. In the absence of HBB completion, FlgM will not be exported, and late substrates will not be made. These include the stators and proteins directing the chemosensory (Che) response. 

## 22.1.2.1 Differences in flagellar components in other bacteria

The L/P rings are missing in the Gram-positive bacterium B. subtilis, which does not have an outer lipopolysaccharide (LPS) membrane (Mukherjee and Kearns, 2014). There are additional components specific to the polar flagellum (Pof) in Vibrio species. An outer membrane sheath encases the external filament, the filament is composed of multiple flagellin subunit types, there are H and T rings (made of proteins called MotX and MotY) attached to the outer L and P rings that stabilize the stators, and the stators conduct Na+ instead of H+ ions (Li et al., 2011). The Pof in Caulobacter crescentus has an E ring below the P ring (Brown et al., 2009). 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/48f91ea0051a3569e655da8b34faf001e4021d5dfe53ceaca99cd05e2da69233.jpg)



Figure 22.1.1 The Salmonella flagellum and its assembly. (a) The basal body (BB) consists of a cytoplasmic C ring, an inner membrane MS ring, the periplasmic rod with its associated L and P rings, and inner membrane stators (MotAB complexes). The rod is continuous with the external hook and a long helical filament. Proteins constituting these structures are secreted via a T3S system at the base of the MS ring. MotAB stators power the rotor by conducting protons (H+). They insert and drift in the membrane till they encounter the basal MS–C rings, upon which their plugged proton channel opens. Expanded inset: During ion flow, protonation of the critical Asp (D) residue in MotB induces a conformational change in the cytoplasmic region of MotA that sets up charge–charge interactions with FliG in the C ring. This interaction causes FliG to move, generating torque transmitted to the structure above. The entire structure from the C ring to the filament rotates. YcgR and H-NS proteins affect rotor–stator interaction in opposite ways. See the main text for more details. OM, outer membrane; PG, peptidoglycan; IM, inner membrane. (b) A master transcription regulator complex FlhDC is responsible for synthesis of the HBB (hook–basal body), as well as for σ28 that controls late genes, and for the anti-σ28 regulator FlgM. Completion of the HBB allows FlgM to be secreted, relieving σ28 repression. This allows synthesis and assembly of the HAP (hook-associated proteins) and filament, as well as synthesis of the stators (MotAB) and chemotaxis proteins. Regulators that control the timing of expression of the flagellar regulon are specific to each species, as are the regulatory checkpoints.


## 22.1.3 The flagellar motor

The term motor refers to two functional entities: the rotor (rotating basal structure) and the stator (stationary ionconducting complexes) (Figure 22.1.1a) (Minamino et al., 2008). In E. coli, there is a maximum of 11–13 stator units located around the MS ring within the inner membrane. These units are inserted randomly into the membrane and are thought to drift until they dock with the rotor. Their location is not permament; they turn over, transiently engaging and disengaging with the rotor (Leake et al., 2006). Each stator unit is composed of two proteins – MotA and MotB – which together form a channel for proton transit. This channel is thought to be closed or plugged prior to stator engagement with the rotor (Hosking et al., 2006). The channel is opened, and the stator is stabilized at its location around the flagellum once the C-terminal domain of MotB unfolds and binds to the peptidoglycan layer through an unknown mechanism (Figure 22.1.1a) (Kojima et al., 2009; Roujeinikova, 2008). In response to proton flow through the MotAB channel, protonation of a specific Asp32 residue in MotB causes conformational changes in the cytoplasmic region of MotA that set up charge–charge interactions with residues in FliG. This protonation event causes FliG to move and generate torque for flagellar rotation (Figure 22.1.1a, expanded inset) (Kojima and Blair, 2001; Zhou et al., 1998). Torque is transmitted via the MS ring and rod to the external hook and filament. In marine Vibrios, homologous units sometimes called PomA–PomB conduct Na+ ions that drive rotation of the Pof (Li et al., 2011). 

In Salmonella and E. coli, a CCW rotor direction is the default state and causes the bacterium to swim forward. Bacteria tumble if the rotation is switched to a CW direction. The switching probability is regulated by a chemotaxis response regulator protein, phospho-CheY (CheY∼P). FliM and FliN in the C ring interact with CheY∼P to switch the rotational direction by changing the conformation of FliG, the protein in contact with MotA (Figure 22.1.1a) (Paul et al., 2011a). It is for this reason the C ring is also called the switch complex. Recent work in E. coli has shown that FliM (Delalez et al., 2010; Zarbiv et al., 2012) and FliN (Branch et al., 2014; Delalez et al., 2014) exchange dynamically in response to the rotational direction. Rotors moving in a CCW direction have higher numbers of FliM and FliN compared to a rotor moving in a CW direction, suggesting that motor remodeling is an important component in tuning responses to chemosensory signals at the motor. FliG conformation, which sets the directionality of motor rotation, also appears to be influenced by stator occupancy (Figure 22.1.1a) (Lele et al., 2013). Cryo-ET images in Borrelia burgdorferi show that the presence or absence of stators has opposing effects on FliG conformation. When stators are present, the C ring assumes a CCW FliG conformation, and when stators are absent, the C ring assumes a CW FliG configuration (Jun Liu, University of Texas– Houston, personal communication). Thus, stator engagement or disengagement modulates C ring conformation. 

A stator can attach to the motor only if it is in the open conformation and is able to generate torque (Tipping et al., 2013b). In E. coli, motors running at very low load (e.g., when only a short filament stub is present) are driven by one or at most two stator units (Lele et al., 2013). Following a load increase (experimentally achieved by adding a plastic or gold bead to the filament stub), motors gradually adapt (over a period of a few minutes) by recruiting additional stators and increasing their speeds. Similar experiments, performed by stalling motors using external magnetic fields (increasing torque), showed that stalled motors contained at least as many stators as rotating motors at high load. Therefore, motors do not have to rotate in order to engage stators; instead, torque production is the essential factor for motor engagement (Tipping et al., 2013a). Nonfunctional stators, mutated only in the critical Asp residue in MotB expected to prevent proton flux, do not bind stably to the rotor (Tipping et al., 2013a). 

## 22.1.3.1 Other players at the motor

A newly identified motor component that improves stator function is the inner membrane protein, FliL (Figure 22.1.1a). This protein is well conserved in flagellated bacteria, but its function is not fully understood. In E. coli and Salmonella, FliL plays a role in the structural stability of the rod (Attmannspacher et al., 2008), in improving stator function (Partridge and Harshey, 2013a; Partridge et al., 2015), and in maintaining torque when external load on the filament is high (Partridge et al., 2015). 

H–NS, a protein with dual roles in organizing the bacterial nucleoid structure as well as in regulating transcription in E. coli and Salmonella (Dorman, 2013; Fang and Rimsky, 2008), binds to FliG (Donato and Kawula, 1998; Marykwas et al., 1996) and enhances motor performance under conditions requiring a strengthened rotor–stator interface (Figure 22.1.1a) (Paul et al., 2011b). There are other proteins, such as YcgR (E. coli and Salmonella) (Boehm et al., 2010; Fang and Gomelsky, 2010; 

Paul et al., 2010) and DgrA–EpsE (B. subtilis) (Blair et al., 2008; Chen et al., 2012; Gao et al., 2013), that destabilize the rotor– stator interface, ultimately reducing or cutting off power to the rotor (Figure 22.1.1a). These proteins act to stop rotation in response to environmental signals. 

## 22.1.4 Flagellar motion as a sensing device

This section will first examine the evidence from several bacteria – Vibrio species, Proteus mirabilis, C. crescentus, and B. subtilis – where conditions implicated in slowing or arresting flagellar rotation lead to cellular responses that aid the particular bacterial lifestyle. The data for Vibrio parahaemolyticus, B. subtilis, and C. crescentus are the most coherent, so these will be discussed first, followed by discussion of P. mirabilis and Vibrio cholera data, which are still ambiguous. A possible common signaling pathway will be proposed for this group of bacteria. (A recent review includes Pseudomonas aeruginosa in this group (Belas, 2014), but the experiments are indirect and will not be discussed here.) A different set of observations in E. coli and Salmonella that implicate the flagellum in enabling wet surface conditions for swarming will be described in this section, and the data will be evaluated in the context of the sensory model proposed for the first group of bacteria. 

## 22.1.4.1 Vibrio parahaemolyticus

This marine bacterium is one of many bacterial species that use flagella to swarm over an agar surface (Harshey, 2003; Kearns, 2010). The surface presents many challenges to motion not encountered during swimming through liquid environments. These include frictional forces such as viscous drag (Partridge and Harshey, 2013b). V. parahaemolyticus, which swims using a single Na+-driven Pof, synthesizes hundreds of lateral flagella (Laf) during swarming, thus overriding viscous drag by increasing propulsive force (Figure 22.1.2) (McCarter, 2004). The bacteria also turn down cell division to produce long cells (speculated to increase their rate of translocation over the surface) and increase the synthesis of virulence factors and enzymes involved with signal transduction (Gode-Potratz et al., 2011). 

How does the bacterium know that it is on a surface and it is time to turn on the swarming program, which includes the Laf system? Several experiments conclude that Laf transcription is induced in response to conditions expected to slow Pof rotation and decrease ion flow through the stators. These include increasing viscosity (Belas et al., 1986, McCarter et al., 1988), or tethering flagella with antibodies directed against the outer membrane sheath surrounding the Pof (McCarter et al., 1988), or blocking sodium ion channels by addition of phenamil (Kawagishi et al., 1996). While phenamil is a drug that directly inhibits Na+ ion transport, thereby shutting down power to the motor, increased viscosity or tethering the flagellar filament with antibody will increase the load on the filament, thereby slowing down or stalling the motor. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/c5973abc45b5cb9023b7563bdfeb2a8f2437c6166fb7e3f8079cb28a9614d484.jpg)



Figure 22.1.2 The flagellum as a surface sensor. Surface contact initially restricts flagellar rotation (indicated by x), signaling different downstream events in the different bacteria that promote either motility or sessility, as described in the text.


Speed and proton flux are linearly related (Meister et al., 1987), so it is expected that at low speed, fewer ions are being transmitted through the stators. The high load on the motor, while decreasing speed, will deliver a high torque; stalled motors are under high torque (Tipping et al., 2013a). Thus, the sensing mechanism might respond to torque, rotor speed, or ion flow. Can we assess which of these parameters is likely to be a common outcome in the experimental conditions that lead to induction of Laf synthesis? 

## Torque

We can rule out that the sensing device measures torque for the following reasons. If we assume that the V. parahaemolyticus Pof responds to external load similarly to E. coli flagella, where application of a load on the filament recruits more stators (see Section 22.1.3, “The Flagellar Motor”) (Lele et al., 2013; Tipping et al., 2013a), then one expects stators to be fully engaged under the high-torque conditions of high viscosity or flagella tethering in V. parahaemolyticus. However, data from Vibrio alginolyticus, a swarming proficient marine bacterium closely related to V. parahaemolyticus (Ulitzer, 1975), would argue that stators are disengaged in the presence of phenamil. Here, addition of phenamil or of a proton ionophore that collapses the sodium motive force, as well as mutation of the critical Na+-binding Asp residue of PomB (equivalent to the Asp residue in MotB; Figure 22.1.1a), all interfere with stator assembly (Fukuoka et al., 2009). If stators are disengaged, there will be no torque. Thus, high torque is not a common component of these different experimental regimes that lead to the same output. 

## Speed and ion flow

These two parameters are intimately linked. We can surmise that some aspect of their status likely serves as a sensory mechanism, because increasing filament load or adding phenamil both lead to a decrease in ion flow. In the former case, the stalled motors have engaged stators that do not conduct ions. In the latter case, stators are disengaged and therefore nonconducting. Thus, the sensing system is likely monitoring some conformation of the stators responsible for ion flow rather than torque. In light of this assessment, let us examine the effect of genetic perturbations of the Pof. 

## Effect of flagellar gene disruptions on Laf transcription

Pof gene mutations that remove stators or C ring proteins induce Laf transcription in V. parahaemolyticus (Boles and McCarter, 2000; McCarter et al., 1988). Note that the C ring is required for T3S function and hence HBB completion (Figure 22.1.1b); therefore, stators will not be made in such mutants. These results support the idea that the ion-conducting state of stators determines the Laf response. However, Laf induction is also observed when C ring assembly is normal and load is light (absence of HAP protein (McCarter, 1995) or a major filament subunit protein (McCarter et al., 1988)). Motors are expected to run under these conditions, so these data cannot be easily reconciled with the conclusions derived from data for the first set of mutants. It is possible that perturbation of the filament in a sheathed Pof affects the stators in a manner we do not yet understand. 

## Summary

The majority of V. parahaemolyticus data are consistent with a stalled motor or absence of ion-conducting stators being the signal for Laf induction. 

## 22.1.4.2 Bacillus subtilis

The evidence for a sensory role of the flagellar motor in controlling biofilm formation is the most clear-cut in this soil bacterium with peritrichous flagella, where inhibition of flagellar rotation results in increased transcription and secretion of poly-γ-glutamate (PGA), a polymer that forms an external slime layer and promotes adhesion to surfaces (Figure 22.1.2) (Cairns et al., 2013; Chan et al., 2014; Mukherjee and Kearns, 2014). Some of the experimental methods to inhibit rotation were patterned after those in V. parahaemolyticus, in that antibodies were used to tether flagella, or genes for the stator proteins MotA–MotB were deleted. Others involved introducing a point mutation in the critical protonated Asp residue of MotB (Figure 22.1.1a), or expression of EpsE, a protein that binds to FliG and acts as a “clutch” to disengage the rotor and stator components (functionally similar to YcgR; Figure 22.1.1a) (Blair et al., 2008; Cairns et al., 2013). Deletion of FliF or FliG elicited the same PGA synthesis response, even when MotA/B were provided (Chan et al., 2014); stators will not engage without an assembled BB. Thus, genetic disruptions of flagellar components or physiological disruption of the rotor–stator interface show that PGA production requires stators to be absent or not to be in their protonconducting state. Transcription of the operon responsible for PGA synthesis is additionally dependent on the two-component system DegS–DegU, which is activated by the reported flagellar disruptions (Mukherjee and Kearns, 2014). 

## Summary

All the data in B. subtilis are consistent with an absence of ionconducting stators being the signal for PGA synthesis. 

## 22.1.4.3 Caulobacter crescentus

This freshwater bacterium has distinct motile and sedentary life cycles, enabled by a H+-driven Pof and an adhesive stalk, respectively (Figure 22.1.2) (Brown et al., 2009). The Pof enables swimming motility. The stalk is a thin cylindrical extension of the cell membrane with an adhesive tip (holdfast) for surface attachment. Stalk biogenesis initiates at the base of the Pof when rotation is restricted, which happens when contact of a motile cell with a surface is stabilized via attachment of Type IV pili to the surface. These pili are located adjacent to the Pof (depicted as short whiskers in Figure 22.1.2). When they attach and retract, they tether the bacterium to the surface and pin the flagellum there as well, rapidly arresting flagellar rotation. This action immediately stimulates secretion of polysaccharides constituting the holdfast (Figure 22.1.2). Holdfast synthesis is independent of new transcription or translation (Li et al., 2012). Viscous agents expected to slow flagellar rotation also stimulate holdfast production, although not all agents have this effect. There are currently no published data examining the effect of mutant stators on the holdfast synthesis response. Overall, the experiments replicate the effects of viscosity and filament-tethering antibodies observed in V. parahaemolyticus, in eliciting a cellular response specific to C. crescentus. 

While pili-dependent contact stimulates immediate attachment and holdfast secretion, holdfast formation is also developmentally programmed and will take place eventually even without contact. During this programmed phase, the MS ring is degraded and the flagellum is ejected (Brown et al., 2009). 

## Summary

While the data exploring the role of stators in the transition of C. crescentus from a motile to a sessile state are not as extensive as those in B. subtilis or V. parahaemolyticus, they do implicate a stalled flagellar motor in signaling holdfast secretion. 

## 22.1.4.4 Proteus mirabilis

Like V. parahaemolyticus, this bacterium also swarms on the surface of agar, accompanied by increased flagellar synthesis, increased expression of virulence genes, and increased cell elongation (Morgenstein et al., 2010; Rather, 2005). P. mirabilis is widely distributed in soil and water in the natural environment and is found as part of the normal flora of the gut. It is not polarly flagellated, but has several H+-driven peritrichous flagella whose synthesis is upregulated on the agar surface (Pearson et al., 2010). Examination of experimental results interpreted as supporting a role for the flagella in surface sensing is equivocal. For example, experiments modeled after V. parahaemolyticus were performed to monitor the effects of increasing viscosity or addition of antiflagellar antibody (Belas and Suvanasuthi, 2005). Cell elongation was used as a swarming marker. While 100% of the bacteria elongate in response to an agar surface (Morgenstein et al., 2010), only a small fraction of the cells elongated in the various conditions examined. Of the flagella mutants tested, those removing FlhD (the master regulator of flagella synthesis), FliM (C ring), FliF (MS ring), FlhA and FliPR (T3S apparatus), FlgB (rod), FlgKL (HAPs), and FlaA (filament) displayed no elongation either in liquid or on a surface. Of these nonmotile mutants, those that have either no flagella (FlhD mutant) or only partial structures, such as the MS-C rings, are not expected to synthesize stators, while those that completed the HBB (HAP and FlaA mutants) are expected to synthesize and engage the stators. Thus, the ion-conducting status of the stators does not correlate with cell elongation. In this same study, FliG (C ring) and FliL (stator-associated protein) mutants showed elongation in the absence of a surface cue (i.e., in liquid media) (Belas and Suvanasuthi, 2005; Cusick et al., 2012). The FliG mutant is expected to have only the MS ring and perhaps the T3S apparatus, and the FliM mutant is expected to have a comparable partial structure; yet the FliG mutant elongated in liquid, while the FliM mutant did not. The FliL mutant also exhibited a significant increase in the expression of a virulence gene in liquid media, normally seen only during swarming (Belas and Suvanasuthi, 2005); this gene is controlled by the flagellar master regulon, and therefore it is a proxy for upregulation of flagellar synthesis. The role of FliL in P. mirabilis is not clear, but the bacteria are expected to have a functional flagellum (Lee and Belas, 2014). Taken together, these experiments cannot be easily interpreted. It is possible that elongation is not a reliable measure of the swarming response because cells elongate in response to many other cues as well. Perhaps the experiments should be revisited by using a quantitative assay that measures FlhD induction, as was done with Laf in V. parahaemolyticus. 

Other experiments monitoring upregulation of the FlhDC master regulator in P. mirabilis implicate a two-component signaling system (see Section 3), as well as membrane and periplasmic proteins in sensing the surface cue to enable the swarming program (Morgenstein et al., 2010). It may well be that these membrane proteins communicate the presence of a surface niche to the flagellar BB by some internal pathway. However, evidence for any flagellar involvement in the surface-sensing pathway hinges only on data showing that absence of FliG (expected to have only the MS ring with no associated stators) and FliL (expected to have a functional flagellum in this bacterium) (Lee and Belas, 2014) produces elongated cells in liquid cultures (Belas and Suvanasuthi, 2005), data that are inconsistent with the phenotype of other P. mirabilis mutants, as discussed in this chapter. 

## Summary

Overall, a convincing case is not yet made for a role for the flagellum in sensing the surface in P. mirabilis. Conflicting data from the elongation of cells during swarming suggest that the experiments should be revisited using a more reliable assay. 

## 22.1.4.5 Vibrio cholera

Like V. parahaemolyticus, this marine bacterium has a $\mathrm { N a ^ { + } } .$ driven Pof, but unlike V. parahaemolyticus, it does not swarm nor does it have a Laf system. In this organism, the sodiumdriven motor is reported to be critical for transcription of vps genes important for biofilm formation. In some studies, complete absence of the flagellum or the presence of partial structures, such as MS–C rings and HBB, induces biofilms (Lauriano et al., 2004; Watnick et al., 2001). The stators are expected to be absent in a majority of these mutants. Yet, deletion of any one of the stator proteins (MotA, MotB, MotX, or MotY) or addition of phenamil inhibits transcription of vps genes and biofilm formation (Lauriano et al., 2004; Watnick et al., 2001). In another study, transcription of the toxin gene toxT increased maximally in response to viscosity, with lower transcription increases in MotY, FlaA, and FliG null mutants, and phenamil having only a slight effect (Hase and Mekalanos, 1999). 

## Summary

While the V. cholerae data clearly show that most genetic mutations that disrupt flagellar function comprise an inducing signal for biofilm formation, the data for stator involvement are still ambiguous. The experiments need to be systematically revisited, perhaps using a single gene reporter assay system. 

## 22.1.5 Model for the flagellar motor as a sensor

Critical examination of data from V. parahaemolyticus, B. subtilis, and C. crescentus shows that stalling motors by viscosity, antibodies, or Type IV pili, or introducing mutations that result in either loss of stators or loss of their engagement with the rotor, leads to induction of a specific cellular response. The common feature of these varied experimental regimes is to create a condition where the motor is stopped and ions are not flowing through the stators. These data can be explained by a model where this particular motor conformation either releases an otherwise bound regulator or promotes binding of an otherwise free regulator to activate the downstream response (Figure 22.1.3). 

What feature of the motor might declare its ion-conducting status to a regulator? Candidates that immediately come to mind are MotA in the stator and FliG in the rotor (Figure 22.1.1a). MotA has a substantial cytoplasmic domain whose conformation is modulated by the charged state of the Asp residue in MotB. In turn, MotA regulates FliG conformation. These conformations must be distinctive enough in a running versus a stalled motor to be discernable by regulators monitoring motor activity. If a binding conformation is available only in a running motor, then absence of this conformation in a stalled motor would dissociate the regulator (Figure 22.1.3a). This model is applicable to V. parahaemolyticus and B. subtilis, where motors stalled either by tethering or high viscosity or by phenamil or stator mutations, or even the complete absence of any flagellar parts, all elicit the same downstream response. Here, the active conformation of the motor must sequester and inactivate the regulator, while a stopped conformation must release it for activity. The observed graded response to viscosity or phenamil in V. parahaemolyticus (Kawagishi et al., 1996) could be explained by inactivation of only a subset of stators. 


Figure 22.1.3 Model for the flagellar motor as a sensor. Two scenarios are suggested for how a stalled motor can signal. The rotor is represented by a large black circle. Ion-conducting stators are red circles, and nonconducting ones are squares. A rotating motor is (+), and a stalled motor is (−). Inactive regulators are represented by blue rectangles, and active ones by red. (a) A running motor can bind and sequester/inactivate the regulator (blue) of a specific downstream response. When the motor stalls in a nonconducting state (−), its altered conformation can no longer bind the regulator, freeing it for activity (red). The (−) state may also be achieved in the absence of the rotor or stators (not shown). (b) A stalled motor can bind an otherwise inactive regulator (blue) to activate it (red). Here, the presence of the motor is required for signaling the downstream response. In both (a) and (b), the stalled motor on the right is generating an “activation” signal by opposite mechanisms (i.e., the functional regulator is either the freed or the bound form).


![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/addfd452158510cceff297ad6336c644d91f4936402b43196bde6e7e22370289.jpg)


The corollary to this model, where regulators are activated by a stalled motor (Figure 22.1.3b), would be applicable to cases where the response requires that the motor be present, and where (unlike in B. subtilis) absence of the flagellum, or perhaps even absence of stators, will not elicit the same downstream response as when motor rotation is arrested. It would be interesting to see if this model is applicable to C. crescentus, in which we do not yet know how absence of stators affects the holdfast synthesis response. 

## 22.1.5.1 Sensing through other parts of the basal body

The stators engage with FliG, whose conformation sets the CW– CCW directionality of motor rotation (see Section 22.1.3, “The Flagellar Motor”). The C ring proteins connected to FliG (FliM and FliN) must also alter their conformation during directional switching. However, these conformations per se are not likely to be sensed at least in B. subtilis, where mutants in the chemotaxis pathway that rotate their motors in predominantly CW or CCW conformation do not induce PGA synthesis. That is not to say that the C ring or proteins associated with it cannot be used as a sensor in other bacteria. For example, H–NS, a positive regulator of flhDC transcription (Soutourina et al., 1999), is present at the C ring, where it binds to FliG and strengthens rotor– stator (FliG–MotA) interactions (Figure 22.1.1a). Other proteins destabilize rotor–stator interactions, such as YcgR in E. coli and Salmonella and DgrA–EpsE in B. subtilis. These proteins could potentially affect regulators monitoring the interface from either the periplasmic or the cytoplasmic side. The C ring also interacts with cytoplasmic members of the T3S system, and could in turn influence flagella regulation via this system (Figure 22.1.1a). For example, the secretion chaperone FliT that promotes flagellin subunit secretion additionally influences FlhDC to control flagella numbers (Aldridge et al., 2010). C ring conformation might also be monitored by energy complexes such as ATP synthase and NADH–ubiquinone oxidoreductase, which were deduced to be in close proximity to this ring (Zarbiv et al., 2012). In response to a stalled motor, such complexes could energize holdfast synthesis in C. crescentus or osmolyte secretion in E. coli and Salmonella, as discussed in this chapter. 

## 22.1.5.2 Other considerations

Flagella numbers increase during swarming in both V. parahaemolyticus (McCarter et al., 1988) and B. subtilis (Mukherjee and Kearns, 2014). If the swarm surface restricts Pof rotation in V. parahaemolyticus, one would expect it to similarly restrict flagella rotation in B. subtilis. Why does this not lead to PGA synthesis on swarm agar for B. subtilis? It is possible that B. subtilis has other pathways to prevent PGA accumulation when conditions are favorable for motility. Motor rotation is likely under greater scrutiny during environmental conditions that are conducive to biofilm formation. Such conditions are known to express the clutch protein EpsE, which disengages the rotor from the stator, to inhibit σD (equivalent to σ28 in Salmonella) (Figure 22.1.1b), which stops stator synthesis; or to activate the guanyl cyclase DgrA, which appears to inhibit motility by interacting with MotA (Mukherjee and Kearns, 2014). 

## 22.1.6 Model for sensory role for the flagellum: a variation

## 22.1.6.1 E. coli and Salmonella



Belas R, Simon M, Silverman M. 1986. Regulation of lateral flagella gene transcription in Vibrio parahaemolyticus. Journal of Bacteriolog y 167(1): 210–218. 





Marykwas DL, Schmidt SA, Berg HC. 1996. Interacting components of the flagellar motor of Escherichia coli revealed by the two-hybrid system in yeast. Journal of Molecular Biology 256(3): 564–576. 



These enteric bacteria swarm but do not induce a swarmingspecific transcriptional program (Wang et al., 2004), and they do not hyperflagellate (Partridge and Harshey, 2013a). Instead, posttrancriptional pathways ensure that there is adequate water on the surface during swarming, so that the bacteria are immersed in fluid and flagella can rotate and generate thrust (Figure 22.1.2). This conclusion was arrived at from the following observations. Mutants impaired in chemotaxis (Che) do not swarm even though chemotaxis is not required for swarming (Burkart et al., 1998). The Che mutants are locked in their rotational direction, either CW or CCW. That the ability of the flagellar motor to switch directions is somehow important for initiating swarming was deduced from the behavior of suppressors, which mapped to the switch protein FliM; these mutants had restored switching but not chemotaxis (Mariconda et al., 2006). The Che mutants were observed to be defective in hydrating their surface-grown colonies, in exporting FlgM under these conditions, and in assembling normal-length filaments (Wang et al., 2005). DNA microarray studies revealed that late flagellar genes were downregulated in these mutants only when propagated on the surface. Artificial hydration of the mutant lawns rescued FlgM export, flagella lengths, and swarming (Wang et al., 2005). The primary defect in these mutants appears to be in their ability to hydrate the surface; the filament assembly defect is likely a secondary consequence of misfolding of flagellin subunits at the growing end of the filament in the absence of hydration, blocking the export of FlgM. 



Belas R, Suvanasuthi R. 2005. The ability of Proteus mirabilis to sense surfaces and regulate virulence gene expression involves FliL, a flagellar basal body protein. Journal of Bacteriology 187(19): 6789– 6803. 





McCarter L, Hilmen M, Silverman M. 1988. Flagellar dynamometer controls swarmer cell differentiation of V. parahaemolyticus. Cell 54(3): 345–351. 



Why are Che mutants unable to hydrate their swarm colonies? It has been speculated that flagellar filaments normally stick to swarm agar and thus get tethered or restricted. Motor switching unsticks or liberates them (Wang et al., 2005). Once free, rotating filaments aid external hydration either by creating turbulence on the surface that draws up the water by capillary action from the agar beneath, or by knocking LPS off the bacterial outer membrane, which in turn might act as an osmotic agent. MotA/B mutants that cannot rotate their filaments also have a dry colony morphology (Partridge and Harshey, 2013a). Support for the LPS-release hypothesis was provided in a study of osmolarity in different regions of an advancing E. coli swarm utilizing fluorescent liposomes as reporters (Ping et al., 2014). Two distinct regions were observed inside the swarm near its leading edge: an outer high-osmolarity band (∼30 mOsm higher than the agar baseline) and an inner low-osmolarity band (∼isotonic to the agar baseline). This study concluded that high osmotic pressure at the leading edge of the swarm extracts water from the underlying agar and promotes motility, and that the osmolyte is of high molecular weight and is probably LPS (Ping et al., 2014). In B. subtilis, where there is no outer membrane or LPS, a non-flagellate mutant completely lacks hydration, as measured by diffusion of MgO beads on the colony surface (Be’er and

McCarter LL. 1995. Genetic and molecular characterization of the polar flagellum of Vibrio parahaemolyticus. Journal of Bacteriology 177(6): 1595–1609. 



Harshey, 2011). Thus, intact rotating flagellar filaments appear to be important for colony hydration, whatever the mechanism.

## 22.1.6.2 Model



Boehm A, Kaiser M, Li H, Spangler C, Kasper CA, et al. 2010. Second messenger-mediated adjustment of bacterial swimming velocity. Cell 141(1): 107–116. 





Meister M, Lowe G, Berg HC. 1987. The proton flux through the bacterial flagellar motor. Cell 49(5): 643–650. 



Borrowing elements from the model proposed in Figure 22.1.3, the following scenario can be envisaged to explain how E. coli and Salmonella hydrate their swarm colonies. Like in the other bacteria, the surface initially restricts flagellar rotation, sending a signal through the stalled motor for activating osmolyte secretion (Figure 22.1.2). With the arrival of water, it follows that the “dry” signal will dissipate and the flagella will now be free to rotate. The ability to switch rotor directions is important at this stage to unstick from the surface. Continued hydration may be promoted by the rotating flagella bundles either by generating LPS or by some other mechanism. In this model, flagella play two active roles: They initially restrict motor rotation to generate the signal that primes hydration, and they subsequently allow rotation to facilitate release of more water from the agar.

This review analyzes the data implicating the flagellum as a sensor in various bacterial species, and attempts to synthesize them by suggesting a unifying model where the ion-conducting conformation of the motor plays a key role in regulating the downstream response. The main objectives in writing this chapter are to invite experimental tests that challenge the proposed model, and thus to help solve this long-standing problem.
