# Biofilm formation and environmental signals in Bordetella

Tomoko Hanawa 

Department of Infectious Diseases, Kyorin University School of Medicine, Shinkawa, Mitaka, Tokyo, Japan 

## 22.4.1 Introduction

Pertussis, also known as whooping cough, is an infectious disease caused by Bordetella pertussis, a Gram-negative aerobic coccobacillus. The incidence of pertussis has been dramatically reduced, due to successful immunization programs in many countries for over 40 years. However, more recent outbreaks of this highly contagious respiratory disease have been occurring in the United States and other countries (Hartzell and Blaylock, 2014; Pillsbury et al., 2014; Sealey et al., 2014), indicating a reemergence of B. pertussis infection in modern society. Unvaccinated children and infants who have not yet received the full course of vaccination are more vulnerable to this infectious disease (Guiso, 2013). In the case of adult infection, symptoms are generally less severe and may only manifest as a prolonged cough, implying that disease prevalence may be more than documented. The strategy that B. pertussis employs in host colonization is therefore an important topic of interest. 

The pathogenicity of B. pertussis relies on the elaboration of various virulence factors, including adhesins and toxins such as filamentous hemagglutinin (FHA), pertussis toxin (PT), and adenylate cyclase toxin (ACT) (Figure 22.4.1). Since B. pertussis is not invasive in the host but exclusively persists on the surface of the respiratory tract, its capability for colonization determines the difference between bacterial life and death. Sensing the host environment and responding to it, bacteria produce virulence factors to facilitate colonization and growth, to establish their niche. Adhesins enabling bacterial attachment to the eukaryotic cell surface often secondarily contribute to form biofilms (Absalon et al., 2011; El-Kirat-Chatel et al., 2014; Oelschlaeger et al., 2002). This therefore suggests that B. pertussis may be able to form robust biofilms. In fact, several reports have previously hypothesized the importance of in vivo biofilm formation for B. pertussis pathogenesis, as described in this chapter. 

The genus Bordetellae is classified in the betaproteobacteria, and nine species are currently identified in this genus. In addition to B. pertussis, Bordetella parapertussis also causes pertussis diseases in humans. B. pertussis is an obligate human pathogen and is not detected in other environments. Bordetella bronchiseptica has been identified in many veterinary syndromes and infects a variety of mammals. Unlike B. pertussis, B. bronchiseptica is thought to survive in the environment and rarely infects humans (Porter et al., 1991). In spite of the differences in their host ranges, B. pertussis, B. parapertussis, and B. bronchiseptica are closely genetically related bacteria that share many similarly regulated virulence factors (Parkhill et al., 2003). 

Formation of in vitro biofilms by B. bronchiseptica, B. perutussis, and B. parapertussis has been reported (Irie et al., 2004; Mishra et al., 2005). in vivo, B. pertussis and B. bronchiseptica biofilm formation has previously been observed using a mouse model system (Conover et al., 2011; Sloan et al., 2007). Furthermore, antibodies against biofilm components have been found in pertussis patient sera (Conover et al., 2010). In summary, these findings strongly indicate that these bacteria exploit biofilms to gain foothold in their host organism. 

This chapter will examine the components of Bordetella biofilms, as well as their formation and architecture. The regulation and molecular signals influencing biofilm formation, including the role of the (p)ppGpp nucleotide signaling molecule (see Chapter 7.1), are also discussed in the context of Bordetella biofilm development and maintenance. 

## 22.4.2 The BvgAS two-component system – the central system regulating Bordetella pathogenicity

Bacterial two-component signal transduction systems are important for sensing and responding to environmental signals (Capra and Laub, 2012). These systems are typically composed of a conserved sensor histidine kinase and response regulator. Upon stimulation, a signaling cascade is initiated by the phosphorylation of the histidine residue in the sensor protein by autophosphorylation, in a reaction using adenosine triphosphate (ATP). Transfer of this phosphate group to an aspartate residue in the regulator protein activates it so that it can bind to specific DNA sequences to promote transcription (see Section 3). 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/c7a20eeb54633d9e5f426f1cddfee835c71ab13ef6d77564c9054cfd300096df.jpg)



Figure 22.4.1 Bordetella virulence factors. Representative Bordetella virulence factors are illustrated. ACT, adenylate cyclase toxin; Bps, Bordetella polysaccharide; FHA, filamentous hemagglutinin; DNT, dermonecrotic toxin; PRN, pertactin; PT, pertussis toxin; TCT, tracheal cytotoxin; TCF, tracheal colonization factor; TTSS, type III secretion system.


The phosphate transfer pathway in the activation of BvgAS is more complex as compared to other two-component systems. In the BvgS sensor kinase, there is one aspartate and two histidine residues that require phosphorylation (Uhl and Miller, 1994). Therefore, the BvgA regulator requires two intramolecular phosphotransfer steps in BvgS to become fully phosphorylated (Figure 22.4.2). Phosphorylated BvgA (BvgA-P) forms dimers that activate the transcription of virulence-activated genes (vag loci), and it inactivates virulence-repressed genes (vrg loci). Since BvgAS governs expression of virulence genes, the activity level of this system is closely related to Bordetella virulence phenotypes. 

The level of BvgAS activation is divided into three phases (Figure 22.4.3). Bacteria in the Bvg+ phase have full activation of BvgAS, transcribe most of vag loci (class 1 and class 2), and do not express vrg loci. In the Bvg− phase, BvgAS are not phosphorylated, and transcription of vag genes is repressed and vrg genes are expressed (class 4 genes). An intermediate phase termed Bvgi (between the Bvg+ and Bvg− phases) shows partially activated BvgAS and transcription of only adhesin genes (class 2 genes) 

and class 3 genes such as bipA but with no transcription of other vag genes. The prominent feature of Bvgi -phase bacteria is the maximal expression of bipA, the function of which remains unknown. 

Although a significant number of environmental factors have been investigated, in vivo activation factor(s) have not been defined, since BvgAS is always activated in the experimental in vivo conditions (Cotter and Miller, 1994; Martinez de Tejada et al., 1998). Sulfate and nicotinic acid are in vitro modulators of the Bvg phase, with culture supplementation of mM concentrations of MgSO or nicotinic acid leading to a Bvg− phase phenotype. Cultures at low temperature (e.g., 25 ◦C) also modulate the Bvg phase, with inactivated BvgAS expressing no vag genes. Intermediate concentrations of MgSO and nicotinic acid elicit the Bvgi phase. 

Bordetella phase locked “on” or “off ” strains in which BvgAS activity is not altered by modulators have been characterized (Cotter and Miller, 1997; Martinez de Tejada et al., 1998). The key role of BvgS in signal transduction is highlighted by the fact that all Bvg− phase-locked mutants are generated by a mutation in bvgS. Amino acid substitutions in the linker region between the transmembrane domain and primary histidine kinase domain exert constitutive activation of BvgAS (Bvg+ phase-locked mutant). The bvgS-l1 allele produced by amino acid substitution near the primary phosphorylation site in bvgS generates a Bvgi phase-locked mutant (Sukumar et al., 2007; Vergara-Irigaray et al., 2005). In-frame deletion of bvgS sequences corresponding to the linker and transmitter domains gives a Bvg− phase-locked mutant phenotype. The use of in vitro modulating conditions and Bvg− phase-locked mutants has enabled assessment of how the Bvg phase affects biofilm formation. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/27a07e938395eac3b57c6cb1491e5047ce47cbb686fe830dfb76093500cd44f4.jpg)



Figure 22.4.2 Activation of BvgAS two-component signal transduction by phosphorelay. BvgS dimer receives environmental signals at the sensor domain (Sensor) in the periplasm. Phosphorylated residues are indicated with H (histidine) and D (aspartate). OM, outer membrane; IM, inner membrane; P, phosphate residue.


Maximal Bordetella biofilm formation is exhibited by cells in the Bvgi phase, with minimum formation in the Bvg− phase (Irie et al., 2004). This is likely to reflect the expression pattern of adhesion genes. However, more recent research has pointed out the complexity of regulation of biofilm formation by BvgAS. Bvg− phase gene expression and its relationship to early phase biofilm formation have been reported (Nicholson et al., 2012). Since Bvg+ bacteria are less tolerant to stress compared to Bvg− phase bacteria, the Bvg− phase is believed to be involved in survival outside the host for B. bronchiseptica, and the Bvg+ phase is specific to in vivo growth (McMillan et al., 1996). Further investigation may be required to define the interaction more accurately. Intriguingly, efficient biofilm formation does not occur with full BvgAS activation (Irie et al., 2004). Adenylate cyclase toxin is an essential virulence factor, and its production is limited to the Bvg+ phase in which biofilm formation is limited. In fact, it has been reported that this toxin causes inhibition of biofilm formation by interaction with FHA (Irie et al., 2004). 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/c8786643d16e60db86c8c3d713ba3671a2e1baac241416ec5c5b3c933d4d34ce.jpg)



Figure 22.4.3 Bordetella Bvg-phases and regulated genes. vag, virulence-activated gene; vrg, virulence repressed gene; vig, virulence genes that are expressed in the Bvgi phase.


## 22.4.3 (p)ppGpp

(p)ppGpp is a collective name for ppGpp (guanosine 3′ - diphosphate 5′ -diphosphate) and pppGpp (guanosine 3′ - diphosphate 5′ -triphosphate), and they are signaling molecules for the stringent response to nutritional starvation produced in various bacteria and plants (see Section 7). Cellular concentrations of (p)ppGpp are controlled by synthetase(s) and hydrolases in many bacteria. RelA is a synthetase that is activated by amino acid starvation. SpoT is a bifunctional enzyme that has both synthetic and degradation activities (Mittenhuber, 2001). Most bacteria utilize (p)ppGpp; however, Chlamydia and some facultative intracellular bacteria are predicted to lack the stringent response as they do not have relA or spoT homologous genes. This is likely because they are highly adapted to the nutrient-rich intracellular environment (Mittenhuber, 2001). 

Induction of (p)ppGpp, which is also known as alarmone, occurs in response to various stimuli such as carbon, nitrogen, phosphate, or fatty acids starvation and iron limitation (Vinella et al., 2005). (p)ppGpp binds to the RNA polymerase beta subunit and alters expression of specific genes at the transcriptional level (Potrykus and Cashel, 2008; Sato et al., 2014) (see Section 7). 

## 22.4.4 Biofilm component correlation with pathogenicity

## 22.4.4.1 Filamentous hemagglutinin (FHA)

FHA is a major adhesin that binds both epithelial cells and phagocytic cells. Antibodies against this protein effectively prevent B. pertussis infection, and therefore FHA is an important component of acellular vaccines (Sato and Sato, 1985). FHA not only functions as an adhesin but also functions as an immunomodulating factor (Asgarian-Omran et al., 2016; Inatsuka et al., 2005; Romero et al., 2014). 

FHA is synthesized abundantly in Bordetella, and a 367-kDa precursor protein is encoded by the fhaB gene. The active 232- kDa FHA protein is generated by cleavage of a signal peptide, allowing passage through the cytoplasmic membrane and processing by the SphB1 autotransporter protease in the periplasm (Coutte et al., 2003). After the mature FHA protein passes through the outer membrane via a two-partner secretion system, it forms a filamentous structure on the bacterial cell surface (Figure 22.4.1). Interestingly, FHA associates with the surface of the bacterium without covalent bonding and can be released into the culture supernatant, especially in the stationary growth phase (Watanabe et al., 1989). Studies using fhaB-deficient mutants have shown that FHA also promotes bacterial autoaggregation and biofilm formation in addition to host cell attachment (Serra et al., 2011). 

Localization of FHA onto the bacterial cell surface facilitates autoaggregation and adhesion to cells. Without processing by SphB1, a significant amount of FHA stays on the bacterial surface and the bacteria form tightly bound microcolonies. The SphB1-deficient mutant strain also colonizes less efficiently than the wild-type strain. This may be due to lack of the released form of FHA, which is known to have modulating effects on the immune system, such as upregulation of interleukin-10 (IL10) (Dirix et al., 2014; McGuirk and Mills, 2000). 

## 22.4.4.2 Flagella

In many bacteria, flagella play a role in generating biofilms as well as in motility (see Chapters 22.1 and 22.2). Genes relating to flagella biosynthesis are inactivated in B. pertussis, and the bacterium is nonmotile, with no reports demonstrating flagella production or motile phenotypes. B. bronchiseptica forms peritrichous flagella. It has been reported that flagella are required for biofilm formation since they are needed for attachment onto abiotic surfaces (Nicholson et al., 2012). After the initial attachment, the bacteria decrease flaA gene expression, as flagella are then no longer needed and have a deleterious effect on biofilm maturation (Nicholson et al., 2012). 

## 22.4.4.3 Fimbriae

Bordetella fimbriae, also known as pili, are long-filamentous surface structures formed by polymerization of pilin monomers (Figure 22.4.1). Fim2 or Fim3 monomers are encoded by the fim2 and fim3 genes, respectively, and these loci are separated in B. pertussis (Cuzzoni et al., 1990). FimD is also a pilin positioned at the tip of the fimbriae to confer adhesive properties. It is thought that fimbriae are assembled by the FimB chaperone and FimC usher system. Since fimbriae are important adhesins, some vaccine products contain fimbrial proteins as active components. Although there is no direct evidence for contribution of fimbriae in biofilm formation in Bordetella so far, fimbriae promote biofilm formation in many other bacteria (Schroll et al., 2010). 

## 22.4.4.4 Bps

The Bordetella Bps polysaccharide, which is antigenically and biochemically similar to the poly-β-1,6-N-acetylglucosamine polysaccharide, is important for biofilm formation both in vitro and in vivo in animal mouse models (Conover et al., 2010; 

Parise et al., 2007). The genes of bpsABCD are predicted to involve the production of Bps according to the similarities of polysaccharide-producing genes in other bacteria. Conover and his colleagues demonstrated that the bpsABCD operon is expressed independently of BvgAS, and transcription of this operon is repressed by BpsR (Conover et al., 2012). However, expression of bpsR in biofilm-forming bacteria is the same as that in planktonic bacteria. This finding suggests the existence of unknown molecules that may function as co-regulators of BpsR (Conover et al., 2012). 

## 22.4.4.5 eDNA

DNA has adherent properties, and extracellular DNA (eDNA) contained in bacterial biofilm is secreted by cell lysis dependent or independent mechanism. The importance of eDNA as a biofilm component in B. bronchiseptica and B. pertussis was found by digestion of the biofilms on abiotic surfaces with DNase I, resulting in decreased biofilm formation. The thickness of Bordetella biofilms formed on the surface of the respiratory tract of mice was also thinned down by treatment with DNase I (Conover et al., 2011). Autolysin is a murein hydrolase that causes autolysis by digestion of the murein. B. pertussis has two genes encoding murein hydrolase activity (BP0959 and BP3214), which may imply the mechanism for generation of eDNA in Bordetella biofilms. 

## 22.4.5 Experimental procedures

## 22.4.5.1 Quantification of biofilm formation in Bordetella pertussis

B. pertussis is a relatively slow-growing and fastidious bacterium. It requires 2–4 days incubation at 35 or $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ on Bordet– Gengou (BG) agar supplemented with 10–15% defibrinated blood to form colonies. After suspending bacterial cells using colonies on BG agar, B. pertussis strains were grown overnight. 

Bordetella is predicted to lack a complete glycolytic cycle according to the data of previous studies and in silico analysis of genomic nucleotide sequences (Armstrong and Gross, 2007). This may then explain the inability of Bordetella to utilize sugars including glucose for carbohydrate metabolism. Liquid culture in synthetic medium has also been reported in which Stainer–Scholte (SS) medium containing abundant glutamate as its primary carbon source is used (Stainer and Scholte, 1970). SS medium has been utilized in biofilm experiments for both B. pertussis and B. bronchiseptica in many laboratories. 

B. pertussis strains were grown for 2 days at $3 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ on BG agar supplemented with 15% defibrinated sheep blood. Growth from BG agar was used to inoculate SS medium. The B. pertussis suspensions were diluted and adjusted to an $\mathrm { O D } _ { 6 0 0 }$ turbidity value of 0.1. One milliliter of bacterial suspension was dispensed into each well of a 12-well plate into which sterile cover slips were placed. After culture at $3 5 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 24 h, the cover slips were washed with sterile water to remove planktonic bacteria, and the remaining biofilm-forming bacteria were fixed by drying. The biofilm material was stained with crystal violet, and excess dye was removed by washing with water. Dye incorporated into the biofilm was extracted with 1 mL of ethanol (95%) containing 10% acetic acid, and the $\mathrm { O D } _ { 5 5 0 }$ values of the extracts were measured. Biofilm morphology was assessed by scanning electron microscopic analysis after fixing with 2.5% glutaraldehyde. 

## 22.4.5.2 Assessment of (p)ppGpp production

After bacterial strains were cultured in SS medium until the exponential growth phase, medium was replaced with SS medium with a low-phosphate concentration (0.4 mM). After incubation at $3 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 4 h to label with 3.7 MBq $\mathrm { m L ^ { - 1 } }$ of $[ ^ { 3 2 } \mathrm { P } ]$ monopotassium phosphate (MP Biomedicals), intracellular (p)ppGpp was extracted by freeze–thawing cycles in 13 M formic acid (Vinella et al., 2005). After removing cell debris by centrifugation, extracts were analyzed by PEI cellulose-F (Merck KGaA) thin-layer chromatography developed using 1.5 M $\mathrm { K H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { P O } _ { 4 }$ (pH 3.4), and (p)ppGpp was visualized by autography on Hyperfilm ECL (GE Health Science). 

## 22.4.6 Results and discussion

We deleted both relA and spoT in B. pertussis to construct a (p)ppGpp-deficient phenotype (Sugisaki et al., 2013). The absence of (p)ppGpp in the resulting mutant strain, PMK21, was confirmed after growth in the presence of serine hydroxamate, which is a stringent response inducer. (p)ppGpp is important for response to nutritional starvation, and for resistance to stresses (see Section 7). Strain PMK21 showed high sensitivity to methyl viologen-mediated oxidative stress, a low survival ratio under nutritional-deficient conditions (Sugisaki et al., 2013), and diminished growth at low $( 2 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } )$ and high temperatures (39 and $4 2 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } )$ (data not shown). Furthermore, lack of (p)ppGpp production led to a decreased autoaggregation ratio and deficiency of biofilm formation on abiotic surfaces. Biofilm formation is often coordinate with autoaggregation. Accordingly, PMK21 was significantly impaired in autoaggregation and lost the capability to form biofilms (Sugisaki et al., 2013). 

In morphological analyses, long filamentous structures were found in B. pertussis biofilm (Figure 22.4.4). B. pertussis produces several kinds of filamentous structures on its surface (Figure 22.4.1). Direct identification of these filamentous structures is in progress, but our data suggest that defective biofilm formation in the (p)ppGpp-deficient mutant is due to lack of these filamentous structures. 

FHA is a major filamentous structure but since it forms short, straight filaments, the structures observed on the Bordetella cell surface (Figure 22.4.4) are not thought to be FHA. Indeed, transcript analyses of mutant PMK21 indicated that expression of the fhaB gene encoding FHA was comparable to wild-type strain. On the other hand, the expression of the bsp22 gene, which encodes a secreted protein of the type III secretion system (TTSS), was significantly decreased, and fimbriae expression was absent (Sugisaki et al., 2013). 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/8bcc617c65bbe67a820043e1ac7147e853475bda62de665bc222c2933f4e77cf.jpg)



Figure 22.4.4 Morphology of biofilm-forming B. pertussis cells. B. pertussis biofilms were observed by scanning electron microscopy at 15,000× magnification.


TTSS is a bacterial secretion system adopted by many Gramnegative bacteria. This machinery is also called the injectisome– injectosome, as this self-assembled needle-like structure is able to deliver effector proteins from bacterial cells to the cytosol of eukaryotic host cells across the cell membrane. B. bronchiseptica has been shown to express TTSS genes and proteins, and the system plays an important role in host interaction (Kuwae et al., 2003, 2006; Siciliano et al., 2006). Although the loci exist in B. pertussis and low levels of transcripts can be detected, despite production of messenger RNA (mRNA) transcripts, TTSS gene products had not been detected. Later, it was demonstrated that TTSS is expressed in B. pertussis using clinical isolates (Fennelly et al., 2008) and laboratory-adapted strains isolated from experimentally infected experimental animals (Gaillard et al., 2011). Furthermore, Brickman and his colleagues showed TTSS gene expression and protein production in both fresh clinical B. pertussis isolates as well as laboratory-adapted strains (Brickman et al., 2011). It is now accepted as common knowledge that B. pertussis expresses TTSS proteins like B. bronchseptica. Intriguingly, the expression of TTSS is increased by iron starvation in Bordetella (Brickman et al., 2011). Although Bordetella Bsp22 is a self-polymerizing protein from the TTSS tip complex (Medhekar et al., 2009), the role of TTSS in biofilm formation has not been investigated. 

TTSS and fimbrie genes belong to class 1 gene and class 2 genes, respectively (see Figure 22.4.3). To elucidate the effect of (p)ppGpp on the expressions of other Bvg-regulated genes in mutant PMK21, ptx and cyaA (class 1) transcript levels and protein production, and fhaB (class 2), were measured. The results showed that the expression of these genes was not affected by (p)ppGpp production, and regulation fim3 and TTSS genes by (p)ppGpp occurred independently of BvgAS (Sugisaki et al., 2013). 

Bis-(3′ -5′ )-cyclic guanosine monophosphate (c-di-GMP) is a signaling nucleotide molecule of bacteria that affects a variety of cellular systems (Sondermann et al., 2012). And it was demonstrated to be a biofilm regulating factor that was also not associated with the Bvg two-component system in Bordetella (Sisti et al., 2013). It is known that c-di-GMP has a central role in switching from a motile lifestyle to a sessile biofilm-forming stage in many bacteria by inducing expression of adhesins and flagella biosynthesis (e.g., Salmonella) (Simm et al., 2004). Multiple enzymes relate to the synthesis and degradation of c-di-GMP. Proteins containing the GGDEF motif have enzymatic diguanylate cyclase activity, and generate c-di-GMP from two GMP molecules. Proteins containing the EAL or HD–GYP motif can hydrolyze c-di-GMP to pGpG by their phosphodiesterase activity (Romling et al., 2005; Tal et al., 1998). 

B. pertussis BpeGReg (BP3507) has been postulated as a gene that encodes a globin-coupled sensor due to identification of both a heme-binding signal domain and GGDEF motifs in its sequence by in silico analysis (Wan et al., 2009). Deletion mutation of BP3507 decreased biofilm formation in B. bronchiseptica, and overexpression of BP3507 in Salmonella increased biofilm formation (Wan et al., 2009). In addition to Wan’s report, increased biofilm formation has been revealed by overexpression of a heterogeneous c-di-GMP synthesis enzyme in B. bronchiseptica, and BB3576 has been shown to encode a protein with a GGDEF domain (Sisti et al., 2013). Expression of BB3576 participates in motility and EPS production. Bvg− phase Bordetella cells form weaker biofilms than cells of other phases. However, similar levels of c-di-GMP are detected in bacteria of all phases. Furthermore, overexpression of BB3576 in Bvg− phase-locked mutants results in strong biofilm formation (Sisti et al., 2013). We can therefore conclude that c-di-GMP regulates biofilm formation independently from the BvgAS system. c-di-GMP is a nucleotide signaling molecules similar to (p)ppGpp. Both molecules regulate biofilm formation in an independent manner from BvgAS and may work synergistically. 

## 22.4.7 Closing remarks

Bacteria face continuous adaptation in order to survive in complex host environments, but pathogenic bacteria can also utilize these signals to express pathogenicity. in vivo biofilm formation is controlled in response to changes in the microenvironment. 

Various types of virulence factors are involved in bacterial colonization of the host. Comprehension of the factors allowing adherence and persistence of B. pertussis in the human respiratory tract is an important issue. Extracellular polysaccharide and eDNA are utilized for attachment of bacteria on the surface and development of biofilms. Results from our work also support a role of filamentous structures in biofilm development, by promoting cell–cell aggregation in vitro. 

Bordetella biofilm is controlled by various environmental factors sensed in the organism’s microenvironment. After initial attachment under BvgAS activation in vivo, bacteria initiate colonization. Therefore, activation of BvgAS could be necessary to form biofilm. (p)ppGpp and c-di-GMP positively regulate biofilm formation in Bordetella. And both are not dependent on the activity of BvgAS. 

Despite a high vaccination ratio, B. pertussis has reemerged. Future investigations about Bordetella biofilms and its regulations will lead to the development of novel vaccines to prevent colonization by Bordetella species.

# Biofilm formation by rhizobacteria in response to water-limiting conditions

Pablo Bogino, Fiorela Nievas, and Walter Giordano 

Departamento de Biolog´ıa Molecular, Universidad Nacional de R´ıo Cuarto, R´ıo Cuarto, Cordoba, Argentina ´ 

## 22.5.1 Introduction

Biofilms are bacterial communities in which cells are embedded in a matrix of extracellular polymeric compounds attached to a surface. Biofilm formation on plant surfaces is associated with symbiotic and pathogenic responses, but how plants regulate such associations is unclear (Bogino et al., 2013b). 

Many rhizobia (nitrogen-fixing soil bacteria that form nodules on plant roots) have been reported to form microcolonies or biofilms when they colonize legume roots. The major components of these biofilms are water and bacterial cells, but the three-dimensional structure of the biofilm is based on an extracellular matrix formed mainly by exopolysaccharides (EPSs). In most rhizobial species studied to date, bacterial surface components (particularly EPSs) in combination with bacterial quorum-sensing signals are crucial for biofilm formation (Rinaudi and Giordano, 2010). Polysaccharides other than EPSs (e.g., glucomannan and cellulose produced by rhizobia) are involved in attachment of bacteria to host root surfaces and consequently in biofilm formation (Janczarek et al., 2014). 

Biofilm formation appears to enhance the ability of soil bacteria to colonize their surrounding habitat and survive common environmental stresses such as desiccation (see Section 12) and nutrient limitation (Bogino et al., 2013a). In the wellstudied species Sinorhizobium meliloti, biofilm formation has been reported to be affected and/or regulated by nutritional and environmental conditions (Rinaudi et al., 2006), EPSs and flagella (Fujishige et al., 2008), ExoR with an ExoS–ChvI twocomponent system (Wells et al., 2007), Nod factors synthesized by nod genes (Fujishige et al., 2008), and regulation of EPS biosynthesis through quorum-sensing systems (see Section 21). Biofilm formation by rhizobia is necessary for overcoming environmental stresses, and it is clearly an important feature of symbiotic ability in certain species (Rinaudi and Giordano, 2010). 

The rhizosphere is the soil niche occupied and affected by plant roots. It is a dynamic and complex microenvironment in which a wide variety of interactions occur between bacteria and plants. Rhizosphere colonization involves migration of bacteria from bulk soil to rhizospheric soil closely associated with plant roots. To be successful in this microenvironment, bacteria must have the ability to establish themselves as microcolonies (Compant et al., 2010). Biofilm development plays an essential role in bacterial survival and physiology, and these bacterial communities must establish themselves as a multispecies biofilm at the rhizospheric level (Burmølle et al., 2007). Biofilms are the primary structures in which bacteria perform their functions in nutrient cycling, interactions (either beneficial or deleterious) with plants and other eukaryotes, reduction of biotic or abiotic plant stress factors, and enhancement of agricultural productivity. Rhizobacterial communities are abundant, diverse, and sensitive to fluctuations in environmental factors such as water availability and temperature because they depend on organic materials derived from plant roots (Buee´ et al., 2009). 

Many factors control or affect survival of bacteria during desiccation (water limitation). Tolerance of desiccation is an indirect result of the ability to cope with osmotic, thermal, oxidative, and other stresses (see Sections 11, 12, 13). Our understanding of desiccation responses of rhizobacteria is fragmentary, but it is clear that desiccation conditions affect bacterial survival and that the ability of certain rhizobial species to survive desiccation is related to their responses to decreased water availability and increased osmotic or salt stress (Vriezen et al., 2007). 

We review in this chapter the mechanisms used by rhizobacteria to grow and survive in environments subject to water deprivation and osmotic stress. 

## 22.5.2 Biofilm development under water-limiting conditions

Terrestrial bacterial communities are exposed to a variety of environmental stress factors. Water limitation is one of the most critical of these factors, and it strongly affects the survival and activity of the communities (Potts, 1994). Water potential (ψ), representing the potential energy of water per unit volume relative to pure water in reference conditions, is a crucial environmental factor affecting bacterial activity in soil. Soil water potential has two components: osmotic potential (from dissolved solutes) and matric potential (from water retention force by soil) (Papendick and Campbell, 1980). Water availability declines with increasing solute content in soil (saline stress) or with decreasing water content from evaporation (matric stress). Degradation of soil quality because of desiccation and salinity affects ∼40% of cultivable land worldwide and is the most severe and widespread problem in modern agriculture. These stress factors often have serious impacts on soil bacteria (Fierer et al., 2003; Griffiths et al., 2003) (see Chapter 12.1). Salinity and desiccation conditions disrupt rhizobia–legume interaction and thereby reduce biological nitrogen fixation (Zahran, 1999). 

Bacteria in terrestrial habitats such as soil and plant surfaces are routinely or periodically water limited (i.e., not fully saturated with water). The mechanisms whereby bacteria survive and grow in environments that undergo periodic desiccation are poorly understood. 

Life in a biofilm has several advantages for soil bacteria, including protection from desiccation (Flemming and Wingender, 2010). Many studies have addressed the effects of drying on survival of inoculants and on their storage, application, and usage in general (Bogino et al., 2011; Catroux et al., 2001; Deaker et al., 2004), but very few studies have focused on bacterial cells in soil. The impact of drying on rhizobacteria is therefore poorly understood. The physiological responses of these bacteria to drying (including biofilm formation ability) remain to be elucidated. 

Bacteria exposed to water-limiting conditions undergo physiological changes to adapt to the stressful situation. Desiccation tolerance in rhizobia appears to be an indirect result of cellular adaptation to osmotic (see Section 11), thermal (see Section 13), and oxidative stresses (see Section 10). Such adaptation involves accumulation of osmoprotectants, compounds that remove reactive oxygen species, and synthesis of heat shock proteins and chaperone molecules (Vriezen et al., 2007) (see Chapter 12.1). Bacteria respond to water deprivation by adjusting their intracellular osmolyte concentration to maintain appropriate cellular turgor pressure. Compounds referred to as compatible solutes are accumulated to achieve proper water balance. The mechanisms of such accumulation may differ under solute versus matric water stress conditions. Cellular dehydration denatures proteins, damages DNA, and causes membranes to undergo phase transitions to gel phase at temperatures at which they would remain in liquid crystalline phase if fully hydrated (Potts, 1994). Increased synthesis and intracellular accumulation of trehalose or sucrose increase membrane fluidity when cells are desiccated and may protect proteins from denaturation (Billi and Potts, 2000). Bacteria exposed to water limitation may also increase EPS production, presumably because of the high water-holding capacity of these molecules. The presence of EPSs results in a more hydrated microenvironment (Roberson and Firestone, 1992), and mutations in EPS biosynthesis reduce desiccation tolerance (Ophir and Gutnick, 1994). Overlapping between mechanisms of tolerance to high osmolarity, extreme temperatures, nutrient limitation, and pollutant exposure may result in redundancies in regulatory pathways that lead to general or specific responses to stress (Mart´ınez-Bueno et al., 2002). 

## 22.5.2.1 Changes in gene expression

van de Mortel and Halverson (2004) surveyed gene expression in Pseudomonas putida (a soil saprophyte and rhizosphere colonist) exposed to low-water conditions. They found that 20 genes were induced by matric stress but not by thermodynamically equivalent solute stress, 11 genes were induced by both matric and solute stress, three genes were induced by solute stress, and three genes were repressed by matric stress. These genes were homologous to sequences present in the completed P. putida KT2440 genome and were involved in protein fate, nutrient or solute acquisition, energy generation, motility, alginate biosynthesis, or cell envelope structure. Several of these functions are directly related to the process of biofilm development. Mutants characterized within the protein fate group were insensitive to desiccation, whereas mutants in the cell envelope structure group showed reduced desiccation tolerance, indicating that desiccation tolerance depends on bacterial components that are related to the biofilm formation process. These findings provide a view of the environment as perceived by bacteria in water-limited habitats, and suggest that adaptation mechanisms for growth in such habitats differ from those for growth in high-osmolarity habitats. Van de Mortel and Halverson (2004) present a model in which several regulatory networks directly or indirectly control gene expression when bacteria are exposed to water-limiting conditions. Key adaptive responses to dehydrating conditions include maintenance of cell envelope structural integrity (to maintain a functional and fluid membrane), macromolecular biosynthesis (e.g., alginate for increased hydration of the microenvironment), energy generation capabilities, and removal of potentially toxic free radical compounds. 

## 22.5.2.2 Production of reactive oxygen species (ROS) and patterns of cell death

As soils dry, microbes undergo water deprivation and/or nutrient deprivation. Both of these stress factors lead to endogenous ROS production, inactivation of antioxidants, and membrane perturbations (Imlay, 2003) (see Sections 10 and 18), processes that lead in turn to cell death. Observed spatial and temporal organization patterns of dead cells in bacterial biofilms indicate that death is a part of biofilm developmental processes (Webb et al., 2003). 

Temporal dynamics and spatial organization of ROS accumulation and dead cells differ in P. putida biofilms developed under water-replete versus water-limiting conditions (Chang et al., 

2009). Under water-replete or solute stress conditions, cell death occurs in a nonrandom manner in arrays of various lengths that are one cell width in diameter, leading to a string-like appearance. Under water-limiting conditions, no such strings of dead cells were observed, although death rates and the degree of spatial stratification of dead cells were higher. This finding is most likely a consequence of the presence of extracellular DNA (eDNA); eDNA may be more abundant in the extracellular matrix under water-limiting conditions rather than waterreplete ones. The contribution, if any, of eDNA to bacterial fitness under water-limiting conditions has not been studied. 

Spatial organization of cell death is similar to patterns of ROS accumulation, which increase substantially under waterlimiting conditions. The stress severity at which ROS accumulation occurs is correlated with the stress severity at which dehydration leads to increased EPS production (Chang et al., 2007) and altered membrane fatty acid composition (Halverson and Firestone, 2000). Alginate (an EPS) reduces the degree of dehydration-mediated endogenous oxidative stress (Chang et al., 2009) and displays antioxidant activity (Simpson et al., 1989). 

## 22.5.2.3 Roles of bacterial components in biofilm formation

Surface and extracellular bacterial components have been studied extensively because they include molecules that play crucial roles during the process of host plant infection, independently of development of a beneficial or pathogenic relationship (Bogino et al., 2013b). Biofilm composition varies depending on the system, but the major components are typically water and bacterial cells, followed by matrix EPSs (Sutherland, 2001). Surface bacterial components such as flagella, pili, fimbriae, and lipopolysaccharides (LPSs) play crucial roles in physical processes during the initial stages of biofilm formation on surfaces. The growth, maturation, and disassembly phases of biofilms depend primarily on biosynthesis of extracellular biopolymers such as EPSs, proteins, and eDNA. These polymers promote immobilization of bacterial cells into the matrix, mechanical stability of the biofilm structure, cohesive interaction with the interface, and the architecture and functionality of the enclosed microbial community (Flemming and Wingender, 2010). Our fragmentary knowledge of the roles of bacterial components in biofilm development under water-limiting conditions is summarized in the remainder of this section. 

## Membrane alterations

Few studies have addressed the role of the cell envelope in desiccation tolerance mechanisms of rhizobia. Several researchers have speculated that LPSs are involved in this process. Gilbert et al. (2007) isolated a ctpA mutant in Rhizobium leguminosarum bv. viciae that displayed altered cell envelope integrity and decreased desiccation tolerance. A small desiccation stress in bradyrhizobia resulted in increased degree of saturation of fatty acids in membrane phospholipids (Boumahdi et al., 2001). 

Microarray analysis showed that numerous EPS and LPS biosynthesis and transport genes in Bradyrhizobium japonicum were upregulated in response to desiccation stress (Cytryn et al., 2007). A R. leguminosarum bv. viciae LPS mutant lacking the 27-hydroxyoctacosanoatic acid of lipid A showed reduced tolerance to desiccation stress and biofilm formation (Vanderlinde et al., 2009b). 

Halverson and Firestone (2000) performed a series of studies on the effects of permeating and nonpermeating solutes on fatty acid composition (including phospholipids and LPSs) of P. putida cell membrane. Matric and solute components of soil water potential had differential effects on the fatty acid composition. A cis-to-trans isomerization of monounsaturated fatty acids occurred in response to growth under matric stress, but not under saline stress. These changes were presumed to reduce the damaging effects of PEG 8000-mediated dehydration on membrane integrity, to counter the lipid-solidifying effects of cellular dehydration on membrane fluidity. When cell ultrastructure was observed by transmission electron microscopy, outer membranes of cells grown under matric stress appeared thicker and convoluted. 

## Role of EPSs

EPSs are present at the interface of bacteria with their surrounding environment, and they play important roles in stress resistance (Jefferson, 2004). EPSs can also chelate toxic metals and enhance metal resistance (Priester et al., 2006), and they enhance bacterial survival under nutrient-depleted conditions by serving as nutrient sources (Wang et al., 2007). An EPS’s function depends on its composition. It is important to evaluate EPS production and composition in response to environmental stresses. 

In both fully hydrated flow-through systems and unsaturated habitats, biofilms are encapsulated by an EPS layer (Priester et al., 2006). Many EPSs are hygroscopic, and their presence presumably creates a more hydrated microenvironment in the immediate vicinity of bacterial cells, thereby enhancing desiccation tolerance. Adaptation to matric stress is a complex process requiring a variety of physiological adjustments to counteract or repair cellular damage. The EPS matrix in biofilms plays an important role in such adaptation (Cytryn et al., 2007) by both creating a hydrated microenvironment that protects cells from dehydration stress (Chang et al., 2007) and trapping nutrients or metabolic waste products released from dead or leaky cells (Davey and O′ Toole, 2000). 

Gluconacetobacter diazotrophicus, a nitrogen-fixing bacterium and an endophyte of roots, stems, and leaves of sugarcane (Cavalcante and Dobereiner, 1988), expresses levansucrase, a¨ fructosyltransferase exoenzyme that displays sucrose hydrolytic and levan biosynthetic activities (Tambara´ et al., 1999). Levan is the major EPS secreted by G. diazotrophicus. It is responsible for the mucoid phenotype of the colony and may help protect against high osmolarity and desiccation stresses (see also Chapter 22.6). A levansucrase mutant strain showed reductions of osmotic (NaCl and sucrose) stress tolerance, desiccation tolerance, and the ability to form cell aggregates on abiotic surfaces. One possible interpretation of these findings is that levan acts as a non-accumulated osmoprotectant, and hydrolysis by levansucrase induces endogenous accumulation of glucose and/or fructose, thereby promoting bacterial growth under highosmolarity conditions (see Section 11). Thus, levan metabolism may contribute to NaCl tolerance (Velazquez-Hern ´ andez ´ et al., 2011). 

Transposon mutagenesis screening of R. leguminosarum bv. viciae 3841 to isolate genes required for maintaining a functional cell envelope revealed a strain with a mutation in the adenosine triphosphate (ATP)-binding protein of a previously uncharacterized ATP-binding cassette (ABC) transporter (RL2975– RL2977) (Young et al., 2006). The mutant, in comparison with the wild type, had a reduced level of EPSs in the cell envelope, which was correlated with decreased desiccation tolerance. This study showed the importance of EPSs in desiccation tolerance and identified a novel genetic element involved in biofilm formation (Vanderlinde et al., 2009a). 

Roberson and Firestone (1992) observed that EPS production by a soil Pseudomonas sp. was higher in desiccated than undesiccated sand cultures, suggesting that resources were allocated to EPS production in response to desiccation. 

EPS production and modifications of biofilm surface properties in response to low pH, high temperature, and osmotic stress (see Section 16, 13, 11) were analyzed in P. putida CZ1, a strain resistant to heavy metals. EPS production increased under these conditions. Both the amount and composition of EPSs were related to biofilm growth conditions. EPS production showed a roughly linear positive correlation with culture time in the air–biofilm interface, and cell volume decreased to compensate for nutrient depletion during aging (Lin et al., 2014). These authors concluded that EPS production created a more hydrated microenvironment around cells, thereby enhancing desiccation tolerance. Increased EPS amount may facilitate adaptation of this strain to osmotic stress without a reduction of growth rate. The findings of this study indicated that alterations of EPS composition and cooperation among cells are key response mechanisms of P. putida CZ1 biofilm to environmental stresses (Lin et al., 2014). 

Fluorescent pseudomonads produce many different types of EPS components, including levan, marginalan, cellulose, alginate, and several as-yet-uncharacterized polymers. Nearly all Pseudomonas species have the ability to produce alginate, but the beneficial effects of this compound remain incompletely understood. Chang et al. (2007) found that total EPS content and alginate production increased with increasing severity of matric stress, but not solute stress, and that alginate was a significant (but not the major) component of EPSs. Alginate functions to maintain cellular hydration even when it is not a major component of the EPS matrix. It is integral to biofilm architecture under matric stress conditions, but not under solute stress or water-replete conditions. Findings to date on biofilm development by P. putida and other Pseudomonas species under water-limiting conditions indicate that alginate promotes maintenance of a hydrated biofilm microenvironment and a biofilm architecture that protect cells from matric or water limitation stresses (Chang et al., 2007). 

The enhancing effect of alginate on desiccation tolerance may be due to its hygroscopic properties and/or its promotion of a biofilm architecture that reduces evaporative loss. A small amount of alginate is capable of protecting biofilm cells from desiccation stress or altering biofilm architecture. The increased fitness associated with alginate production under water-limiting conditions presumably explains why most biofilm-forming species transiently express alginate biosynthesis genes immediately after exposure to a desiccation event (Li et al., 2010). Because EPSs are a shared resource, production of a small amount of alginate by a subset of residents may contribute substantially to enhanced desiccation tolerance or improved biofilm architecture in single-species or multispecies biofilms. The ability of almost all pseudomonads to synthesize alginate (Fialho et al., 1990) suggests that this ability is an important fitness trait that provides a competitive advantage under certain environmental conditions (e.g., drought) or in potentially water-limited habitats (e.g., rhizospheres and aerial leaf surfaces) (Ramos-Gonzalez´ et al., 2005). 

Two putative EPS-encoding genes (pea and bcs) that affect biofilm development in various habitats and under various environmental conditions have been identified in the genome sequence of P. putida KT2440 (Nielsen et al., 2011). The EPS locus termed putida EPS A (pea) is involved in production of a novel glucose–galactose–mannose-enriched polymer that promotes cell–cell interactions necessary for pellicle and biofilm formation and stability. The expression of pea is induced strongly (and transiently) under matric stress but only slightly under saline stress. bcs encodes a cellulose-like polysaccharide that is important for biofilm maintenance following attachment and for rhizosphere fitness. bcs expression is strongly induced under water stress conditions. Cells defective in Pea and/or Bcs expression displayed increased dehydration-mediated cell envelope stress. These two proteins contribute to biofilm matrix hydration under water-limiting conditions, although to a lesser degree than alginate (Nielsen et al., 2011). 

Improved understanding of stress responses of P. putida biofilms in unsaturated habitats provides the basis for a model of maintenance of bacterial survival in the rhizosphere. 

## 22.5.2.3.1 Alterations of biofilm structure

The complex development of biofilm architecture in aquatic systems is affected by hydrodynamics, nutrient composition, and biological properties such as quorum sensing, EPS production, and motility (Davies et al., 1998). In unsaturated habitats, the absence of laminar fluid flow causes striking changes in nutrient availability patterns, metabolic waste accumulation and disposal, accumulation of cell-signaling molecules, and (consequently) biofilm architecture. 

The biofilms that bacteria form to colonize terrestrial habitats are not consistently saturated with water, and their thickness varies depending on environmental conditions. When biofilms are completely saturated, water potential (ψ) is based almost completely on osmotic potential (from dissolved solutes). In contrast, water potential of unsaturated biofilms in a dry habitat consists primarily of matric potential (Papendick and Campbell, 1980). Osmotic and matric potential are equivalent in a thermodynamic sense, but the two forms of water deprivation affect bacterial physiology differently. During osmotic stress (see Section 11), bacteria are embedded in a medium with low-water activity, whereas during matric stress they undergo desiccation by physical removal of water present in the environment (Chang et al., 2007). 

Studies of Pseudomonas have shown that biofilm development in conditions of low-water availability is a dynamic process that affects ultrastructural properties of biofilms (Auerbach et al., 2000; Chang and Halverson, 2003). Unsaturated biofilm formation in P. putida had three distinct phases: early, intermediate, and maturation. Reduced water availability alters cell morphology, thereby affecting microcolony size and ultrastructure. Cells are generally arranged in columnar fashion, perpendicularly to the surface. Biofilms resulting from dehydration (matric stress) treatment are composed of smaller cells, but are taller, are more porous, and have a thicker EPS layer at the air–biofilm interface. EPS overproduction under these conditions leads to changes in microcolony development and biofilm architecture (Chang and Halverson, 2003). 

Chang et al. (2007) found that alginate in P. putida and other fluorescent pseudomonads altered biofilm architecture, resulting in biofilms that were taller, covered less surface area, and had a thicker EPS layer at the air interface than those formed by an mt-2 algD mutant under water-limiting conditions. These properties presumably reduce evaporative water loss. Thus, alginate produced by pseudomonads under water-limiting conditions affects biofilm development and physiochemical properties of EPSs. Collectively, these responses facilitate maintenance of a hydrated microenvironment, protecting biofilm residents from desiccation stress and enhancing survival. 

The increasingly heterogeneous distribution of cells under matric stress may create chemical gradients as a result of the greater tortuosity of diffusional paths around the cells, and such gradients may alter biofilm properties. In solute stress treatments, cell filamentation occurred more frequently in the presence of high concentrations of ionic (but not nonionic) solutes, and biofilm architecture was strikingly altered in the filamentous cells (Chang and Halverson, 2003). Bacterial biofilm formation under unsaturated conditions is a multistage process and is affected by water availability (i.e., reduced water availability changes cellular morphology, growth rate, and physiology, all of which impact biofilm formation and properties). 

## 22.5.3 Bacterial communities

Our knowledge of bacterial biofilms has been greatly increased by studies during the past decade. Biofilms are now generally recognized as a bacterial phenotype characterized by a certain level of organization, including signaling in response to the number and identity of cells present and to the surroundings (Parsek and Greenberg, 2005). Most findings to date arise from in vitro studies of monospecies biofilms. However, the majority of biofilms in real-world habitats contain multiple bacterial species and (in many cases) fungi, algae, and/or protozoa as well. For this reason, research focus in recent years has gradually shifted toward the complexity and interactions of multispecies biofilms (Burmølle et al., 2014). 

Complex bacterial communities are shaped and dominated by interspecific interactions that are categorized roughly as cooperative or competitive, depending on the underlying type of social behavior and the occurrence of such molecular mechanisms as bacterial signaling, co-aggregation, and co-metabolism (Elias and Banin, 2012). 

Lee et al. (2014) evaluated relative and spatial species distribution in a multispecies biofilm consortium composed of three Gram-negative bacteria: P. aeruginosa, Pseudomonas protegens, and Klebsiella pneumonia. Monospecies biofilms of these three strains had varying levels of resistance to tobramycin and sodium dodecyl sulfate (SDS), whereas multispecies biofilms were resistant to both compounds. Species composition in multispecies biofilms was not notably affected by exposure to tobramycin or SDS, indicating that these compounds did not cause enhanced abundance of the more resistant species, but rather a protective effect by the resistant species that extended to the whole community. 

In the study by Lee et al. (2014), multispecies biofilms had delayed development and structures distinct from those of corresponding single-species biofilms. The multispecies biofilms were also more resistant to antimicrobial agents, and such protection at the community level was affected by spatial organization of the community members. Naturally occurring biofilms were typically composed of multiple species that lived together in communities. The biofilms were sometimes dominated by a single primary species, but secondary species were invariably present. 

## 22.5.3.1 Rhizobial multispecies biofilms and their protective effect against stress factors

Bacteria living in biofilms are better protected against various stress factors than are planktonic, exponentially growing cells (Jefferson, 2004). Protective effects against antimicrobial agents, immune system activity, and protozoan grazing have been demonstrated for various combinations of compounds and monospecies biofilms. The protective effects of biofilms are based on many phenomena, often occurring together, including altered structures and reduced diffusion rates of compounds in the matrix, modified gene expression patterns, and low cell growth rates. The structure and composition (up to 97% water) of biofilms protect cells from desiccation and other environmental stressors. Bacteria in biofilms thus have a fitness advantage over planktonic cells in many environments, particularly under stressful conditions (Burmølle et al., 2007). 

Soil bacteria play essential roles in cycling of nutrients (e.g., nitrogen, phosphorus, and carbon) and plant productivity. The impacts of agricultural practices such as tillage and soil fertilization on abundance, composition, and activity of soil bacterial communities have been extensively studied during the past decade. However, >99% of bacterial strains are unculturable and can be identified only by molecular approaches (Jorquera et al., 2014). 

The mechanisms of tolerance of rhizobacteria to environmental stresses may partially overlap, with associated redundancies of the regulatory pathways that underlie general and specific responses to stress (Mart´ınez-Bueno et al., 2002). The biochemical and physiological impacts of exposure to water-limiting conditions on rhizobial biofilm formation are poorly understood (Bogino et al., 2013a). 



Cavalcante V, Dobereiner J. 1988. A new acid-tolerant nitrogen fixing ¨ bacterium associated with sugarcane. Plant Soil 108: 23–31. 





Roberson E, Firestone M. 1992. Relationship between desiccation and exopolysaccharide production in a soil Pseudomonas sp. Applied and Environmental Microbiology 58: 1284–1291. 



Most studies on bacterial community structure have focused on effects related to plant traits, agricultural management, or soil properties (Castellanos et al., 2009). Very few have addressed the effects of water-limiting conditions on bacterial communities established as biofilms in the rhizospheric microenvironment. We reported recently (Bogino et al., 2013a) that rhizospheres of alfalfa (Medicago sativa) plants exposed to differing water-limiting conditions (saline stress or desiccated stress) harbored distinct bacterial communities (microconsortia) with differing abilities to develop biofilms and thus to establish themselves in this microenvironment. The community structures varied depending on exposure to stressful conditions, and the variability was related to promotion of bacterial community survival, plant growth, and protection from the stress (Bogino et al., 2013a).

## 22.5.4 Conclusion



Chang WS, Li X, Halverson LJ. 2009. Influence of water limitation on endogenous oxidative stress and cell death within unsaturated Pseudomonas putida biofilms. Environmental Microbiology 11: 1482– 1492. 





Sutherland IW. 2001. Biofilm exopolysaccharides: A strong and sticky framework. Microbiology 147: 3–9. 



Bacteria living in soil face contrasting environments: the rhizosphere (rich in nutrients from root exudates) and the soil matrix (often deficient in nitrogen, phosphorus, water, or other nutrients). Adaptation to and survival under adverse environmental conditions, for most rhizobacteria, depend on their ability to live attached to various surfaces (soil particles, organic matter, and roots) in an associative biofilm lifestyle. 



Chang WS, van de Mortel M, Nielsen L, Nino de Guzman G, Li X, et al. 2007. Alginate production by Pseudomonas putida creates a hydrated microenvironment and contributes to biofilm architecture and stress tolerance under water-limiting conditions. Journal of Bacteriology 189: 8290–8299.

Our knowledge of the mechanisms whereby bacteria adapt to desiccation stress, which is common in soils worldwide, is limited in spite of the importance of bacterial ecology in soil ecosystems. More extensive research in this area is urgently needed, particularly in view of the important role of rhizobacteria in maintaining soil quality, and the magnitude of water limitation problems in agricultural lands worldwide. 



Compant S, Clement C, Sessitsch A. 2010. Plant growth-promoting bac- ´ teria in the rhizo- and endosphere of plants: their role, colonization, mechanisms involved and prospects for utilization. Soil Biology and Biochemistry 42: 669–678. 





van de Mortel M, Halverson LJ. 2004. Cell envelope components contributing to biofilm growth and survival of Pseudomonas putida in low-water-content habitats. Molecular Microbiology 52: 735–750. 



Our long-term goal is to fully elucidate the mechanisms whereby rhizobacteria respond to the various forms of water deprivation commonly encountered in soil. It is increasingly clear that biofilm formation is a crucial mechanism for survival and activity of bacteria under desiccation stress conditions. Further investigation and understanding of biofilm formation at the physiological and genetic levels are critical for agricultural planning and maintaining the microbial diversity required for conservation of soil quality.
