# Stress conditions triggering mucoid-to-nonmucoid morphotype variation in Burkholderia, and effects on virulence and biofilm formation

Leonilde M. Moreira, Ines N. Silva, Ana S. Ferreira, and M ˆ ario R. Santos ´ 

Institute for Bioengineering and Biosciences (iBB), Instituto Superior Tecnico, Universidade de Lisboa, Lisboa, Portugal ´ 

## 22.6.1 Introduction

The genus Burkholderia contains presently more than 80 validly named species (http://www.bacterio.cict.fr) isolated from diverse environments, including soil, water, industrial settings, manmade products, symbiotic association with plants, invertebrate intestinal tracts, and human and animal infections (Coenye and Vandamme, 2003; Suarez-Moreno´ et al., 2012). This group of Gram-negative bacteria with biotechnological potential in bioremediation, biocontrol, and plant growth promoting is also commonly associated with diseases such as melioidosis (Lazar Adler et al., 2009), and with respiratory infections of immunocompromised patients and patients with cystic fibrosis (CF) or chronic granulomatous disease (Drevinek and Mahenthiralingam, 2010). Despite the vast knowledge on these microorganisms, infections caused by Burkholderia are still difficult to treat mainly due to their high antibiotic resistance (Loutet and Valvano, 2011). 

One common feature across the Burkholderia genus is the production of extracellular polysaccharides or exopolysaccharides (EPSs), high-molecular-weight sugar polymers implicated in different cellular functions such as signaling, protection against toxic compounds, dehydration, and environmental stress, among others (Ferreira et al., 2011a,b) (see also Chapter 22.5). At least seven different exopolysaccharides of Burkholderia have been identified and structurally characterized (reviewed in Ferreira et al., 2011b). Depending on the strains, some produce only one type of EPS, while others produce a mixture. For instance, Burkholderia contaminans IST408 (formerly Burkholderia cepacia) and Burkholderia multivorans ATCC 17616 only produce cepacian (EPS-II) (Cescutti et al., 2000; Ferreira et al., 2010), while Burkholderia cenocepacia C9343 produces simultaneously EPS-I, cepacian, and α-1,6-glucan (Conway et al., 2004), and Burkholderia kururiensis M130 produces cepacian and EPS B (Hallack et al., 2010). Regardless of the different polysaccharides encountered in Burkholderia, the most commonly produced among clinical and environmental isolates is cepacian (Cescutti et al., 2000; Conway et al., 2004; Ferreira et al., 2010; Hallack et al., 2010), a branched acetylated heptasaccharide repeat-unit polymer with D-glucose, Drhamnose, D-mannose, D-galactose, and D-glucuronic acid in the ratio of 1:1:1:3:1 (Cerantola ´ et al., 1999; Cescutti et al., 2000). Despite the determination of the chemical structure of the several polysaccharides of Burkholderia, the genetic basis for their biosynthesis is unknown. The exception is cepacian, known to be synthesized by a cluster of 20 genes named bce (Burkholderia cepacia exopolysaccharide), which can be together in the same genomic locus or split into two different regions (clusters bce-I and bce-II) (Ferreira et al., 2010; Moreira et al., 2003). A comparison of genome sequences from 17 Burkholderia species identified the bce genes in every available genome except for Burkholderia rhizoxynica HKI 454, which lacks bce-I and bce-II regions, and Burkholderia mallei isolates, which lack the bce-I region (Ferreira et al., 2011b). 

Cepacian biosynthesis has been elucidated in B. contaminans IST408, a clinical isolate of the Burkholderia cepacia complex (Bcc), which presently includes 18 closely related species (Peeters et al., 2013), all associated with respiratory infections of CF patients. Cepacian production begins with the formation of activated sugar-nucleotide precursors required for repeatunit assembly. The gene products required for this first step are BceA, BceM–BceN, BceT, BceC, and UGE, which are involved in the synthesis of GDP-D-mannose (Sousa et al., 2007a), GDP-D-rhamnose (Sousa et al., 2013), UDP-D-glucose, UDP-Dglucuronic acid (Loutet et al., 2009; Rocha et al., 2011), and UDP-D-galactose biosynthesis, respectively (Figure 22.6.1a). The next step of cepacian biosynthesis is the assembly of the heptasaccharide repeat unit by the action of glycosyltransferases and 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/f19b47e887fa5b529c67bceaf1f7dc2f18e3c34032dabca0dc6d2c4f34568809.jpg)


![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/208e5dac72e0dfb72200f77709672ce5d77f770aee6b7d0b71ea2a33a47bfbd8.jpg)



Figure 22.6.1 Biosynthesis of the exopolysaccharide cepacian by Burkholderia. (a) Proposed pathway leading to the formation of the nucleotide sugar precursors required for cepacian repeat-unit biosynthesis. (b) Schematic representation of the cepacian assembly and polymerization–export steps: (1) The repeat units of the polymer are initiated in the cytoplasmic side of the inner membrane in a reaction carried out by the BceB enzyme that catalyzes the transfer of glucose-1-phosphate to the isoprenoid lipid carrier. The remaining steps of the heptasaccharide unit assembly are performed by glycosyltransferases BceG, BceH, BceJ, BceK, and BceR and putative acyltransferases BceO, BceS, and BceU. (2) The lipid-linked repeat units are exported across the inner membrane by BceQ, a putative flippase. The EPS polymerization and export processes are dependent on BceI, a putative polysaccharide polymerase, and BceE, which forms a channel structure for export of EPS chains to the outside. (3) EPS chain-length determination is controlled by the action of the tyrosine kinase BceF and the phosphotyrosine phosphatase enzyme BceD, responsible for dephosphorylating BceF. PGM, phosphoglucomutase; PGI, phosphoglucose isomerase; PMI, phosphomannose isomerase; PMM, phosphomannomutase; UGE, UDP-glucose epimerase; IM, inner membrane; OM, outer membrane; PL, peptidoglycan layer.


acyltransferases. The sugar transferases involved are the priming glycosyltransferase BceB required for the transfer of glucose-1- phosphate to the isoprenoid lipid carrier (Videira et al., 2005), BceG, BceH, BceJ, BceK, and the putative bifunctional BceR (Figure 22.6.1b, part 1) (Ferreira et al., 2011b), although the specific sugar added by these transferases is unknown. The enzymes involved in acetylation of the repeat unit are likely BceO, BceS, and BceU, but none was functionally characterized. The enzymes involved in the last steps of repeat-unit polymerization and export to the cell surface are BceQ, BceI, BceE, BceD, and BceF. BceQ is a putative flippase that exports the lipid carrier-linked heptasaccharide repeat units across the inner membrane, BceI is the putative polysaccharide polymerase, and BceE is an outer membrane protein possibly involved in polysaccharide export across the outer membrane (Figure 22.6.1b, part 2) (Ferreira et al., 2011b). Proteins BceD and BceF are a phosphotyrosine phosphatase and tyrosine kinase, respectively, and are most likely implicated in polysaccharide chain length regulation (Figure 22.6.1b, part 3) (Ferreira et al., 2007). In addition, BceF tyrosine kinase has been implicated in other traits such as resistance to stress, motility, biofilm formation, and virulence (Ferreira et al., 2013). 

## 22.6.2 Exopolysaccharide biosynthesis and mucoid-to-nonmucoid transition in Burkholderia cepacia complex clinical isolates

Since the demonstration that strains of the genus Burkholderia produce exopolysaccharide (Sage et al., 1990), several studies assessed this property in clinical isolates usually recovered from respiratory infections of CF patients and in environmental isolates. One of the first studies analyzed the EPS production in 16 clinical isolates from a Portuguese CF center that included isolates from B. cenocepacia, B. contaminans, and Burkholderia stabilis (Richau et al., 2000). This study evidenced the high proportion of isolates with a mucoid morphotype due to EPS production in 100% of the B. stabilis, 75% of B. contaminans, and 50% of B. cenocepacia (genomovar III) isolates analyzed. In another study, six clinical and three environmental strains of B. cenocepacia belonging to the recA lineages IIIA and IIIB were shown to produce cepacian (Chiarini et al., 2004). In a comprehensive survey covering more than 550 isolates from 100 CF patients and 92 from environmental sources, more than 80% of the isolates of B. cepacia, B. multivorans, B. vietnamiensis, B. ambifaria, B. anthina, and B. pyrrocinia displayed the mucoid morphotype in mannitol-containing medium (Zlosnik et al., 2008). The species with fewer mucoid representatives were B. cenocepacia with 48% and B. stabilis with 23%. These findings highlight the ability of Bcc bacteria to be EPS producers independently of living freely in the natural environment or during infection of a CF patient’s airways. These observations differ from those for Pseudomonas aeruginosa, of which mucoid strains are rarely isolated from the environment (Pedersen et al., 1992). 

The study of Zlosnik and colleagues also assessed the mucoid morphotype variation in sequential isolates of single CF patients (Zlosnik et al., 2008). Altogether, 15 transitions were found, 13 of them from mucoid to nonmucoid and two from nonmucoid to mucoid (Zlosnik et al., 2008). This predominant mucoid-tononmucoid switch, observed mainly in B. multivorans isolates, contrasts with the other major CF pathogen, P. aeruginosa, in which the first colonizing isolates are nonmucoid, but with the course of the chronic infection become highly mucoid due to alginate production (Pedersen et al., 1992). Figure 22.6.2 shows an example of sequential clonal isolates of B. multivorans recovered from the same patient over a 13-year period (Silva et al., 2011), with the first isolate being an EPS producer. During the course of the infection, several lineages evolved, some of them not being able to produce EPS while others retained this trait. 

## 22.6.3 Environmental triggers of mucoid-to-nonmucoid morphotype variation

During chronic colonization of the CF patient’s airways, bacteria are exposed to several stress conditions, including low oxygen tension, high osmolarity, nutrient starvation, oxidative stress, and antimicrobial therapeutics, to name a few (Doring ¨ et al., 2011; Radtke and O’Riordan, 2006) (see Sections 11, 10; Chapter 2.9). Therefore, bacteria must constantly adapt to these conditions either by changes at the expression level and/or by genetic mutations. This explains the mucoid morphotype variation observed for both Pseudomonas aeruginosa and Burkholderia cepacia complex bacteria during chronic colonization of the CF patient’s airways (Pedersen et al., 1992; Zlosnik et al., 2008). The host environment is very complex and variable. Therefore, it is difficult to identify exactly what are the causes and the consequences of genetic mutations occurring in bacterial genomes across the long-term chronic colonization period. For that reason, experiments have been carried out in which bacteria are exposed under laboratory conditions to one or more stresses that mimic the lung environment and alterations in a phenotypic trait are evaluated. Using this approach, Silva and colleagues tested the ability of several stress conditions to trigger the mucoid morphotype variation by clinical and environmental isolates of Bcc and from environmental non-Bcc isolates (Silva et al., 2013). A prolonged stationary phase due to 21 days of growth under static conditions or growth in the presence of 2.5 times the minimal inhibitory concentration of ciprofloxacin were identified as mucoid-to-nonmucoid triggers in the Bcc strains of B. cepacia, B. multivorans, B. stabilis, B. dolosa, B. ambifaria, and B. anthina and the non-Bcc strains of B. phymatum and B. xenovorans with a frequency of nonmucoid variants ranging from 0.2 to 30% (Silva et al., 2013). The strain with the highest rate of mucoid-to-nonmucoid transition under the reported conditions, B. multivorans D2095, was exposed to other stresses such as inhibitory concentrations of antibiotics like ceftazidime, amikacin, and kanamycin; oxidative, osmotic, and nitrosative stresses; temperature below and above the optimum; and microaerophilia (see Sections 10, 11, 13, 15, 17; Chapter 2.9). Except for micro-aerophilia and nitrosative stress, all the other stresses exerted an effect on the mucoid phenotype, with 1–30% of the colonies being nonmucoid variants. Additionally, the mucoid-to-nonmucoid transition was stable since the alleviation of the stress imposed did not restore the mucoid phenotype of the obtained variants (Silva et al., 2013). In another study, the antibiotics ciprofloxacin and ceftazidime also have been shown to trigger the mucoid-to-nonmucoid switch in B. cenocepacia and B. multivorans clinical isolates in vitro (Zlosnik et al., 2011). 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/af0a51748ab1d43bcd59b5d2bc1a8eaab277024d38ec137624fdc7ffc21b363f.jpg)



Figure 22.6.2 Mucoid and nonmucoid isolates of B. multivorans recovered from a CF patient during a 13-year chronic infection period. Mucoid appearance in yeast extract mannitol agar plates and EPS produced by each isolate after 3-day culture in SM liquid medium and precipitated in ethanol.


Although these environmental factors triggering the mucoidto-nonmucoid switch were identified under laboratory settings, one cannot exclude that they may act alone or together under the host environmental conditions. During the course of a chronic lung infection of CF patients, adverse conditions of nutrient limitation, oxidative stress, and antibiotic therapies are likely triggering the mechanisms of adaptation that alter bacterial traits. 

## 22.6.4 Implications of the mucoid-to-nonmucoid morphotype variation in the expression of other phenotypic traits

Several studies reported the importance of exopolysaccharide production by Bcc bacteria for the full expression of virulence. In vitro studies confirmed the ability of Burkholderia EPS to interfere with the innate immune system by neutralizing reactive oxygen species, inhibiting neutrophil chemotaxis, and interfering with neutrophil phagocytosis (Bylund et al., 2006; Conway et al., 2004), most likely due to the masking of bacterial surface antigens recognized by immune cells. Another important role attributed to EPS is the mediation of bacterial resistance against antimicrobial peptides produced by epithelial and phagocytic cells. The exposure of Bcc bacteria to the human antimicrobial peptides cathelicidin LL-37 and α-defensin hBD-3 and other mammal peptides has shown that EPS-producing bacteria were more resistant to these antimicrobials (Benincasa et al., 2009). Among the models of infection used to study Bcc virulence (reviewed in Leitao˜ et al., 2010), the wax moth Galleria mellonella and mice have been used to assess mucoid and nonmucoid Bcc strains’ virulence. For example, mutations in the B. contaminans IST408 bceF and bceI genes encoding the tyrosine kinase and the putative polysaccharide polymerase, respectively, involved in cepacian biosynthesis were found attenuated in G. mellonella and the gp91phox−/− mouse (Ferreira et al., 2013; Sousa et al., 2007b). Infection of a BALB/c mouse pulmonary infection model with two clonal clinical isolates of B. cenocepacia displaying different morphotypes (mucoid vs. nonmucoid) showed that the mucoid clinical isolate had increased persistence in the lungs (Conway et al., 2004). Similarly, another pair of mucoid–nonmucoid clinical isolates from B. multivorans D2095 has shown acute virulence attenuation in G. mellonella for the nonmucoid isolate (Silva et al., 2011). This decreased ability of the nonmucoid isolates to establish acute infections in G. mellonella was also observed for several of the B. multivorans D2095–derived nonmucoid variants obtained under several stress conditions (Silva et al., 2013). Overall, this evidence suggests that EPS production seems to favor acute virulence and can be considered a virulence determinant. However, when clinical data were analyzed, there was a negative correlation between EPS production ability and CF patients’ lung function deterioration (Zlosnik et al., 2011). These authors analyzed the mucoid morphotype of all the Bcc isolates recovered from 62 CF patients attending a Vancouver clinic and assessed lung function decline. Data evidenced that patients infected exclusively with nonmucoid Bcc had a more rapid decline in lung function than those infected with mucoid bacteria. Even if one considers that 16 out of 62 of these patients were colonized by B. cenocepacia isolates, which are mostly nonmucoid and with higher intrinsic virulence, this study clearly shows that patients colonized predominantly with nonmucoid isolates have the worst prognosis in terms of lung function deterioration and disease progression. 

Observations from this last study seem to be in conflict with the role of EPS as a virulence determinant, as described in this chapter. One possible explanation for these differences is that when analyzing patient data, we are looking at long-term chronic infections that may last months or years, whereas in the tested animal infection models we are looking at acute infections that last a few days. Another possibility is that when mucoid-tononmucoid transition occurs, this particular phenotype is not the only one affected; other traits, such as biofilm formation, have relevance in virulence during chronic infections. Mutations in genes that prevent cepacian biosynthesis (bceF and bceI), or that affect its molecular mass (bceA, bceC, and bceD) or acetylation pattern (bceS), give rise to Burkholderia mutants with lower ability to form biofilms than the wild-type strains (Cunha et al., 2004; Ferreira et al., 2007, 2010), suggesting that a wild-type cepacian is required for optimal biofilm development. Nevertheless, when clonal sequential isolates from CF patients are analyzed, the ones with increased biofilm formation are usually low or non-EPS producers. This is the case with the well-characterized mucoid–nonmucoid pairs of B. multivorans D2095–D2214 and B. cenocepacia C9343–C8963, in which the nonmucoid isolates form more biofilm (Conway et al., 2004; Silva et al., 2011). Similarly, the nonmucoid variants obtained under laboratory stress conditions of B. multivorans D2095, B. dolosa CEP0743, B. anthina FC0974, B. ambifaria CEP0958, and B. stabilis LMG18888 had increased biofilm formation ability (Silva et al., 2013). The exception was the nonmucoid variant of B. contaminans IST408, which produces the same amount of biofilm as the parental strain. 

The increase of biofilm formation observed in nonmucoid clinical isolates and laboratory nonmucoid variants can be the result of compensation from other traits subjected to the same regulatory networks. As previously shown, low levels of cepacian should be detrimental to biofilm formation (Ferreira et al., 2007), but if this effect is suppressed by the overproduction of other structures with relevance to biofilm initiation or maturation, such as pili and adhesins, it may result in increased biofilm formation. By conducting experimental evolution studies, Traverse and colleagues have shown that adaptation of B. cenocepacia to a biofilm lifestyle is accompanied by numerous mutations in genes that control important features, like cyclic diguanosine-5′ -monophosphate (c-di-GMP)-based signaling; cell wall modifications, namely LPS and EPS; and metabolic adjustment (Traverse et al., 2013). 

## 22.6.5 Determinants of biofilm formation in the Burkholderia cepacia complex

Biofilms are structured microbial communities attached to a solid surface and encased in a hydrated polymeric matrix consisting of polysaccharides, proteins, and DNA. Biofilm formation is initiated with attachment to surfaces by planktonic bacteria, followed by formation of microcolonies and subsequent maturation into differentiated structures (Costerton et al., 1995; Stoodley et al., 2002). Biofilm cells differ from the planktonic ones in the genes and proteins they express, resulting in altered phenotypes that include resistance to antibiotics and the human immune system (Sauer, 2003). It is, then, not surprising to find so many genes and proteins governing regulatory networks implicated in biofilm formation (Joo and Otto, 2012). As in many other bacteria, biofilm formation is also a virulence trait of Burkholderia, being associated with the persistence of the infection and increased antibiotic resistance, particularly in patients with CF (Caraher et al., 2007). Due to this, considerable research has been accomplished in order to understand the molecular mechanisms involved in biofilm formation by these bacteria (reviewed in Fazli et al., 2014). These studies have resulted in the identification of the secondary messenger c-di-GMP, quorum sensing (QS) (see Section 21), two-component transduction (TCS) systems (see Section 3), and posttranslational modifications like protein tyrosine phosphorylation as implicated in biofilm formation. Figure 22.6.3 shows some of the mechanisms known to be involved in biofilm formation in Bcc. 

It is well established that c-di-GMP is a general key regulator of biofilm formation (Hengge, 2009). Its levels are controlled through the opposing activities of diguanylate cyclases (DGC) and phosphodiesterases (PDE), which contain sensory domains that sense and respond to environmental signals (Romling ¨ et al., 2013). In general, high intracellular levels of c-di-GMP are associated with decreased motility and extracellular enzyme secretion, but increased biofilm formation (Ma et al., 2011). The first evidence in Burkholderia of c-di-GMP involvement in biofilm formation was obtained by Huber and colleagues by analyzing transposon mutants with defects in this trait (Huber et al., 2002). One of these B. cenocepacia H111 mutants showing decreased biofilm formation carried the transposon inserted in a gene encoding a protein with both the diguanylate cyclase and phosphodiesterase domains, now named RpfR. Interestingly, this protein also responds to quorum-sensing signaling, demonstrating a complex interplay between c-di-GMP levels and QS molecules levels (Deng et al., 2012). In another study, Fazli and colleagues identified the Crp–Fnr superfamily regulator BCAM1349 of B. cenocepacia J2315 as a transcriptional regulator that binds c-di-GMP and regulates the formation of biofilms (Fazli et al., 2011). In a subsequent study, it was shown that BCAM1349 binds in a c-di-GMP-dependent way to the promoter region of genes BCAM1330–1341 of B. cenocepacia J2315, the products of which are responsible for the biosynthesis of an unknown polysaccharide, but different from cepacian (Fazli et al., 2013). Indeed, mutants in this region were not affected in initial attachment, but later stages of biofilm development were impaired. Another demonstration of the involvement of c-di-GMP in biofilm formation by Burkholderia comes from an in vitro evolution experiment showing that adaptive mutations important for the wrinkled colony phenotype and enhanced biofilm occurred in genes predicted to be involved in c-di-GMP signaling (Traverse et al., 2013). 

Another relevant mechanism influencing biofilm formation is through QS (see Section 21). The two main QS systems in Burkholderia are the CepIR acylhomoserine lactone (AHL)- based system (Lewenza et al., 1999) and the RpfFR Burkholderia diffusible signal factor (BDSF)-based one (Figure 22.6.3) (Boon et al., 2008; Ryan et al., 2009). It has been shown that BDSF-based QS positively regulates AHL signal production by controlling the transcription of the cepI gene in B. cenocepacia J2315 (Udine et al., 2013), showing a complex interplay between both QS systems. Analysis of the biofilms produced by cepI and cepR mutants showed similar attachment to the surface as the parental strain, but the mutants’ biofilms failed to develop into mature ones (Huber et al., 2001, 2002). Among other genes, CepIR regulate the expression of genes encoding the lectins BclACB and the large surface protein BapA (Inhulsen¨ et al., 2012). As deletion of the bapA gene results in reduction of biofilm formation, also the inactivation of the bclACB lectin encoding genes results in biofilms that contain hollow microcolonies, suggesting that lectins are important for biofilm structural development (Inhulsen¨ et al., 2012). In a following study using RNA-Seq and shotgun proteomics, it was shown that the expression of bclACB and bapA genes is also regulated by BDSF, and that both AHL and BDSF signal molecules are required for maximal expression of those genes (Schmid et al., 2012), providing evidence that both QS systems interact to regulate biofilm formation. In addition to the surface proteins BapA and lectins BclACB, trimeric autotransporter adhesins (TAAs) also contribute to biofilm formation. Two TAAs of B. cenocepacia 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/04696493d149f92be126ccce711204d328d58fb0efd2716b6f4ec84bab39ae5f.jpg)



Figure 22.6.3 Schematic representation of the molecular mechanisms controlling biofilm formation in Burkholderia cepacia complex bacteria, both at the signaling-cascade level consisting of the synthesis and sensing of signaling molecules and at the effector level comprising putative or already-studied effector regulators.


J2315, BCAM0223 and BCAM0224, have been implicated in adhesion of this bacterium to biotic and abiotic surfaces, with both mutants displaying reduced motility, biofilm formation, and adhesion to fibronectin-coated surfaces and to epithelial cells (Mil-Homens and Fialho, 2012; Mil-Homens et al., 2014). 

Another gene found to positively regulate biofilm formation is the gene encoding the LysR-type transcription factor ShvR of B. cenocepacia J2315 (Figure 22.6.3). The effect on biofilms played by ShvR occurs possibly through regulation of genes BCAS0201–BCAS0208 (Subramoni et al., 2011). Genes in this region were shown to influence utilization of branched-chain fatty acids, tricarboxylic acid cycle substrates, and branchedchain amino acids, and they are required for biofilm and pellicle formation. 

Posttranslational modification such as protein tyrosine phosphorylation and dephosphorylation are also implicated in biofilm formation by Burkholderia. This was demonstrated by mutations in the BceF tyrosine kinase and the BceD phosphotyrosine phosphatase (Figure 22.6.3) of B. contaminans IST408, which showed decreased biofilm formation (Ferreira et al., 2007). Furthermore, the BceF tyrosine kinase seems to regulate the expression of genes related to stress response, motility, and c-di-GMP signaling (Ferreira et al., 2013), traits known to be important for biofilm formation. Effector proteins in this pathway are unknown. 

The involvement of TCS (see Section 3) in biofilm formation in Bcc is exemplified by the hybrid sensor kinase AtsR (Figure 22.6.3). This protein of B. cenocepacia K56-2 shows homology with RetS from Pseudomonas aeruginosa, a well-known regulator of determinants involved in biofilm formation and cytotoxicity (Goodman et al., 2004). Inactivation of the atsR gene leads to increased biofilm production and a hyperadherent phenotype in both abiotic surfaces and lung epithelial cells (Aubert et al., 2008). Similarly to AtsR, the cognate response regulator AtsT also negatively regulates biofilm formation, quorum sensing, type-VI secretion, and protease secretion (Aubert et al., 2013; Khodai-Kalaki et al., 2013). 



glucuronic acid residue in a heptasaccharide repeating unit. European Journal of Biochemistry 260: 373–383. 





Loutet SA, Valvano MA. 2011. Extreme antimicrobial peptide and polymyxin B resistance in the genus Burkholderia. Frontiers in Cellular Infection and Microbiolog y 1: 6. 



## 22.6.6 Concluding remarks



Cescutti P, Bosco M, Picotti F, Impallomeni G, Leitao JH,˜ et al. 2000. Structural study of the exopolysaccharide produced by a clinical isolate of Burkholderia cepacia. Biochemical Biophysical Research Communications 273: 1088–1094. 





Ma Q, Yang Z, Pu M, Peti W, Wood TK. 2011. Engineering a novel c-di-GMP-binding protein for biofilm dispersal. Environmental Microbiology 13: 631–642. 



Bacteria from the Burkholderia genus produce several virulence factors, including exopolysaccharides, which play important roles in protection against the host’s innate immune system, possibly by masking bacterial surface structures recognized by immune cells. In this chapter, we have described the mucoid phenotype due to cepacian production, the conditions responsible for triggering the mucoid-to-nonmucoid transition observed during CF patients’ chronic lung infections, the implications of EPS production in virulence and biofilm formation, and its regulation in the B. cepacia complex. 



Chiarini L, Cescutti P, Drigo L, Impallomeni G, Herasimenka Y, et al. 2004. Exopolysaccharides produced by Burkholderia cenocepacia recA lineages IIIA and IIIB. Journal of Cystic Fibrosis 3: 165– 172. 





Mil-Homens D, Fialho AM. 2012. A BCAM0223 mutant of Burkholderia cenocepacia is deficient in hemagglutination, serum resistance, adhesion to epithelial cells and virulence. PLoS One 7: e41747. 



The importance of cepacian in virulence has been confirmed in several animal models of infection, and surveys to assess the ability of clinical isolates recovered from CF patients’ airways to produce EPS confirmed its high prevalence. In addition, Bcc isolates recovered from CF patients’ airways during persistent colonization were seen to have frequent transitions from mucoid to nonmucoid. In vitro studies showed that stress conditions such as the ones that bacteria face in the CF patient’s lungs trigger mucoid-to-nonmucoid transitions. These conditions include nutrient starvation, antibiotics, and osmotic and oxidative stresses, among others. If, however, the in vitro– obtained variants do not produce EPS, they showed virulence attenuation in an acute model of infection, higher potential to withstand nutrient starvation, and increased biofilm formation. The increased ability to form biofilms shown by nonmucoid variants can be the result of compensation from other traits within the same regulatory network and may foresee an adaptation mechanism to possibly deteriorating lung conditions. 



Coenye T, Vandamme P. 2003. Diversity and significance of Burkholderia species occupying diverse ecological niches. Environmental Microbiology 5: 719–729. 





Mil-Homens D, Lec¸a MI, Fernandes F, Pinto SN, Fialho AM. 2014. Characterization of BCAM0224, a multifunctional trimeric autotransporter from the human pathogen Burkholderia cenocepacia. Journal of Bacteriology 196: 1968–1979. 



The current knowledge on the molecular mechanisms involved in regulation of biofilm formation in Bcc points toward the action of the secondary messenger c-di-GMP, quorum sensing, two-component transduction systems, and posttranslational modifications such as tyrosine phosphorylation. As different cellular structures involved in biofilm formation have been shown to be co-regulated, the cross-interaction between regulatory systems has also been demonstrated. This shows that it is the concerted action of different regulatory players affecting more than one trait that leaves Bcc bacteria with increased ability to produce biofilms and perhaps to persist longer within the host. A better understanding of the individual regulatory mechanisms and the complex networks behind EPS production and biofilm formation will allow future identification of potential therapeutic targets.

# Effect of environmental conditions present in the fishery industry on the biofilm-forming ability of Staphylococcus aureus

Daniel Vazquez-S ´ anchez ´ 

Seafood Microbiology and Technology Section, Marine Research Institute, Spanish National Research Council (IIM-CSIC), Vigo, Spain 

## 22.7.1 Introduction

Staphylococcus aureus is a major foodborne bacterial pathogen causing food poisoning due to the ingestion of food containing heat-stable enterotoxins (EFSA, 2012; Hennekinne et al., 2012). The repeated presence of S. aureus in food products, including seafood (Novotny et al., 2004; Papadopoulou et al., 2007; Vazquez-S ´ anchez ´ et al., 2012), is mainly due to a crosscontamination from food handlers and food contact surfaces (DeVita et al., 2007; Simon and Sanjeev, 2007; Sospedra et al., 2012). Novel trends in food production (e.g., minimal processing, mass production, and globalization) have additionally introduced new scenarios that can enhance the presence and subsequent growth of S. aureus. 

Biofilms are the prevailing lifestyle of S. aureus (Otto, 2008), in which surface-adhered cells are embedded in an extracellular matrix mainly composed by polysaccharide intercellular adhesins (PIAs). PIA are formed by poly-β(1,6)-N-acetyl-dglucosamine glycans (PNAGs), the synthesis of which is controlled by the chromosomal intercellular adhesion (ica) operon carried by most S. aureus (Cramton et al., 1999; Fitzpatrick et al., 2005; O’Gara, 2007). The ica operon is basically repressed by icaR (Jefferson et al., 2003), which is regulated by the stressinduced sigma factor B (SigB), the Staphylococcus accessory regulator A (SarA), and the regulator of biofilm formation (Rbf), among others (Cerca et al., 2008; Cue et al., 2009). These gene products are also involved in the resistance of S. aureus to various environmental stresses (Lim et al., 2004; Rachid et al., 2000). Biofilm formation may contribute to the persistence of S. aureus in food-related environments, which enhances the recurrence of food contamination. Therefore, the aim of the present study was to investigate the effect of relevant fishery-related conditions on biofilm-forming ability of natural S. aureus isolates. 

## 22.7.2 Materials and methods

## 22.7.2.1 Bacterial strains

Twenty-six putative enterotoxigenic S. aureus strains isolated from fishery products (Vazquez-S ´ anchez ´ et al., 2012) and the reference strains S. aureus ATCC 6538 (a known biofilm former) and ATCC 43300 (MRSA strain) (Spanish Type Culture Collection, Spain) were tested (Table 22.7.1). Stock cultures were maintained in tryptic soy broth (TSB; Oxoid, UK) containing 20% glycerol (v/v) $\mathrm { a t } - 8 0 \ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ . All strains were thawed and subcultured twice in TSB for 24 h at ${ } ^ { 3 7 } \ { } ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ 200 rpm, prior to use. 

## 22.7.2.2 Adhesion to polystyrene under different ionic-strength conditions

Methodology described by Giaouris et al. (2009) was followed, with some modifications (Vazquez-S´ anchez´ et al., 2013). Overnight cultures were washed twice and adjusted to $\mathrm { O D } _ { 6 0 0 \mathrm { n m } } = 0 . 8$ in 1.5 or 150 mM NaCl (Merck, Switzerland). Three replicates of 200 μL of each sample were then added in flatbottomed 96-well microplates (Nunc, Denmark) and incubated for 4 h at $2 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ . After measuring the $\mathrm { \mathrm { O D } } _ { 6 0 0 \mathrm { n m } }$ , the microplates were washed three times with peptone water (Oxoid) and airdried for 2 h. Wells were then stained with 0.5% (w/v) crystal violet (CV; Merck) for 15 min, followed by three rinsing steps with distilled water. The microplates were air-dried for 15 min, and the bound CV was extracted with 150 μL of 33% (v/v) glacial acetic acid (Merck) for 30 min at room temperature. The mixture was 1:1 diluted with 100 μL of 33% glacial acetic acid prior to reading the $\mathrm { O D } _ { 5 6 2 \mathrm { n m } } .$ . These experiments were repeated twice using independent bacterial cultures.

## 22.7.2.3 Biofilm formation under different fishery-related environmental conditions

An optimized protocol described by Vazquez-S ´ anchez ´ et al. (2013) was used. Three replicates containing 100 μL of an overnight bacterial culture diluted 1:100 in TSB and 100 μL of TSB with or without 5% glucose, 5% NaCl, or 1 mM $\mathrm { M g C l } _ { 2 }$ (Merck) were added in polystyrene microplates. Biofilm formation was evaluated as aforementioned, but after 24 and 48 h of incubation at 25 and $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ These temperatures were selected for their relevance in the food industry and hospitals $( 2 5 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } )$ and in infectious disease $( 3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } )$ . 

## 22.7.2.4 Transcriptional analysis

Methodology previously described by Vazquez-S ´ anchez ´ et al. (2013) was followed to assess the expression levels of the genes icaA, sarA, rbf, and sigB. RNA was extracted from overnight cultures grown in TSB with or without 5% glucose, 5% NaCl, or 5% glucose + 5% NaCl. After treatment with RNAprotect Bacteria Reagent (Qiagen, Germany), bacteria were lysed by adding 200 μL of a mixture containing TE buffer, 40 mg/mL lysozyme, and 1 mg/mL lysostaphin (Sigma, USA). RNA was isolated by using the Qiagen RNeasy Mini Kit (Qiagen) and including a DNase treatment. Concentration and purity of RNA were assessed with a ND-1000 spectrophotometer (NanoDrop Technologies, USA). 

Reverse transcription of the RNA isolates was carried out following the Rode et al. (2007) protocol with slight modifications (Vazquez-S ´ anchez ´ et al., 2013). A mixture $( 1 3 \mu \mathrm { L } )$ with 300 ng RNA, 100 ng Random Primers, and 10 mM of each deoxyribonucleotide triphosphate (dNTP) (Invitrogen) was denatured at $6 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 5 min, incubated on ice for 1 min, and briefly centrifuged. Six microliters of 5× first strand buffer, 0.1 M DTT, and 200 U Superscript III reverse transcriptase (Invitrogen) were then added. Samples were incubated at $2 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 5 min, heated at $5 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 45 min, and immediately incubated at $7 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 15 min to inactivate the reaction. A brief centrifugation between each step was carried out. Six reverse transcriptase reactions were generated for each sample, including three negative controls without enzyme. 

Quantitative real-time polymerase chain reaction (qRT-PCR) was performed in an Abi Prism 7900 HT Sequence Detection System (Applied Biosystems, USA). PCR mixtures contained 1× TaqMan Buffer $\mathrm { A } ; 5$ mM MgCl ; 0.2 mM of dATP, dCTP, and dGTP; 0.4 mM dUTP; 0.2 μM primer; 0.1 μM probe 0.1 U AmpErase uracil N-glycosylase 1.25 U Ampli-Taq Gold DNA Polymerase (Applied Biosystems); 10 ng of complementary DNA (cDNA); and $\mathrm { d H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O }$ ultrapure DNAse and RNAse free (Gibco, Invitrogen Corporation, USA) up to a final volume of 25 μL. Primers and TaqMan probes designed by Rode et al. (2007) were used. PCR conditions consisted of one cycle at $5 0 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 2 min and at $9 5 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 10 min, followed by 40 cycles at $9 5 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 30 s and at $6 0 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 1 min. $\mathrm { C _ { T } }$ values were estimated on SDS 2.2 software (Applied Biosystems). The differences between the $\mathrm { C _ { T } }$ of the reference gene 16S and the $\mathrm { C _ { T } }$ of other genes analyzed $( \Delta \mathrm { C _ { T } } )$ were calculated to see possible changes in gene expression. One unit change represents a log of twofold change. 

## 22.7.2.5 Statistical analysis

Data were statistically treated using the software package IBM SPSS 19.0. Results from the adhesion and biofilm formation assays were normalized and expressed as $\mathrm { O D } _ { 5 6 2 \mathrm { n m } } / \mathrm { O D } _ { 6 0 0 \mathrm { n m } } ,$ due to the variation in total growth at $2 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ and $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Rode et al., 2007). Statistical significance analysis was carried out using a one-way ANOVA. Homogeneity of variances was examined by a post-hoc least significant difference (LSD) test. Otherwise, we performed Dunnett’s T3 test. We also performed an independent-samples t-test to compare strains in pairs. Bivariate correlations were analyzed using the Pearson correlation coefficient. Significance was expressed at the 95% confidence level $( p < 0 . 0 5 )$ or greater. 

## 22.7.3 Results and discussion

Initial adhesion to polystyrene was positively correlated $( r =$ 0.577, $ { p } < 0 . 0 1 )$ with ionic-strength conditions (Vazquez-´ Sanchez ´ et al., 2013), as shown in Figure 22.7.1 The presence of high ionic strengths probably caused the attenuation of repulsive electrostatic interactions between the highly negatively charged bacteria and the negatively charged polystyrene surface (Giaouris et al., 2009), so the use of seawater during fishery processing could increase the risk of biofilm formation and cross-contamination. A high variability in adhesion to polystyrene among S. aureus strains was also observed for both ionic-strength conditions, hence suggesting possible differences in cell wall electronegativity, as described by Giaouris et al. (2009) in Lactococcus lactis. 

Seawater and fishery waste are also important sources of salt that have been implicated in the promotion of bacterial aggregation and in the stability of biofilms in polystyrene (Møretrø et al., 2003; Rode et al., 2007). However, the effect of NaCl on biofilm-forming ability of tested S. aureus strains was markedly affected by environmental temperatures (Vazquez- ´ Sanchez ´ et al., 2013), being significantly $\left( p < 0 . 0 1 \right)$ decreased at 37 ◦C (Figure 22.7.2b). Xu et al. (2010) also detected an increase in adhesion of S. aureus ATCC 12600 in polystyrene in the absence of NaCl for the first 48 h at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ . A possibility proposed by Lim et al. (2004) could be the repression of biofilm formation either directly or through overexpression of rbf with concentrations of 5% NaCl. Nevertheless, the expression of rbf in the presence of NaCl was normal, as shown by data obtained during transcriptional analysis by qRT-PCR of S. aureus St.1.07, St.1.14 and St.1.29 (Figure 22.7.3), three strains selected for their characteristic biofilm-forming properties. Meanwhile, Rachid et al. (2000) described an osmotic stress resistance and biofilm formation induced by $s i g B ,$ , but its expression was low in S. aureus St.1.29, which had a remarkable biofilm-forming ability in the presence of salt. Similarly, the biofilm-forming promoter of sarA was downregulated in St.1.29 by the presence of NaCl, whereas the other two strains showed a higher expression. Therefore, these results indicate a great variability of regulatory responses against osmolarity stress conditions during the development of staphylococcal biofilms. 

The availability of nutrients such as glucose also influenced biofilm formation of S. aureus strains (Vazquez-S´ anchez´ et al., 2013). Thus, the presence of glucose significantly $( p < 0 . 0 1 )$ ) enhanced staphylococcal biofilm formation under all conditions tested (Figure 22.7.2c), which is in agreement with data obtained by Rode et al. (2007). However, the effect of glucose was affected by temperature conditions, with the highest increases produced in the first 24 h at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ and after 48 h at $2 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ . This nutrient is considered a limiting factor of biofilm formation due to its

requirement during the production of the extracellular matrix components (Ammendolia et al., 1999). In fisheries, glucose is an additive frequently used to reduce the water activity of surimis and smoked fish. The biofilm-forming ability of most isolates (70%) from these kinds of products was highly influenced by glucose, so its presence could potentiate the contamination by S. aureus. 

According to Møretrø et al. (2003) and Rode et al. (2007), the presence of glucose, salt, or both promotes biofilm formation by inducing the ica operon in S. aureus. In this study (based on Vazquez-S ´ anchez ´ et al., 2013), an increase in icaA expression with the addition of glucose was also observed in S. aureus St.1.07, St.1.14, and St.1.29 (Figure 22.7.3). However, although icaA expression remained high, biofilm formation was lowered when both glucose and NaCl were present (Figure 22.7.2d), suggesting that other ica-independent pathways are implicated, as proposed previously by different authors (Fitzpatrick et al., 2005; Kogan et al., 2006). 

Another important environmental factor is the presence of magnesium chloride on fishery-contact surfaces from seawater and fishery waste generated during production. In this study (based on Vazquez-S´ anchez´ et al., 2013), biofilm formation of S. aureus strains was significantly $( p < 0 . 0 5 )$ increased only by the presence of $\mathrm { M g C l } _ { 2 }$ after 48 h at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Figure 22.7.2e). These results were in concordance with previous works that relate MgCl with biofilm stabilization at optimal growth conditions (Akpolat et al., 2003; Barnes et al., 1999; Dunne, 2002; Planchon et al., 2006). 

In conclusion, data obtained in this study (based on Vazquez- ´ Sanchez´ et al., 2013) supported that fishery-related conditions affected the adhesion and biofilm formation of S. aureus. Therefore, the prevalence of S. aureus on fishery-contact surfaces may be linked to their environmental adaptability during fishery production. These findings are relevant for food safety and may be of importance when choosing the safest environmental conditions and materials during processing, packaging, and storage of seafood products.

# Biofilm development and stress response in the cholera bacterium

Anisia J. Silva and Jorge A. Benitez 

Morehouse School of Medicine Department of Microbiology, Biochemistry and Immunology, Atlanta, Georgia, USA 

## 22.8.1 Introduction

Cholera is a waterborne diarrheal disease caused by the Gramnegative and motile bacterium Vibrio cholerae of serogroups O1 and O139. Like other members of the Vibrionaceae family, the cholera bacterium is a natural inhabitant of aquatic ecosystems. Cholera continues to be a major public health concern in endemic areas of South Asia and Africa, where outbreaks follow a seasonal pattern that correlates with climatic changes. V. cholerae O1 can be divided in two biotypes, classical and El Tor (Kaper et al., 1995), which differ in the severity of clinical symptoms and the expression and regulation of major virulence factors (Beyhan et al., 2006; Kaper et al., 1995). Humans have experienced seven recorded cholera pandemics. The seventh and current pandemic is characterized by the predominance of the O1 serogroup of the El Tor biotype, with periodic emergence of O139 strains, which emerged from the El Tor biotype and exhibit a new lipopolysaccharide and a capsule (Albert, 1994). 

## 22.8.2 Genetic determinants of virulence and pathogenicity: virulence factors

The cholera bacterium expresses two major virulence factors: cholera toxin (CT), which is responsible for the profuse ricewatery diarrhea typical of this illness (Finkelstein, 1992; Kaper et al., 1995), and the toxin-coregulated pilus (TCP). CT is an $\mathrm { A B } _ { 5 }$ family toxin that consists of one A subunit exhibiting ADPribosyltransferase activity and five B subunits that form the binding site for the CT receptor, the ganglioside GM located at the apical side of enterocyte plasma membrane (Finkelstein, 1992; Kaper et al., 1995). TCP is a type IV pilus required for colonization of the suckling mouse and human small intestine (Herrington et al., 1988; Thelin and Taylor, 1996). 

The expression of CT and TCP is coordinately regulated by a complex regulatory network. At the top of the regulatory cascade, proteins AphA and AphB concertedly enhance the transcription of the transmembrane regulators TcpP and TcpH (Hase and Mekalanos, 1998; Kovacikova and Skorupski, 2001).¨ TcpPH, together with the transmembrane regulators ToxR and ToxS (Miller et al., 1989; Miller and Mekalanos, 1985), activates the transcription of the soluble regulator ToxT (DiRita et al., 1991). Finally, ToxT interacts with the ctxA and tcpA promoters to activate production of CT and TCP (DiRita et al., 1991). 

In addition to CT and TCP, flagellar motility plays an important role in V. cholerae pathogenicity (see Chapters 22.1 and 22.2). Motility is required for V. cholerae to establish infection, for colonization of the small intestine, to detach and spread along the gastrointestinal tract, and/or to exit the host and return to the environment (Butler and Camilli, 2004; Lee et al., 2001; Nielsen et al., 2006; Silva et al., 2006). The cholera bacterium expresses a polar flagellum energized by sodium motive force (Kojima et al., 1999). The structure of the polar flagellar system has been recently reviewed in Zhu et al. (2013) (see Chapter 22.1). Briefly, the flagellar motor or basal body consists of a rotary part (rotor) and a stationary part (stator). The rotor is composed of several rings (C, MS, P, and L) connected through a rod to the hook, which transmits torque to the filament composed of flagellins. The stator consists of four proteins: PomA and PomB that form the sodium-conducting channel, and MotX and MotY that form a T-ring that facilitates incorporation of PomAB to the motor (Zhu et al., 2013). The genes required for the biosynthesis and assembly of the polar flagellum are located in several clusters in the V. cholerae large chromosome (Prouty et al., 2001). The genetic organization of polar flagellar genes in the Vibrionaceae has been reviewed by McCarter (2001). 

The expression of motility requires a four-tier hierarchical regulatory cascade involving the alternative RNA polymerase subunits $\sigma ^ { 5 4 }$ (RpoN) and $\sigma ^ { 2 8 }$ (FliA) and the $\sigma ^ { 5 4 } .$ -dependent transcriptional activators FlrA and FlrC (Prouty et al., 2001; Syed et al., 2009). At the top of this cascade, the single class I gene flrA encoding FlrA activates the transcription of class II genes, which include flrBC encoding $\mathrm { F l r C } ,$ , and fliA encoding $\sigma ^ { 2 8 }$ . When phosphorylated by FlrB, phospho-FlrC activates the transcription of class III genes. Finally, $\sigma ^ { 2 8 }$ activates the transcription of class IV genes (Prouty et al., 2001). 

## 22.8.3 Intestinal colonization

To cause disease, toxigenic Vibrios need to overcome the gastric acid barrier, adhere to the intestinal mucosa, and colonize the small intestine. Recent advances in cellular imaging (Millet et al., 2014) and the development of in vivo expression technology (Camilli and Mekalanos, 1995; Lee et al., 1999) are dissecting the infective process into discrete spatiotemporal events during which infective bacteria sense, integrate, and respond to local environmental cues in the host; express chemotaxis and motility to reach preferred anatomical sites within the small intestine; temporarily and spatially express virulence factors; form biofilm communities in vivo; downregulate the expression of major virulence factors late in infection; and detach to return to the aquatic environment (Zhu et al., 2002). 

## 22.8.4 Vibrio cholerae and the environment

Vibrios are autochthonous members of freshwater, estuarine, and marine ecosystems. Environmental counts of toxigenic V. cholerae range from $1 0 ^ { 1 }$ cells/ml in pristine areas to $1 0 ^ { 5 }$ cells/ ml during outbreaks in sewage-contaminated water (Morris, 2011). Approximately $1 0 ^ { 7 } - 1 0 ^ { 9 }$ virulent V. cholerae cells/ml are shed into the environment in cholera stool during outbreaks. Thus, V. cholerae essentially exploit human infection to increase its number in nature. In the aquatic ecosystem, V. cholerae persists and can be found in the form of planktonic free-swimming cells or as sessile biofilm communities associated with phytoplankton and zooplankton (Huq et al., 1983; Islam et al., 1990). In this scenario, Vibrios are subject to diverse physical, chemical, and biological stresses that include nutrient limitation (see Section 7), extreme temperatures (see Section 13), oxidative stress (see Section 10), bacteriophage infection, and protozoan grazing (Faruque et al., 2005; Matz et al., 2005). 

In sum, V. cholerae has evolved to effectively colonize disparate ecological niches: the human small intestine and the aquatic environment. Studies at the interface of infectious disease ecology and pathogenesis have revealed that the cholera bacterium, whether in the human host or in the aquatic environment, employs common survival stratagems. These strategies involve (i) the activation of specific and general stress responses, (ii) expression of flagellar motility and chemotaxis, (iii) attachment to surfaces, (iv) formation of biofilms, and (v) detachment. Critical to V. cholerae survival in the host and estuarine waters is its ability to switch between motile (planktonic) and sessile (biofilm) lifestyles in response to chemical and physical changes in the extracellular milieu. 

## 22.8.5 The biofilm

Biofilm formation appears to be ubiquitous in the bacterial world. A comprehensive definition of a biofilm is a microbially derived sessile community characterized by cells that are attached to a substratum, an interface, or each other; are embedded in a self-produced matrix; and exhibit an altered phenotype with respect to growth rate and gene transcription (Donlan and Costerton, 2002). This definition includes bacterial aggregates in suspension, floccules, and pellicles formed at the liquid–air interface of static cultures. Furthermore, it defines the biofilm as a distinct physiological stage that differs from planktonic cells at the transcriptome, proteome, and phenotypic levels. For organisms like V. cholerae that can adopt alternative lifestyles, we should add that biofilm cells must also exhibit a built-in mechanism to ensure their detachment and return to a planktonic lifestyle when the environmental conditions dictate. 

Biofilm formation is a complex multistep developmental process. The first step is a transient bacterial attachment to a surface to form a monolayer (Watnick and Kolter, 1999). At this stage, cells are attached to the surface but not to one another. In some cells, the initial temporary association could transit into a more permanent attachment to form a monolayer biofilm. We and others have shown that flagellar motility enhances the primary adherence event to borocilicate and mucin-coated borosilicate surfaces (Silva et al., 2006; see also Chapter 22.2). It has not been conclusively determined how motility promotes adherence. In some studies, this effect appears not to involve the flagellum itself but the flagellar motor (Lauriano et al., 2004; Van Dellen et al., 2008). However, a later study suggested that the flagellum can function as an adhesin and act synergistically with other pili to promote surface selection and attachmment (Utada et al., 2014). Adherence requires the expression of cell envelope proteins that could be surface specific. For instance, the mannosesensitive hemagglutinin (MshA) is required for the formation of static biofilms on borosilicate surfaces (Watnick et al., 1999; Watnick and Kolter, 1999) and for V. cholerae adherence to the exoskeletons of the planktonic crustacean Daphnia pulex (Chiavelli et $a l . ,$ , 2001). The TCP pilus has been shown to mediate biofilm formation on chitinaceous surfaces (Reguera and Kolter, 2005). The N-acetylglucosamine-binding protein GbpA has been reported to mediate bacterial adherence to marine chitin and intestinal mucin (Wong et al., 2012). 

Following surface attachment, the formation of threedimensional biofilms – consisting of pillars of bacteria and channels for nutrient acquisition and toxic metabolite disposal – requires the biosynthesis of an extracellular matrix composed of exopolysaccharides and proteins. The genes responsible for V. cholerae exopolysaccharide (vps) biosynthesis are located in two clusters (vpsU and vpsA–K) and vpsL–Q on the V. cholerae large chromosome (Yildiz and Schoolnik, 1999). These clusters comprise two operons in which vpsA and vpsL are the first genes of operon I and II, respectively (Yildiz and Schoolnik, 1999). A third gene cluster (rbmA–F, located between the vpsA–K and vpsL–Q operons) and bap1 encode protein components of the biofilm matrix (Fong and Yildiz, 2007). 

The protein components of the biofilm matrix are delivered through the type II secretion pathway (Johnson et al., 2014), which is also responsible for the secretion of cholera toxin and hemagglutinin (HA)–protease. The matrix protein RbmA has been proposed to participate in early cell-to-cell contact (Giglio et al., 2013), while Bap1 functions to promote adherence of the biofilm to surfaces (Berk et al., 2012) and facilitates the recruitment of motile bystander cells to the biofilm community (Absalon et al., 2011). Additional proteins identified within the biofilm matrix included MshA, flagellins FlaABCD, hemolysin, HA–protease, and chitinase (Absalon et al., 2011). The presence of HA–protease in the biofilm matrix is particularly interesting given that this protein has been suggested to facilitate V. cholerae detachment from the intestinal mucosa late in infection (Finkelstein et al., 1992; Hase and Finkelstein, 1991). The¨ possibility exists that HA–protease could also function as a “detachase” in V. cholerae biofilms. 

In addition to exopolysaccharides and proteins, the biofilm matrix also contains extracellular DNA (eDNA). The role of eDNA in V. cholerae biofilm has not been studied in much detail. It has been shown that V. cholerae eDNA is regulated by the extracellular nucleases Dns and Xds (Seper et al., 2011). Double mutants lacking both nucleases exhibited elevated biofilm mass compared to the wild type (Seper et al., 2011). The differences between mutant and wild type, however, were detected in biofilms incubated for more than 24 h, suggesting that the nuclease activities affect biofilm mass by promoting detachment. 

It has been established that V. cholerae cells in planktonic, monolayer, and mature biofilm stages differ in their global transcription profiles (Moorthy and Watnick, 2004, 2005). The transition from planktonic to biofilm lifestyle is accompanied by a downregulation of motility and chemotaxis gene expression and induction of genes required for the biosynthesis of the biofilm matrix (Moorthy and Watnick, 2004, 2005). In the biofilm microenvironment, cells are packed within a smaller volume, nutrient accessibility is limited by diffusion, and so is the elimination of toxic metabolic waste products. These conditions could favor an early entry of cells into quorum-sensing mode and the stationary phase. In effect, it has been reported that the concentration of cholera autoinducer-1 (CAI1) is higher in biofilms, resulting in earlier expression of the master quorumsensing regulator HapR (Liu et al., 2007). In Escherichia coli, DNA microarray analysis revealed that cells in the biofilm show a gene expression pattern similar to that of wild-type planktonic cells in the stationary phase (Ito et al., 2008), and that the expression of an rpoS–gfp fusion was detected at an early stage of biofilm development (Ito et al., 2009). It is reasonable to anticipate that Vibrio biofilms will exhibit a similar behavior dictated by the biofilm physicochemical microenvironment. 

## 22.8.6 Role of biofilm in V. cholerae persistence in the environment and pathogenesis

The capacity of V. cholerae to form biofilm communities enhances its survival in the aquatic environment (Faruque et al., 2006; Joelsson et al., 2006: Matz et al., 2005). Certain mutations that enhance the ability of V. cholerae to form biofilms also induce the formation of a rugose colonial morphology. Both biofilm formation and adoption of a rugose colonial morphology correlate with the production of V. cholerae matrix exopolysaccharide (Yildiz and Schoolnik, 1999). The V. cholerae rugose colonial variant is more resistant to chlorinated water (Morris et al., 1996; Rice et al., 1992) and to osmotic and oxidative stresses (Wai et al., 1998; Yildiz and Schoolnik, 1999) (see Sections 10, 11). 

In aquatic ecosystems, V. cholerae can also be found as conditionally viable environmental cells (CVECs), which are large aggregates of partially dormant cells that resist cultivation in conventional medium but can be recovered as virulent Vibrios by animal passage (Faruque et al., 2006). These aggregates appear to be similar to the previously described viable but nonculturable cells (Xu et al., 1982) (see Section 23). Environmental biofilms have been suggested to serve as precursors for the development of CVECs (Kamruzzaman et al., 2010). Furthermore, environmental biofilms are more resistant to the initial low-pH stress encountered after ingestion by humans (Zhu and Mekalanos, 2003) (see Section 16). Accordingly, mutations that block the biosynthesis of the biofilm matrix exopolysaccharide diminish intestinal colonization in the suckling mouse cholera model (Fong et al., 2010). V. cholerae can form biofilms during infection, and these biofilms, present in fresh cholera stool, are in a stage of transient hyperinfectivity (Faruque et al., 2006; Tamayo et al., 2010). This hyperinfective stage explains the rapid spread of V. cholerae during outbreaks despite its relatively high infective dose (Morris, 2011). The hyperinfective biofilms are dispersed upon re-entering the human small intestine and redevelop later in infection. It has been suggested that the bile component taurocholate promotes the early dispersal of hyperinfective biofilms (Hay and Zhu, 2014). Interestingly, other bile components such as cholate and deoxycholate enhance biofilm formation (Hung et al., 2006). 

In Table 22.8.1, we illustrate potential advantages of V. cholerae being motile or sessile. Thus, the capacity of V. cholerae to colonize different ecological niches clearly resides in its ability to switch between alternative lifestyles. 

## 22.8.7 Switching between planktonic and biofilm lifestyles

The transition of V. cholerae between motile and sessile lifestyles is regulated by the second messenger, cyclic diguanylic acid (c-di-GMP) (Beyhan et al., 2006; Tamayo et al., 2007; Tischler and Camilli, 2004). Cyclic di-GMP is synthesized from GTP through the activity of diguanylate cyclases (DGCs) exhibiting GGDEF domains and is hydrolyzed to GMP by phosphodiestrerases (PDEs), which can exhibit EAL or HD-GYP domains (Galperin, 2004; Simm et al., 2004). These proteins commonly exhibit complex domain architectures that include additional response receiver (phosphoaceptor), periplasmic or membrane sensor domains, helix-turn-helix DNA-binding domains, and so on (Galperin, 2004). The V. cholerae genome contains 31 genes encoding proteins with GGDEF domains, 12 genes encoding proteins with EAL domains, 10 genes encoding proteins with both GGDEF and EAL domains, and nine genes encoding proteins with HD-GYP domains (Tamayo et al., 2007). Due to this redundancy, deletions of single DGC and PDE genes have a modest effect on motility and biofilm formation. However, deletion of multiple genes encoding DGCs such as CdgA, CdgH, CdgL, CdgK, and CdgM significantly diminished biofilm formation and enhanced motility, suggesting that these enzymes act additively to increase the c-di-GMP pool (Shikuma et al., 2012). An alternative approach to identify the cellular processes regulated by c-di-GMP has been to artificially manipulate the c-di-GMP pool by overexpression of DGC and PDE enzymes. These experiments have conclusively revealed that elevated c-di-GMP levels enhance biofilm formation and inhibit motility (Beyhan et al., 2006; Tischler and Camilli, 2004). Inhibition of motility gene expression results from binding of c-di-GMP to FlrA to inhibit its activity at the flrBC promoter (Srivastava et al., 2013) and terminate transcription of the entire flagellar system. 


Table 22.8.1 Fitness benefit of a dual lifestyle.


<table><tr><td>Motile lifestyle</td><td>Sessile lifestyle</td></tr><tr><td>Swimming toward a source of nutrients or moving away from repellentsUsing the flagellum as an attachment organelleUsing the flagellum as a mechanosensor (Anderson et al., 2010)Maintenance of intracellular sodium homeostasisCoupling of sodium flux through the flagellar motor to the regulation of other cellular processes (Silva et al., 2006; Syed et al. 2009)</td><td>Enhanced resistance to chemical and physical stressors in the environmentEnhanced resistance to grazing by heterotrophic protists (Matz et al., 2005)Lower infective dose (Tamayo et al., 2010)Facilitation of lateral gene transferPossible differentiation into dormant environmental persisters (Kamruzzaman et al., 2010)</td></tr></table>

A number of V. cholerae proteins have been confirmed to directly bind and sense c-di-GMP levels to regulate virulence, motility, and/or biofilm formation. These proteins include FlrA (Srivastava et al., 2013); VpsR, a homolog of the NtrC subclass of response regulators (Srivastava et al., 2011; Yildiz et al., 2001); the LuxR-type regulator VpsT (Casper-Lindley and Yildiz, 2004; Krasteva et al., 2010) (see Section 21); and five proteins containing PilZ domains (Pratt et al., 2007). Of these proteins, only VpsR and VpsT are required for biofilm development (Casper-Lindley and Yildiz, 2004; Yildiz et al., 2001). 

## 22.8.8 Environmental regulation of biofilm development

V. cholerae regulates biofilm development by integrating multiple environmental signals that include cell density, carbon source, nucleosides and amino acids, extracellular phosphate, polyamines, indole, iron, and calcium. Table 22.8.2 summarizes the major known environmental cues affecting the biosynthesis of the biofilm exopolysaccharide and protein matrix, as well as the signal transduction pathways and major transcription factors involved in this process. 

As shown in Table 22.8.2, most signal transduction pathways that control biofilm development converge at modulating the intracellular c-di-GMP pool and the activity of the transcription factors VpsR and VpsT. VpsT has been shown to interact with the vpsL promoters to activate transcription of the vps operon II (Krasteva et al., 2010). 

## 22.8.9 Role of quorum sensing

Quorum sensing allows bacteria to synchronize their behavior to function socially by excreting and sensing small molecules known as autoinducers (see Section 21). In V. cholerae, quorum sensing negatively regulates biofilm development (Hammer and Basler, 2003; Waters et al., 2008; Zhu and Mekalanos, 2003). At low cell density, the concentration of the CAI1 is low and V. cholerae expresses the regulator AphA (Rutherford et al., 2011), which activates the expression of VpsT (Yang et al., 2010). Expression of VpsT is also activated by the transcription factor VpsR (Srivastava et al., 2011). In addition, cells at low cell density exhibit higher intracellular levels of c-di-GMP (Waters et al., 2008), a condition that enhances the activity of VpsR and VpsT (Shikuma et al., 2012; Srivastava et al., 2011). At high cell density, accumulation of CAI1 in the medium results in the expression of the master quorum-sensing regulator HapR (Jobling and Holmes, 1997; Miller et al., 2002), which negatively affects biofilm formation by lowering c-di-GMP levels (Waters et al., 2008) and by directly repressing the vpsT and aphA promoters (Srivastava et al., 2011; Waters et al., 2008). 

## 22.8.10 Stringent response and cyclic adenosine monophosphate (cAMP)

General nutrient limitation and carbon source availability are major environmental determinants of biofilm development. V. cholerae relA, spoT, and relV mutants that do not synthesize the stringent response mediator guanosine tetra phosphate (ppGpp) (see Section 7) make reduced biofilms due to lower expression of VpsR (He et al., 2012). Nutrient limitation also affects the intracellular concentration of cAMP. We have shown that adenylate cyclase and the cAMP receptor protein (CRP) are required for the biosynthesis of CAI1 (Liang et al., 2007a,b, 2008). Thus, the cell density at which cells enter quorum-sensing mode and make HapR is modulated by carbon source and nutrient limitation. Growth conditions conducive to elevated cellular cAMP enhance quorum sensing (HapR) and negatively affect biofilm formation. Accordingly, mutants lacking adenylate cyclase or CRP expressed an elevated biofilm (Fong and Yildiz, 2008; Liang et al., 2007a).

## 22.8.11 The phosphoenolpyruvate phosphotransferase system

The phosphoenolpyruvate (PEP) phosphotransferase system (PTS) is a phosphoryl cascade that allows the transport and phosphorylation of sugars (Deutscher et al., 2006). The PTS acts as a sensory system to regulate bacterial behavior in response to the availability of sugars in the medium and the energy state of the cell (Lengeler and Jahreis, 2009). In the PTS, transport and phosphorylation of sugars occur at the expense of PEP through the soluble pathway-specific proteins enzyme I (EI) and HPr, and the sugar-specific enzyme II (EII) complexes. The different EII complexes are characterized by their domains A, B, and C. The absence of a transportable sugar in the medium results in the accumulation of phosphorylated forms of the PTS. It has been shown that phospho-EI, phospho-HPr, phospho-FPr, and glucose EIIBC diminish vps transcription, while phospho-EIIA has the opposite effect (Houot and Watnick, 2008; Houot et al., 2010a,b). Recently, it was also shown that the mannitol-specific EII activates biofilm and vps transcription (Ymele-Leki et al., 2013). The mechanism by which components of the PTS modulate vps transcription is not clearly understood. However, these studies suggest that once V. cholerae attaches to a surface, it exploits the PTS to sense the nutritional quality of the substratum to either develop a mature biofilm or detach. 

## 22.8.12 Extracellular phosphate

Inorganic phosphate is a limiting nutrient in freshwater and estuarine ecosystems where Vibrios survive and persist outside the human host. In E. coli, deprivation of phosphate induces the expression of the PhoB regulon (Lamarche et al., 2008). PhoB is part of the PhoR–PhoB two-component regulatory system (see Section 3). PhoR is an inner membrane histidine kinase that responds to periplasmic orthophosphate through its interaction with the phosphate transport system. Under conditions of phosphate limitation, phosphorus is transferred from PhoR to the response regulator PhoB. Then, phospho-PhoB binds to DNA pho boxes to activate or repress the transcription of target genes (Lamarche et al., 2008). V. cholerae PhoB negatively regulates biofilm formation (Pratt et al., 2009, 2010; Sultan et al., 2010). We have shown that expression of VpsR is enhanced in a V. cholerae phoB deletion mutant (Sultan et al., 2010). 

## 22.8.13 Role of the histone-like nucleoid structuring protein

The histone-like nucleoid structuring protein (H-NS) is a nucleoid-associated protein and transcriptional silencer (Ueguchi and Mizuno, 1993). It consists of an N-terminal oligomerization domain connected by a flexible linker to a nucleic acid–binding domain (Cerdan et al., 2003; Nye and Taylor, 2003). Both DNA binding and oligomerization are required for the biological activities of H-NS, which include DNA condensation and the regulation of transcription (Spurio et al., 1997). In transcription regulation, H-NS acts by binding to promoters exhibiting AT-rich curved DNA regions (Dame et al., 2001; Lang et al., 2007; Owen-Hughes et al., 1992). H-NS is a highly abundant protein that is expressed at low and high cell densities. 

As mentioned, VpsT is active at low cell density to activate biofilm formation, and it is repressed by quorum sensing at high cell density. We hypothesized that V. cholerae would require an added layer of regulation to switch between planktonic and biofilm lifestyles at low cell density. We found that H-NS is a repressor of biofilm formation (Wang et al., 2012b). An RNAseq analysis of wild-type and Δhns V. cholerae showed that the hns mutant overexpresses VpsT, operon I and II vps genes, and rbmA (Table 22.8.3). The mutant produces elevated indole – a signaling molecule that positively affects biofilm formation (Mueller et al., 2009) – due to de-repression of tryptophanase (tnaA), and it expresses lower levels of PTS components that negatively affect vps transcription (Table 22.8.3). Electrophoresis mobility shift assays and chromatin immunoprecipitation confirmed that H-NS binds to the vpsT, vpsA, and vpsL promoters (Wang et al., 2012b). 

It is well established that repression by H-NS can be relieved in response to environmental cues that activate the expression of other regulators whose binding site overlaps that of H-NS (Dorman, 2004, 2013; Dorman and Kane, 2009). We thus suggest that expression of VpsT and VpsT-regulated genes at low cell density would require the induction of an anti-repressor to override H-NS transcriptional silencing. Expression of vps genes and biosynthesis of the exopolysaccharide–protein matrix would continue until cells enter quorum-sensing mode and make HapR to terminate vps gene transcription. 

## 22.8.14 Biofilm formation and the general stress response are inversely regulated

The alternative RNA polymerase sigma subunit $( \sigma ^ { \mathrm { S } } )$ encoded by rpoS initiates transcription of numerous genes in response to environmental stresses (Hengge-Aronis, 2002). V. cholerae rpoS mutants are more sensitive to carbon starvation, high osmolarity, and oxidative stresses (Yildiz and Schoolnik, 1998). Therefore, RpoS is believed to be essential for V. cholerae survival in the aquatic environment. In addition, it has been reported that rpoS mutants are less effective in establishing infection in the cholera suckling mouse model (Merrell et al., 2000), suggesting that its expression enhances the capacity of V. cholerae to withstand host-specific stressful conditions. Late in infection, expression of RpoS is required for V. cholerae to detach and exit the host, a process known as mucosal escape (Nielsen et al., 2006). In mucosal escape, RpoS acts by enhancing motility (Nielsen et al., 2006; Silva et al., 2008; Wang et al., 2012a) and activating the expression of HA–protease (Silva and Benitez, 2004). We proposed a model for RpoS activation of motility in the stationary phase (Figure 22.8.1) (Wang et al., 2012a). Basically, transcription of rpoN encoding $\sigma ^ { 5 4 }$ and class I gene flrA are diminished by H-NS (Wang et al., 2012a). The expression of RpoS in the stationary phase enhances rpoN and flrA transcription by two parallel mechanisms (Figure 22.8.1). In mechanism I, RNA polymerase containing $\sigma ^ { \mathrm { S } }$ is more effective in initiating transcription at the rpoN and flrA promoters in the presence of H-NS. It has been recognized that transcription initiation by RNA polymerase containing $\sigma ^ { \mathrm { S } }$ is more resistant to H-NS repression (Shin et al., 2005). In mechanism II, RpoS diminishes H-NS occupancy at the rpoN and flrA promoters indirectly by enhancing the expression of a second nucleoid-associated protein, the integration host factor (IHF). IHF then competes with H-NS for binding to rpoN and flrA promoter DNA (Wang et al., 2012a). Expression of RpoS in the stationary phase also lowers the c-di-GMP pool (Wang et al., 2011), a condition expected to enhance the activity of FlrA.

Figure 22.8.1 A model for RpoS-mediated enhancement of motility. The expression of RpoS in the stationary phase enhances rpoN and flrA transcription by two mechanisms. In mechanism I, RNA polymerase containing σS can initiate rpoN and flrA transcription more effectively in the presence of H–NS. In mechanism II, RpoS enhances the expression of the IHF, which competes with H–NS for binding to promoter DNA.


Contrary to biofilm development, expression of RpoS is enhanced by HapR at high cell density and repressed by c-di-GMP (Wang et al., 2011). Therefore, the general stress response and biofilm formation are inversely regulated cellular processes. Repression of RpoS by c-di-GMP is abrogated by deletion of vpsT (Wang et al., 2014). We have shown that VpsT binds to the rpoS promoter at a position that overlaps one transcription initiation site (Wang et al., 2014). Deletion of vpsT significantly enhanced rpoS expression in V. cholerae biofilms that do not make HapR (Wang et al., 2014). This result suggested that VpsT and c-di-GMP silence the transcription of rpoS in the biofilm prior to cells entering quorum-sensing mode. In Figure 22.8.2, we propose a model for the interplay between HapR and VpsT in the regulation of RpoS expression in planktonic and biofilm cells grown in nutrient-rich media. In V. cholerae planktonic cells cultivated in rich media, the c-di-GMP pool is not sufficiently elevated for VpsT to repress rpoS transcription. Furthermore, in a nutrient-rich medium, the stress conditions required to induce RpoS occur in the stationary phase. At this stage (high cell density), expression of HapR further diminishes the c-di-GMP pool and silences vpsT transcription (Waters et al., 2008). Thus, VpsT plays a minor role in rpoS regulation in planktonic cells under these growth conditions (Figure 22.8.2a). In this case, HapR enhances RpoS expression by a mechanism independent of VpsT. We propose that cells in the biofilm exhibit higher intracellular c-di-GMP levels and are subject to stress conditions, resulting in earlier expression of RpoS. As a result, VpsT could play a major negative role in rpoS expression by binding to the rpoS promoter and inhibiting transcription initiation prior to expression of HapR (Figure 22.8.2b). As the cell density in the biofilm increases, HapR represses vpsT transcription and relieves rpoS from VpsT repression. We propose that at this stage, expression of RpoS in the biofilm enhances motility and

## 22.8.15 Conclusions

Switching between motile and biofilm lifestyles in response to environmental cues plays a fundamental role in V. cholerae survival in the environment, pathogenesis, and disease transmission. V. cholerae develops biofilms in response to multiple environmental signals, which converge in regulating the intracellular concentration of c-di-GMP. Detachment and return to the planktonic lifestyle involve an interplay between multiple global regulators, mainly CRP, H-NS, HapR, VpsT, and the general stress reponse regulator RpoS.

## 22.8.16 Open questions for future research



Fong JC, Yildiz FT. 2007. The rbmBCDEF gene cluster modulates development of rugose colony morphology and biofilm formation in Vibrio cholerae. Journal of Bacteriology 189(6): 2319–2330.
