Biofilm is a young field of research in which the literature is growing at a formidable rate. Certain aspects of biofilm development remain totally unexplored, such as the mechanics and bioenergetics of bacteria interaction with surfaces, liquid–air interfaces, or each other. A generalization of many findings continues to be difficult due to the use of different strains, different culture conditions, and different experimental setups. 



Fong JC, Yildiz FH. 2008. Interplay between cyclic AMP-cyclic AMP receptor protein and cyclic di-GMP signaling in Vibrio cholerae biofilm formation. Journal of Bacteriology 190(20): 6646–6659. 





Lee SH, Hava DL, Waldor MK, Camilli A. 1999. Regulation and temporal expression patterns of Vibrio cholerae virulence genes during infection. Cell 99(6): 625–634. 





Silva AJ, Leitch GJ, Camilli A, Benitez JA. 2006. Contribution of hemagglutinin/protease and motility to the pathogenesis of El Tor biotype cholera. Infection and Immunity 74(4): 2072–2079. 



For instance, there is growing evidence that the regulatory mechanisms controlling V. cholerae biofilm development differ under static and dynamic conditions (Seper et al., 2014). V. cholerae can form vpsA-independent biofilms under flow conditions (Muller¨ et al., 2007). This prompts us to wonder: What are the determinants of biofilms that develop during infection, and how do these biofilms affect the host response to cholera? Studies on the regulation of biofilm formation have mainly centered at the level of transcription. A recent study suggested that the biosynthesis of the biofilm matrix is also controlled at the level of translation through the intervention of small RNAs (Song et al. 2014; see Section 5). Finally, V. cholerae biofilms formed in nature are likely to be heterogeneous. What roles do these biofilms play in pathogen evolution, and how do biofilms further differentiate into viable but nonculturable cells? Solutions to the above unknowns should bring the disease cholera closer to eradication.

# Outer membrane vesicle secretion: from envelope stress to biofilm formation

Thomas Baumgarten1 and Hermann J. Heipieper2 

1Center for Biomembrane Research, Department of Biochemistry and Biophysics, Stockholm University, Stockholm, Sweden 2Department Environmental Biotechnology, Helmholtz Centre for Environmental Research – UFZ, Leipzig, Germany 

## 22.9.1 Introduction

Many soil bacteria are capable of degrading pollutants like organic solvents. To cope with the toxicity of hydrocarbons and other forms of environmental stresses, these bacteria have developed adaptive response mechanisms. As one mechanism, bacterial cells can change the physicochemical surface properties of their cell envelope (see Section 18). These changes include the cell surface hydrophobicity, which can be monitored by changes in the water contact angle. The solvent-tolerant bacterium Pseudomonas putida DOT-T1E changes its water contact angle in the presence of toxic concentrations of 1-decanol (Neumann et al., 2006). Interestingly, this increase of the cell hydrophobicity is dramatic and fast. This adaptation of the surface properties of the bacterium to the presence of organic solvents appears to be very similar to previously reported responses observed in Pseudomonas aeruginosa. This bacterium infects the human lung tissue in patients suffering from cystic fibrosis. During infection, P. aeruginosa secretes outer membrane vesicles (OMVs) containing high concentrations of certain lipopolysaccharides (LPSs) and virulence factors (Makin and Beveridge, 1996a; Mashburn-Warren and Whiteley, 2006). The increased secretion of these vesicles has previously been associated with the presence of different stressors (heat shock, antibiotics, or low oxygen content) and biofilm formation (Deatherage et al., 2009; Kadurugamuwa and Beveridge, 1995; Makin and Beveridge, 1996a; Sabra et al., 2003; Schooling and Beveridge, 2006). 

In the present study, we performed a systematic investigation on the effect of toxic concentrations of n-alkanols and chlorophenols on the cell surface properties of P. putida DOT-T1E. These two groups of solvents were chosen as they represent commonly occurring environmental pollutants. Additionally, the toxicity of these compounds on P. putida and its response are well studied (Kabelitz et al., 2003; Loffler et al., 2010; Neumann et al., 2005). Surprisingly, only long-chain alkanols caused a dramatic and very fast increase in surface hydrophobicity, whereas toxic concentrations of chlorophenols had no comparable effect. Furthermore, other cell surface–affecting conditions (heat shock, or high-NaCl or high-EDTA concentrations) caused an increase in cell surface hydrophobicity similar to that of long-chain alcohols. We could show that, under all these conditions, OMVs were released. The released vesicles were analyzed by electron microscopy, dynamic light scattering, mass spectrometry, and fatty acid methyl ester gas chromatography. Notably, the stressed, more hydrophobic cells showed elevated levels of biofilm formation. 

## 22.9.2 Materials and methods

Culture conditions: P. putida DOT-T1E (CECT 5312) (Ramos et al., 1995) was cultivated in mineral medium as described by Hartmans et al. (1989) with 4 g/L disodium succinate as both carbon and energy source. Cells were grown in 50 mL shake cultures at 30 ◦C in a horizontally shaking water bath at 180 rpm. Then, either the stressor was added (final concentrations: 1-decanol, 10 % (v/v); 1-octanol, 1.25 mM; NaCl, 2 M; and EDTA, 10 mM) or the temperature was increased. Cells were harvested by centrifugation for 10 min at 6000×g at the time points indicated. The pelleted cells were washed and finally resuspended in 10 mM KNO solution for further analysis. The supernatant was used to isolate membrane vesicles as described before (Kadurugamuwa et al., 1993). 

Cell surface analysis: The bacterial cell surface was analyzed as described previously (van Loosdrecht et al., 1987). Bacterial lawns needed for contact angle (θW) measurements were prepared by collecting cell suspensions in 10 mM $\mathrm { K N O } _ { 3 }$ on 0.45-μm-pore-size micropore filters (Schleicher & Schuell, 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/1360f8320d7718b454faaead9a21759c2dfccf74920a92be31c801109b8b461a.jpg)


![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/1e7bf294f8c917be390ec4751ba748803acbe69ae4ab4fc2a84bf904227d922a.jpg)



Figure 22.9.1 (a,b) Surface properties of P. putida DOT-T1E grown in a bioreactor in the presence or absence of 10% (vol./vol.) 1-Decanol: (a) contact angle; and (b) zeta potential.


Germany), mounting the filters on glass slides, and drying them for 2 h at room temperature. Cells exposed to the solvents were washed six times with 10 mM $\mathrm { K N O } _ { 3 }$ in order to guarantee the measurement of the cell properties and concomitantly to avoid measurement interferences by the physicochemical effect of the solvents adhering to or accumulating in the cells. Cell surface hydrophobicities were derived from $\theta _ { \mathrm { w } }$ of water drops on the bacterial lawns using a Kruss drop shape analysis system DSA 100 (Kr¨ uss GmbH,¨ Germany) (van Loosdrecht et al., 1987). 

Cryo–transmission electron microscopy (TEM): One droplet of the isolated MV was applied to a copper grid covered by holey poly-L-lysine-coated carbon film (Quantifoil R3.5/1 Micro Tools, Germany), and excess of liquid was blotted automatically for 1 s between two strips of filter paper. Subsequently, the samples were rapidly plunged into liquid ethane (cooled $\mathrm { t o } - 1 8 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } )$ in a cryobox (Carl Zeiss NTS GmbH, Germany). Excess ethane was removed with a piece of filter paper. The samples were transferred with a cryo-transfer unit (Gatan 626-DH) into the precooled cryo-electron microscope (Philips CM 120, The Netherlands) operated at 120 kV and viewed under low-dose conditions. The images were recorded with a 1k CCD camera (FastScan F114, TVIPS, Germany). 

Biofilm formation assay: The quantification of the biofilm formation was performed according to O’Toole and Kolter (1998). A sample of 1 ml of the stressed cells was adjusted to an $\mathrm { O D } _ { 5 6 0 }$ of 0.05 and incubated on polystyrene microtiter plates (Multiwell Primaria, VWR International GmbH, Germany) at $2 8 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 18 h. Then, 250 μl of a 0.1% solution of crystal violet (CV) was added, and the plates were incubated for an additional 30 min at room temperature. Subsequently, the supernatant was removed, and each well was washed three times with distilled water. After the plates were dried, 1 ml of ethanol was added twice to remove the bound CV, and the absorption was determined at 590 nm. 

## 22.9.3 Results and discussion

P. putida DOT-T1E is known to tolerate high concentrations of organic solvents, but only little information has been available about the physiochemical adaptation on the cell surface of this strain. Therefore, P. putida DOT-T1E was grown in the presence of 10% (v/v) 1-decanol, and the water contact angle was measured. Interestingly, P. putida DOT-T1E showed a rapid and dramatic increase in the cell surface hydrophobicity upon addition of the organic solvent (Figure 22.9.1a,b). 

To check if this increase was derived from the simple attachment of the solvent to the cell surface, cells were killed with $\mathrm { H g C l } _ { 2 }$ 30 min before exposure to the organic solvent. This treatment did not cause any change of the cell surface hydrophobicity. Immediately after adding the 1-decanol, the dead cells showed a moderate increase of the water contact angle. This response was faster than that observed for the living cells (Figure 22.9.2). 

Therefore, it can be excluded that the increase in cell surface hydrophobicity of P. putida DOT-T1E appears only due to the physiochemical interaction and adsorption of the solvent to the cell surface. 

To test if this response is unique to 1-decanol, P. putida DOT-T1E was incubated with 1-alcohols of increasing chain length, and the water contact angle was measured (Table 22.9.1). With increasing chain length of the alcohol, the toxicity increased as well as the cell surface hydrophobicity. 

Strikingly, no or only a mild response was observed for 2,4-dichlorophenol, although this compound has a similar hydrophobicity and toxicity. 

It could be speculated that the difference of the molecular size of the different stressors causes a deeper penetration into the membrane, leading to a stronger signal to increase cell surface hydrophobicity (Baumgarten et al., 2012). This hypothesis would explain the mild response of 2,4-dichlorophenol because this compound could be too small to trigger a cellular response in the membrane. On the other hand, 2,4-dichlorophenol could potentially inhibit a signaling mechanism that would lead to the increased cell surface hydrophobicity. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/bc9c1daae93abd19544a337219da69d7701d32ee0f6cba5929a84f211ebde89f.jpg)



Figure 22.9.2 Effect of pretreatment of P. putida DOT-T1E with toxic concentrations of HgCl . Contact angle of living ( ) and dead cells ( ).


In summary, P. putida DOT-T1E responds to 1-alcohols of increasing chain length with a fast increase in the cell surface hydrophobicity. This response could not be detected using chlorophenols. 

So what mechanism underlies the increased cell surface hydrophobicity? The cell surface of all Gram-negative bacteria, including P. putida DOT-T1E, consists of an outer membrane. The outer leaflet of this outer membrane contains a lipopolysaccharide (LPS) layer, which is exposed to the environment. In order to test whether the observed increase in the cell surface hydrophobicity in response to 1-alcohols leads to an alteration in the LPS layer, the LPS composition was analyzed by micellar electrokinetic chromatography (MEKC). Control cells showed a high-molecular-weight signal, and this signal shifts toward lower molecular weight with increasing cell surface hydrophobicity (Baumgarten et al., 2012). 

In keeping with this observation, it has been reported that P. putida Idaho changes its LPS composition in the presence of o-xylene in a similar way (Pinkart et al., 1996). To test if the increased cell surface hydrophobicity correlated with the loss of LPS is a reversible process, P. putida DOT-T1E was incubated with 1-octanol. As expected, a rapid increase of the cell surface hydrophobicity was observed. After 30 min, the cells were pelleted, washed, and resuspended in fresh medium without 1-octanol. Over a long time period of 7 h, the cell surface hydrophobicity decreased gradually to pre-stress levels (Figure 22.9.3a). This observation points toward a slow recovery of the high-molecular-weight LPS layer, probably due to de novo biosynthesis. For the extensively studied pathogen P. aeruginosa, different studies showed that the LPS composition changes rapidly in response to heat shock or membrane intercalating antibiotics (Kadurugamuwa and Beveridge, 1995; Makin and Beveridge, 1996a). The connection between an increased cell surface hydrophobicity and an alteration in the LPS composition leads us to speculate that both membrane and LPS destabilizing conditions would trigger a response increasing the cell surface hydrophobicity. Therefore, we tested increased temperatures (membrane destabilizing) and high salt concentrations as well as EDTA for causing a change in the surface water contact angle of P. putida DOT-T1E. Indeed, for all conditions tested, an increased cell surface hydrophobicity could be observed (Figure 22.9.3b,c). 


Table 22.9.1 Hydrophobicities (logP), toxicities (EC50) of organic compounds and their effects on the contact angle of living (−HgCl ) and dead (+HgCl ) cells of P. putida DOT-T1E.


<table><tr><td rowspan="2">Compound</td><td rowspan="2">logP</td><td rowspan="2">EC50 [mM]</td><td colspan="2">Δ contact angle</td></tr><tr><td><eq>-HgCl_2</eq></td><td><eq>+HgCl_2</eq></td></tr><tr><td>1-Butanol</td><td>0.89</td><td>49</td><td>10.2</td><td>3.6</td></tr><tr><td>1-Hexanol</td><td>1.87</td><td>6.35</td><td>23.8</td><td>13.3</td></tr><tr><td>1-Heptanol</td><td>2.41</td><td>3.6</td><td>45.2</td><td>15.3</td></tr><tr><td>1-Octanol</td><td>2.92</td><td>0.8</td><td>54.3</td><td>20</td></tr><tr><td>1-Nonanol</td><td>3.45</td><td>0.31</td><td>64.1</td><td>20.2</td></tr><tr><td>1-Decanol</td><td>3.97</td><td>0.11</td><td>65</td><td>21.4</td></tr><tr><td>2,4-Dichlorophenol</td><td>2.4</td><td>0.96</td><td>14.2</td><td>5.2</td></tr></table>

Interestingly, for P. aeruginosa, the loss of the highmolecular-weight LPS could be linked to the formation of OMVs (Kadurugamuwa and Beveridge, 1995; Makin and Beveridge, 1996a). Hence, we tested if P. putida DOT-T1E releases OMVs under the described stress conditions. Electron microscopy and dynamic light scattering confirmed the secretion of OMVs (Baumgarten et al., 2013). Notably, vesicles derived from cells treated with high salt concentrations or EDTA appeared to be smaller (Figure 22.9.4a–d). So far, it has become clear that P. putida DOT-T1E responds to a membrane destabilizing stressor by releasing OMV containing high-molecular-weight LPS. This rapid formation of OMV leads to a dramatic increase in the cell surface hydrophobicity. 

The physiological importance of OMV formation is well described (Kulp and Kuehn, 2010; Mashburn-Warren et al., 2008b; Mashburn-Warren and Whiteley, 2006). It has been shown that P. putida releases OMVs in response to toluene and that the vesicles would function as a system to transport the toxic solvent away from the cell (Kobayashi et al., 2000).

Figure 22.9.3 (a–c) Surface properties of P. putida DOT-T1E in response to treatment with 1-Octanol, heat shock, NaCl, and EDTA. (a) Decrease of the contact angle of P. putida DOT-T1E after short incubation with 1-Octanol. (b) Increase of the contact angle of P. putida DOT-T1E after incubation with 1-Octanol, heat shock, NaCl, and EDTA; and (c) the kinetics of these changes.


The mechanism of OMV formation has been a subject of intensive studies. In P. aeruginosa, it was shown that PQS interacts specifically with outer membrane lipids and triggers the formation of OMV (Mashburn-Warren et al., 2008a; Pesci et al., 1999; Tashiro et al., 2010). So far, for P. putida only, the precursor of PQS HHQ has been identified (Diggle et al., 2006; Dos Santos et al., 2004), but it seems reasonable that PQS or a similar signaling molecule causes the formation of OMVs in P. putida. Kinetic studies from our lab and others imply that the regulatory system for the formation of OMVs is most probably continuously expressed and therefore allosterically regulated (Baumgarten et al., 2013). However, the enzyme responsible for the conversion of HHQ into PQS has not been identified yet. 

In previous studies using P. aeruginosa, a relation between the secretion of OMVs and the formation of biofilms was assumed (Beveridge et al., 1997; Schooling and Beveridge, 2006), and one could argue that a more hydrophobic cell surface would allow an easy adhesion of cells to both each other and a hydrophobic surface. To test this hypothesis, stressed cells of P. putida DOT-T1E were incubated in polystyrene microtiter plates, and biofilm formation was quantified. Indeed, all conditions causing an increased cell surface hydrophobicity led to elevated levels of biofilm formation (Figure 22.9.4e).

Kadurugamuwa JL, Lam JS, Beveridge TJ. 1993. Interaction of gentamicin with the A band and B band lipopolysaccharides of Pseudomonas aeruginosa and its possible lethal effect. Antimicrobial Agents Chemotherapy 37(4): 715. 



In conclusion, P. putida DOT-T1E responds to an envelope stressor by increasing its cell surface hydrophobicity via the release of OMVs. This process facilitates the adhesion of cells to hydrophobic surfaces, leading to increased biofilm formation (Figure 22.9.5). Taking into account the rising number of reports about OMV formation in Gram-negative bacteria, it seems likely that this process is highly conserved (Chen et al., 2014; McBroom et al., 2006; Pierce et al., 2014).

## Viable but nonculturable (VBNC) cells

# Resuscitation of vibrios from the viable but nonculturable state is induced by quorum-sensing molecules

Mesrop Ayrapetyan1, Tiffany C. Williams1, and James D. Oliver1,2 

1Department of Biology, The University of North Carolina at Charlotte, Charlotte, North Carolina, USA 2Nicholas School of the Environment, Duke University Marine Laboratory, Beaufort, North Carolina, USA 

## 23.1.1 Introduction

After its discovery in 1982 (Xu et al., 1982), the viable but nonculturable (VBNC) state is now accepted as a dormancy phenomenon utilized by at least 85 species of bacteria (Li et al., 2014) in their effort to circumvent damaging environmental instability (Oliver, 2010). As the name suggests, VBNC cells fail to grow on routine laboratory media; however, they are viable, as evidenced by possession of intact cell membranes and continued gene expression, and maintain the ability to return to an active state when the stress is alleviated (Oliver, 2005a). Therefore, VBNC cells evade not only environmental stressors but also detection, thereby posing a major public health concern (Fakruddin et al., 2013). 

The VBNC state can be induced by a variety of stressors such as starvation, growth-inhibiting temperatures, low oxygen, nonoptimal salinity (see Section 11), and stressful pH (see Section 16) (Li et al., 2014). However, once in the VBNC state, cells can better withstand normally fatal stressors similar to persister cells, such as prolonged antibiotic treatment (Maisonneuve and Gerdes, 2014) as well as other potentially lethal environmental factors (Nowakowska and Oliver, 2013). Several studies have also demonstrated that VBNC cells are capable of resuscitating in vivo and maintaining their pathogenic potential (Baffone et al., 2003, 2006; Oliver, 2010; Oliver and Bockian, 1995; Rivers and Steck, 2001). Since these cells are unharmed by conventional antibiotics and can resuscitate within the host, it is very likely that they contribute to recurrent infections such as those seen with Mycobacterium tuberculosis and Helicobacter pylori (Oliver, 2010). 

While VBNC cells likely play a role in human disease, their ability to suppress metabolic activity for a chance to survive in harmful conditions has likely evolved for ecological purposes rather than to evade antibiotic treatment. In the environment, the VBNC state is associated with the seasonality of Vibrio spp. abundance. Culturability decreases during coldweather months, during which time some cells enter the VBNC state, and increases during warmer months when cells begin to resuscitate and grow (Oliver and Bockian, 1995). Although there is an abundance of literature on the formation of VBNC cells and their physiological status, very little is known about the mechanisms used by these cells to sense environmental permissiveness and to exit this dormancy state. The elucidation of resuscitation mechanisms used by bacteria will allow for the development of improved detection methods. 

Vibrio vulnificus, a pathogen responsible for 95% of seafoodrelated deaths in the United States (Oliver et al., 2001), is a model organism for investigating the VBNC state. This pathogen produces human infection after the ingestion of raw or undercooked oysters (Oliver, 2006) or by entry into open wounds (Oliver, 2005b), being especially harmful to individuals with liver disease or immunodeficiency (Jones and Oliver, 2009). Although dormancy dynamics can be complex, the VBNC state of V. vulnificus is easily studied in vitro using low temperature for induction of dormancy and resuscitation by a simple temperature upshift (Oliver, 2005). 

We have previously found that, upon addition of exogenous V. vulnificus or E. coli cells to oysters, the culturability of the normal flora of Vibrio spp. within these oysters dramatically increased and remained high even after the added bacterial populations had been purged by the oysters (Froelich and Oliver, 2013). This suggested that preexisting and naturally present VBNC populations within these oysters resuscitated in response to the added metabolically active bacteria. However, it was not clear whether direct cell–cell interactions or some type of signaling induced this resuscitation. In a similar finding, Bari et al. (2013) observed enhanced culturability of Vibrio cholerae when environmental water samples were enriched with the quorum-sensing molecules, CAI1 and AI2. 

Quorum sensing is the production of and response to signals (autoinducers) generated by bacteria of the same or other species in an effort to gauge the population density in a certain environment or to evaluate their environmental location (Bassler, 1999) (see Section 21). Quorum sensing leads to global changes in gene expression (Withers et al., 2001) and has been shown to orchestrate virulence (Antunes et al., 2010) and fitness factor production (McDougald et al., 2003). For a thorough review on quorum sensing, please see Rutherford and Bassler (2012; see Section 21). Interestingly, some environmental factors that affect quorum sensing, such as temperature, salinity, and pH, have independently been shown to play a role in VBNC dynamics (Cunningham et al., 2009; Li et al., 2014; Oliver, 2005a,b), suggesting that the two processes may be interconnected. Given these findings, we asked whether quorum-sensing autoinducers mediate the resuscitation of VBNC cells. 

## 23.1.2 Materials and methods

## 23.1.2.1 Strains and growth conditions

Strains used in this study were Vibrio vulnificus: CMCP6, C7184, JDO1, and AH1; V. parahaemolyticus: Sak-11; V. harveyi BB170; and E. coli: K12. Strains were stored $\mathrm { a t } - 8 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ in BactoTM Luria broth (LB) (BD, USA) containing 20% glycerol, and grown in BactoTM Heart Infusion (HI) broth (BD) for 24 h at $3 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ with aeration. Strains JDO1 and AH1 are luxS and rpoS mutants, respectively, and were derived from parent strain C7184. JDO1 is unable to produce 4,5-dihydroxy-2,3-pentanedione (DPD), an essential precursor to the autoinducer AI2 (Beam, 2004), and AH1 is unable to produce the stress-related sigma factor rpoS (Hulsmann et al., 2003). JDO1 and AH1 were grown with additions of 2 and 15 μg/ml chloramphenicol, respectively. All bioluminescence quorumsensing assays were performed using autoinducer bioassay (AB) medium (Greenberg et al., 1979). 

## 23.1.2.2 Resuscitation of dormant cells in oysters

Oysters (Crassostrea virginica) were collected from the North Carolina (USA) coast during September 2012. Oysters were maintained in aerated aquaria (30 L) containing 20‰ artificial seawater (ASW) (Instant Ocean; Aquarium Systems, USA) and were fed Phyto-Feast algal culture daily (Reed Mariculture, USA). Cell-free supernatant (CFS) was obtained from overnight cultures of V. vulnificus C7184 and JDO1 by filter sterilization (0.22 μm filter; Millipore, Ireland). To confirm sterility of CFS, aliquots were spread plated onto HI agar. Oysters were placed in 3 L tanks (four oysters per treatment condition) of 20‰ ASW with aeration. CFS was added to oyster tanks at a 1:100 v:v ratio. After 24 h, oysters were shucked using a sterile knife and homogenized in 20‰ ASW (1:1 w:v ratio) using sterile blender cups (Waring, USA). Individual oyster homogenates were serially diluted in phosphate-buffered saline (PBS) and plated onto ChromAgar Vibrio (ChromAgar, France). ChromAgar Vibrio was employed to quantify total vibrios as it has been previously demonstrated that this medium indiscriminately allows for growth of all Vibrio species tested (Williams et al., 2013). Oysters that were incubated with CFS were compared to control oysters that were supplemented with only ASW. As an additional control, some oysters were supplemented with HI broth to ensure that nutrient carryover had no effect on Vibrio levels. 

## 23.1.2.3 Production and resuscitation of in vitro VBNC cells

VBNC cultures were prepared as described in Whitesides and Oliver (1997). Briefly, V. vulnificus cells were grown overnight in HI broth, then added to fresh HI broth at a 1:100 v:v ratio and grown to the logarithmic phase $\mathrm { ( O D } _ { 6 1 0 } \ 0 . 1 5 \mathrm { - } 0 . 2 5 \mathrm { ) }$ . Cells were subsequently washed twice with 15‰ ASW to remove nutrient, resuspended in 15‰ ASW at a 1:100 v:v ratio, and stored statically at $4 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ . Cultures were quantified daily until V. vulnificus was no longer culturable on HI agar (<10 CFU/ml). 

To resuscitate VBNC cells, cultures were transferred from the $4 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ incubator to room temperature $( 2 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } )$ . Cultures were immediately supplemented with nutrient-free CFS (collected as described above) from V. vulnificus, V. parahaemolyticus, or E. coli, all of which are known to produce AI2 (see Chapter 21.5), at a 1:100 v:v ratio, or were supplemented with 15‰ ASW at a 1:100 v:v ratio as a control. Additionally, we utilized nutrientfree CFS from a V. vulnificus luxS mutant strain (JDO1) unable to produce AI2. These mixtures were serially diluted in PBS and plated at regular intervals onto HI agar to monitor resuscitation. Resuscitation curves were compared to control resuscitation cultures that received ASW. Three biological replicates were used for each treatment. 

## 23.1.2.4 Use of synthetic autoinducer to induce resuscitation

VBNC cultures were prepared as described here. The synthetic autoinducer, (s)-4,5-dihydroxy-2,3-pentanedione (DPD) (OMM Scientific, USA), spontaneously forms the quorumsensing molecule AI2 in liquid suspension (Tsuchikama et al., 2011). AI2 was prepared by adding a 10-fold molar excess boric acid to DPD, making a 50 μM DPD stock solution. To resuscitate VBNC cells, dormant cultures of C7184, JDO1, and AH1 were moved to $2 0 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ and AI2 was immediately added to each culture at a final concentration of 250 nM. Cultures were serially diluted and plated onto HI agar at regular intervals to monitor resuscitation. Resuscitation times were compared to control resuscitation cultures that received only ASW. Experiments were performed in triplicate. 

## 23.1.2.5 Use of quorum-sensing inhibitor to prevent resuscitation

Cinnamaldehyde is a quorum-sensing inhibitor that prevents binding of LuxR, the master regulator of quorum sensing, to 

DNA (Brackman et al., 2008). We used a cinnamaldehyde concentration of 150 $\mu \mathrm { M } ,$ , which is noninhibitory to growth as previously reported (Brackman et al., 2008). The cinnamaldehydesupplemented VBNC cultures (prepared as described here) were allowed to resuscitate by incubation at $2 0 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ . Cultures were serially diluted and plated onto HI agar at regular intervals to monitor resuscitation. Resuscitation curves were compared to control resuscitation cultures that received an equal volume of ASW. Experiments were performed in triplicate. 

## 23.1.2.6 Quorum-sensing bioluminescence assay

The quorum-sensing bioluminescence assay was performed as described in Bassler et al. (1997), with slight modifications. Briefly, V. harveyi strain BB170, which is bioluminescent only in response to AI2 signals, was grown overnight on AB agar at $3 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ Cells were then removed and diluted in AB broth to an $\mathrm { O D } _ { 6 1 0 }$ of 0.7–0.8, then incubated with shaking at $3 0 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ until the $\mathrm { O D } _ { 6 1 0 }$ reached 1.1. A 1:5000 dilution of this culture into fresh AB broth was prepared to create the working suspension. Nutrientfree CFS was added to the working solution at a 1:10 v:v ratio to a total of 1.5 ml and incubated at $3 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ with shaking for 4 h. After incubation, 200 μL of sample was transferred to a flatbottomed 96-well plate and read using a GloMax 96 Microplate Luminometer (Promega, USA). Background luminescence was assayed by quantifying control samples containing 15‰ ASW and the working solution of BB170 at a 1:10 v:v ratio, for a total of 1.5 ml. Data are presented as sample luminescence (arbitrary units) with the average background luminescence subtracted. Experiments were performed in triplicate. 

## 23.1.2.7 Statistical analysis

Data were analyzed using an unpaired Student t-test, or a oneway analysis of variance (ANOVA) followed by Tukey’s post-hoc test for multiple comparisons. Statistical significance was determined using an alpha level of 0.05. Error bars in all figures represent standard deviations. All data were analyzed using GraphPad Prism (version 6.0; GraphPad Software Inc., USA). 

## 23.1.3 Results and discussion

## 23.1.3.1 Addition of CFS resuscitates dormant vibrios in oysters

When live oysters were exposed to CFS from AI2-producing (see Chapter 21.5) V. vulnificus cultures, the culturability of Vibrio spp. harbored by these oysters increased significantly (Figure 23.1.1), similar to the increases observed after the addition of live bacterial cultures to oysters as reported by Froelich and Oliver (2013). This finding indicated that an extracellular signaling factor promoted resuscitation, as opposed to direct cell–cell interactions. Conversely, when CFS from a mutant lacking the ability to produce AI2 was added to oysters, a similar increase was not observed (Figure 23.1.1), suggesting that the resuscitationpromoting factor was likely the interspecies autoinducer, AI2. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/69179c809d3c4a60a7c91714567a2080b091f2e7ef5922fc45e8f23a77a0dd44.jpg)



Figure 23.1.1 Increase in Vibrio spp. culturability in oysters upon addition of CFS. Log fold change in culturability of Vibrio spp. in oyster tissues after 24 h of treatment with CFS from V. vulnificus C7184 (parent) or JDO1 (luxS mutant) relative to control oysters that received artificial seawater (ASW). Wild-type CFS significantly increased culturability of Vibrio spp. in oysters (one-way ANOVA, p < 0.0001), whereas CFS from the luxS mutant did not. Since HI broth had little effect on culturability, nutrient addition did not affect the culturability of Vibrio spp. Error bars represent SD of four oysters.


## 23.1.3.2 CFS and AI2 induce rapid resuscitation of in vitro VBNC cultures

Since AI2 induced resuscitation of VBNC cells in oysters, we tested its ability to resuscitate in vitro VBNC cultures. Resuscitation of V. vulnificus is usually observed approximately 7– 9 h after transferring VBNC cultures from 4 to $2 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ (Whitesides and Oliver, 1997). We hypothesized that the addition of AI2 to VBNC cultures immediately after a temperature upshift would induce resuscitation much earlier. When VBNC cultures were allowed to resuscitate in the presence of CFS from AI2- producing bacteria (V. vulnificus, V. parahaemolyticus, and E. coli), resuscitation was observed in as little as 3 h after temperature upshift (Figure 23.1.2a). However, addition of CFS from a V. vulnificus luxS mutant unable to produce AI2 was unable to produce an earlier resuscitation (Figure 23.1.2a). To rule out the effect of any other component of CFS on the resuscitation of VBNC cells, we tested the ability of synthetic AI2 to induce early resuscitation in in vitro VBNC samples. The addition of synthetic AI2 resuscitated VBNC cultures in 3–4 h after temperature upshift (Figure 23.1.2a), indicating that AI2 is the main resuscitation-promoting factor in CFS. 

## 23.1.3.3 Inhibition of quorum sensing delays resuscitation

To test whether the master quorum-sensing regulator, SmcR (a LuxR homolog) (Bassler et al., 1997), is required for the AI2- mediated resuscitation we observed, we employed a quorumsensing inhibitor that obstructs the downstream effects of LuxR activity by preventing LuxR from binding to DNA. The LuxR inhibitor, cinnamaldehyde, delayed resuscitation of VBNC cells by at least 40 h (Figure 23.1.2b). This indicates that the induction of resuscitation by AI2 is mediated at least partially by the classical quorum-sensing regulatory pathways. This finding also indicates that the inhibition of quorum sensing is a viable method to inhibit the resuscitation process. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/c1e3840a005ffc55238255b9e9bfd70bd95dbce0b5f6cc541a2be438bc96fec9.jpg)


![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/e41ecd94871d69cc600bbe6c0cf3ece80b5334702e1722338acfcbd25d0c238d.jpg)



Figure 23.1.2 Resuscitation of VBNC V. vulnificus supplemented with CFS, synthetic AI2, or cinnamaldehyde. (a) Culturability of V. vulnificus after temperature upshift supplemented with CFS from V. vulnificus C7184 (parent; closed circle), JDO1 (luxS mutant; open circle), V. parahaemolyticus (closed square), E. coli (closed triangle), or synthetic AI2 (closed downward triangle). Control cultures (open diamond) received an equal volume of ASW. All wild-type CFS and synthetic AI2 induced early resuscitation of VBNC cells, while CFS from the luxS mutant did not. Error bars represent SD of three replicates. (b) Culturability of V. vulnificus after a temperature upshift, with (closed circles) and without (open circles) 150 μM cinnamaldehyde. Error bars represent SD of three replicates.


## 23.1.3.4 Natural resuscitation occurs in response to increased AI2

Since VBNC cultures artificially supplemented with autoinducers promoted resuscitation, and because VBNC cultures of V. vulnificus can resuscitate without the addition of autoinducer, we speculated that this quorum-sensing-mediated process might also occur naturally during a temperature upshift. To test this, we simultaneously monitored the production of AI2 and the culturability of cells during resuscitation from the VBNC state. During resuscitation, there was a rapid increase in AI2 levels, reaching a maximum value at 5 h after temperature upshift even though culturability was not yet detected by this time (Figure 23.1.3). Interestingly, in repeated studies, resuscitation was detected only after AI2 reached such a maximum independent of the temporal occurrence of the maximum, suggesting that AI2 levels must reach a threshold value before resuscitation can begin. We questioned, however, how AI2 levels were able to increase before resuscitation could be detected following the temperature upshift. We suggest either that VBNC cells are able to produce AI2 before they become culturable or that a small still-undetected population is signaling to the dormant population to initiate resuscitation. A recent study by Buerger et al. (2012) provided evidence for Epstein’s (Epstein, 2009) microbial “scout” hypothesis, which proposes that a small portion of a dormant population resuscitates stochastically (becomes “scouts”) and independent of environmental signals. If environmental conditions are not permissive, these scouts are thought to die. However, if acceptable environmental conditions are sensed, these scouts would signal to their dormant counterparts that growth-permissive conditions have been met (Buerger et al., 2012). We propose that, at least for V. vulnificus and likely other bacteria, quorum-sensing autoinducers are the signals employed by these scouts to rouse the rest of the dormant population. This hypothesis is substantiated by our observed initial increase in AI2 production without detectable culturability, indicating that cells that are below our detection limits have stochastically resuscitated and are signaling to the rest of the population, via quorum sensing, that acquiescent conditions have been reached. Since we have shown in our studies that resuscitation in response to autoinducers is not species specific, it appears that metabolically active cells may function as scouts for a dormant population of a different genus or species through AI2 signaling. To confirm that resuscitation during a temperature upshift occurs in response to AI2, we tested the ability of VBNC cultures of the V. vulnificus luxS mutant to resuscitate. The mutant was unable to resuscitate from the VBNC state during a temperature upshift unless the culture was supplemented with AI2, indicating again that AI2 is required for resuscitation to occur in V. vulnificus (Figure 23.1.4a).

Figure 23.1.3 Production of AI2 during temperature upshift–induced resuscitation from the VBNC state. Depicted is culturability of V. vulnificus cells (black bars) as they resuscitated from the VBNC state, with corresponding AI2 levels (gray curve) in these cultures as measured by the AI2 bioluminescence assay.



(a)


![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/46c9f2aa48480bba23657ec08a2abc0d5b59809e9c2dc6592e92b4032e35a392.jpg)



(b)


![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/c09c8bb5926772aff0589d0613a69d8dc690e8970400f8cbf13a720206ed166f.jpg)



Figure 23.1.4 Resuscitation of V. vulnificus luxS and rpoS mutants during temperature upshift. (a) Culturability of luxS mutant (JDO1; closed circles), rpoS mutant (AH1; closed diamonds), and parent strain (C7184; open squares) after a resuscitation-inducing temperature upshift. JDO1 was unable to resuscitate within 24 h unless supplemented with exogenous AI2 (open circle), indicating the requirement of AI2 for the resuscitation of VBNC V. vulnificus. AH1 displayed a delayed ability to resuscitate, likely due to decreased survival at $4 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ . Importantly, resuscitation of AH1 was not affected by the addition of AI2 (open diamond), indicating the importance of RpoS for AI2-mediated resuscitation. (b) Proposed mechanism of resuscitation. An increase in AI2 signaling causes the de-repression of LuxR, a positive regulator of RpoS. Increased RpoS activity leads to an increase in production of catalase (KatG), which allows cells to be culturable on routine media containing $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ .


## 23.1.3.5 Proposed mechanism of resuscitation

We have previously shown that V. vulnificus cells in the VBNC state are nonculturable on laboratory media largely due to reduced catalase activity, which sensitizes the cells to the presence of reactive oxygen species (see Section 10) and therefore prevents replication of these cells on media that routinely contain $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ (Kong et al., 2004). Furthermore, we have shown that catalase production by cells resumes upon their resuscitation from the VBNC state, resulting in renewed culturability (Smith and Oliver, 2006). The stress-related alternate sigma factor, RpoS, is known to be necessary for the production of catalase (Park et al., 2004) and to play an important role in resuscitation from the VBNC state (Boaretti et al., 2003). Furthermore, Joelsson et al (2007) reported that LuxR enhances the expression of rpoS. Another study found that expression of rpoS significantly increased during resuscitation from the VBNC state (Coutard et al., 2007). Here, we found that a V. vulnificus rpoS mutant had a restricted ability to resuscitate within 24 h. Importantly, the addition of AI2 to a VBNC culture of the rpoS mutant did not induce early resuscitation (Figure 23.1.4a), as was observed with VBNC cells of the wild-type parent, indicating that RpoS is an important factor in AI2-mediated resuscitation. Combining the existing literature with our current findings, we propose a model of resuscitation in which AI2 signaling stimulates expression of rpoS through the action of LuxR. This subsequently induces the expression of catalase (katG), thereby allowing cells to overcome the toxic properties of $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ in bacterial media and to return to a culturable state (Figure 23.1.4b). However, it cannot be excluded that quorum sensing has an effect on other processes related to dormancy, such as toxin–antitoxin regulation (see Section 6), which has been implicated in VBNC and persister cell formation (Demidenok et al., 2014; Korch and Hill, 2006; Maisonneuve et al., 2013; Pedersen et al., 2002; Rotem et al., 2010). 

## 23.1.4 Conclusion

Following the discovery of quorum sensing, researchers proposed that these systems may be targets for potential antimicrobials (for a recent review, see Zhu and Kaufmann, 2013). Although reasonable, it is possible that inhibiting quorumsensing pathways may actually prevent resuscitation from dormancy and subsequently promote infection latency and antibiotic tolerance. As previously proposed by Buerger et al. (2012), and in consideration of the findings herein, we suggest the feasibility of employing resuscitation-promoting factors, such as autoinducers or more potent derivatives, to resuscitate dormant (and antibiotic-persistent) bacteria to a metabolically active form, to allow for more effective antimicrobial killing. Furthermore, since we found that resuscitation may be averted by quorum-sensing inhibitors and induced by autoinducers, it may be possible to maintain control over dormancy dynamics and possibly even pathogenesis by manipulating the resuscitation process. 

These studies provide insight into the mechanisms for resuscitation of V. vulnificus cells not only through evidence that interspecific quorum-sensing autoinducers have the capability of resuscitating dormant bacterial populations both in vivo and in vitro, but also by elucidating the active interplay between quorum-sensing autoinducer levels and in vitro VBNC dynamics. We also found that quorum-sensing signals are produced by stochastically resuscitated cells within the dormant population when permissive conditions are reached, which causes the resuscitation of the remaining population. Since planktonic VBNC cells in seawater are not likely to sense a high concentration of quorum-sensing molecules, their incorporation into bacterially diverse microenvironments such as the oyster digestive system, phytoplankton, marine aggregates, or sediments may provide a more suitable environment for detecting quorum-sensing signals and, therefore, for their resuscitation. As proposed by Manina and McKinney (2013), the use of real-time single-cell analysis technology will allow researchers to study the resuscitation of dormant microorganisms with much greater resolution. Although the mechanisms deduced from this study remain to be examined in other major pathogens, these findings may provide important insights for clinical intervention and for the development of innovative antimicrobial agents in the fight against multidrug-tolerant dormant cells.

# Differential resuscitative effects of pyruvate and its analogs on VBNC (viable but nonculturable) Salmonella

Fumio Amano 

Laboratory of Biodefense and Regulation, Osaka University of Pharmaceutical Sciences, Takatsuki, Osaka, Japan 

## 23.2.1 Introduction

Salmonella is one of the most frequent causes of foodborne diseases, and is detected not only in food but also in natural environments, including river water, soil, manure, and airborne dust (Braden, 2006; Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, 2009; Hiramatsu et al., 2005; Little et al., 2008; Pedersen et al., 2008). Among Salmonella spp., Salmonella enterica serovar Enteritidis (SE) is the dominant species, and it has sometimes caused mass food poisoning in Japan (Infectious Disease Surveillance Center, 2009) and the United States (Braden, 2006). In consideration of the strong infectivity of Salmonella, a better understanding and appropriate risk management for environmental Salmonella are necessary to control the spreading of Salmonella to foods in general (Amano, 2011; Morishige et al., 2013). Contamination of food by environmental Salmonella is an important matter for food safety. However, Salmonella is known to persist and survive in various environments even after exposure to such sanitizers as hydrogen peroxide $\left( \mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } \right)$ (Amano, 2011), which is widely used. 

Since Xu et al. (1982) reported the existence of the VBNC (viable but nonculturable) state over 30 years ago, a large number of papers have been published by researchers worldwide on this subject, documenting the VBNC phenomenon in a wide variety of bacteria. Many pathogens, such as Escherichia coli, Vibrio cholerae, Vibrio vulnificus, Shigella sonnei, Shigella flexneri, Campylobacter jejuni, Legionella pneumophila, SE (Chmielewski and Frank, 1995), and others (see reviews by Li et al., 2014; Oliver, 2010; Ramamurthy et al., 2014), can enter the VBNC state after exposure to adverse environmental conditions such as high and low temperature (see Section 13), osmotic stress (see Section 11), oxidative stress (see Section 10), and nutritional starvation (see Section 7) (Asakura et al., 2008; Cook and Bolster, 2007; Mizunoe et al., 1999; Oliver, 2010; Wong and Liu, 

2008). The VBNC state is now generally accepted as a state in which a cell is metabolically active but is incapable of undergoing cell division necessary for growth and colony formation on solid growth media (Lleo` et al., 2000; Nilsson et al., 1991; Rahman et al., 1994), as shown in Figure 23.2.1. 

In this chapter, we used the colony-forming activity of the cells on Luria Bertani (LB)–agar plates as the definition of culturability, and metabolic activity to reduce CTC as that of viability. According to these definitions, the VBNC state corresponds to the CTC reduction–positive but colony formation–negative state. Table 23.2.1 summarizes several methods for the resuscitation of VBNC cells that have been used to study the factors involved in reversing the VBNC state of a variety of bacterial species (see also Chapter 23.1). 

For example, V. vulnificus and Salmonella Typhimurium are resuscitated after a temperature change (Gupte et al., 2003; Nilsson et al., 1991; see Chapter 23.1); and Enterococcus spp. when grown in tryptic soy broth (TSB) (Lleo` et al., 2001). In a recent report, we showed that $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ exposure rapidly compels SE cells to enter the VBNC state and that the addition of sodium pyruvate to the $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -treated SE cells effectively resuscitates these cells from the VBNC state to the growing state (Morishige et al., 2013). 

Sodium pyruvate, a well-known compound and an intermediate key metabolite of glycolysis, is known as a bacterial growth inducer for cells treated with $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ (Calabrese and Bissonnette, 1990; Giandomenico et al., 1997; McDonald et al, 1983; Mizunoe et al., 1999). Although this upregulating effect of pyruvate on resuscitation is known, the precise mechanism underlying this pyruvate-mediated promotion of growth of bacteria in the VBNC state is not yet clear. 

We also found that stressed Salmonella release a large amount of pyruvate during long-term culture in M9 minimal medium containing a high concentration (0.8%) of glucose, and its cells were later proven to be in the VBNC state (Morishige et al., 2014). Consequently, we hypothesized that pyruvate is not only a metabolic intermediate of glycolysis but also a certain repair molecule that functions under various stress conditions. 

![image](https://cdn-mineru.openxlab.org.cn/result/2026-06-13/8a496fd2-f9e2-4693-b316-810d735c0ef6/eb2d02d49a57b40ac802e6322b506e7559f08a74a8a0e57e7a9fd8ab2f3eab5b.jpg)



Figure 23.2.1 A model of VBNC formation. Viability and culturability (colony-forming activity in solid culture, in this study) are shown schematically from high to low levels, respectively. Intact cells have both high viability and colony-forming activity, but they proceed to lose both of these activities in various adverse environments in response to stresses, tracing to dead cells that have no viability or colony-forming activity (shown by broken line). However, this process to the death pathways does not seem to be straightforward, but instead is composed of multiple steps that involve losing viable-cell functions. VBNC cells correspond to the cells that are losing culturability but showing some of the viable-cell functions. Some cells in the VBNC population can be prompted to undergo cell division and colony formation, a process called resuscitation.


The aim of this study was to show the induction of the VBNC state of SE cells with $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ and the resuscitative effect of pyruvate and its analogs on these VBNC cells. We focused on the metabolic activities of VBNC cells, such as respiration and DNA and protein synthesis, and examined them in the course of the resuscitation process from the VBNC state, as described in detail in an earlier paper (Morishige et al., 2013). Many of the results in this chapter are based on the experiments performed in the preceding study (Morishige et al., 2013). 

## 23.2.2 Materials and methods

## 23.2.2.1 Bacterial cell culture

An environmental isolate of the SE clone, SE Cl#15-1, obtained from the CAF Laboratories (Japan) (Terai et al., 2005), was used throughout this study. The bacteria were cultured overnight in 10 mL of LB medium (Difco, USA) in a 50 mL tube at ${ 3 7 } ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ with shaking at 150 strokes/min, and then suspended in fresh LB medium at $\mathrm { O D } _ { 5 5 0 } = 0 . 0 5$ . Thereafter, they were incubated at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ with shaking at 150 strokes/min for 105 min until the growth had reached the mid-logarithmic phase. The precultured bacteria were harvested by centrifugation at $2 8 5 0 \times g$ for 20 min at $4 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ washed twice with 10 mL of ice-cold phosphate-buffered saline without calcium $\mathrm { ( P B S ( - ) ) }$ , pH 7.5, and finally suspended in ice-cold PBS(−) at $\mathrm { O D } _ { 5 5 0 } = 6 . 3 0$ (about $1 \times 1 0 ^ { 9 } \mathrm { C F U / m L } )$ .

## 23.2.2.2 Induction of the VBNC state by oxidative stress exposure

Precultured bacteria were suspended in a 50 mL tube containing 10 mL of fresh LB medium to which 0.1–10 mM $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ (Wako, Japan) had been added. The cells $( 1 \times 1 0 ^ { 7 }$ CFU/mL) were incubated at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 60 min, with shaking at 150 strokes/min. The bacteria were harvested by centrifugation, as described in Section 23.2.2.1, and subsequently resuspended in fresh LB medium at $1 \times 1 0 ^ { 7 }$ cells/mL. After serial dilution with ice-cold PBS(−), 25 μL aliquots were plated onto LB–agar plates, which were incubated at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ overnight. The resultant number of colonies was counted to estimate the number of viable bacterial cells (CFU), as an indicator of culturability. 

## 23.2.2.3 Resuscitative effect of pyruvate or its analogs in liquid culture

The $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -treated bacteria were harvested by centrifugation, suspended in M9 medium (Difco) at $1 \times 1 0 ^ { 7 }$ CFU/mL without or with 0.3–30 mM sodium pyruvate, and then incubated at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 0–60 min. Bacterial growth was monitored both by the density of the bacterial solution at 550 nm $( \mathrm { O D } _ { 5 5 0 } )$ and by CFU. 

Besides pyruvate, its analogs such as bromopyruvate, phenylpyruvate, and alpha-ketobutyrate (Sigma-Aldrich, USA) used at 30 mM were also evaluated for their resuscitative effects by incubating VBNC cells with each analog at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 30 min. 

## 23.2.2.4 Use of confocal laser-scanning microscopy for validation of the respiratory activity of the bacteria after the resuscitation process

Bacteria in M9 medium containing 0.4% (w/v) glucose were incubated with the reagents of a Bacstain CTC Rapid Staining Kit (DOJINDO, Japan). CTC is an indicator of bacterial aerobic respiration (Cappelier et al., 1997; Rodriguez et al., 1992). The following procedures were performed essentially in the dark throughout the experiment: The bacteria were incubated without agitation for 60 min in an incubator at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ They were washed with 500 μL of PBS(−) by centrifugation at $1 8 , 1 0 0 \times g$ for 5 min at $4 ~ { } ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ . Finally, the resultant cell pellets were resuspended in 40 μL of PBS(−) and placed in the wells of a sterile, poly-L-lysine-coated, 8-well glass slide. The cells were allowed to adhere to the glass by standing the slide for 30 min on ice, and then they were fixed on ice for 30 min with 40 μL of 4% (w/v) paraformaldehyde/PBS(−). Cellular DNA was stained with 5 μL of 20 μg/mL DAPI (4′ ,6-diamino-2-phenylindole lactate; Sigma, USA), after which the slide was rinsed twice with PBS(−). Perma Fluor Mountant Medium (Thermo Scientific, USA) was applied to the slide, which was sealed with a cover glass and thereafter examined under a confocal laser-scanning microscope (LSM510; Carl Zeiss, Germany). 

The viability of the bacterial cell was determined from the following formula: 

$$
\text { Viability } (\%) = 1 0 0 \times \text { Live   cells   (CTC / DAPI - positive   cells) } /
$$

$$
\text { Whole   cells   (DAPI - positive   cells) }
$$

## 23.2.2.5 Validation of the biosynthesis of DNA and protein of SE under resuscitation conditions

Precultured SE cells were suspended in 250 mL of LB medium containing 3 mM $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ at $1 \times 1 0 ^ { 7 }$ CFU/mL in a 1000 mL Erlenmeyer flask and incubated at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ with shaking at 150 strokes/min to cause oxidative stress (see Section 10). The bacteria were then harvested by centrifugation as described in Section 23.2.2.1, and resuspended at $1 \times 1 0 ^ { 8 }$ cells/mL in M9 medium without glucose in the presence or absence of 0.3 mM sodium pyruvate, after which they were incubated at $3 7 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 0–60 min. The cells were incubated with [methyl-3H] thymidine (PerkinElmer, USA) or $\mathrm { L } { - } \bigl [ \mathrm { } ^ { 3 5 } \mathrm { S } \bigr ]$ methionine $/ ~ \mathrm { L } { - } [ ^ { 3 5 } \mathrm { S } ]$ cysteine (EXPRE35S35S [35S]-Protein Labeling Mix; PerkinElmer) for 0–60 min for evidence of resuscitation. After incubation, 10% trichloroacetic acid (TCA; Wako) was added to each sample with sudden chilling on ice, to stop the incorporation of the radiolabel and to extract and remove the acid-soluble materials. The samples were centrifuged at $1 6 , 1 0 0 \times g$ for 5 min at $4 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } _ { : }$ , and the resultant precipitates were washed three times with 5% TCA– PBS(−) by centrifugation. Finally, the acid-insoluble material in the precipitates was dissolved in 0.1 M NaOH, which was then neutralized with HCl; and the material was thereafter resuspended in ACS II liquid scintillation cocktail (Amersham, USA). Radioactivity of both [3H] and $[ ^ { 3 5 } \mathrm { S } ]$ was measured by use of a TRI-CARB 1600TR liquid scintillation counter (Packard, USA). 

## 23.2.2.6 Statistical analysis

Statistical analysis and estimations of the significance of difference between groups with comparable variances were performed by one-way analysis of variance (ANOVA), followed by Tukey–Kramer’s post hoc test or by Student’s t-test. 

## 23.2.3 Results and discussion

In this study, we first examined the effect of hydrogen peroxide $\left( \mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } \right)$ on the culturability of SE cells with different doses and incubation times (see Section 10). Incubation of the cells with 3 mM $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ for 60 min reproducibly decreased the CFU to approximately $1 / 1 0 ^ { 4 }$ of the control, and so this concentration was used as the standard stress condition for SE cells throughout this study. 

We next examined the ability of various chemicals to resuscitate the $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -treated bacteria. We first tested whether pyruvate would have any effect on the $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -treated cells, because we had earlier found that a large amount of pyruvate accumulated in the culture supernatant of SE cells after their incubation with 0.8% (w/v) glucose in M9 minimal medium, under which condition the cells had lost their culturability almost entirely, but retained some metabolic activity in terms of their ability to reduce CTC (Morishige et al., 2014). 

As shown in Figure 23.2.3, pyruvate showed a resuscitative effect on the $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -treated SE cells in M9 medium. Incubation with 30 mM pyruvate for 30 min restored the culturability of SE cells by 17.5-fold over the control without pyruvate, as indicated by the resuscitation ratio. However, the optical density $( \mathrm { O D } _ { 5 5 0 } )$ of the cultures did not increase under these conditions (data not shown). These results suggest that pyruvate increased the number of viable and culturable bacteria by promoting an increase in the cell population able to divide and form colonies in LB–agar plate after the resuscitation process. Besides, even intact bacteria (not treated with $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } )$ in the logarithmic phase did not multiply in M9 medium in the presence or absence of pyruvate during 60 mins of incubation, as monitored by changes in both CFU and $\mathrm { O D } _ { 5 5 0 }$ under these conditions (data not shown). 

These results suggest that the increase in CFU by the incubation with 30 mM pyruvate was not due to replication of the intact cells that had remained throughout the $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ treatment, but probably to a change in the dormant, nonproliferative cells into growing, colony-forming cells. 

In addition, to ascertain the chemical properties of pyruvate required for the resuscitation, pyruvate analogs (bromopyruvate, phenylpyruvate, and α-ketobutyrate) were examined. As shown in Figure 23.2.3, α-ketobutyrate increased the resuscitation ratio by 15.4-fold during incubation, suggesting the resuscitative effect of this analog. Phenylpyruvate had no effect, whereas bromopyruvate actually reduced the culturability of the $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -treated cells to 0.067 of the control, probably through inhibition of glycolysis by interruption of hexokinase II activity (Ko et al., 2001). These results suggest that the α-keto carboxy residue was necessary for the resuscitation effect but that the structure of this moiety alone was inadequate to manifest the effect (Figure 23.2.2). 

α-Ketobutyrate is converted to propionyl-CoA by oxidized ferredoxin and 2-oxobutyrate synthase (EC 1.2.7.2), and subsequently to succinyl-CoA through three metabolic processes, then finally integrated into the TCA cycle (Halarnkar and Blomquist, 1989). However, our preliminary experiments suggest that the activation of the TCA cycle was not essential and responsible for the resuscitation, because thiamine, a cofactor of the pyruvate dehydrogenase complex that promotes the generation of acetyl-CoA, did not have any significant effect on the restoration in the presence of pyruvate (data not shown). Besides, the resuscitative effect of pyruvate did not differ between experiments performed under aerobic and anaerobic conditions, where, in the latter case, the TCA cycle would have been inactive (data not shown). Therefore, the generation of energy under aerobic conditions itself does not seem to be linked to the recovery of the colony-forming activity. These results seem to suggest a correlation with reports from other laboratories showing that nonculturable bacteria, induced by low temperature and starvation, are restored in culturability by $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -degrading substances such as catalase, pyruvate, and α- ketoglutaric acid (Calabrese and Bissonnette, 1990; McDonald et al., 1983; Mizunoe et al., 1999; Mukamolova et al., 2003; Rayman et al., 1978) and that these substances, such as α-keto acid and pyruvate, are not used as nutrients by nonculturable bacteria, but rather act via their $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -degrading effect. 

In order to avoid the possibility that pyruvate caused resuscitation by simply degrading intracellular $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } ,$ , we also determined the amount of intracellular peroxide of $\mathrm { \ddot { H } } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -treated cells by using a PeroXOQuantTM Quantitative Peroxide Assay Kit (Thermo Scientific, USA). As a result, we found no detectable amount of $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ or its adduct by using this assay kit; both $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } \cdot$ - treated and intact cells and the cell extract of intact SE cells degraded exogenous $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ (data not shown). Supporting this, α-lipoate, N-acetyl-L-cysteine, and D-mannitol, which are wellknown antioxidant or radical-scavenging reagents, were ineffective in resuscitating the $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -treated cells (data not shown). 

These results suggest that the resuscitative effects of α-keto acids and pyruvate were partly related to the cellular metabolism

Figure 23.2.3 Resuscitative effects of pyruvate and its analogs on $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -treated SE cells. Logarithmic cells $( 1 \times 1 0 ^ { 7 }$ cells) were treated with 3 mM $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } ,$ washed, and then incubated at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 30 min in 1 mL of M9 medium without glucose but with a 30 mM concentration of one of the chemicals shown in Figure 23.2.2 (Morishige et al., 2013). Colony-forming activity (log cfu/mL) was determined by plating cells onto duplicate LB agar plates, and the results are shown as the resuscitation ratio, defined as follows:


Resuscitation ratio = (CFU of the experiment after incubation)/(CFU of initial) 

where “initial” indicates the CFU of the cells treated with $3 \mathrm { m M H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ but subsequently without additive and/or incubation. Data are presented as the mean ± SEM for three independent experiments. $^ { * * } p < 0 . 0 1$ versus none; $^ { * } p < 0 . 0 5$ versus BrPA; one-way ANOVA, then Tukey–Kramer’s post hoc test. 

centered on the substrates around glycolysis and/or oxidative phosphorylation, not just degrading intracellular peroxide, as opposed to previous reports suggesting the direct degrading effects of pyruvate on ROS, including $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ (Calabrese and Bissonnette, 1990; Ducret et al., 2014; Giandomenico et al., 1997; McDonald et al., 1983; Mizunoe et al., 1999; Mukamolova et al., 2003; Rayman et al., 1978; Wai et al., 2000). 

Under the resuscitative conditions, we investigated various metabolic activities of $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -treated cells, including respiratory activity reflected by CTC reduction, DNA synthesis by [3H]-thymidine incorporation, and protein synthesis by $[ ^ { 3 5 } \mathrm { S } ]$ ]-methionine $- [ ^ { 3 5 } \mathrm { S } ]$ -cysteine incorporation, together with culturability in terms of CFU. As shown in Figure 23.2.4d, the respiratory activity did not decrease significantly after $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ treatment, being about 68.0% of that of the intact cells, and it did not change during resuscitation incubation in the presence or absence of pyruvate. Contrary to its lack of effect on the respiration activity, the addition of 0.3 mM pyruvate to the $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } -$ treated cells increased the CFU from 0.001% to 0.113% of the control, whereas the non-pyruvate-treated control remained at 0.001% (Figure 23.2.4a). These results suggest that the oxidative stress due to 3 mM $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ caused only moderate damage to the respiratory activity of Salmonella, keeping the cells in the VBNC state, and that addition of pyruvate seemed to make some of them colony forming (Figure 23.2.4). 

On the other hand, unlike in the case of the respiratory activity, the synthesis of protein and DNA in the $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -treated cells was quite different in the presence and absence of pyruvate during the resuscitation process. When the cells were exposed to oxidative stress due to 3 mM $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } ,$ both DNA and protein synthesis decreased to 1.8% and 10.2%, respectively, of the values for the nontreated control (Figure 23.2.4b,c). The addition of 0.3 mM pyruvate elevated these activities with time: DNA synthesis increased to a level 3.2-fold higher than the basal level (0 time) by 60 min, and protein synthesis increased similarly but more moderately to a level 1.6-fold higher than the basal one, whereas incubation without pyruvate failed to increase either of these activities (Figure 23.2.4).

Figure 23.2.4 Time course of resuscitation of $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -treated SE cells in the presence or absence of pyruvate. Logarithmic cells $( 1 \times 1 0 ^ { 7 }$ cells for (a) and (d), or $1 \times 1 0 ^ { 8 }$ cells for (b) and (c)) were treated with 3 mM $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ , washed, and incubated in 1 mL of M9 medium without glucose in the absence (blank bars) or presence (dashed bars) of 0.3 mM pyruvate at $3 7 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ for 0, 30, or 60 min. (a) Culturability was estimated by CFU; (b) DNA synthesis, by [methyl-3H]thymidine incorporation; (c) protein synthesis, by $[ ^ { 3 5 } \mathrm { S } ]$ -methionine–cysteine incorporation; and (d) respiratory activity, by CTC reduction. Each activity is shown as a relative percentage to the positive control (intact logarithmic bacteria) in each experiment. Data are presented as the mean ± SEM for three independent experiments. Significance of difference between in the presence and in the absence of pyruvate is shown as $^ { * } p < 0 . 0 5$ and $^ { * * } p < 0 . 0 2$ after Student’s t-test, respectively. The results on culturability in these experiments with either $1 \times 1 0 ^ { 7 }$ cells or $1 \times 1 0 ^ { 8 }$ cells were essentially similar (data not shown).


Taken together, these results imply that some population of the VBNC cells had acquired the ability to return to the culturable state by restoration of biosynthesis of macromolecules by pyruvate, especially that of DNA. 

Besides, we assayed the incorporation of $\left[ 3 - { } ^ { 1 4 } \mathrm { C } \right]$ -pyruvate into $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ -treated and/or resuscitated SE cells. The $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } .$ - treated SE cells incorporated the radioactive pyruvate biphasically, as they had incorporated [3H]-thymidine; the pyruvate was rapidly incorporated by 15 min and then gradually by 60 min, similar to the case of [3H]-thymidine (data not shown). In keeping with these results, our study suggests that pyruvate was incorporated by the VBNC bacteria, resulting in restoration of DNA synthesis and cell division (Figure 23.2.4). 

DNA synthesis and cell division, both of which are essential to manifest “culturability,” are restored rapidly in 15 min in the former, and gradually but greatly in the latter, only in the presence of 0.3 mM pyruvate (Morishige et al., 2013). Protein synthesis is also restored gradually in the presence of pyruvate. These results suggest that the $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ treatment induced a VBNC state in SE cells, and that the pyruvate helped to restore DNA synthesis first and then the culturability, although we cannot completely rule out the possibility that a certain number of bacteria with culturability but with very low metabolic activity for DNA or protein synthesis would gain such an activity during reincubation with pyruvate (Figure 23.2.1). The effect of pyruvate might not have been primarily due to any antioxidative effect but rather to the triggering of certain metabolic activities necessary for resuscitation. Pyruvate may have activated certain metabolic pathways for energy metabolism, probably those including CTC reduction. 

Further studies are required to understand the precise molecular mechanism of resuscitation of VBNC bacteria by pyruvate and α-ketobutyrte, in order to improve risk management toward pathogenic VBNC cells threatening food safety.
