# Environmental persistence of Shiga toxin–producing E. coli

Philipp Aurass and Antje Flieger 

Division of Enteropathogenic Bacteria and Legionella (FG11), Robert Koch-Institut, Wernigerode, Germany 

## 23.3.1 Introduction to intestinal pathogenic Escherichia coli

Intestinal pathogenic E. coli (IPEC) is an important agent of gastrointestinal infections and, depending on its virulence gene repertoire, may lead to further severe sequelae, such as hemolytic-uremic syndrome (HUS) as observed for infections with enterohemorrhagic E. coli (EHEC). Important virulence factors of classical Shiga toxin–producing strains (STEC), which include EHEC strains, are the Shiga toxin Stx and a type III protein secretion system encoded by a pathogenicity island, the locus of enterocyte effacement (LEE). Stx is responsible for the severe pathology of HUS, and LEE for intimate attachment of the bacteria to epithelia of the intestine (Kaper et al., 2004). STEC-inducing HUS in many cases belongs to the serovar O157:H7/H− and others, such as O26:H11/Hnm and O103:H2/Hnm (Fruth et al., 2015). Other important intestinal E. coli pathovars include enteropathogenic E. coli (EPEC), enteroaggregative E. coli (EAEC), enteroinvasive E. coli (EIEC), and enterotoxigenic E. coli (ETEC). More recently, the groups of diffusely adherent E. coli (DAEC) and adherent invasive E. coli (AIEC) were added to this classification. Characteristic virulence features, pathogenesis of related disease, and epidemiology of IPEC pathovars have been extensively reviewed and will not be further addressed in this article (Clements et al., 2012; Croxen et al., 2013; Kaper et al., 2004). 

The O104:H4 strain, which caused the large outbreak of gastroenteritis, bloody diarrhea, and HUS in Germany and other countries in 2011, combines traits of both EHEC and EAEC. Therefore, it represents a so-called EHEC–EAEC mixedpathovar strain. For example, among other genes typical for EHEC, it harbors the Shiga toxin stx2a gene but not the genes for LEE and additionally comprises genetic elements for expression of several attributes characteristic of EAEC, such as type I aggregative fimbrae (AAFI) encoded on the pAA EAEC virulence plasmid (Bielaszewska et al., 2011; Frank et al., 2011). 

It has been discussed that the combination of stx2 and AAF is an important determinant for the extraordinary pathogenicity of this hybrid pathovar, which is underlined by the high percentage of HUS occurring during the 2011 outbreak (Frank et al., 2011; Jandhyala et al., 2013; Zhang et al., 2013). A variety of distinct EHEC–EAEC O104:H4 strains and further EHEC–EAEC strains of other serovars has been described in association with human disease (Dallman et al., 2012; Grad et al., 2012, 2013; Iyoda et al., 2000; Jourdan-da Silva et al., 2012; Mellmann et al., 2011; Monecke et al., 2011; Morabito et al., 1998; Prager et al., 2014; Tietze et al., 2015). 

## 23.3.2 Transmission and reservoirs of Shiga toxin–producing E. coli

Transmission of members of the different intestinal E. coli pathovars differs in some aspects; however, doubtlessly the main route is fecal-oral, mainly through contaminated food or water, person-to-person spread, or animal contact (reviewed in Croxen et al., 2013). Major reservoirs of EHEC and other STEC generally include asymptomatic human carriers and ruminants, the latter acting as super shedders in the environment, and transmission occurs via contamination of soil, water, and other living vectors. Ruminants colonized by EHEC O157:H7 in general remain asymptomatic, and therefore it is difficult to identify and isolate shedders in field conditions (Beutin et al., 1993). It is interesting to note that the EHEC–EAEC O104:H4 outbreak strain was not found in association with cattle, which as with other EAEC points to a predominant association with humans or another unknown reservoir (Wieler et al., 2011). 

Furthermore, insects, protozoa, and plants including fresh produce have been implicated in survival, reproduction, and transmission of EHEC (Itoh et al., 1998; Solomon et al., 2002). Contaminated lettuce, fruits, sprouts, and vegetables seem to be of special importance (Berger et al., 2010; Buchholz et al., 2011; 

Xicohtencatl-Cortes et al., 2009), and persistence and adherence of intestinal pathogenic E. coli on plant surfaces or inside plant tissue have been shown in multiple studies (Barak et al., 2002; Brandl, 2006; Hsu et al., 2006; Mathews et al., 2014; Stine et al., 2005). 

In a report by Cohen et al. (1991), the relevance of fly control in military field bases was correlated with drastically reduced rates of diarrheal disease (reduced by 42%) and shigellosis (reduced by 85%), as well as with a considerable reduction in seroconversion for development of antibodies against Shigella and ETEC antigens (reduction by 76% for Shigella and 57% for ETEC). This finding suggests that flies may considerably contribute to transmission of enteric pathogens, which may also be important for IPEC (Cohen et al., 1991). 

It is further hypothesized that predatory protozoa, like grazing amoebae, shaped human pathogens from the evolutionary point of view, but additionally comprise a protective niche and vector for pathogen transmission at present (Greub and Raoult, 2004). Various human pathogens are known to survive grazing or even reprogram amoebae to form a niche permissive for intracellular replication and subsequent dissemination when conditions are favorable. Amoebae, like Acanthamoeabae or Hartmanellae, are frequently found in soil and aqueous environmental habitats and can become highly enriched in manured soils, effluents, silage, cooling towers, and various other human-made reservoirs, where they coexist with bacteria (Rodriguez-Zaragoza, 1994; Srikanth and Berk, 1994). Furthermore, many protozoa have been detected as part of the rumen or the human gut microflora. Additionally, a recent study identified diverse freeliving protozoa in 34 out of 38 randomly chosen used household dishcloths, and foodborne bacterial pathogens in 22 of 38 dishcloths, including Salmonella enterica and S. aureus. This indicates that potential vectors of transmission, such as freeliving amoebae, ciliates, or flagellates, may contribute to transmission and spread of amoeba-resistant pathogens in the household (Chavatte et al., 2014). 

It is further important to note that upon facing unfavorable conditions, amoebae can form cysts and thus have a great capacity to resist extreme conditions such as chlorinated water, osmotic pressure, or even fumigation with a variety of biozides (Coulon et al., 2010). Even without encystment, intracellular association with A. castellanii, T. pyriformis, or Cyclidium sp. enabled E. coli and other potential pathogenic coliform bacteria to survive chlorination, which without protozoa was shown to be bactericidal (King et al., 1988). 

The role of protozoa in the environmental life cycle of pathogenic E. coli is much less well examined compared to classical obligatory or facultative intracellular bacteria. Nevertheless, laboratory studies on coliform bacteria, including STEC/EHEC O157:H7 and invasive E. coli K1, showed their ability to survive, replicate, and persist for a limited time within Acanthamoebae (Barker et al., 1999; Chekabab et al., 2012; King et al., 1988). These studies therefore suggest that amoebae may constitute a reservoir for STEC/EHEC and other pathogenic E. coli. Transcriptional profiling of STEC/EHEC 

O157:H7 during an intracellular state in A. castellanii showed induced expression of genes related to virulence (stx, type III secretion genes), stress response, quorum sensing, and other functions (Carruthers et al., 2010). This suggests that certain E. coli strains are able to coordinate a phenotypic adaptation facilitating an intracellular lifestyle in protozoa. 

Various studies conducted on the free-living ciliate Tetrahymena or the amoeba A. castellanii showed involvement of Shiga toxin in the bacterium–protozoon interaction. Lainhart et al. suggested that Shiga toxin is part of the bacterial defense system toward eukaryotic predators, and a subsequent study proposed an undefined receptor enabling Stx to enter and kill Tetrahymena (Lainhart et al., 2009; Stolfa and Koudelka, 2012). Accordingly, it was found that STEC is more resistant toward grazing protozoa in artificial microcosms than strains lacking stx (Mauro et al., 2013). 

Furthermore, Chekabab et al. demonstrated an increased culturability of EHEC O157:H7 (EDL933) but not E. coli K12 in coculture with A. castelanii within a timeframe of 3 weeks (Chekabab et al., 2012). However, in a subsequent study with isogenic stx mutants of strain EDL933, the authors reported that the toxic effect of Shiga toxin also reduced intracellular survival rates of EHEC O157:H7 compared to the Δstx strain (Chekabab et al., 2013a). This finding suggests that factors in addition to stx contribute to successful survival within A. castellanii. Characterization of these will be beneficial for future intervention aiming at a reduction of environmental persistence of pathogenic E. coli in association with micro-eukaryotes. In addition to protozoa, there are reports on intracellular survival and replication of STEC O157:H7 in the multicellular soil eukaryote Caenorhabditis elegans (Anderson et al., 2006; Kenney et al., 2005), suggesting that multiple ways and opportunities of STEC–eukaryote interaction exist in soils. 

## 23.3.3 Persistence of Shiga toxin–producing E. coli in different environments

In addition to association with other organisms, rather little is known regarding the persistence of IPEC in the environment and its relevance for infection. Whereas foodborne transmission undoubtedly is linked to large and frequent outbreaks, there is still little information available concerning transmission via direct environmental exposure to IPEC. Environmental reservoirs include recreational and natural waters, sewage, as well as soils and wild animals (Chekabab et al., 2013b; Jiang et al., 2002; Miko et al., 2009). Water can be a major transmitter between and within reservoirs, especially when agricultural runoffs, floods, or sewage are concerned (Gagliardi and Karns, 2000). 

A study analyzing E. coli O157 outbreaks between 1994 and 2003 in Scotland pointed out that environmental exposure may constitute an important risk factor. Specifically, 54% of outbreaks in Scotland were linked to environmental transmission routes, especially to farm contacts, whereas 40% were identified as foodborne (Strachan et al., 2006). Despite STEC/EHEC 

O157:H7 strains constituting a major cause of human disease, several studies revealed a prevalence of non-O157 STEC strains linked to human disease (Fey et al., 2000; Fruth et al., 2015; Karch et al., 2005). A recent analysis investigated 65 STEC strains of serotype O113:H21 from food, clinical, and environmental sources by means of genetic markers associated with human infections and showed that the investigated environmental isolates of this prevalent serotype, which constitutes a common LEE-negative cluster, have the potential to sicken and cause HUS (Feng et al., 2014). Another study identified numerous non-O157 STEC obtained from feces and soil samples of different farms, where most of them had been associated with human disease (Scott et al., 2009). Further reservoirs for transmission in the direct farm setting included paddle waterers, farm animals $( \mathrm { e . g . }$ , cats, dogs, and rats), as well as feed and especially dust collected in barns (Joris et al., 2013). Several clinically relevant STEC serovars were isolated in natural water sources in the leafy-green growing zone of central California within a 2- year survey (Cooley et al., 2014). The mean overall prevalence of STEC O157:H7 was 8%, and continued isolation of clonal strains, as determined by macrorestriction and pulsed-field gel electrophoresis (PFGE), without a clear source of reintroduction indicated persistence over several months. Reintroduction of strains persisting in soil, especially after rainfalls or continuing introduction via animal reservoirs, has also been discussed (Cooley et al., 2014). 

Another recent study compared persistence of various pathogenic and nonpathogenic O157 and non-O157 strains in soil and found that strain-specific as well as soil-specific differences significantly affected survival times (Ma et al., 2014). Thus, prevalence of uncommon serotypes of STEC linked to human disease should be considered, and persistence studies targeted to non-O157 serogroups are of interest. However, numerous surveys, mainly performed on STEC O157:H7, report on its superior capability to persist for extended periods of time in a culturable or unculturable (i.e., viable but nonculturable (VBNC)) state in either soil or water. Unlike other pathogens, it seems that STEC O157:H7 may well tolerate prolonged incubation at dry conditions (Berry and Miller, 2005; Iijima et al., 1998; Jiang et al., 2002; Maule, 2000), although other studies report that dehydration significantly decreases persistence (Williams et al., 2005). 

Scott et al. (2006) compared survival of STEC O157:H7 in cattle feces or in water with or without preceding animal passage. The strain survived culturable for up to 97 days in feces and 109 days in water, with extended survival time of bacteria inoculated after animal passage (Scott et al., 2006). However, the origin and temperature of the water used also significantly influenced survival of a nontoxigenic O157:H7 strain (NCTC 12900) (McGee et al., 2002), which has implications for the reinfection of cattle. Others showed that STEC O157:H7 persists culturable for more than 2 weeks in marine water and tolerates increased (5%) salt conditions (Miyagi et al., 2001). Multiple studies showed longtime survival of pathogenic E. coli in water. A systematic comparison of survival of EHEC O157:H7 over 91 days in tap water, reservoir water, and lake water, each at different temperatures, showed regardless of the water used that cold temperature $( 8 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } )$ supported persistence, as indicated by generally higher culture-based survival rates. Furthermore, the water source significantly influenced survival, and viability staining suggested efficient formation of VBNC cells within water microcosms (Wang and Doyle, 1998). 

In another study, STEC O157:H7 culturability dropped from 106 colony forming units (CFU)/ml to 0 CFU/ml within 27 days in river water, whereas the strain survived in a culturable state in soil cores containing rooted grass for more than 130 days with an apparent decline in CFU from initially $1 0 ^ { 8 }$ to between $1 0 ^ { 6 }$ and $1 0 ^ { 7 }$ CFU per gram (Maule, 2000). The same group showed that the strain survived as an air-dried deposit on stainless-steel surfaces for more than 60 days. Warburton et al. published that E. coli O157:H7 can persist for 300 days in artificially contaminated bottled drinking water stored at ambient temperature $( 2 2 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } )$ (Warburton et al., 1998). However, due to the complexity of water composition, data obtained from undefined sources like municipal water hardly allow to infer general conclusions on survival rates (Aurass et al., 2011). 

Like in water, multiple studies show that enterohemorrhagic or nonpathogenic E. coli O157:H7 is capable of surviving several months in soil (Islam et al., 2004; Ma et $a l . ,$ 2014; Oliveira et al., 2012). The pathogen remained culturable for more than 226 days in manure-amended, sterilized soil with CFU/g dropping from approximately $1 0 ^ { 6 }$ to $1 0 ^ { 3 } .$ . The strain survived best at $1 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ slightly less efficiently at $2 1 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } ,$ and less efficient at $5 ^ { \circ } C$ (reached detection limit at <80 days). In nonsterilized soil, continuously less bacteria were culturable than under sterilized conditions (Jiang et al., 2002). A similar laboratory-based study described that a reduction in the total microbial load in environmental water samples supported persistence of EHEC O157:H7 (EDL933), O91:H21 (B2F1), and O14:H4 (LB226692) within the investigated timeframe of 110 h. This study, however, used quantitative polymerase chain reaction (qPCR) to determine persistence via relative quantification of E. coli DNA (Mauro et al., 2013). Thus, competition with or repression by present microbiota, as well as abiotic factors like pH, moisture, organic matter content, and surface or soil texture, likely constitute important determinants for persistence of newly introduced bacteria (Gagliardi and Karns, 2000; Semenov et al., 2008; Wang et al., 2014; Williams et al., 2005). Likewise, biofilms may be important for increased persistence and higher resistance to fumigation by STEC (reviewed in Vogeleer et al., 2014). 

Moreover, prevalence of STEC O157:H7 in cattle herds fluctuates between high prevalence during summer and early fall and very low levels in winter (Douglas and Kurien, 1997; Edrington et al., 2006; Fraser et al., 2013). A recent study used topological data analysis to study survival of stx-containing O157 and non-O157 strains in 32 soils from California or Arizona on a complex community level (Ibekwe et al., 2014). This approach allows integration of environmental parameters with community structure and survival of the pathogen. Herein, an $\mathit { s t x } ^ { - }$ O157:H7 isolate persisted significantly longer than the stx+ EDL933 strain of serotype O157:H7; moreover, two non-O157 strains survived longer than O157 strains, showing strain-specific differences that depend on microbial community structures and soil parameters. However, in a previous study, the authors found that the presence of stx and intimin genes (an important adhesin encoded on the LEE pathogenicity island) may not be important factors for survival of E. coli O157:H7 EDL933 in soil, as demonstrated with isogenic mutants (Ma et al., 2011). Ibekwe et al. determined survival of EHEC O157:H7 for more than 90 days even under adverse conditions of chemical fumigation with high concentrations of methyl iodide and methyl bromide in artificial microcosms (Ibekwe et al., 2007). 

Taken together, the manifold available studies demonstrate that Shiga toxin–producing E. coli are equipped to survive and persist for long periods in various natural and human-made environments. It also is evident that the chance to successfully persist is highly dependent on complex and interacting environmental contributions, which have implications for risk assessment and careful agricultural working practice. Most studies use culturability and colony counting or conventional DNA-based qPCR as a proxy for survival, although numerous studies exist on nonsporulating bacteria (including pathogenic E. coli) entering a nonculturable state, usually referred to as the VBNC state, after experiencing stress (Asakura et al., 2008; Aurass et al., 2011; Li et al., 2014; Liu et al., 2009, 2010). This in part is likely due to difficulties in handling and analyzing bacteria that temporarily do not grow on standard media. However, VBNC bacteria should not be ignored and instead should be taken into consideration as they provide sources for reemergence of pathogens after possible resuscitation, as well as in preservation of genetic information of pathogens in the complex environment. 

## 23.3.4 The E. coli VBNC state

Bacteria that lost their ability to grow on routine media on which they would normally grow, but that retain distinctive signs of life that distinguish them from dead bacteria, are considered VBNC (Oliver, 2010; Xu et al., 1982). The critical point is to identify and interpret these distinguishing properties of life correctly, a task that is complicated by the multifactorial nature of circumstances leading to inactivation, deterioration, or killing of bacteria, and the difficulty to precisely define bacterial cell death (Bogosian and Bourneuf, 2001). Multiple structural or physiological cellular traits have been used as a marker of life, such as intact cell membranes, respiration, messenger RNA or ribosomal RNA synthesis, and protein expression. However, the ultimate proof of viability constitutes the ability of VBNC cells to become culturable again in response to various stimuli. The stimuli seem to depend on the circumstances that led the cells to become VBNC and include diverse variations of stress relief, animal or single-cell-amoeba passage, and the action of resuscitation-facilitating molecules, such as autoinducers (AIs) (Liu et al., 2009; Reissbrodt et al., 2002) (see Chapter 23.1) or resuscitation-promoting factors (Rpfs) (Mukamolova et al., 1998, 2002, 2006) (see Chapter 23.2). The latter are wellestablished muralytic signaling molecules in Micrococcus luteus and mycobacterial dormancy that stimulate conversion of formerly dormant to vegetative cells (Mukamolova et al., 1998, 2002, 2006). On the basis of gene expression studies and investigations on Rpf deletion mutants in mice, it has been suspected that Rpfs play a role in reactivation of latent tuberculosis (Tufariello et al., 2004, 2006), however, this and a possible role of Rpfs for resuscitation of environmental mycobacteria are uncertain. In Gram-negative bacteria, the presence and resuscitation activity of another unrelated protein of unknown activity, also termed Rpf, have been reported also (Panutdaporn et al., 2006). However, subsequent studies are currently missing. 

Bacteria in the VBNC state are prevalent in the environment. They have been found in diverse surroundings (e.g., seawater and freshwater bodies, soil, and food) (Byrd et al., 1991; Maalej et al., 2004; Pawlowski et al., 2011; Xu et al., 1982). Moreover, VBNC cells of STEC O157:H7 have been induced in the phyllosphere of lettuce (Dinu and Bach, 2011). IPEC in numerous studies has been shown to enter a VBNC state in vitro (Asakura et al., 2008; Grey and Steck, 2001; McKay, 1992; Mizunoe et al., 1999; Pinto et al., 2011; Wang and Doyle, 1998; Yaron and Matthews, 2002). Makino observed entry of STEC O157:H7 in the presence of high salt concentrations (13% NaCl) into the VBNC state, an observation with implications in food preservation (Makino et al., 2000). Furthermore, it has been shown that EHEC O157:H7 may survive for more than 10 months after entry into the VBNC state in river water by detecting mRNA of characteristic genes (rfbE and fliC) (Liu et al., 2009). Strikingly, the continued presence of Shiga toxin gene transcripts has been observed in 90-day-old distilled water microcosms containing solely VBNC EHEC O157:H7, suggesting ongoing expression of the toxin (Yaron and Matthews, 2002). Liu confirmed expression of stx1 and stx2 in VBNC STEC O157:H7, and furthermore showed the presence of Stx in aged (17 months old, induced by river water) and freshly induced (by chloraminated water) VBNC cells by means of Stx enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay (ELISA) and Vero cell toxicity assay (Liu et al., 2010). Moreover, Voigt et al. showed Shiga toxin production by EHEC strains of various serotypes is triggered by the presence of enterobacterial heat-stable autoinducer of growth (AI) (Voigt et al., 2006), a signaling molecule previously implicated in resuscitation of VBNC EHEC (Reissbrodt et al., 2002). These combined results implicate a benefit for STEC in holding Stx available when persisting. In the context of Stx involvement in coordinating STEC interaction with amoebae (Barker et al., 1999; Chekabab et al., 2012; King et al., 1988), this points not only to a role in the sense of an antipredator weapon (Lainhart et al., 2009), but further to a possible role of Stx as a colonization factor of soil protozoa. 

Taken together, it appears likely that VBNC forms of IPEC are prevalent and a source for transmission of (by means of culture) undetectable pathogens. Our previously published results on the EHEC–EAEC O104:H4 strain linked to the 2011 outbreak in 

Germany underline this assumption due to its capacity to persist in water and enter a VBNC state (Aurass et al., 2011).

EHEC–EAEC strains such as the 2011 O104:H4 outbreak strain do not represent a classical EHEC on which some data on persistence in different microenvironments are available. Therefore, we aimed to investigate its behavior upon incubation in aqueous environments.

In previous work, we presented data on the persistence of the EHEC–EAEC O104:H4 outbreak strain (RKI 11-02027) in different water microcosms over a timeframe of 40 days (Aurass et al., 2011). The strain was inoculated in buffered saline with or without copper ions $( 5 0 0 ~ \mu \mathrm { M } )$ and tap water from two different origins. To test whether temperature influences survival, we stored the microcosms at two different temperatures, 23 and $4 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ . We found that in a nutrient-poor microenvironment, various stresses such as toxic concentrations of copper ions or certain types of tap water are able to render the bacteria unculturable within a few days. Specifically, samples with copper ions completely lost culturability after 5 days at $4 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ and after 3 days at $2 3 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ This shows that incubation with stressors at $2 3 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ compared with $4 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ hastened this observed loss of culturability. Without copper ion stress, the majority of cells remained culturable for at least 40 days. The integrity of a considerable fraction of unculturable copper ion– and tap water–stressed bacteria was demonstrated by live–dead staining and microscopy. Remarkably, a fraction of VBNC cells produced at $4 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ resuscitated after chelation of copper ions (i.e., stress relief) to again become culturable, thereby retaining fitness and phenotypic and genotypic traits of the parental strain, such as stx2, AAF-related genes, and specific phenotypes (ESBL resistance and auto-aggregation typical for EAEC). In agreement with other studies on EHEC O157:H7, we found that low temperature promoted survival of the O104:H4 strain in a culturable state compared to the $2 3 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ condition (Wang and Doyle, 1998). We further observed that intact bacteria of the outbreak strain in unculturable microcosms tended to enrich in microaggregations, which presumably depend on AAF/I fimbriae encoded on $\mathrm { \ p A A }$ . This leads to the assumption that the aggregative phenotype might support persistence of EAEC or EHEC–EAEC not only in the gut but also in aqueous environments.

## 23.3.6 Conclusions



Brandl MT. 2006. Fitness of human enteric pathogens on plants and implications for food safety. Annual Review of Phytopathology 44: 367–392. 





Iyoda S, Tamura K, Itoh K, Izumiya H, Ueno N, et al. 2000. Inducible stx2 phages are lysogenized in the enteroaggregative and other phenotypic Escherichia coli O86:HNM isolated from patients. FEMS Microbiology Letters 191(1): 7–10. 





Oliver JD. 2010. Recent findings on the viable but nonculturable state in pathogenic bacteria. FEMS Microbiology Reviews 34(4): 415–425. 



Reviewing PubMed entries linked to environmental persistence, survival, or prevalence of IPEC shows that E. coli constitutes an extraordinarily multifaceted and opportunistic bacterium. This is reflected in its broad repertoire of serotypes and pathotypes, and additionally in its universal distribution. IPEC have been isolated from diverse waters, soils, and animals. Moreover, the bacteria are equipped to persist and multiply within protozoa and in the nematode C. elegans, both of which may serve as a reservoir for transmission in the environment and in humanmade ecological niches. Its interaction with $( { \mathrm { i . e . } }$ , adhesion to, persistence on, or even invasion in) plant tissue is of outstanding importance, as transmission via the “green route” is becoming increasingly evident. It is a frequent cause of large outbreaks that in the past mostly were attributed to foodborne sources, but there is increasing evidence on the importance of environmental exposure as a second major health threat. Its environmental fitness and capacity to survive adverse conditions have been shown to depend on numerous biotic and abiotic variables, making it challenging to fight contamination. IPEC may render culture undetectable by becoming $\mathrm { { V B N C } , }$ a circumstance that should become routinely addressed in studies focused on persistence of E. coli. Assessing the degree of environmental prevalence and persistence by means of comparable sampling techniques is needed and will improve our understanding of the environmental biology of this outstanding pathogen. Furthermore, the specific virulence gene set of different IPEC pathovars might majorly influence persistence properties and should be taken into consideration.

# Of a tenacious and versatile relic: the role of inorganic polyphosphate (poly-P) metabolism in the survival, adaptation, and virulence of Campylobacter jejuni

Issmat I. Kassem and Gireesh Rajashekara 

Food Animal Health Research Program, Ohio Agricultural Research and Development Center, Department of Veterinary Preventive Medicine, The Ohio State University, Wooster, Ohio, USA 

## 23.4.1 Of Campylobacter jejuni and humans

Campylobacter jejuni is a Gram-negative, microaerophilic bacterium that is considered a leading cause of human foodborne gastroenteritis worldwide (On, 1996; Vandamme and De Ley, 1991). This pathogen is occasionally associated with debilitating and life-threatening neuropathies such as the Guillain–Barre´ syndrome (On, 1996; Vandamme and De Ley, 1991). In developed countries, C. jejuni is primarily contracted via the consumption of contaminated foods. Poultry, in particular, constitute a major source for the transmission of C. jejuni to humans (Hermans et al., 2011; Wagenaar et al., 2008). This is not surprising, because C. jejuni can readily and efficiently colonize the guts of broiler chickens and turkeys. Colonized birds can harbor (≥108 CFU per g of ceca) and shed (>106 CFU per g of feces) high numbers of Campylobacter (Hermans et al., 2011; Wagenaar et al., 2008). Once the first population and group of birds are colonized, rapid transmission occurs, potentially contaminating the entire flock within a matter of days (Shreeve et al., 2000). The control of C. jejuni in poultry and other food animals has proved to be challenging due in part to the atypical pathobiology and not fully understood stress response mechanisms of this bacterium. For example, C. jejuni is considered fastidious, requiring microaerophilic environments to flourish; however, this pathogen is prevalent in disparate niches. Furthermore, C. jejuni lacks classical stress response mechanisms and virulence factors associated with other enteric pathogens such as a type III secretion system and an RNA polymerase sigma S factor (RpoS)-mediated global stress response mechanism (Parkhill et al., 2000). These properties of C. jejuni necessitate a closer examination of all its possible survival strategies in order to reduce this pathogen in the food chain. Here, the metabolism of inorganic polyphosphate (poly-P) and its potential impacts on C. jejuni’s survival, adaptation, and control are discussed. 

## 23.4.2 Enters poly-P: one molecule to rule them all

Inorganic polyphosphate (poly-P) is a linear polymer composed of orthophosphate (Pi) residues that are linked together by high-energy phosphoanhydride bonds (Kornberg et al., 1999). Today, poly-P is present in every known cell in nature (prokaryotic, eukaryotic, animal, and plant cells) (Kulaev and Vagabov, 1983). However, poly-P was first observed as a metachromatic granule in the cytoplasm of the bacterium Spirillum volutans and was called “volutin” (Achbergerova and Nah´ alka, 2011;´ Kornberg, 1995). In bacteria, poly-P can be localized on the cell surface and in the periplasm, the cell membrane, and the cytoplasm (Achbergerova and Nah´ alka, 2011). The cellular localiza-´ tion might influence the disparate biological functions associated with poly-P (Kornberg, 1995). 

The poly-P granule efficiently stores energy and phosphate (Achbergerova and Nah´ alka, 2011; Kornberg, 1995). It follows´ that this granule constitutes an important source of energy in the cell, providing adenosine triphosphate (ATP) for ∼500 reactions (Rao et al., 2009) that include phosphorylation of sugars, nucleosides, and proteins and the activation of the precursors of fatty acids, phospholipids, polypeptides, and nucleic acids. poly-P also contributes to a plethora of other functions and cellular mechanisms, such as chelation of metal ions (Archibald and Fridovich, 1982), buffering alkali ions (Pick and Weiss, 1991), gene regulation (Tsutsumi et al., 2000), the entry of DNA through membrane channels, capsule composition, maintaining nutritional requirements during starvation, fidelity of DNA replication, growth, motility, biofilm formation, quorum sensing, bacterial signaling, antibiotic resistance, stationary phase survival, virulence, invasion and intracellular survival and host colonization, as well as resistance to a wide variety of other stresses (Kornberg et al., 1999; Rao et al., 2009; Rao and Kornberg, 1999; Rashid and Kornberg, 2000; Rashid et al., 2000a,b). While this staggering list of functions highlights the scope of the importance of poly-P in the survival, adaptation, and success of bacteria, it also emphasizes the need to understand the mechanisms that govern poly-P-associated contributions to the cell. 

The metabolism of poly-P is facilitated by several enzymes, mainly the polyphosphate kinases (PPKs) and exopolyphosphatases (PPXs) (Akiyama et al., 1992; Brown and Kornberg, 2008; Ishige et al., 2002; Rao et al., 2009; Rao and Kornberg, 1999). The PPKs (PPK1 and PPK2) are widely conserved in bacteria, including major human pathogens (Akiyama et al., 1992; Brown and Kornberg, 2008; Ishige et al., 2002; Rao et al., 2009; Rao and Kornberg, 1999). However, it should be noted that while most bacterial species possess homologs for both PPKs, either PPK1 or PPK2 homologs have been detected in some species (Rao et al., 2009). Polyphosphate kinase 1 (PPK1) is highly conserved and is the most studied of the poly-P-associated enzymes (Kornberg et al., 1956; Rao et al., 2009). PPK1 is the principal enzyme responsible for synthesis of poly-P (Akiyama et al., 1992; Zhang et al., 2007) via catalyzing the reversible polymerization of the terminal phosphate of ATP into a linear poly-P chain (Akiyama et al., 1992; Rao et al., 2009). In comparison, PPK2 preferentially mediates poly-P-driven generation of guanosine triphosphate (GTP) (Ishige et al., 2002; Kuroda and Kornberg, 1997), a molecule known to have important roles in cell signaling and DNA, RNA, protein, and polysaccharide synthesis. Additionally, the PPXs mediate the release of Pi via hydrolyzing the terminal residues of poly-P (Brown and Kornberg, 2008). Notably, an exopolyphosphatase identified in E. coli is guanosine pentaphosphate (pppGpp) hydrolase (PPX2– GPPA) (Keasling et al., 1993; Rao et al., 2009), which facilitates the production of the alarmone guanosine tetraphosphate (ppGpp) and, subsequently, plays a role in the bacterial stringent response (Kornberg, 1995) (see Chapter 7.1). 

The PPKs and PPXs have been associated with several important phenotypes in bacteria (Akiyama et al., 1992; Brown and Kornberg, 2008; Chuang et al., 2013; Ishige et al., 2002; Rao et al., 2009; Rao and Kornberg, 1999). For example, null mutants of ppk1 generated in Pseudomonas aeruginosa (PA01), an opportunistic human pathogen of the immunocompromised and those afflicted with cystic fibrosis, exhibit defects in motility on semisolid agar plates, quorum sensing (see Section 21), formation of thick and differentiated biofilms (see Section 22), and virulence in a burned-mouse model (Rashid et al., 2000a,b). Furthermore, nucleoid compaction, cell envelope distortion, susceptibility to a β-lactam antibiotic, and desiccation (see Section 12) have been observed in a P. aeruginosa ppk1 mutant (Fraley et al., 2007). Additionally, a ppk1 deletion mutant of the meningitic Escherichia coli K1 strain E44 is defective in survival under osmotic (see Section 11) and acidic (see Section 16) stresses as well as in adhesion and the ability to cross the blood–brain barrier of rats (Peng et al., 2012). In Mycobacterium smegmatis, the impairment of ppk1 negatively impacts the synthesis of ppGpp, the stringent response regulator, and affects the ability of this bacterium to survive under prolonged hypoxia (Sureka et al., 2007). As for ppk2, insertion mutagenesis of this gene affects the ability of M. tuberculosis, the bacterium that causes tuberculosis, to survive in J774 macrophages and mouse lungs (Chuang et al., 2013). Sureka et al. (2009) showed that a ppk2 knockout of M. smegmatis is deficient in survival under heat, acid, or hypoxic stress. It has also been shown that PPK2 contributes to the regulation of the intracellular nucleotide pool, because the intracellular ATP–GTP ratio was higher in the ppk2 knockout in comparison with the wildtype M. smegmatis strain (Sureka et al., 2009). Similar to the PPKs, it has been reported that the impairment of the expression of a PPX encoding gene (ppx2) in M. tuberculosis results in poly-P accumulation and slows the growth of the bacterium (Chuang et al., 2013). The manipulation of the expression of ppx2 also leads to enhanced survival under heat and acidic pH and in macrophages, and increased cell wall thickness. The latter has been associated with reduced susceptibility to isoniazid, possibly due to reduced cell wall permeability (Chuang et al., 2013). A ppx null mutant generated in P. aeruginosa showed that the cognate exopolyphosphatase is involved in facilitating virulence such as the production of quorum-sensing homoserine lactones and biofilm formation (Gallarato et al., 2014; see Sections 21 and 22). Furthermore, studies on the human pathogen Neisseria meningitidis show that a mutant strain that lacked PPX is more resistant to complement-mediated killing in comparison to the parental strain (Zhang et al., 2010). Taken together, the aforementioned examples highlight an important role for poly-P, the PPKs, and the PPXs in the survival, adaptation, and virulence of important bacterial pathogens. 

## 23.4.3 Revelations on a recalcitrant pathogen: poly-P metabolism and the survival and adaptation of Campylobacter jejuni

The interest in the role of poly-P and cognate metabolic enzymes in the pathogenesis of C. jejuni has been piqued by the following observations: (a) Virulence and stress response mechanisms of C. jejuni differ in comparison to those of other conventional enteric pathogens. This necessitates exploring all possible factors that might contribute to the success of C. jejuni as a pathogen. (b) Homologs for polyphosphate kinases (PPK1 and PPK2) and exopolyphosphatases (PPX1 and PPX2) are detected in the sequenced genomes of C. jejuni strains (Gangaiah et al., 2009, 2010; Kassem and Rajashekara, 2011; Malde et al., 2014; Parkhill et al., 2000). C. jejuni has a relatively small genome, and it is expected that smaller genomes would retain relatively more essential genes and fewer auxiliary genes (Friis et al., 2010; Raskin et al., 2006). Therefore, functional genes encoding the PPKs and PPXs are possibly important in C. jejuni. (c) Studies have shown that the disruption of the PPKs or the PPXs can affect stress-associated phenotypes in C. jejuni (see details further in this chapter); and (d) the pleiotropic effects that are usually associated with impaired accumulation of poly-P in other pathogens represent an appealing possibility for novel therapeutic controls that specifically target poly-P-associated enzymes in C. jejuni. The latter is of particular significance in light of increasing resistance of C. jejuni to clinically important antibiotics. Taken together, these observations suggest that characterizing poly-P metabolism in C. jejuni constitutes a cornerstone of unharnessed potentials (Kassem and Rajashekara, 2011). 

The first study showed that poly-P accumulation and ppk1 contribute to C. jejuni pathogenesis was published in 2007 (Candon et al., 2007). Specifically, the authors showed that the deletion of ppk1 results in decreased levels of poly-P in the mutant cells of C. jejuni during all growth phases. The ppk1 mutant strain also exhibits increased susceptibility to osmotic and nutrient stresses, a decreased capacity for intracellular survival in epithelial cells in vitro, and a dose-dependent defect in chicken colonization. The pleiotropic effects associated with impairment of ppk1 in C. jejuni suggest a focal role and possibly a regulatory contribution for poly-P in mechanisms that facilitate the survival of this pathogen. For example, it has been reported that ppk1 of C. jejuni contributes to modulating different stages of biofilm formation (Drozd et al., 2014) (see Section 22). Furthermore, the deletion of ppk1 impacts the expression of genes associated with biofilm formation, including polysaccharide and flagellar biosynthesis genes (Drozd et al., 2014). Notably, the overexpression of csrA (global posttranscriptional regulator), which contributes to biofilm formation and oxidative resistance in C. jejuni (Fields and Thompson, 2008; see Sections 22 and 10), has also been reported in a C. jejuni ppk1 mutant (Gangaiah et al., 2009). This might explain the tolerance of the C. jejuni ppk1 mutant to oxidative stress and its increased capacity for biofilm formation (Gangaiah et al., 2009). 

In other organisms, poly-P accumulation has been observed in response to stresses and changing environmental conditions. Subsequently, it is notable to observe that the deletion of spoT, a stringent response regulator that encodes a bifunctional ppGpp synthetase–hydrolase, also resulted in decreased poly-P levels in C. jejuni at all growth phases (Candon et al., 2007; Dalebroux et al., 2010). spoT facilitates the synthesis of guanosine tetraphosphate (ppGpp), which allows organisms to deal with stress by altering transcription (Dalebroux et al., 2010) (see Section 7). Since the deletion of spoT was associated with the simultaneous upregulation of genes associated with inorganic phosphate uptake, a relationship between the stringent response and poly-P might exist in C. jejuni (Candon et al., 2007; Gangaiah et al., 2009). An association between spoT and poly-P has also been suggested after observing that spoT and the genes of the phosphate regulon are overexpressed in a C. jejuni ppk1 deletion mutant (Gangaiah et al., 2009). Taken together, these observations are important, because a relationship between PPK1 and SpoT and ppGpp in the context of stress response might contribute to understanding the biology of C. jejuni as well as emphasize a possible regulatory function for poly-P and associated enzymes in this pathogen (Kassem and Rajashekara, 2011). 

The role of ppk2 in mediating key C. jejuni responses has been investigated for the first time in 2010 (Gangaiah et al., 2010). The deletion of ppk2 impaired growth under osmotic, nutrient, and antimicrobial stresses and decreased the strain’s capacity for chicken colonization and intracellular survival in a human intestinal epithelial cell line (Gangaiah et al., 2010). Like ppk1, ppk2 contributes to modulating biofilm formation in C. jejuni (Drozd et al., 2014). Furthermore, the deletion of ppk2 results in a significant decrease in poly-P-dependent GTP synthesis and an increase in intracellular ATP–GTP ratio. The impact on GTP levels would have serious ramifications on cell physiology, including possible effects on glycoconjugates such as glycosylated proteins. This is plausible because PO and NTPs, particularly GTP, are needed to generate cell wall glycoconjugates. In C. jejuni, defects in glycosylation can impact host colonization and cell invasion as well as natural transformation capabilities (Guerry et al., 2006; Larsen et al., 2004; Szymanski et al., 2002). Interestingly, a ppk2 deletion mutant has altered glycoprotein profiles compared to the wild type (WT), which correlates with defects in virulence properties, mainly the invasion of a human intestinal epithelial cell line (Pina-Mimbela et al., 2015). Therefore, it has been hypothesized that PPK2 might modulate C. jejuni protein glycosylation and, consequently, regulate the colonization and virulence of this pathogen. 

The C. jejuni ppk2 mutant is not defective in poly-P and ppGpp synthesis, suggesting that PPK2-mediated stress tolerance is not associated with ppGpp (Gangaiah et al., 2010). This might be of paramount importance, because this suggests, at least partially, that the PPK2-associated responses might be different and/or complementary to those observed for PPK1. This increases the scope and importance of the PPKs in the pathobiology of C. jejuni. 

Recently, high-throughput RNA-seq analysis of the C. jejuni ppk1 and ppk2 deletion mutants has been performed (Chandrashekhar et al., 2015). Differentially expressed genes include genes that encode flagellin and flagellar components, heat shock response proteins, as well as genes associated with glycosylation, phosphate uptake, and protein stabilization and synthesis. The results of the sequencing analysis confirm the multifaceted contributions of poly-P and the PPKs to diverse cellular functions and highlight their potential regulatory role in C. jejuni. 

The contribution of ppx1 and ppx2 to the pathogenesis of C. jejuni has recently been reported (Malde et al., 2014). Increased accumulation of poly-P has been noted in ppx1 and ppx2 deletion mutants (Malde et al., 2014). Like the ppks, the deletion of the ppx resulted in pleiotropic phenotypes, which include defects in motility, biofilm formation, nutrient stress survival, and invasion and intracellular survival in human intestinal epithelial cells. However, both ppx1 and ppx2 mutants are less susceptible to complement-mediated killing by human serum (Malde et al., 2014). Notably, the ppx1 mutant possesses decreased levels of ppGpp, which suggested that the PPX1 in C. jejuni might be functionally similar to exopolyphosphatase–guanosine pentaphosphate (pppGpp) hydrolase (PPX2–GPPA) in E. coli (Keasling et al., 1993; Kornberg, 1995; Rao et al., 2009). Furthermore, ppk1 and spoT are upregulated in both ppx1 and ppx2 mutants, which highlights a relationship between poly-P, PPK1, and ppGpp metabolism and the cognate stringent response (see Section 7). Based on the studies listed here, it is becoming more apparent that characterization of poly-P metabolism and cognate enzymes will shed new light on the survival, adaptation, and virulence of C. jejuni. 

## 23.4.4 Survival incognito: of energy, poly-P, and VBNC formation in C. jejuni

Viable but nonculturable cells (VBNCs) are broadly defined as cells that enter a nonculturable state in response to stress while maintaining a detectable but reduced metabolism, relatively high ATP levels, and aspects of cellular integrity such as intact chromosome content and cell membrane (Gregori´ et al., 2001; Oliver, 2005, 2010). VBNCs occasionally exhibit changes in cell morphology, most commonly a “rounding up,” and a reduction in the size of the cells, which is thought to maximize the surface area available for nutrient uptake while minimizing cell mass (Colwell, 2000; Oliver, 2005). Furthermore, the formation of VBNCs might constitute a mechanism by which bacteria can survive for prolonged periods under unfavorable conditions (Jackson et al., 2009). VBNCs, as the name implies, cannot be cultured or isolated using standard culture-based techniques (Colwell, 2000; Oliver, 2005). Since VBNCs can arguably retain virulence and can be resuscitated back to a “normal” culturable physiological state under favorable conditions (Oliver, 2005, 2010) (see Chapters 23.1 and 23.2), the VBNC state may potentially contribute to undetectable contamination and the spread of infectious agents to susceptible hosts (Oliver, 2005, 2010). This is particularly relevant when considering the possibility of VBNC contamination of food products (Dinu and Bach, 2011; Rowan, 2004) and medical equipment (Zandri et al., 2012), which can go undetected, jeopardizing food safety and threatening the lives of susceptible patients. 

It is fairly well documented that C. jejuni can form VBNCs under stress (Jackson et al., 2009; Rollins and Colwell, 1986). It has also been shown that C. jejuni VBNCs can colonize suckling mice (Jones et al., 1991). C. jejuni VBNCs can also adhere to the skin of chicken carcasses (Jang et al., 2007) and express a protein (CadF) that facilitates attachment to host cells (Patrone et al., 2013). Furthermore, genes encoding flagellin proteins, the cytolethal distending toxin, and a Campylobacter invasion antigen that are involved in invading or interacting with the host’s intestinal cells maintain a low level of expression in C. jejuni VBNCs (Chaisowwong et al., 2012). Despite these observations, it should be noted that most of the research that investigated C. jejuni VBNCs mainly describes the physical, chemical, and environmental triggers that induce this state, such as exposure to oxygen, persistence in aquatic microcosms, changes in temperature and pH, and nutrient starvation (Jackson et al., 2009, Kassem et al., 2013). In addition, research efforts has also focused on strategies for resuscitation of C. jejuni VBNCs in vitro or in vivo (Bovill and Mackey, 1997; Cappelier et al., 1999; Chaveerach et al., 2003; Baffone et al., 2006, Kassem et al., 2013). However, like in many VBNC-forming bacteria, the genetic factors or mechanisms that facilitate the formation of C. jejuni VBNCs are still largely unknown (Kassem et al., 2013). 

The investigation of a relationship between poly-P and VBNC formation in C. jejuni was prompted by noticing that VBNC formation is mainly for energy conservation and that poly-P is an energy source (Kassem et al., 2013; Kassem and Rajashekara, 2011). Therefore, it is conceivable that poly-P and associated enzymes might play a role in VBNC formation in C. jejuni. This synthesis has been further confirmed by studies showing that poly-P occurs in starved and morphologically altered Vibrio parahaemolyticus (Chen et al., 2009) and in structurally intact coccoid forms of starved Helicobacter pylori (Nilsson et al., 2002). However, no direct mechanistic association between poly-P and the formation of VBNCs has been described previously (Kassem et al., 2013). Recently, it has been shown that the deletion of ppk1 and ppk2 might impact VBNC formation in C. jejuni (Gangaiah et al., 2009, 2010). Specifically, live–dead cell-staining analysis showed that the C. jejuni ppk1 and ppk2 deletion mutants possess a significantly reduced ability to form VBNCs after challenge with formic acid (Gangaiah et al., 2009, 2010). This has been confirmed using flow cytometry analysis that shows a significant change in the cell size and granularity of the ppk1 deletion mutant in comparison with the wild-type strain (Gangaiah et al., 2009), possibly indicating an increase in dead cells in the acid-stressed mutants (Kusters et al., 1997). In the ppk1 mutant, the expression of the phosphate regulon genes (phosR, pstS, pstC, and the periplasmic substrate binding protein-encoding gene, CJJ81176 0750), the multidrug resistance efflux pump gene (cmeC), the global posttranscriptional regulator (csrA), and the stringent response regulator (spoT) are not affected in the acid-stressed mutant cells (Gangaiah et al., 2009). However, ppk2 is significantly downregulated in the formic acid–challenged ppk1 mutant, but the expression of ppk2 is not affected in similarly treated wild-type cells, which further implicates the ppks in C. jejuni’s VBNC formation (Gangaiah et al., 2009). Since the ppk1 mutant is deficient in the accumulation of poly-P and the ppk2 downregulation would possibly reduce the associated GTP production (Gangaiah et al., 2009), the poly-P-dependent ATP–GTP pools and ratios in the ppk mutants might be deficient compared to that of the wildtype strain. This assumption is supported by the lower levels of poly-P-dependent GTP and the higher ATP–GTP ratios that have been detected in the cells of the ppk2 mutant (Gangaiah et al., 2010). Therefore, it appears that the disruption of the poly-P-associated enzymes alters the cellular poly-P-dependent ATP– GTP homeostasis, which affects the ability of C. jejuni to form VBNCs. Although further studies are needed to firmly delineate the mechanisms that govern the relationship between poly-P metabolism and VBNC formation, the advances highlighted here are novel and unique and will facilitate future research on VBNC formation (Kassem et al., 2013). For example, the ppk2 mutant exhibits reduced VBNC formation when challenged with formic acid but not other organic acids (acetic acid and propionic acid) and hydrochloric acid (Gangaiah et al., 2010). This observation has prompted investigations into a possible relationship between formic acid and VBNC formation in C. jejuni. Furthermore, since ATP–GTP pools are not only affected by poly-P and its enzymes, cellular features that might be linked to the metabolism of energy and formic acid have been sought. It was noted that C. jejuni possesses a highly branched respiratory chain that serves in energy production (Myers and Kelly, 2004), whereas one of the major energy sources for this bacterium is formate that is metabolized by the periplasmic respiratory protein, formate dehydrogenase (Hoffman and Goodman, 1982; Kassem and Rajashekara, 2014; Weerakoon et al., 2009). The inactivation of the formate dehydrogenase (fdh) in C. jejuni results in round cells similar to those associated with the VBNCs (Kassem et al., 2013); however, the fdh mutant does not lose culturability in a nutrient-rich medium (Kassem et al., 2012). Furthermore, when the fdh mutant is challenged with formic acid, as described in this chapter, it shows a significant decrease in culturability and viability as compared to the wild type, indicating a severe inability to form VBNCs (Kassem et al., 2013). Therefore, formate dehydrogenase and formate metabolism might be associated with VBNC formation in C. jejuni, likely via their role in energy production. Subsequently, it might be of benefit to investigate whether the energy produced by formate metabolism may be linked to poly-P accumulation and degradation and whether a cognate link exists between VBNC formation phenotypes in the fdh and ppk mutants. 

## 23.4.5 The last act: conclusions and closing remarks



Chen SY, Jane WN, Chen YS, Wong HC. 2009. Morphological changes of Vibrio parahaemolyticus under cold and starvation stresses. International Journal of Food Microbiology 129(2): 157–165. 





Bacteria: diversity of prokaryotic respiratory systems (ed. Zannoni D). Boston: Kluwer Academic, pp. 63–77. 



It appears that poly-P metabolism and associated enzymes have a wide and thorough effect on the biology of C. jejuni (Kassem and Rajashekara, 2011). Furthermore, since the disruption of poly-P associated enzymes resulted in pleiotropic phenotypes, it might be worth targeting these enzymes to develop novel therapeutics against C. jejuni. As mentioned in this chapter, poly-P and cognate enzymes occur in many bacterial species (Kornberg et al., 1999; Rao et al., 2009), and many of these species have a confirmed ability to enter the VBNC state (Kassem et al., 2013). This increases the case for a possible poly-P link as a potential universal phenomenon that governs the formation of VBNC. While the latter needs further experimental proof, there is adequate evidence to suggest that poly-P contributes to VBNC formation in C. jejuni. Finally, the bearings of the findings discussed here are important for efforts that aim at reducing C. jejuni in the food chain, because they highlight some of the strategies adopted by this remarkable pathogen to cope with a variety of stresses and persist in the host–environment continuum.
